Electrical Failure and Damage Analysis of  Multi-layer Metal Films on Flexible Substrate  during Cyclic Bending Deformation


Byoung-Joon Kim1, Hae-A-Seul Shin1, In-Suk Choi2, and Young-Chang Joo1*


1) Department of Materials Science & Engineering, Seoul National University, Seoul, 151-744, Korea 2) Korea Institute of Science and Technology, Seoul, 136-791, Korea  Tel. No.+82-2-880-8986; Fax No. +82-2-880-8197;


E-mail: *ycjoo@snu.ac.kr


Abstract- The electrical resistance Cu film on flexible substrate was


investigated in cyclic bending deformation. The electrical


resistance of 1 m thick Cu film on flexible substrate increased up


to 120 % after 500,000 cycles in 1.1 % tensile bending strain.


Crack and extrusion were observed due to the fatigue damage of Cu films were deposited on the 125 um thick polyimide (PI)metal film. Low bending strain did not cause any damage on metal  film but higher bending strain resulted in severe electrical and


mechanical damage. Thinner film showed higher fatigue resistance and I um. The sample was cut by 4 mm width and 60 mm length.


because of the better mechanical property of thin film. Cu film


with NiCr under-layer showed poorer fatigue resistance in tensile


bending mode. Ni capping layer did not improve the fatigue


resistance of Cu film, but Al capping layer suppressed crack


formation and lowered electrical resistance change. The NiCr


under layer, Ni capping layer, and Al capping layer effect on


electrical resistance change of Cu film was compared with Cu only


sample.


I. INTRODUCTION


The flexible electronics will be adopted at various purposes


such as flexible display, flexible battery, and e-skin. The


research on the mechanical fracture of metal film on flexible


substrate was performed widely to enhance the stretchability of


metal film [1-3]. The electrical failure of the flexible electronics


by the repeating deformation has been one of the main reliability


issues since the metal interconnects on the flexible substrates


experience bending, stretching, and twisting during their usage.


The electrical resistance changes of metal thin films under


monotonic tensile deformation or cyclic stretching have been


studied by several research groups, but the main focus was on films were deposited on the 188 um thick polyethylene the failure of metal thin films under uniaxial cyclic deformation.


Besides this monotonic tensile deformation, the repeated


deformations of metal interconnect also lead to resistance


increase prior to mechanical failures [4, 5]. Mechanical fatigue


damage of metal film by cyclic tensile deformation has been


studied by several groups. Most of these fatigue studies of metal


film assumed and represented the cyclic tensile deformation.


However, besides this tensile deformation, the bending


deformation is expected by the other frequent deformation of


flexible display or other flexible electronics. In this study, we


focused on the electrical resistance change of metal film in


repeated bending condition which represented the real operation


condition of flexible electronics. Also, the under-layer and


over-layer effect were investigated to enhance the resistance to  fatigue damage.


resistance of 1 um thick Cu film on flexible substrate increased up


II. EXPERIMENT


Cu films were deposited on the 125 m thick polyimide (PI) by thermal evaporation. The thickness of Cu films was 200 nm  and 1 m. The sample was cut by 4 mm width and 60 mm length. The schematic of test system is illustrated in Fig. 1. This method  was inspired in flexural endurance test method which is used to  estimate the flexural fatigue life cycles of flexible printed wiring


[6]. Metal film of flexible substrate was located between two  plates. The upper plate was fixed, and the lower plate repeated  reciprocating motion. The resistance changes of three samples  were measured simultaneously during cyclic bending by  multi-meter (Agilent 34410A) and switch box (Keithley 7007). The sliding frequency was 5 Hz, and the sliding distances which  was the distance of reciprocating motion was 10 mm. In bending  condition, the strain of metal film can be calculated by following  equation:


8=-

hsub+hjilm


where, hsub, hfilm and r is an applied strain, substrate thickness, film thickness, and radius of bending shape, respectively. The  gap between two plates was 7.8, 11.4, and 17.9 mm, so the  applied strain on thin film was 1.6, 1.1, and 0.7 %, respectively. Also, to investigate the under-layer and over-layer effect, Cu  films were deposited on the 188 m thick polyethylene  terephthalate (PET) by thermal evaporation. The 10 nm thick  Ni95Cr5 under-layer between Cu and PET was prepared by  sputtering. Ni 10 or 74 nm capping layer samples and Al 10 or  100 nm capping layer samples were prepared to study the  capping layer effect by comparing with non-capped samples. In  the under-layer and capping layer test, the gap between two  plates was fixed by 17.1 mm which could be converted to 1.1 % bending strain. The morphology of samples was observed by  scanning electron microscopy (SEM) after 500,000 bending  cycles. The damage zone and undamaged area were cut by 4 mm  X 4 mm and their electrical sheet resistances were measured by 4-point probe.

III. RESULTS AND DISCUSSION


Fig. 2 shows the surface morphology of Cu film after 500,000


cycle bending cycle. Cracks which were vertical to bending


direction and extrusions were observed. The damage evolution


of metal film by tensile bending deformation was similar with


that of previous reports which were investigated by tensile of 200 nm thick and 1 um thick Cu films under 1.1 % bendingfatigue test [7, 8]. This damage morphology implied that the strain condition. The electrical resistance of 1 um Cu filmrepeated bending caused fatigue damage evolution by  dislocation motion. R. Schwaiger et al. reported that the void


formation by vacancy accumulation at the interface played an


important role of crack initiation [7]. Alike the tensile fatigue


test, the fatigue damage in the tensile bending condition would


occur by the dislocation motion during cyclic bending


deformation which formed crack through film thickness and


extrusion at the surface. This crack and extrusion caused the


increase of electrical resistance of the Cu film. However, in the


central area of bending shape which is commonly expected as


weakest region of bending shape, the crack and extrusion was


not observed because the applied strain was maintained by 1.1 400% due to the continuous bending shape during bending cycle.


Fig. 3 shows the electrical resistance change during cyclic


1.6 % bending strain bending deformation. Under 0.7 % bending strain, the electrical 300


resistance did not increase as the bending deformation repeated. )%

electrical resistance increase. To verify the crack effect on  electrical resistance change, the sheet resistance of damage area  and central undamaged area was measured. The sheet resistance  of undamaged area was 33.37 m/ of undamaged area was 33.37 mQ/,but that of crack damaged, but that of crack damaged

Fig. 2. SEM image of crack and extrusion morphology in Cu/PI sample


The mechanical property of thin is strongly affected by the  film thickness [9]. Fig. 4 shows the electrical resistance changes  of 200 nm thick and 1 m thick Cu films under 1.1 % bending  strain condition. The electrical resistance of 1m Cu film  increased as the bending deformation repeated. However, the  electrical resistance of 200 nm Cu film did not increase as  shown in Fig. 4. It is reported that the thinner film had better  resistance to fatigue damage because of the better mechanical  property. R. Schwaiger et al. reported that the vacancy was  annihilated in thin film more easily so that the fatigue damage  did not occur in thin film [7]. In bending deformation, the  thinner film showed better fatigue resistance than thicker film  similarly with the tensile fatigue test.


Fig. 3. Resistance change of Cu film on PI as a function of bending cycles   with 1.6, 1.1 and 0.7 % bending strain


Fig. 4. Monitored electrical resistances change of   200 nm and 1 m thick Cu film.


The crack initiation is important issues in fatigue damage. X. J. Sun et al. reported that the crack initiation could be detected  by electrical resistance change [10]. Fig. 5 shows the electrical  resistance change of 1.1 % strain condition. Note that the x-axis  is log and the y-axis is electrical resistance. At the early stage of  cyclic bending, the electrical resistance was not changed, but  after 10,000 cycles the electrical resistance increased abruptly. This slope change meant the crack initiation. For high reliability  of flexible interconnect, the crack initiation has to be suppressed  and the crack propagation has to be retarded. This log-linear plot  could expect the crack nucleation cycle by the inflection point  and the crack propagation rate by the slope.


We performed bending fatigue test on under-layer and  over-layer sample to develop the method enhancing the fatigue  resistance. Fig. 6 shows the resistance change of Cu only sample

and Cu with NiCr under-layer. The resistance increase of  sample with NiCr under-layer was larger than that of Cu only  sample. NiCr under-layer is commonly used by adhesion layer  in the industry. Although the adhesion of sample with  under-layer is better than that of sample without under-layer, the  resistance increase with bending cycle was much larger than the  other sample. It is expected that the relatively high elastic  modulus of NiCr would affect the crack nucleation and  propagation during bending fatigue.


Fig. 7 shows the resistance change of Ni capping (10, 74 nm) samples and uncapped Cu sample. After 500,000 cycles, the  resistance increase was 50, 54, and 50 % for uncapped, Ni 10 nm, Ni 74 nm capped samples, respectively. At the surface of those  samples, the crack formation was observed. The resistance  change of 10 nm Ni capping sample was not much different with  the uncapped sample. Fig. 8. Resistance change of Cu only on PET, Al 10nm capping,  and Al 100 nm capping sample


sample. It was concluded that the Ni capping layer did not affect  the crack evolution because Ni is more brittle than Cu.


Fig. 8 shows the resistance change of Cu only, Al 10 nm  capping, and Al 100 nm capping sample. The final resistance  increase ratio was 50, 27, and 11 % for uncapped sample, Al 10  nm capping, Al 100 nm capping sample, respectively. Al 10 nm  capping samples had lower resistance increase than Cu only  sample, and Al 100 nm capping sample showed the lowest  resistance change. At the surfaces of Al 10 nm capping sample, a few crack formation was observed but the density was much  lower than that of uncapped Cu sample. In Al 100 nm capping  sample, the crack formation was not observed. The drop of  resistance increase and no crack formation meant the capping  thickness effect. It is expected that Al capping layer suppressed  crack evolution of Cu film because Al is relatively softer than  Cu. This capping effect is more effective in thicker capping  layer.


IV. CONCLUSION


The electrical resistance change of Cu film was investigated  by cyclic bending test. The cyclic bending deformation where  the area repeated bending and unfolding caused the fatigue  damage on Cu film such as crack and extrusion, but the central  area of bending shape was not observed any damage because  fatigue behavior of metal film did not occur. This crack  formation resulted in the electrical resistance increase. At low  bending strain range the damage was not generated, but at high  bending strain condition, larger electrical resistance increase  occurred. Thinner film showed higher fatigue resistance because  of the higher mechanical property. The effect of under-layer and  capping layer were estimated by the cyclic bending test. Cu film  with NiCr under-layer showed poorer fatigue resistance than Cu  only on PET sample because of the stress incompatibility  between NiCr layer and PET substrate. Ni capping layer did not


affect the resistance change of Cu film, however, Al capping  layer enhanced the resistance to fatigue. Also, the thicker Al


capping sampler had more improvement because the crack


driving was lowered by the soft Al capping layer.

A Position-Sensitive, Single-Photon Detector with Enhanced NIR Response


Franco Stellari, Peilin Song, Alan J. Weger


IBM T.J. Watson Research Center, 1101 Kitchawan Rd, Yorktown Heights, NY - stellari@us.ibm.com, 914-945-3223


Tomonori Nakamura, Stanley Kim, Robert Roche


Hamamatsu Photonics, Japan & USA


Abstract


In this paper, we evaluate a novel, position-sensitive, singlephoton detector with enhanced Near InfraRed (NIR) sensitivity [1-3] for taking 2D Time Resolved Emission (TRE), also known as Picosecond Imaging for Circuit  Analysis (PICA), in future low voltage SOI technologies. In  particular, we will investigate and quantify the sensitivity of  two generations (Gen. I and Gen. II) of PICA cameras by  Hamamatsu Photonics as a function of the power supply  voltage on an IBM 45 nm SOI test chip. Additionally, we will  compare the results to the performance obtained with an  InGaAs Single Photon Avalanche Diode (SPAD) from DCG  Systems [4]. Finally we will show a case study and an  advanced analysis and localization technique that takes  advantage of the 2D capability of the camera.


Introduction


The use of hot-carrier photon emission from VLSI circuits for  localizing and identifying failures has been widely used for  several decades. First, with the introduction of Emission  Microscopy [5] it soon became an essential instrument for  physical failure analysis by localizing hot-spot emission, shorts, non-uniform quiescence/stand-by current of the chip (IDDQ) [6-8], etc. More advanced extensions have also been  added to this technique during the years, based for example on  the detection of the Light Emission due to Off-State Leakage  Current (LEOSLC) [9-12]: circuit logic states mapping [13], power grid drop calculation [7,11,14], circuit internal  temperature and gate self heating measurements [15], etc. However, a real revolution happened in 1995 when the  concept of Picosecond Imaging Circuit Analysis (PICA) was  introduced and used by Kash and Tsang [16]. This technique  allows the observation in time of the faint Near-InfraRed (NIR) light pulses emitted by hot-electrons during the  switching transitions of CMOS transistors. From the optical  waveforms it is therefore possible to extract propagation  delays, signal skews and other time-dependent problems in a  non-invasive and very effective way that dictated its  immediate widespread adoption by the testing and diagnostic  community.


The evolution and improvement of PICA capabilities have  been also influenced by different detectors that have been  adopted in the years to measure the arrival time of the photons


compared to a reference signal (trigger). The Mepsicron S-25  detector [17] was originally adopted because of its capability  of measuring the spatial coordinates of the position at which  the photon arrives, besides the instant in time. This allowed  the creation of images in time (movies) of the evolution of the  chip, thus extremely simplifying the interpretation of the data. However, the low sensitivity of the detector in the NIR region  of the emission spectrum mostly limited the technique to the  observation of light pulses coming form n-FETs. The  emission from the p-FET is in fact more than one order of  magnitude weaker and shifted towards longer wavelengths, i.e. lower photon energy. As a consequence, the delay and  skews can be calculated only between gates having the same  signal phase and in particular in correspondence of the falling  edge of the gate output [18].


Nowadays, the continuous trend of the modern semiconductor  industry towards smaller devices and lower supply voltages


[19] is causing significant changes in the intensity and  spectrum shift of the light emitted by present CMOS  generation: the total detectable light decreases exponentially  with the electric field in the transistor, and linearly with the  lateral dimension of the FET. In particular, the progressive  shift of the spectral distribution of emitted light towards  longer wavelengths [20] pushed for the development and  adoption of innovative detectors. Two that demonstrated  significantly better Quantum Efficiency (QE) in the NIR  region of the spectrum are the Superconducting Single Photon  Detector (SSPD) [21,22] and the InGaAs Single-Photon  Avalanche Diode (SPAD) [23-30]. Although all these  detectors offer only single-point detection capability as  opposed to the imaging capability of the S-25 detector, they  allow a significant reduction of the acquisition time for the  light pulses produced by n-FETs (more that 1,000,000 times  as shown in [31,32]). Moreover they allow for the observation  of the light pulses emitted by the weaker p-FETs (corresponding to the rising edge of a gate output signal) [33]. This simplifies and extends the capabilities of TRE techniques  allowing the evaluation of signal pulse width [34], duty cycle

On the other hand, the capability of observing the behavior of  the circuit with 2D imaging capability has a lot of advantages. Firstly, when the location of a failure is not precisely known, the possibility of inspecting a larger area containing several  gates allows for faster localization. Furthermore, the capability


of observing signals from a lower magnification point of view  permits to notice systematic and unexpected macro behaviors; for example, delayed arrival of clock signals in certain  portions of the chip, etc. Additionally, the capability of  measuring the spatial distribution of emitted photons in  combination with their arrival time allows development of  additional techniques to better separate contributions from  neighboring gates, thus significantly simplifying the  interpretation of the collected data.


2D PICA camera


The novel 2D imaging PICA camera [1-3] is housed into a  Hamamatsu TriPhemos system [1-3] with both long working  distance optics and exchangeable Solid Immersion Lenses (SILs) for different chip backside thicknesses. The SIL  optimized for thinner samples (70 μm) has a Numerical  Aperture (NA) of ~2.5 that allows a magnification of ~175X  when used in combination with the 50X lens. The microscope  has three exit ports housing a Laser Scanning Microscope (LSM) for high resolution navigation as well as for laser  stimulation techniques, a static emission port with a Peltier  cooled high resolution (640 x 512) InGaAs static camera, and  a position-sensitive, single-photon detector (a.k.a. 2D PICA  camera) cryogenically cooled down to 120 K to reduce the  noise, i.e. Dark Count Rate (DCR). The camera consists of a  thin GaAs/InGaAs cathode of approximately 5 mm x 5 mm in  size that absorbs the impinging emission on one side and  releases photo-electrons on the other side. A high electric field  is then used to extract these photo-electrons and inject them  into a Micro-Channel Plate (MCP) that multiples them  producing a detectable current. A resistive anode is then used  to measure both the arrival time and the spatial XY position of  each electron pulse, corresponding to a detected photon, with  a time jitter of ~110 ps-FWHM (Full-Width at Half the  Maximum) and an equivalent number of “pixels” of about  200 x 200. The manufacturing of this new cathode with  extended sensitivity is very challenging and critical to the  detector performance. In particular, material growth has to be  tightly controlled to achieve good uniformity and avoid hot  spots. Additionally the thickness of the material has to be  properly engineered to achieve the best compromise between  photon absorption and the electron extraction.


Two different generations of 2D PICA cameras, Gen. I and  Gen II, with different cathode materials were evaluate during  the course of this work. The Gen. I detector has an estimated  QE > 0.1% at room temperature and a wavelength cut off at ~1450 nm at room temperature; while the Gen. II detector has  an enhanced sensitivity ~10X higher at 1400 nm and an  extended wavelength response up to ~1650 nm at room  temperature (see Fig. 1). (Note that the wavelength cut off is  estimated at room temperature, 273 K, and a 0.6 nm/K shift  towards shorter wavelengths is expected due to the cathode  band-gap increase at lower operating temperature) The Gen. II  PICA detector has a moderately higher DCR due to the  extended sensitivity and also a lightly lower optimal operating  temperature (<120 K) that could not be achieved with our  current temperature controller (a firmware fix will be  available in the future). On the other hand the Gen. II detector  demonstrated a slightly lower jitter response of ~110 psFWHM compared to the previous ~140 ps-FWHM of the  Gen. I detector. This decreased jitter response is due to a  slight reduction in the distance between the cathode and MCP  that reduces the dispersion in the electron extraction time. The  PICA camera is also equipped with two additional very useful  features: a 3X magnification tube lens that can be used in SIL  mode to further improve the separation of neighboring  emitting gates, and a computer controller Neutral Density  Filter (NDF) wheel in front of the detector that can be used to  dim very bright emission sources and extend the dynamic  range of the camera, e.g. during low magnification  measurements when many sources are in the field of view, or  in presence of bright defects.


Each generation of PICA camera was evaluated individually  to verify the performance and the various improvements  between generations. Additionally, some waveforms were also  compared to the ones collected using the Emiscope III  microscope from DCG Systems and Phase II+ InGaAs SPAD  described in [4]. Several gates along a scan data path of an  IBM SOI 45 nm test chip were used as emission sources for  all the analysis and comparison discussed in this paper (see  Fig. 2). The chip is exercised using a HP82000 tester  providing a 10 ns reference clock and a 20 ns “1010…” data  along the scan chain of interest.

A circuit diagnostics case was studied using the Gen. I PICA  camera. Referring to Fig. 2, the TRE waveforms from four

A 10 ns clock and a 20 ns “101010…” data packet  was used to exercise the gates during the experiment. The  PICA camera was setup for 10 min acquisitions, a 400 ns  trigger loop, and a 200 ns acquisition window, corresponding  to a ~49 ps histogram bit size. Fig. 3 shows the 2D emission  images and the corresponding TRE waveforms for the four  gates at a power supply voltage of 1 V. A 4 GHz low  pass filter was applied to the waveforms shown in Fig. 3.


Fig. 4 shows a zoom in comparison of the four waveforms that  was used to calculate the propagation delay for each stage by  means of Gaussian fitting. The delay data calculated from the  emission peaks is reported in Fig. 4.


Low voltage sensitivity for Gen. I detector


The sensitivity of the detector as a function of the circuit  supply voltage was also investigated during this study to  evaluate its applicability for future lower voltage technologies. The buffer #5, shown in Fig. 2, was used as a reference device  while the circuit supply voltage was varied during the  following analysis.


Fig. 5 shows a couple of clock cycles for several TRE  waveforms acquired from the larger n-FET of the second  stage of the buffer at different values of supply voltages. For  all measurements the trigger loop was maintained at 400 ns, the acquisition window at 200 ns (corresponding to ~49 ps  histogram bin size), and the acquisition time at 20 min. All the  data was acquired using the 175X SIL in combination with the  3X tube lens, and it is shown raw without filtering or  numerical folding.

Fig. 5: TRE waveforms acquired at different Vdd.


Fig. 6 shows a detail of one of the waveforms so that one can  notice the switching emission peaks, corresponding to signal  transitions, and the square Light Emission From Off State  Leakage Current (LEOSLC) waveform, corresponding to the  differences in leakage due to the gate logic state changes. Additionally, we define as high LEOSLC and low LEOSLC  levels the emission measured when the gate is in a “1” or “0” state, and as switching signal amplitude the height of the  switching emission peaks above the low level LEOSLC  signal. For our Signal to Noise Ratio (SNR) calculations, we  are also interested in the “step” difference between the two  leakage conditions and in the noise band measured as standard  deviation.


Fig. 7(a) shows the values of signal and noise levels extracted  from the waveforms shown in Fig. 5. A decay of ~3.1X / 100 mV is observed in the measured signal strength. Additionally the noise level is observed to be a strong function  of the circuit supply voltage and to be much higher than the  intrinsic detector noise. This suggests that the predominant  source is background emission coming from neighboring  gates and being coupled in the area of interest by the buried  oxide. Fig. 7(b) shows the calculated SNR for both the  switching emission peaks and the digital LEOSLC waveform. The former reaches SNR = 1 at ~0.6 V, while the latter  reaches the same values at ~0.7 V.


Gen. I vs. Gen. II upgrade


After a first year of evaluation for the Gen. I PICA detector, Hamamatsu Photonics was able to manufacture a camera with  a new cathode material with ~10X enhanced QE and an  extended sensitivity towards the longer wavelengths that  better correspond to lower supply voltage applications (see  Fig. 1). Additionally, a variable NDF wheel was placed in


front of the new PICA camera to protect it from excessively  bright emission sources.


Fig. 8 shows TRE waveforms acquired from the same buffer #5 used for the previous analysis. Each measurement lasted  for 20 minutes and was acquired using the SIL without the 3X  additional tube lens. After the acquisition completed, the data  was folded numerically over a 40 ns time span, corresponding  to two data cycles “1010”. The first row of data corresponds  to the Gen. I detector while the second and third rows have  been acquired using the Gen. II detector and the NDF wheel  setup for 10% and 100% transmission, respectively. One can  notice that while the Gen. I camera loses sensitivity at 0.8 V, the new camera with higher sensitivity requires the use of an  attenuation NDF filter to acquire waveforms at 1.1 V because  of saturation effects. The NDF filter works very well for  extending the dynamic range of the camera when  measurements with particularly bright sources are acquired.


Fig. 9(a) shows a detailed comparison of the extracted signal  and noise levels for the Gen. I PICA detector and the Gen. II  camera with 10% and 100% NDF in place. At 0.8 V of circuit  supply voltage, the signal strength improves by ~7.5X when  the NDF is removed. Additionally, the slope of the signal  decay with the supply voltage is reduced, due to the extended

sensitivity of the cathode towards longer wavelengths. The  noise level in Gen. II increases slightly compare to the Gen. I  detector and it is dependent on the circuit supply voltages. This suggests that the dominant noise source is the  neighboring emitting gates that create a voltage dependent  emission background in the region of interest. The emission  intensity of such background will be increased with the higher  sensitivity of the Gen. II PICA camera.


Fig. 9(b) shows the SNR for the Gen. I and Gen. II PICA  detectors calculated using the data reported in Fig. 9(a). One  can notice that an improvement of a factor 4X-8X is achieved  by the new camera without any NDF attenuation. The highest  improvement is obtained at the lower supply voltages due to  the extended spectral sensitivity towards longer wavelengths  of the new detector that reduces the decay of the signal as a  function of the circuit voltages. Most importantly, the  combined effect of the higher sensitivity and reduced voltage  decay produces ~400 mV sensitivity extension towards lower  supply voltages, with an estimated SNR = 1 intersection at ~0.4 V.


Comparison with InGaAs SPAD


A comparison with the single point InGaAs SPAD [4] available on the Emiscope III by DCG Systems was also  performed during our evaluation of the new PICA camera. During Emiscope III measurements, the trigger loop length  was changed to 2.5 μs, the minimum allowed by the tool. Additionally, the TRE waveforms acquired in the 200 ns  acquisition window were re-binned from 4.8 ps to 48 ps in  order to better match the ~49 ps bin size of the TriPhemos  PICA camera previously reported. For the purpose of the  comparison, the PICA waveforms were acquired using the  SIL and the 3X tube lens. All waveforms, for both detectors, were acquired from buffer #5 (Fig. 2) and they were  numerically folded to a 40 ns period, corresponding to two  data cycles “1010”.


Fig. 10(a) shows both the switching emission signal strengths  and the noise background standard deviations as a function of  the circuit supply voltages for the InGaAs SPAD and both  generations of PICA cameras. Both the signal and the noise of  the InGaAs SPAD are significantly larger than those of the 2D  PICA, since the InGaAs SPAD has a nominally higher QE


and intrinsic DCR. Taking 0.8 V as a reference voltage for the  comparison, the InGaAs SPAD signal is ~20 stronger while  the noise level is ~10X larger, compared to the Gen. II PICA  camera. One can also notice that the InGaAs SPAD noise is  independent from the circuit supply voltage, since the intrinsic  high DCR of the detector is the dominant factor, while the 2D  PICA camera noise is a strong function of the circuit supply  voltage due to the background contribution collected by the  detector.


Fig. 10(b) reports the SNR calculated using the data in  Fig. 10(a). Taking 0.8 V as a reference voltage for the  comparison, the InGaAs SPAD SNR appears to be >2X  higher than the Gen II 2D PICA camera and ~6X larger than  the Gen I detector: the lower sensitivity of the PIC camera is  compensated by the much lower noise. The difference in SNR  between the two detectors reverberates into ~150 mV  sensitivity extension towards lower supply voltages for the  InGaAs SPAD.


Advanced analysis and localization technique


Although the sensitivity of the detector is a very important  parameter for collecting waveforms in a timely fashion, we  believe that the capability of observing the behavior of the  circuit in 2D has a lot of advantages. As mentioned in the  introductory section, the location of a failure may not be  precisely known before PICA measurements are undertaken. Therefore, the possibility of inspecting a larger area  containing several gates allows for a faster localization of the  problem. Furthermore, the capability of observing signals  from a lower magnification point of view permits observation  of systematic and unexpected macro behaviors; for example, delayed arrival of clock signals in certain portions of the chip, etc. In the next paragraphs we will also show that the  capability of measuring the spatial distribution of emitted  photons in combination with their arrival time allows  development of additional techniques to better separate  contributions from neighboring gates, thus significantly  simplifying the interpretation of the collected data.


The 2D time integrated image of the emission acquired with  the PICA camera using the 175X SIL and the 3X tube lens is  shown in Fig. 11(a). Fig. 11(b) shows the corresponding  layout of the circuit in the area of interest with a red rectangle

Fig. 11:  2D integrated PICA image is compared to the  local circuit layout


around buffer #5 that was used during some of the previous  analysis. For this field of view, only buffer #5 is actually  switching during the circuit operation (see rectangle in  Fig. 11(a)), while all other surrounding circuits contribute  with a stationary LEOSLC background. We can imagine many  circumstances when one would like to be able to easily  localize and isolate switching gates among a large quantity of  other gates that are not involved in the current circuit testing  and are just bystanders. Of particular interest are: (1) quick  localization of a gate, (2) simplifying the correlation and  alignment with the layout, (3) improving the separation of the  emission contributions among gates and among different  transistors of the gates, and (4) simplifying the definition of  the regions of interest for reconstructing the TRE waveform  with the best possible SNR.


For this particular example, we take advantage of the  knowledge of characteristics of the particular test pattern  being used to exercise the chip. In particular, given that the  clock is running at a 10 ns period and the data at a 20 ns  period, we expect to have emission modulated at 100 MHz  and 50 MHz frequency, and their harmonics. One approach to  highlight such contributions consists of subdividing the entire  field of view in NxM Regions Of Interest (ROI), calculating  the TRE waveform for each ROI (Fig. 12(a)), applying a Fast  Fourier Transformation (FFT) to the emission waveform (Fig. 12(b)), and analyzing the FFT waveform for  contributions at those frequencies, and computing a Figure Of  Merit (FOM) that measure such contributions.


Fig. 13 shows the results obtained by applying the post  processing method above to the raw PICA data shown in  Fig. 11(a). In this case, the FOM was defined as the amplitude  of the FFT at the 50 MHz frequency. Different cases  corresponding to different numbers of subdivisions are shown:


(a) N = M = 16, (b) N = M = 32, and (c) N = M = 64. One can  immediately note that the coarsest subdivision ((a) N = M = 16) is already very effective in discriminating the  gates affected by the particular exerciser from neighboring  bystanders. On the other hand, by paying with a longer  analysis time, one can achieve a much higher localization of  the gate with details that allow to clearly separate the  contributions of various transistors inside the gate ((c) N = M = 64).


Conclusions


In this paper, we have evaluated a novel PICA camera with  enhanced NIR sensitivity [1-3] for taking 2D TRE waveforms  in future low voltage SOI technologies. In particular, we have  investigated and quantified the sensitivity of two generations (Gen. I and Gen. II) of detectors by Hamamatsu Photonics as  a function of the power supply voltages of an IBM 45 nm SOI  test chip. Additionally, we have compared the results to the  performance obtained with an InGaAs SPAD from DCG  Systems [4]. Finally we have shown a case study and an  advanced analysis and localization technique that takes  advantage of the 2D capability of the camera.

48.1-48.9.

Advanced Scan Chain Failure Analysis Using Laser Modulation Mapping and  Continuous Wave Probing


Steven Kasapi, William Lo, Joy Liao, Bruce Cory, and Howard Marks


NVIDIA, 2701 San Tomas Expressway, Santa Clara, CA 95050 USA  Contact: skasapi@nvidia.com


Identification of defects causing scan chain failures continues  to be a challenge and scan chain failures continue to be a significant fraction of yield loss. The complexity of modern  design-for-test (DFT) implementation techniques and the  increasingly subtle failure mechanisms of sub-40 nm  technologies leave significant room for improvement in shift  defect isolation, and we anticipate the problem getting worse  at smaller device geometries.


Software techniques for analyzing shift failures rely on  capturing data from defective chains into defect-free chains  and analyzing the output data from the good chains[1].


Software chain diagnosis often returns a range of flops, usually too large to proceed straight to PFA. In our  experience, software-based fault isolation techniques have had  poor success at achieving physical failure analysis (PFA) rootcause results on commercial products.


A variety of EFA techniques have been deployed to improve  scan chain failure isolation. The most successful include laser  perturbation (both static and dynamic)[2, 3], static emission (of both leakage and on-current)[4], and transistor-level  probing techniques such as time resolved emission (TRE) microscopy[5] and laser voltage probing (LVP)[6]. We have  found in practice that each of these techniques has serious  limitations.


Laser perturbation techniques refer to techniques which  locally modify the circuit behavior with a laser beam. Laser  perturbation techniques require either coupling to the tester  power supply to measure small changes in voltage or current,or identifying pass/fail conditions very rapidly on a tester.


Both are challenging on a production tester with thousands of  pins, thousands of cycles of pattern depth, billions of  transistors, and many amps of current.


Static emission signals have a strong exponential dependence  on voltage, often requiring long integration times to achieve  acceptable image signal-to-noise. Emission signals can also be  difficult to interpret in complex designs since a single defect  can cause changes in static emission from multiple transistors.  Probing techniques can be very effective but require exact apriori knowledge of where to probe. If the operator fails to


acquire a signal it is often not clear whether the operator made  a mistake, the system is malfunctioning, the signal is buried in  noise, or if there really is no signal. Like static emission, the  TRE signal strength also suffers from an exponential  dependence on voltage and we have not found TRE practical  at 40nm technologies and below.


In an earlier paper we presented a new image-based laser  technique we call modulation mapping (MM) to identify the  first failing flop in the broken scan chain of stuck-at failures[7, 8]. The primary advantages of image-based MM  measurements are that no additional external connection to the  tester pins or power supply is required (aside from a trigger  signal for probing), the data provides simultaneous  measurements on multiple flops, the signal is linear in  voltage[9], and if probing is necessary the images provide  clear guidance on exactly where to probe[10, 11].


In contrast to other laser techniques, MM does not require  electrically perturbing the device. The signal is obtained from  the reflected laser beam, not from observing some external  electrical behavior of the circuit (voltage, current, pass/fail, etc.) We operate at a wavelength of 1319 nm, well above the  silicon bandgap, and relatively low laser powers of less than 2  mW into the objective lens. The measurement is thus  effectively non-invasive.


We present three case studies involving scan chains with  subtle resistive and leakage failure mechanisms, including  transition, bridge, and slow-to-rise/fall failures, using a  combination of modulation mapping (MM) and continuouswave (CW) laser probing.


Review of MM and CW Probing


Modulation mapping is a technique for imaging circuit activity  at a specific frequency. When a laser beam is focused onto the  active layer of a CMOS circuit, a small amount of light is  reflected by various interfaces. The reflected laser beam’s  amplitude and phase are modified by free-carrier absorption  and phase delay in the doped silicon.


A high-bandwidth photodetector converts the reflected laser  light into an electrical signal, which we then amplify and filter  with a spectrum analyzer. The spectrum analyzer’s analog  output voltage is proportional to the total radio frequency (RF)


power at the frequency of interest. The resolution bandwidth (RBW) sets the bandwidth of the RF filter, and the video  bandwidth (VBW) filters the output video signal. We typically  use a RBW and VBW of 300 Hz or 1 kHz.


We use an LSM to scan the laser beam across the field-ofview (FOV) of the objective lens. For all the measurements  presented here, the objective lens was a silicon solidimmersion lens (SIL) with a nominal numerical aperture of


2.6. The analog voltage output of the spectrum analyzer is the  video input signal to the spectrum analyzer. We set the dwell  time per pixel to match the  RBW and VBW filters. The MM  LSM system supports 3 simultaneous input channels, so we  are able to detect the reflected-light image and signals from  two different spectrum analyzers simultaneously to observe  two different activity frequencies.


Continuous-wave (CW) probing uses the same hardware, except the scanning mirrors of the LSM are fixed to point at a  particular point in the field-of-view, and the amplified voltage  output from the photodetector is sent to a high-speed digital  sampling oscilloscope to convert the photoelectric signal into  a waveform.


The laser wavelength for modulation mapping and probing is  1319 and we typically use 2 mW of light into the SIL  objective lens.  As already mentioned, we have not observed  change in electrical behavior of the device by the laser beam  for this laser wavelengths and power levels.


Review of Shift Debug Using MM


A scan chain is a series of scan flip-flops connected so that  one flip-flop feeds a subsequent flip-flop in the chain. In this  paper, the scan flip-flops are edge-triggered D flip-flops with a two-input mux is added to the input of the flop to select  between loading the flop with the combinational logic (scan  mode) or the preceding flop in the chain (shift mode). Shift  mode refers to the condition in which the MUX receives the  input from the previous flop in the chain.


A defect causing corruption of the data anywhere in the scan  chain during shift will propagate through the subsequent good  flops on the chain and give a failing signature on the output. Merely observing the output gives no indication of the  location of the defect because of the serial nature of the scan  chain.


Software techniques attempt to isolate the location of a scan  chain defect by capturing data from a failing chain into good  chains and observing the output over many input patterns. However, software techniques have serious “real-world” limitations. They are relatively ineffective at isolating multiple  scan chain failures, clock defects, defects in the fan-out logic, multiple failures within a single scan chain, and intermittent or  marginal failures – all of which are common occurrences.


Modulation mapping has proven highly effective at isolating  all of these cases, with a success rate of subsequent PFA  greater than 95%. Since we have already presented the  workflow for using MM to isolate shift defects, we will only  briefly review it here.


During shift testing both the clock and the data through the  flops are active. We load the scan chains with a repeating  11001100… pattern, so the data frequency is ¼ the clock  frequency.


We also align the modulation mapping system to a computeraided design (CAD) system, and load an overlay depicting the  location of every flop in the failing scan chain with the  reflected-light image from the DUT. This link between the  CAD and the system allows us to navigate to any region in the  scan chain quickly. Mechanical inaccuracies in the system do  require constant small adjustments in the alignment between  the CAD and system. We perform these small adjustments by  overlaying a CAD layer (usually the diffusion layer) on the  image and adjusting the overlay until the image and CAD  outlines match.


As already mentioned, the clock and data signals operate at  two distinct frequencies. If the clock frequency is f, the data  frequency is f/4. By monitoring f and f/4 we can monitor the  scan chain behavior at any flop in the chain. As we will see, for some transition defects it is also useful to observe the  signal at frequency f/2.


Probing is straightforward once we have modulation maps. The modulation maps of the clock and data frequencies  indicate the optimal laser probe placement of those signals.


Case Studies


Case Study #1. Transition failure involving resistive bridge


The first case study is a 40 nm device failing shift. The input  shift pattern was a repeating 11001100… pattern loaded  serially into the chain. The chain contained approximately 250  flops.


We first tried a software-based shift diagnosis technique to  identify 31 failing flop candidates, but the area was too large  and the associated nets too complex to perform successful  PFA.


We acquired MM images at the clock (10 MHz) and data (2.5  MHz) frequencies of the suspect flops. We ran the pattern in a  continuous loop repeating in a multiple of four clock cycles (we typically use 20 clock cycles). The reflected-light, data  frequency, and clock frequency images were all acquired  simultaneously using two spectrum analyzers, providing  alignment among the 3 images to within one pixel. The  spectrum analyzer resolution bandwidth (RBW) and video  bandwidth (VBW) were both set to 1 kHz, and the pixel dwell  time on the LSM as 870 s. time on the LSM as 870 us. The laser wavelength was 1319The laser wavelength was 1319


nm.  Each set of MM images (clock and data frequencies) took  approximately 2 minutes to acquire. We used the reflectedlight image to align our CAD system to the reflected-light  images, making it straightforward to navigate to any flop in  the chain.


The MM images of the candidate failing flops identified an  anomalous modulation location at the Q output of flop C135 in the chain at the data frequency. The modulation mapping  image at the clock frequency showed normal activity.  Taking  the image difference of the DUT and a good reference unit  revealed clear difference at the output of flop C135 (Figure 1). The modulation mapping images determine the optimal  location to position the laser for CW laser waveform probing.


To measure a waveform, we point the laser at a point showing  strong modulation at the frequency of the signal to be  measured near the port of interest. To trigger the waveform  acquisition, we created an 80 ns pulse every 20 clock cycles (2  s with a 10 MHz clock) using an unused tester channel1us with a 10 MHz clock) using an unused tester channel'..


Guided by the modulation mapping images, we were able to  probe any flop in the field of view. The typical waveform  acquisition time was a few seconds. Both the MM image and  probing signal strength are approximately linear in voltage.


The failing and reference waveforms from the Q-outputs of  flops before C135 showed normal behavior. Each successive  flop showed a waveform delayed by one clock cycle, as  expected. However, the waveform Q-output of flop C135 on  the failing device was distorted, and the output of the


G137


following flops were advanced one clock cycle (Figure 2). The he flops are fed in inverse order – that is, flop C136 feeds flop


C135, and so on.


The laser MM images and waveforms not only isolated the  scan shift failure to a single flop, but also determined that the


C11.5prcr


failure was a transition (timing) failure of one full clock cycle.

Case Study #2. Transition defect using 2nd harmonic MM  imaging


The second case study is a 28 nm device failing scan shift on a  chain with over 30,000 flops. The shift clock was at 25 MHz


and the input data was a repeated 11001100… pattern, for an


C29814C29813


effective data rate of 6.25 MHz.


We again used a binary search to isolate the location of the  defect causing the shift failure. MM images of the first flop of  the chain at the data and clock frequencies looked normal.


Modulation mapping at the both data and clock frequencies  did not reveal any anomalies (Figure 3) even at the end of the  chain. However, by looking at the 2nd harmonic of the data  rate, we determined that the data pattern was changing from


the input 11001100… pattern to a failing 10001000… pattern. A 11001100… square-wave pattern has harmonics only at odd  multiples of the 6.25 MHz data frequency, whereas a  10001000… pattern has harmonics at even frequencies. By  looking for locations of activity at 2x the data rate (12.5 MHz) we identified flops in the field of view with the incorrect  10001000… output data pattern. MM images of the last flops

in the chain showed modulation at the 2nd harmonic, indicating

Case Study #3 Transition defect


The third case study is also a 28nm device failing shift on one  chain containing more than 30,000 flops.


We started modulation mapping acquisition using binary  search. Modulation maps at both data and clock frequencies  did not reveal any anomalies.  A modulation map at the 2nd harmonic of the data frequency of the last flop (C00000) revealed that the output was a transition fault: data patterns  changing from 11001100… to 10001000… Probing of the Q  output of C00000 confirmed that the output data was  10001000… pattern.


We performed a binary search using modulation mapping  images at the 2nd harmonic of the data frequency. The binary  search identified first toggling flop C06397, while the Qoutput of the preceding flop C06398 and a following buffer  between the C06398/Q output and C06397/SI input did not  exhibit transistor activities at the 2nd harmonic (Figure 6) but  did exhibit activity at the data frequency. Lack of data at the  2ndharmonic indicates that the data is the correct 11001100… pattern.


A五彩店HD


Probing indicated that the data from the previous flop C06397  and the C06397/CP input clock signal were both correct, but  the Q-output of C06397 went high when it should not.


Zooming in to the transition edge revealed approximately 1ns  delay “after” the clock pulse (CP) input to C06397.


We submitted the device for PFA, recommending analysis of  the net between C06398 and C06397 and the flops themselves.


Figure 6. Laser modulation mapping at the 2nd harmonic  frequency showed flops toggling with abnormal data pattern  10001000….  The first toggling flop C06397 was observed.


Summary


Combining modulation mapping with laser probing has proven  to be a very effective and efficient methodology for isolating  shift defects, even challenging timing-related shift defects. So  far every device submitted for PFA using this workflow has  led to successful root cause identification. The techniques  presented here are sufficiently non-invasive and  straightforward that we believe they can be successfully  applied at wafer level for volume, yield-oriented EFA[8].


We are currently investigating applying these techniques to  additional applications, including DFT capture failures with  massive diagnostic callouts, 2-clock scan transition failures,and memory-related failures.

Thermal Frequency Imaging: A new application of Laser Voltage Imaging  applied on 40nm technology


Guillaume Celi a,1, Sylvain Dudit a, Thierry Parrassin a, Philippe Perdu b, Antoine Reverdy c, Dean Lewis d,  Michel Valleta


a STMicroelectronics, 850 rue Jean Monnet, 38920 Crolles, France b CNES Laboratory, 18 avenue Edouard Belin, 31401 Toulouse, France c SECTOR Technologies, 2 avenue de Vignate, 38610 Gières, France d Univ. Bordeaux, IMS Laboratory, 351 cours de la libération, 33405 Talence, France


1  Corresponding author: Tel. : +33 438 922 392  E-mail address : guillaume.celi@st.com (G. Celi)


For Very Deep submicron Technologies, techniques based  on the analysis of reflected laser beam properties are widely  used. The Laser Voltage Imaging (LVI) technique, introduced in  2009, allows mapping frequencies through the backside of  integrated circuit. In this paper, we propose a new technique  based on the LVI technique to debug a scan chain related issue. We describe the method to use LVI, usually dedicated to  frequency mapping of digital active parts, in a way that enables  localization of resistive leakage. Origin of this signal is  investigated on a 40nm case study. This signal can be properly  understood when two different effects, charge carrier density  variations (LVI) and thermo reflectance effect (Thermal  Frequency Imaging, TFI), are taken into account.


Laser Voltage Imaging is a non-invasive technique which  allows frequencies mapping on active devices. The frequency  imaging is a new method to track and to find defects. This  innovative kind of measurement can play an important role onto  the failure analysis of integrated circuit.


In this article, we introduce a new methodology which  allows resistive leakage localization. This new technique is  based on LVI mapping, with some electrical setup  modifications. The use of frequency mapping through the  thermo reflectance effect [1] gives us an unique opportunity to  locate a resistive leakage.


It has been validated by a scan chain related defect  localization case study, using Laser Voltage Imaging [2] on  advanced CMOS technology (40 nm). Then, we applied our  new technique called Thermal Frequency Imaging [3] to  overcome some of the limitations of the LVI, EMMI and TRE


[4] techniques.

III. CASE STUDY ON  40NM SCAN CHAIN PATH


A. Context


CK


Design for testability (DFT) becomes more and more


important to enable good test coverage of advanced devices. It InFF also facilitates efficient failure analysis process. Presented case


study is a scan chain related failure on 40nm technology.


OutFF


Scan chain is a specific structure designed by DFT GOOD


engineers. Goal is to increase the fault coverage of a given


device by inserting virtual probe points inside the logic area. A Out FFspecial signal called scan enable is added to a design. When this FAIL


signal is asserted, every flip-flop (FF) in the design is connected


into a long shift register, one input pin provides the data to this


chain, and one output pin is connected to the output of the chain.


In this scan mode, using the chip clock signal, a known value


can be set in each FF of the scan chain. Then, we disable this


scan mode and each FF sends its content to a limited section of


logic gates. The result of this combinatory test is then captured


by the scan chain, and all value can be read out by enabling one


more time the scan mode.  See on figure 2 schematic of a scan  chain.

C. EMMI / SDL results


Fault localization started with backside light emission  microscopy. A Photon emission spot was detected in the vicinity  of the suspicious flip-flop (FF 160) (figure 5). This EMMI  acquisition was performed in “dynamic mode”, looping on the  test pattern that emulates the electrical failure


Figure 5: EMMI Dynamic localization. Left: 20X acquisition, right: SIL 220 X  acquisition.


This EMMI spot refers to a clock buffer that drives the  clock signal to another block cell (see figure 7), so it is expected  to observe strong emission from this buffer. Thus, EMMI  technique did not give us interesting information on the defect  localization.


Then, we tried to address this defect with the most appropriate  technique when dealing with soft defect, Dynamic Laser  Stimulation (SDL).


Figure 6: Soft defect localization results. Up: SDL acquisition with SIL 220X  Bottom: Layout localization


A sensitive area is found at the output of the delay chain  between two flip flops (FF160 and FF161). This first SDL  localization is interesting, because this area is just after the flip  flop 160, diagnosed as fail by the ATPG software. To be more  confident in that first localization, we used the LVI technique to  confirm this first result.

D.


LVI and Cw LVP results


LVI technique maps specific operation frequencies of  transistors. This enables rapid identification of which areas of  the die are active at a particular frequency.


With this technique we can check all signals which are  present in the Flip Flop after and before the Flip Flop diagnosed  as fail. The first LVI acquisition (figure 8) is made at the clock  frequency (20MHz) to check if the clock signal is present on all  the flip flops. The second acquisition (figure 9) is made at the  data frequency (10MHz) to verify the data signal.

II. THERMO-REFLECTANCE EFFECT


A. Origin of the signal / Theory


The reflected light coming from the surface of a sample  provides information about the thermo-mechanical behavior of  the device under test via parameters such as amplitude, phase, polarization, etc. TFI technique is based on the existing


relationship between the variation ΔR of the optical reflection  coefficient R and the variation ΔT of the temperature T of a


given material.


The thermo-reflectance coefficient is on the order of  and


per degree Kelvin for most materials [5]. This  coefficient depends on wavelength, material, and even surface  texture [1]. The Thermo reflectance measurement could be a  useful non contact technique to measure temperature variations  of a device supplied by a current and heated by Joule effect.


When periodic electrical current flows through an  electronic device, the latter is submitted to a temperature


variation ΔT, which creates a reflectivity variation ΔR at the


device surface. The reflectance spectrum is modulated by  periodic variation of the temperature of the sample:

E. Thermal Frequency Imaging setup and results


In the LVI setup, we generally use a clock or a signal  frequency to map the corresponding active area on the DUT.  In our application, a modulation signal is directly applied to the  power supply with VDD for clock high level, and VDD - 20% for low level [3].


The purpose is to emulate and localize with a dynamic  approach the direct leakage path on the power supply. With this  electrical setup, we create a modulation on the power supply, which creates a temperature variation while the current flows  through the defect. Local Lattice Strain Measurement using Geometric Phase Analysis   of Dark Field Images from Scanning Transmission Electron Microscopy


Jayhoon Chung and Guoda Lian


Texas Instruments, Dallas, TX USA


Lew Rabenberg


Texas Materials Institute, University of Texas, Austin, TX USA


Since strain engineering plays a key role in semiconductor  technology development, a reliable and reproducible technique  to measure local strain in devices is necessary for process  development and failure analysis. In this paper, geometric  phase analysis of high angle annular dark field - scanning  transmission electron microscope images is presented as an  effective technique to measure local strains in the current node  of Si based transistors.


Strain engineering to improve device performance is now  widespread in the Si-based device industry. As such, there is a  need for a technique that measures the local lattice strain on a  routine basis at the size scale of the device to fully understand  the processing and perform failure analysis (FA). This  metrology should be sensitive to small strains and should be  able to resolve the spatial variations of the strain across the  device. It should be practical and reproducible to apply on a  routine basis.


All techniques that map strains at the sub-100 nm scale rely on  some form of transmission electron microscopy or diffraction. [1-4] Although the high angle annular dark field mode of a  scanning transmission electron microscope (HAADF-STEM) is common for high-resolution imaging, geometric phase  analysis (GPA) of HAADF-STEM has not been used  extensively. HAADF-STEM imaging has two strong  advantages compared to normal high-resolution transmission  electron microscopy (HRTEM). One is that HAADF-STEM  images are more reproducible images because they are less  sensitive to experimental parameters. The other is that thicker  samples can be imaged in HAADF-STEM, which can reduce  strain relaxation effects due to sample thinning. However, hysteresis in scanning is a critical defect in the application of  GPA to HAADF-STEM images.


Potential scanning errors in HAADF-STEM


The largest potential source of error in strain measurement in  HAADF-STEM is the beam shift between scan lines due to the  deflector and lens hysteresis. That is, when the beam reaches  the end of its scan across the specimen, it should fly back to


the start. If it fails to arrive precisely at the start, the


subsequent line will be displaced, ux(y), as shown in Fig. 1. This “fly-back” defect involves a relatively large beam


displacement; it should be the largest defect at modest


magnifications. But, since it is essentially 1-dimensional, its


effects on strain measurements can be eliminated by choosing  scan directions that are parallel to a principal axes of strain in  the specimen.


Figure 1: Schematics of possible "fly-back" defect in scanning  mode for HAADF-STEM imaging.


current significantly depends on the device structure and


process parameters.

We have shown that Geometric Phase Analysis of High Angle  Annular Dark Field - Scanning Transmission Electron  Microscope images can be an effective, reproducible technique  to measure local strains in the current node of Si based  transistors.

Correcting for spherical aberrations in solid immersion microscopy using a  deformable mirror


Y. Lu, E. Ramsay, C. Stockbridge, F. H. Koklu, A. Yurt, J. Mertz, T. G. Bifano, M. S. Ünlü and B. B. Goldberg


Departments of Mechanical Engineering, Physics, Electrical and Computer Engineering, Materials Science and  Engineering and Photonics Center, Boston University, 8 St. Mary's Street, Boston, Massachusetts, 02215, USA  Email: luyang@bu.edu


We present a method for correcting spherical aberrations in  solid immersion microscopy through the use of a deformable  mirror. Aberrations in solid immersion imaging for failure  analysis can be induced through off-axis imaging, errors in  lens fabrication or mismatch of design and substrate wafer  thickness. RMS wavefront error correction of 30% is  demonstrated in the case of substrate wafer thickness error.


Since their invention in 1990 [1], solid immersion lenses (SILs) have become widely used in the semiconductor failure  analysis industry to improve resolution and signal collection  for backside photon-based imaging techniques. SILs are  generally of a plano-convex design, where the planar surface  sits in optical contact with the substrate of the device under  test (DUT) and the convex surface faces the backing objective  in the imaging system. Two types of SIL geometries are  shown in Fig. 1, both of which allow the possibility to perform  aberration-free imaging. The first image light at the center of  the radius of curvature of the immersion lens, and is known as  a central SIL – Fig. 1(a). The second image light at the  aplanatic point of the immersion lens, a distance R/n below the  center, and is known as an aplanatic SIL – Fig. 1(b). In a  perfect, on-axis, model system, both designs are aberration  free. The aplanatic SIL also increases the numerical aperture  of the imaging system, and is often referred to as a numerical  aperture increasing lens, or NAIL [2].


Figure 1(a) - Central SIL configuration.


Figure 1(b) - Aplanatic SIL configuration.


When imaging a real DUT with an immersion lens of either  type, many factors lead to aberrations: Off-axis imaging, errors in fabrication of the surfaces, and an unknown thickness  of the substrate and distance from back surface to transistor  level. Since spherical aberration free imaging is only possible  if the region of interest sits at the center or aplanatic point of  the SIL, any mismatch between the design thickness and the  actual thickness of the substrate will rapidly cause an increase  in the focus spot size [3].


In this paper we report a novel technique for correction of  aberrations due to a mismatch between the thickness of a  central SIL and substrate. Various research groups have  studied spherical aberration correction in index mismatched  materials using adaptive optics [4-6]. In our study, a  continuous face-sheet MEMS-based deformable mirror (DM) with 140 electrostatic actuators (Boston Micromachines Corp.) was used to tailor the wavefront of the illumination light to  compensate for the error in thickness. The DM features 3.5μm  stroke and 400μm pitch between actuators. In order to best  control the phase profile of the illumination beam, the DM  was controlled in an open-loop fashion, using techniques  developed previously by Diouf et al [7]. Arbitrary shapes can  be made with this control approach, with shape errors  typically less than 25 nm RMS. Measurements were made of  the reflected spot size, and reflected wavefront error where  possible, and these parameters were minimized using  correction of the input beam. The DM we used was  approximately 97% efficient as the mirror fill factor was over  99%. The mirror surface had a gold coating with reflectivity

Experiment 2: Solid immersion in visible


In order to understand aberration in a central silicon SIL, which operates in the near-infrared, we used the analogy of a  central sapphire SIL operating in the visible. We modeled the  optical system in Zemax (see Fig. 5), using a perfect paraxial  lens in lieu of an objective lens, to match the NA of the  objective that we intended to use in our experiment (a


Mitutoyo 20X, 0.4NA, infinity-corrected objective). Rays  from this lens were focused through a sapphire SIL with a  radius of curvature of 2.38mm and an index of refraction of


1.77. A 150µm-thick slab of N-BK7 glass coverslip was  placed between the SIL and the mirror to provide aberration, in this case both substrate thickness and index mismatch. Figure 6 shows the spot diagram obtained for this  configuration; the black circle shows the Airy disk for this  system, clearly showing less than diffraction limited  performance. We then designed an experiment to correct for  the induced aberration.

The optical setup schematic is shown in Fig. 7. Light from a


helium-neon laser was directed from a DM with an active 0 aperture size of 3.6mm to the pupil of the Mitutoyo infinity-


-3-2-10123


corrected objective. The DM surface was conjugated with the


back pupil plane of the objective using a telescope with a  magnification of 2. The objective focused the light at normal  incidence onto a silvered mirror surface. The reflected light  was then directed via a beam splitter to two detectors; one a  camera and the other a Shack-Hartmann wavefront sensor, which was positioned conjugate to the DM and the pupil  plane. This configuration was used to make a baseline  measurement of the aberration in the system. The sapphire SIL  was then placed on the surface of the mirror and fixed using a  small film of water, before light from the laser was focused to  the center of the hemisphere. As expected, no increase in


spherical aberration was seen in this configuration. Finally, we  introduced a 150µm thick glass cover slip between the SIL  and mirror to create a non-optimal SIL/substrate combination, as in the simulation above. The system was then refocused on  to the mirror surface and an analysis of the wavefront was  made. This showed a dominant spherical aberration term in  the wavefront. To correct for this, open-loop spherical  aberration correction was applied using the DM. This  increased the intensity by a factor of around 1.4, as can be  seen in Fig. 8, and the RMS wavefront error decreased by  30%, leading to a near-diffraction limited spot with low  residual aberration.

Experiment 3: Solid immersion in infrared


SIL applications to failure analysis and imaging of silicon  integrated circuits occur in the near-infrared. We therefore  utilized the same DM to perform wavefront correction in the  infrared using a central silicon SIL optical configuration is  similar to that shown in Fig. 7, where an InGaAs camera  replaced the silicon CCD, a fiber-coupled laser diode at


1.36µm replaced the HeNe as our illumination source, and we  did not use a wavefront sensor. Finally, a 100µm thick silicon  wafer replaced the 150µm glass slide to provide mismatch in  the substrate thickness. Zemax simulation of this configuration  showed that the expected level of spherical aberration due to  the mismatch between the silicon thickness and the SIL  dimension would be 16.9nm RMS of wavefront error. In our  experiment we achieved the best focus using 17.6nm RMS of  wavefront correction. These results are shown in Fig 9.


Figure 9 - Spherical aberration correction for the silicon SIL  and 100 µm silicon wafer.


Most commercial FA microscopes use a higher NA backing  objective than that which we show here; a higher NA  objective will give rise to a significantly larger aberration. When the backing objective has an NA of 0.85, the spherical  aberration is over 800nm (compared to ~15nm above) for a  100µm substrate mismatch, leading to an (uncorrected) RMS  spot size of over 2.5µm at 1.36µm wavelength.


Discussion and Further Work


We have shown in the previous section that we are able to use  a DM to correct for substrate depth error in the central SIL  configuration for modest numerical apertures. In the central  SIL configuration the NA increases by a factor of n, whereas  in the aplanatic configuration this increase is by a factor of n2 (to a maximum NA of n), where n is the refractive index of the  immersion medium. It is not possible to realize an NA of n in  the central configuration. In silicon, the aplanatic 0 configuration allows the realization of NAs close to 3.5. As  the NA of the microscope increases, the system becomes less  tolerant of departures from the ideal substrate thickness, and  greater aberrations occur.


We constructed a model in Zemax for the aplanatic  configuration, using the FA industry common substrate  thickness of around 100µm. An aplanatic silicon SIL of 2mm  radius of curvature was placed on the surface of a 100µm  thick substrate. The SIL height was chosen to match the


100µm depth (aberrations were added later). We used a  paraxial objective with an NA of 0.27 which gave a system  NA of 3.26. In this configuration our simulation gave a spot  size of 4nm, significantly lower than the diffraction limit. This  is a consequence of using a paraxial objective and ray tracing  and should not be seen as physically achievable. However, minimizing the spot size to this baseline provided a useful  method of determining whether the height of the aplanatic SIL  was exactly matched to the substrate depth.


We then added 25µm to the substrate thickness and used the


-Before correction


default merit function to optimize the system to provide the


smallest RMS spot size without altering the dimensions of the


SIL. The only parameter that was varied was the distance  between the objective and the sample. The smallest achievable  spot size using this optimization routine was 41µm, which is


品


almost 200 times the diffraction limit at the analysis  wavelength. An analysis of the wavefront in this system shows


04


that the spherical aberration is around 1.1 λ, although there is  also a strong defocus error present. Our current DM is able to


correct this level of spherical aberration. We are planning to


perform this experiment in the near future and present our  results at ISTFA.


02-1012


We have performed open-loop correction using a deformable  mirror to compensate for wavefront aberration in the central  SIL configuration when the substrate thickness does not match  the designed thickness. We have shown a reduction in RMS  wavefront error of 30% in sapphire using a glass coverslip, and 10% in silicon where the mismatch is purely geometric. This work will be extended to failure analysis applications in  silicon integrated circuits using aplanatic SILs in the nearinfrared where aberration correction is required to realize the  highest possible NA and subsequent resolution and light  gathering power.
