The Challenges of Backside Focused Ion Beam (FIB) Editing in  the Presence of Deep Trench Decoupling Capacitors


Steven B. Herschbein, Carmelo F. Scrudato


George K. Worth, Edward S. Hermann


IBM Systems & Technology


2070 Route 52, Hopewell Junction, NY, 12533


For most advanced semiconductor products, Focused Ion  Beam (FIB) circuit modification and node access through the  backside of the chip is the only viable approach.  The high  density of interconnect wiring and the presence of C4 solder  bumping for chip to module attachment has made complex  edits virtually impossible with long standing conventional  frontside techniques.  Unfortunately, the presence of buried  circuit elements on the very latest designs greatly complicates  the backside editing formula.


The introduction of deep trench capacitors as a distributed  circuit element in logic designs has had a profound impact on  the established methods of backside FIB chip editing.  In  many cases wide area preparatory trenching down to the  underside of circuitry cannot be done without damage to  structures that penetrate the silicon adjacent to active  transistors by as much as 10 microns.  The decision whether to  remove these devices or attempt to work around them requires  an analysis of the impact on circuit performance and an  assessment of the working space (control of anisotropy of  etch, aspect ratio issues, etc.) available for executing the edit. IBM is in the process of developing a new set of procedures  for performing FIB backside edits on circuits that incorporate  these buried structures.


Focused Ion Beam (FIB) tools are routinely used in failure  analysis, process development laboratories and wafer  fabrication facilities throughout the semiconductor industry.  Ion beam induced microsurgery provides the ability to  selectively remove existing materials and deposit new  conductor and insulator layers.  This enables electrical node  access and thus the opportunity to substantially modify or edit  circuits deep within fully processed silicon.  As a result, Focused Ion Beam based circuit edit has become an important  part of the time-to-market strategy of many chip makers and  foundry customers.


Given the easy access afforded by wire bond packaging, FIB  circuit modification was initially performed from the frontside


of the device.  Early products contained sufficient white space  between the upper level metal interconnects to allow  unencumbered access to lower levels.   As devices become  more complex, with more global wiring levels, tighter  geometries, and more extensive use of pattern fill, conventional frontside FIB methods began failing to deliver  functional hardware.  Flipchip packaging also put severe  constraints on frontside FIB success.  Even if one had the  opportunity to work on bare die prior to flipchip package  mounting, the dense distribution of raised solder bumps on the  chip surface results in large regions of the circuitry below  being obscured and inaccessible.  And yet flipchip packaging  also presented a great opportunity, by positioning the bare  backside of the die in easy reach of the analyst.  It only made  sense that a major paradigm shift would occur to keep FIB  circuit modification viable.


In response to the above challenges, backside circuit access by  trenching through the substrate silicon became the access  method of choice.  Historically, some of the specifics of the  backside trenching methods naturally vary depending on the  silicon device structure employed, such as bulk verses siliconon-insulator (SOI), and whether you mechanically pre-thin the  chip or edit through full thickness silicon.  However, the basic  backside process flow converges around establishing a wide  open working floor in close vertical proximity to the circuits  to be edited.  Over time, the result was the development of an  extremely robust methodology for performing backside FIB  operations that ensured a consistent low aspect ratio starting  point for the subsequent drilling, and a simple direct routing  for the interconnect lines.[1]


In the most recent CMOS chip generations, however, the  incorporation of deeply buried subsurface circuit structures  throughout the design has required a fundamental change in  the “trench down to the bottom” procedures in common use.


Vertical Integration & Deep Trench Structures


RIE etched and backfilled deep trench capacitors first made a  commercial appearance with the discrete individually  encapsulated 1Meg DRAM designs over 20 years ago.  At that  time, two competing structures were introduced to replace the  common planar capacitor used as the cell storage element.[2]


By replacing the space limited horizontal planar capacitor  structure with a vertically integrated structure of up to 10 um  deep, area driven cell capacitance and signal margin rose  dramatically.  This has enabled greater levels of integration  and horizontal feature shrinkage, without some of the  limitations and compromises imposed by planar or stacked  cells.  It should be noted, however, that the industry is still


骨


divided on the best approach, with about half going with


trench, and the other half pursuing various forms of stacking.


Trench also scales well, as capacitance can be manipulated


thru dielectric film selection and the depth of the drill and


backfill.    Today’s commercially available DRAM chips run


all the way up to 4 gigabits, with only a relatively modest


increase in die size over the period.  Compared to SRAM, an DT BlockIdealCompromise equivalent number of DRAM bits can operate on considerably


less power and takes up far less space, even when one factors


spotspot


in the required cell refresh functions that need to be added.


Deep Trench on Microprocessors


Modern multi-core microprocessors typically contain large  blocks of memory supporting the L1, L2 and even L3 cache  functions.  When implemented in traditional SRAM, it is not  uncommon for the actual area consumed by memory blocks to  rival or even exceed that of the core logic.  Modeling  performed on an existing single core processor showed that  substituting DRAM for L3 cache SRAM could reduce the  overall chip size by around 40%.[3]  The progression towards  multi-core microprocessors accelerated the push to replace (six transistor cell) SRAM with something more space  efficient.   Given the quest for more storage and overall  functionality while attempting to contain overall die size, it  was not unexpected that DRAM would eventually be  embedded into logic chips to displace bulky SRAM.  The term  eDRAM was coined for the embedding of previously  standalone DRAM into processor chips. [4]


The integration of buried trench into designs other than just  commodity memory was a paradigm shift, but it also opened  up the possibility of using this type of structure throughout the  random logic sections and general circuitry to replace ordinary  planar circuit capacitors.[5] [6]


The Impact on FIB Editing


At first, deep trench decoupling capacitors (DT caps) showed  up only in large isolated blocks.  They were often located in  the ‘dead space’ between logic functional segments, and were  used just for global power supply noise suppression.  These  did not present a huge issue to the backside edit process, as we  could bias the trench center and control the width to steer  clear and leave them undisturbed.  In subsequent designs they  became more prolific and distributed, tuned to individual  circuit needs.  We began to have to narrow our trench profiles  considerably to fit into the pattern of DT block, or even settle


for less than ideal electrical equivalencies for our edits to  avoid them entirely.  Clearly, their presence was impacting the  efficiency and effectiveness of our work, and we were  compromising our output in an extreme effort to avoid dealing  with the problem head-on.


Figure 1: The best opportunity to edit the highlighted net (lots  of white space) was immediately adjacent to a DT block.  The  compromise location represents the closest site that backside  trenching would not disturb these structures. Surrounded by  dense logic, it was a more difficult place to work.


Eventually we faced situations dictated by layout in which  there was no choice but to work close to DT caps.  The  decision whether to delete part of the bank or attempt a set of  nested mills to only clear beside them required an analysis of  the impact on circuit performance, and an assessment of the  working space available for executing the edit.


In almost all of these cases we were advised by the design  team that power supply noise suppression in the region was  sufficiently robust that removal of a portion of the caps was  not an issue.  We found that with a high reliance on  spontaneous XeF2 milling and minimum beam exposure (typical of the SOI backside clearing method) the surrounding  silicon could be etched away leaving the exposed trenches  standing.  Oxide coverings and/or post metal deposition  cleanup processes allowed full editing capability in the  adjacent circuitry without breaking through their thin outer  shells and inducing trench power supply shorts.


Several years back this team pioneered the use of rapid  backside silicon excavation for full thickness editing.  This  allows us to skip the usual machining and polishing of silicon  down to sub 100um, and greatly increases the mechanical  robustness of the repackaged product.  For a growing portion  of our work this has become the preferred starting method.   Unfortunately, the visibility and gas efficiency at the bottom  of a 775um deep hole generally suffers, and more care is  needed.  In order to see exactly what was happening to the  surrounding trenches during this stage of process  development, we temporarily suspended full thickness editing  and went back to pre-thinning.

Chip designs continue to evolve, and in our most recent set of  edits we faced a new set of challenges.  Small sets of DT caps  are now located throughout the entire logic, used to suppress  local switching power supply sag or noise.  In some cases we  can still remove selected sections of caps, but in general the  designers would like to leave as many of the structures intact  as possible.


义


Figure 3.  CAD @ 100um wide field of view showing the  distribution of small DT banks throughout a logic section of a  recent chip design.  FIB editing of circuitry residing between  these banks will require a special approach to not disturb  capacitor functionality.


--


This forced us to take a more ‘bulk silicon edit’ approach


DT


rather than our usual ‘SOI’ XeF2 drive down to the buried  oxide.  Using a combination of IR measurements and low kV


Block


imaging to monitor our position in Z-space, the high volume  large area trenching was terminated just above the ends of the  capacitor tips, and a floor established.  Moderately high aspect  ratio ‘access’ mills were used to clear the space between DT  cap sets down to the underside of the active circuitry – the  plane where most edit work would normally begin.


Figure 4.  Top: Drawing shows the termination of high  volume silicon trenching just above the DT tips.  Bottom: Completed process shows localized access mill trenching  between DT banks with two individual edits, and a connecting  strap.


Figure 5. Top surface was terminated several microns above  the tops of DT tips, and a box milled to the active circuitry  between cap banks.  Exposed bottom area is approximately 7  um, and one side was sloped for the interconnect exit (tungsten over oxide).  Milled DT caps visible in slope wall.


In cases where an interconnect line to another site is needed, one or more sides of this access mill can be sloped to better  enable oxide and metal deposition.  True tall vertical  depositions can be difficult to perform reliably, even with a  tilting stage.  Ideally, the slope is placed where DT caps are  not located.  In the event that the site is totally boxed in, some  caps may need to be cut down and covered over to enable a  suitable exit site.


Deep trench structures offer designers a way of obtaining an  extraordinary level of capacitance for various functions in a  very compact package.  Unfortunately, anything that extends  into the substrate (and these can by as much as 10 um), also  greatly complicates the backside FIB chip edit process.  In  many situations large sections of a cap bank can be disabled  during the trenching process without any functional impact, and edit activity can take place immediately beside them.  In  some cases, however, their decoupling function must remain  intact and undisturbed.  The only way to edit these sites  involves termination of the backside trenching process prior to  reaching the DT tips, and establishing a raised ‘floor’ as a  base from which to work.  Several methods of localized  trenching (anisotropic box or inverse pyramid) can then be  etched down to the active devices and chip wiring between the  capacitor sets.


Another approach is to simply terminate the mill a few  additional microns above the caps and mill an inverted  pyramid down to the active region.  The resulting final floor  area is smaller, but this method works well if the edit site is  well centered between cap banks.  The uniform and gentle  slopes give additional options for line deposition and can aid  imaging (better secondary electron exit).

We continue to watch the activity of the design community, and have advised them that overuse of DT structures could  have a negative impact on future FIB success.


Charge Considerations


One concern with global clearing of all silicon on typical SOI  backside FIB operations is the issue of implanted charge over  a large area.  By terminating the mill far above the active  region and only touching down in a minimal area, total  implanted charge is somewhat minimized.  Whether this will  have an impact on the need for a post edit bake is yet to be  determined.


State of the Art Substrate Manipulation


As a Tool for Enhancing Product Performance


Michael A. Gonzales, Jose Cabanillas


Qualcomm, San Diego, CA, U.S.A.


michaelg@qualcomm.com, (858) 651-8605


Substrate modifications on the Integrated Circuit (IC) package provide opportunities for the Failure Analyst (FA) to troubleshoot a routing failure or allow a design engineer  to create new routing possibilities for a prototype device. The results can mean the difference in finding the root  cause of the problem and being early or late to market.  This paper describes a variety of methods to open sections  of the package circuit board to access and cut I/O traces


200.0 μm


interwoven throughout the package substrate.  It also  describes the use of conductive epoxies for connecting  traces, vias and solders bumps.  Restoring the solder mask  with an ultraviolet (UV) light curing conformal coating is  also discussed. This method was used to characterize  ground sensitivities and simulate inductance effects on the  package. The flexibility and fast turnaround time this  method enables has already enhanced  product  performance.

Figure 3: Comparison of empirical and simulated sputter  yields for (a) silicon and (b) copper using Ne+ and Ga+ ions


density* volume Sputter Yield =-total incident ionstotal number of sputtered atoms=dose*area(molar weight).A


at beam energies from 9 to 35 keV.


The exposure dose was 1.6E+17 ions cm-2 in all three  experiments. At 20 keV, the defects created by helium ions  have an asymmetric distribution with the majority of the  defects occurring near the stopping range of the helium ions (~ 200 nm). For neon and gallium ions, the defects mostly form  close to the substrate surface at 20 keV. Gallium staining  appears to be close to the top 30 nm of the overall 50 nm of  amorphized silicon region. Unlike helium, the implanted neon  bubbles in silicon formed close to the substrate surface instead  of close to the stopping range.


Microcircuit analysis and repair have a long history in  microsurgery technology [1]. There are many references to  accessing the chip [2] directly but relatively few on  accessing the chip via the package substrate.  The package  substrate serves as the link between the IC circuitry and the  application/phone Printed Circuit Board (PCB).  It is here  that an opportunity to effect the routing of the circuitry is  easiest by virtue of being the most accessible.  Multichip  Module substrate packages with two or more die lying in  the same plane also provide access to the substrate circuitry  between the dice.


The good news is: Package substrates do not follow  Moore’s law [3]. Package board geometries have  not  scaled down proportionally with the transistors in the die.  It is in the relatively large substrate package where there is  still access to make cuts, jumpers or combinations of both  cuts and jumpers, as well as providing locations for probe  access. [Figure 1.] Despite recent developments in throughsilicon-stacking which will introduce thousands of inter-die  connections, the board-to-package connections are still  relatively abundant.


Typical trace geometries are tens of microns wide while  metal interconnects in the silicon die can be tens of  nanometers wide.


This method can be incorrectly referred to as a “Package  FIB” in reference to the “Focused Ion Beam” based circuit  editing that incorporates high vacuum and charged ion  beam chemistries for silicon die layout edits.  Unlike  focused ion beam modifications, substrate modifications  can be performed in the open air and use traditional lasers  mounted on a microscope as shown in Figure 2.


Figure 1:  An example of a modification on the package


substrate involving the cutting of  two signal lines.  The


exposed area will be insulated to prevent reshorting during   mounting and testing.


Figure 2: Laser mounted microscope with controls for  wavelength, power and navigation.


Substrate circuit editing began as simple requests from  Failure Analysts who wanted to quickly troubleshoot the  circuitry in question.  By selectively shorting or  disconnecting selected copper traces, we could test possible  sources of failure or determine if the change would  preserve, re-create or alter the status of the failure thus  narrowing the field of possible causes of the “condition”.  It became apparent to designers that these package/board  design modifications could simulate design changes before  committing them to the package or silicon.  They could  also alter routings whose geometries cause undesirable  induction effects.  Finding such bugs early creates a  savings in time and money that are measureable, improve  confidence for the next product revision, and ultimately  lower the time to market for a new product.


Signal trace cutting and substrate removal


Laser mounted microscope


Access to the traces can be made with either a small drill, milling machine or by chemical dissolution.  However, the  most successful method in accessing the substrate circuitry  comes from a laser capable of cutting copper traces, vias  and pads as well as fiberglass embedded in organic resin.


We use a continuous laser that is incorporated in the optical  path of a four objective high power microscope. [Figure 2]  This selectable “green and UV wavelength” laser  microscope uses a 10X, 20X, 50X and 100X objectives.  The lower two objectives are for site location while the  50X and 100X are for simultaneously lasing and viewing  of the sample.  The narrow beam of the 100X objective  results in increased power density. Power is variable for  bulk vs. thin layer removal.


Mechanical milling and chemical etching


Mechanical milling [Figure 3], drilling and selective  chemical etching [Figure 4] provide another means of  accessing the substrate trace lines.  For example, Metal 1  die to die level traces that are covered with over-mold  compound [Figure 5] can be accessed by mechanically  milling off  material  without damaging the dice followed  by a selective acid etch to dissolve any remaining overmold compound and/or solder mask.  The acid used is  composed of fuming nitric, fuming sulfuric acids or  mixtures of both.  The main consideration here is to know  that nitric acid readily dissolves copper while sulfuric acid  does not.  It then becomes a balancing act to provide  enough nitric acid to dissolve any remaining mold  compound or solder mask that was not removed by milling.  This mixture is usually 1:1.  Premilling allows the  selectivity and the short etch times to fully expose the  copper traces without damaging them.


Figure 3: Mechanical milling tool with x,y sweep and z


control with interchangeable drill bits for different material  removal.


Figure 4: Jet etcher used for chemical removal of mold  compounds and solder mask with sulfuric acid, nitric acid, or mixtures of both.


An example of milling the overmold compound followed  by selective acid etching is shown in Figure 5.  Approximately 50 traces were cut to isolate the dice and  enable the Failure Analysis engineers to investigate leakage  from individual die in the package.  This is a rare  opportunity to access M1 copper in the substrate material  due to the fact that the space between the die offered a  unique window of opportunity.  Advances in stacked die  and through silicon via (TSV) products may soon close this  window.


Figure 5:  An example of milling access in the package  between two die to laser cut traces at metal 1 of the  package substrate.


Signal Trace Addition and Insulation


Conductive Epoxy for New Signals


In addition to cutting signal traces, it is often necessary to  add connections to re-route power and signals.  This is  often more challenging than eliminating signals on the  package substrate.  A two part conductive epoxy is mixed  in equal parts and carefully placed to make contact with the  desired surfaces.  The epoxy contains silver particles and  cures in 15 minutes at 100ºC.  The result is a conductive  path that does not shrink and can withstand reflow  temperatures. [Figure 6].


Figure 6:  The image shows the proximity of a newly  created path to several existing solder pads.  Here, the  silver conductive paste is in a lower plane than the solder  pad next to it and is covered with a layer of insulating  conformal coating.


Insulative Conformal Coating


For package substrate modification, a UV cure conformal  coating is used to repair any of the conformal coating or  solder mask to prevent reflow solder from entering an  access point and re-shorting the traces to each other.  The  coating is applied with a microprobe tip to ensure accurate  placement.  It is then treated with a UV source for  approximately 40 seconds to fully cure it.  This material is  capable of withstanding multiple attachments to boards and  solder ball re-flows.


Figure 7 shows multiple applications of the UV cure on the  package substrate.  The solder mask has been removed in  selected areas, allowing access to the metal 1 traces.  Both  cuts at metal 1 isolated those pads from the ground plane, and enabled the signals to communicate.  The UV cure is  seen on top and is used to protect the cut locations from  solder reflowing into the gap.  More than 100 samples were  created and yielded a 66% success rate.


200 μm


Figure 7: This area of the substrate has 2 cut locations at  package M1.  Each of these cut locations are insulated with  UV cure material to prevent solder reflowing into the  space.


Low noise amplifiers are prone to oscillate if a feedback  path between its input and output exists. These feedback  paths can exist on the die but also on the package. Parasitic  capacitances or interaction between inductors are typical  examples of on-chip elements that can introduce spurious  oscillations. There are also package elements than can  cause unwanted oscillations; package interaction between  the different grounds of an amplifier are a typical case of  that. The plot in figure 8 shows the small signal gain of an  amplifier with two different package connections. Initially, the small signal gain showed unacceptable gain peaking as  corresponding to a quasi un-stable amplifier. After


modifying the ground connectivity [Figure 9] of the  package through a package modification, the gain peak was  removed, and the stability of the amplifier (as well as its  performance) was considerably improved.


Figure 8: Shows the gain correction after modifying the  ground connection.


Figure 9: Shows the ground connection modifications.


Key Points to Ponder


With package substrate modifications, there are no  submicron features that need to be resolved for alignment.  The 50X and 100X objectives are sufficient to provide the  necessary resolution.  A typical FIB is working close to  potentially thousands of connections, whereas the substrate  modification is dealing with only a dozen or so in any


particular region of interest (ROI).  Also substrate  modifications typically deal with four metal layers (M4 to  M1) alternately sandwiched between fiberglass and epoxy  substrate layers.


Low Noise Amplifier


Programmable Lasers


Problems to Overcome


Navigation


G


Since navigation is a major component of successful


◆→LNA2


substrate modifications, it would seem that the next logical


占


-■-LNA1


progression would be to make these modifications using a


Gain  Signal(dB)Small


programmable laser.  Laser decapsulators are available in


today’s market that allow us to administer the necessary  laser cuts in a sequential and repeatable manner.  Each  progressive cut could be selectively programmed to handle


the variety of substrate materials, including metal pads, and


1850D60052499分合018901930


traces of varying thickness.


Frequency (MHz)


For now, computer software aids in visualizing the spatial  relationships of the substrate layers.  CAD-to-image  software may be useful when we update to a programmable  laser for navigation and improved selectivity of the  material being removed.


300 μm


Endpoint detection


Endpoint detection however could still be a major concern  for a programmable laser. Despite good programming, it  cannot make on-the-fly judgment calls to keep from lasing  too deep in alternating layers of metal and fiberglass.  Especially challenging would be to mill through vertical  vias.


Repeatability on multiple samples


The ability to make consistent repeatable cuts on a large  sample set is a programmable laser’s best advantage.  However, substrate edits come in typically one, or two-ofa-kind sample sets.  The advantages of a programmable  laser may be offset by a typically small sample size.  A  definite requirement for repeatability with a programmable  laser would be the inclusion of a consistently positionable  sample stage.


Insulation deposition


Currently, it is difficult to reinsulate a trace when it is near  a proposed conductive pathway.  For now, we try to  preserve substrate material that serves to protect a  conductive path.  In the future, it would be advantageous to  find a way to selectively lay down an insulator as easily as


it is to remove it.  This is definitely an advantage that FIB  has over this process.


Conclusion


Whether it is Failure Analysis troubleshooting, design  modification/correction or projecting future design  possibilities, substrate modifications have improved our  ability to make these changes with a minimum turnaround  time and a savings in mask re-spin iterations.  As word  spreads, more and more designers and engineers will see  the many advantages this technique offers.  This method  lends itself well to design for test (DFT) designers to make  more accessible trace routings and include internal probe  pads and fuse like links that could make the cuts easier  especially in the proximity of neighboring traces, vias or  solder bumps.  Whatever future this technique holds, it  provides real opportunity for quick, opportunistic  modifications that will improve our time to market or  advance the possibilities for an innovative design engineer.


In addition, as focused ion beam (FIB) tool resources  become stretched, substrate modifications could ease the  load by reconfiguring some of the routings on the substrate.

Shida Tan, Richard H. Livengood, Roy Hallstein, Darryl Shima, and Yuval Greenzweig


Intel Corporation, Intel Architecture Group, MS: SC9-68, Santa Clara, CA 95054-1549, USA  Shida.tan@intel.com 408-765-8806

Nanomachining capability scaling both in the areas of  machining precision and novel gas chemistries are required for  focused ion beam technology to keep pace with process  technology advancement. In this paper, we review the  nanomachining potential for the Helium Ion Microscope (HIM) and the Neon Ion Microscope (NIM). The paper also  includes an in depth analysis of NIM imaging resolution, subsurface material interaction, and nanomachining  performance.


The semiconductor industry continues to scale in accordance  with Moore’s Law. Microprocessors based on 32 nm process  have been commercially available for more than a year. The  first 22 nm generation microprocessors are due to be released  in early 2012 based on Intel’s architecture and silicon cadence


[1]. These great advances in process technologies pose  challenges in various analytical tools, methods, and  instrumentations.  One specific challenge is in the areas of  nanomachining required for fault isolation, TEM prep, mask  repair, and backside circuit edit.  The recent investment from  the Circuit Analysis Tool (CAT) program under Intelligence  Advanced Research Projects (IARPA) clearly echoes the  critical demands for such advancement from industry [2].


Focused ion beam (FIB) is used for circuit analysis and  modification of semiconductor devices. One of the most  challenging FIB applications is silicon backside circuit  modification. In this application, devices and interconnects are  accessed through the backside of the silicon substrate on flipchip mounted packaged dies in order to modify the circuitry. Gallium liquid metal ion source (LMIS) technology is the  most commonly used charged particle beam for the focused  ion beam (FIB) industry in the past few decades. The state of  the art Gallium FIB column provides a spot size of 5 nm [3]. While R&D efforts continue to improve the gallium beam spot  size, various alternative charged particle beam technology  innovations have been put forth by researchers around the  world.  Examples of these innovations include helium or neon  gas field ionization source (GFIS), lithium or chromium


magneto optical ionization source (MOTIS), multicusp plasma  ion source, and alloy liquid metal ion sources [4-7].


Gas Field Ion Source (GFIS)


1. Helium GFIS


Since its official launch in 2007, helium ion microscope (HIM) has attracted a lot of attention from scientists in various  fields. The HIM and the GFIS technologies have some very  impressive properties, such as high brightness, low energy  spread (<1eV), and an image resolution of 0.35 nm. Recently, helium ion beam has been used for lithography; nanomachining in thin membranes; beam induced chemical  deposition and etching applications [8-10].


Investigations were made to study the suitability of helium ion  in backside circuit analysis [11]. It was found that with a low  atomic mass of 4 amu, the sputter yield of helium is too low  for typical bulk semiconductor materials (<0.1 atom/incident  ion). At a typical operating voltage of 35 keV, helium implant  damage created in silicon is as deep as 431 nm.  At 20 keV, the implant damage is still at a depth of 283 nm, enough to  penetrate multiple process layers in a semiconductor device. At a dose greater than 1.6E17 ions cm-2, (beyond normal  imaging dosages) implanted helium starts forming nano scale  bubbles that can eventually deform the substrate. Subsurface  helium trapping and surface distortion are particularly  significant in crystalline substrates. The net result from helium  straight sputtering in bulk semiconductor materials is typically  surface swelling instead of appreciable material removal.


Other researchers have demonstrated that the HIM can  produce 5 nm vias in thin (100 nm) gold membranes [10]. However, the material removal mechanism in a thin  membrane is different from bulk materials. While helium is  very promising for certain nanomachining applications, it  cannot be successful for backside circuit edit without  introducing beam chemistries.  The large implant range of  helium, which creates a potential for causing damage in  multiple process layers, is also a significant concern.


2. Neon GFIS


Based upon our experience with the HIM, it was recognized  that an ion species with greater mass is desirable for backside  nanomachining applications. Feasibility of using other noble  gases with GFIS technology was explored [12, 13]. A paper


study of ionization energies for several noble gases and  common impurities is summarized in table 1 [13]. Besides


100nm


)e(


helium, the most viable candidate for GFIS is neon; since the  rest of the noble gases have ionization energies similar to  common impurities.

In the GFIS, the electric field required to ionize the field for  neon atoms is about 20% less than required for helium atoms.


Resolution=2.0nm


At this lower field strength, the neon beam commonly has a


(25 keV 0.2 pA)


limited lifetime and suffers from instabilities in the emission  current [14].  Recently, researchers at Carl Zeiss and Intel  Corporation have jointly improved the stability and lifetime of


the neon GFIS to the point where it has become a suitable  source for a neon ion microscope (NIM) [15].  The following  experimental results are based upon a proof of concept neon  ion microscope at the Zeiss laboratories.


NIM Results and Discussions


In this section, we discuss results obtained from investigating  NIM, including imaging resolution, sputter yield, ion beam  material interaction, and machining precision.


Resolution=2.4nm(10 keV 0.2 pA)


1. Resolution analysis


The imaging resolution test for the NIM was conducted using  highly oriented pyrolytic graphite (HOPG), which has sharp  features suitable for edge analysis. High resolution images  were captured at 10, 25, and 28 keV. The resolution test  results are shown in Fig. 1. The measurements displayed show  average results from 3 different image processing software  packages. The best neon image resolution (Fig. 1(a)) was  achieved at 28 keV with 100 fA beam current. The neon  image resolution degraded by ~2x with a reduced beam energy  of 10 keV (Fig.1(c)).


Figure 1: NIM resolution test results on HOPG sample at (a) 28 keV, (b) 25 keV, and (c) 10 keV. Average edge resolution  values (35 to 65) obtained from three analysis algorithms are  listed on the micrographs.


2. Sputter yield analysis


It is important that the ion species used in circuit edit have a  significant sputter rate. This is critical because, some materials  used in the semiconductor industry are not easily removed by  chemical etch alone.  In these experiments, the neon beam’s sputter yield on Si, Cu, and SiO2 have been characterized in  the energy range of 9-34 keV.

4. Initial Characterization of Nanomachining Precision


With an understanding of the neon beam’s image resolution  and subsurface material interaction, the nanomachining  precision was further investigated. Bulk polycrystalline Cu  and SiO2 were used as a substrate for this analysis. A series of  rectangular vias with varying widths (15, 25, and 50 nm) were  milled in to Cu and SiO2 at 5, 10, and 20 keV beam energy. The vias were protected by E-beam Pt prior to creating the  cross-section samples.

Figure 6: (a) Measured via width vs. depth profile (b)


The ion beam profile and ion material interaction are two  factors in determining the ultimate nanomachining precision.


For heavier elements such as gallium, the ion beam profile (including beam tails) is the dominating factor in  nanomachining precision. For lighter elements, ion lateral and  longitudinal ranges increase dramatically with increased beam  energy. The reduced beam energy brings benefit in reduced  ion-material interaction volume. Ion beam profile and ion  material interaction change with beam conditions such as  energy, and probe forming optics, beam condition needs to be  carefully optimized for each intended applications.


Conclusions and Future Work


In this work, we performed an initial characterization of the  prototype NIM’s imaging resolution, sputter yield, and  nanomachining precision.  For higher atomic mass ions (such  as gallium), the ion beam profile is the dominating factor in  determining nanomachining precision. Whereas for neon, it is  likely that the probe size has been significantly reduced to the  point where it is no longer the limiting factor for the  nanomachining precision. Rather, the nanomachining of neon  may be limited by other factors that will be the subject of  future investigations.


The authors plan to continue to investigate gas assisted milling  and deposition for neon. Experiments will be carried out to  characterize nanomachining precision under various gas  chemistries and the resulting subsurface damage during these  processes. Testing will include the use of 32 nm  microprocessors to help understand NIM’s application  potentials.

Furthermore a comparison of diffractive and refractive solid  immersion lenses is presented, both created in a similar process. Apart from general aspects of dSILs and rSILs (refractive SILs), details of the designs presented in this work are compared. This  leads to the insight of which method (dSIL or rSIL) has its  advantages for which type of application.


While the increasing number of metal layers and flip-chip  packages force failure analysts to analyze devices from the  backside of the chip through the bulk silicon material, it also  offers the possibility to use this material by shaping it into optical  components. The high index of refraction of silicon is very  beneficial for immersion microscopy [1] to regain the resolution  that is lost due to the longer wavelength needed for an analysis  through silicon.


Several works were presented in this area with different solid  immersion lens (SIL) concepts. They can roughly be divided by  the type of lens they use and how they are applied to the sample. Probably most popular is placing on the surface of a sample a  SIL based on the principles of refraction (rSIL) [2]. A bit  different, but also placing a SIL on the sample, are solutions


using a SIL based on the principles of diffraction (dSIL) made of  highly refracting glass [3] or also as presented in a recent work  made of silicon [4]. dSILs became known among failure analysts, because they can be created out of silicon using a combination of  ion beam lithography by a focused ion beam (FIB) and a plasma  etch [5, 6]. Though being more complex to make, rSILs were  also created out of backside silicon material using computer  numerical control (CNC) milling [7] or a FIB process [8].


Since the creation of an ideal refractive SIL with a FIB remains  challenging, even when using a chemistry-assisted FIB process


[9], it was chosen to also follow previous works on diffractive  SILs [3, 5] and based on those to develop a way of creating  dSILs out of silicon material using only a single FIB process. In  addition, the created dSILs are compared to the rSILs presented  earlier [9], showing the differences and advantages of both  methods. The images of Fig. 1 and Fig. 2 illustrate the basic  principle of dSILs and rSILs created out of silicon material with  a chemistry-assisted FIB process. It should be noted, that despite  changing the surface of the sample, they can easily be removed  by polishing [10] to restore an unaltered sample surface for  further SIL creation or other analysis methods.

Creating the dSIL


The dSILs used in this work are binary dSILs or Fresnel phase  plates, to be precise. They are an approximated version of the  Fresnel lens, consisting of a series of concentric rings with  predefined radii, milled into the silicon material to a consistent  depth. The basic principle is to create constructive interference at  the focal point by sequentially changing the surface structure. Adjacent areas of the surface need to be adjusted so that  incoming waves hitting the surface at various distances from the  center of the lens reach the focal point with a difference in  optical path length of a multiple of the wavelength. This, however, only works for monochromatic wavelength  applications. Brunner et al. present a detailed model of dSILs [3] and how to calculate the radii of the dSIL using Eq. 1-5. The  variables used in these equations and the basic principle are  illustrated in Fig. 3.

The involved  variables ,  and  (Eq. 3, 4, 5) consist of the index of refraction  n of the lens material (3.5 in this case for silicon), the focus shift  factor k (chosen to be 1 in this case, to create the equivalent of a  hemispheric or central refractive SIL), the value m describing the  order of diffraction as the optical path length by a multiple of the  wavelength and the integer j enumerating the radii. , which is  the wavelength in air that is used.


Calculating the radii rj for integer values of jm indicates the  positions, at which the wave-fronts hit the focal point in phase, creating constructive interference. The lens surface has to be on  the same level for all of these values. The positions, where rj

Optimization of the FIB created dSILs


The following aspects of the process were analyzed and  optimized in search for the best dSIL recipe: influence of the not  ideally shaped outer rings, redesign of the bitmaps taking the ion  beam diameter into account, starting at a low level in the center  of the dSIL, increasing the grayscale values towards the outside  of the dSIL, using a smaller beam current with smaller beam  diameter and also applying anti-aliasing during the creation of  the FIB bitmap.


The influence of the not ideally shaped outer rings (see Fig. 9) of  the dSIL was analyzed by creating several dSILs with a similar  recipe but different diameters. The processed area and other  process parameters were kept constant to isolate the variation of  the dSIL diameter. Fig. 12 shows some of the bitmaps used in  this analysis (150 μm as regular diameter, 120 μm and 90 μm).


Figure 12:  Three FIB bitmaps of the same dSIL pattern with  different diameters in order to analyze the effect of the outer  rings.


The results were optically working dSILs even down to a  diameter of 90 μm instead of the initial 150 μm. As expected  from common optics, the smaller dSILs showed less  improvement in resolution and therefore fewer image details, due  to their smaller opening angle of the lens. The aberrations in the  image did not improve. In conclusion, this analysis showed that


the outer rings of the initial 150 μm dSIL have a positive effect


on the imaging properties of the lens, even though they are not


ideally shaped.


The next aspect in the optimization of the FIB created dSILs was


to take the diameter of the ion beam into account while


programming the FIB bitmaps. The initial bitmaps assumed a


small ion beam diameter of negligible size. Now, the bright areas


of the bitmap were shifted by half an ion beam diameter (see


diameter symbol and dashed line in Fig. 13). In this area the


surface of the sample is exposed to the ion beam according to 1.4 μm H Eq. 6 and creates the lowered regions of the dSIL. Using this


kind of bitmap again produced optically working dSILs, but did


not lead to an improvement in image quality (see Fig. 14).

The part of the FIB created dSIL process, which was modified  second to last was the ion beam current. Generally, working with  smaller ion beam currents can create finer structures due to  smaller beam diameters, but also removes less material. In this  ase an ion beam current of 1 nA was used instead of the c


initial   nA. The experimental results showed that with this smaller  current and the same processing time of 15 minutes a positive  effect on the dSIL was noticed, even though the ideal depth of  the rings was not reached. Fig. 18 shows a laser scanning image  with a dSIL design including the smaller 1 nA beam current and  a corresponding beam diameter onsideration (see Fig. 13). The  aberrations in the center of the image were reduced, while the  rest of the image still left room for improvement.

The last presented optimization of the FIB dSIL process deals  with the programming of the FIB bitmap. The use of only two different grayscale values resulted in artifacts while fitting the


ed during the scaling, which helps to  better recreate the round shape by using intermediate grayscale


theoretically round patterns into a rasterized pixel bitmap. These  artifacts were visible off-centered rings in the bitmap, which in  fact were also etched into the dSIL structure. This problem was  improved by the application of anti-aliasing. The resolution of  the bitmap was temporarily increased by a factor of 8 in height  and width (4096 instead of 512 pixels) and then scaled down to  the 512 by 512 pixel bitmap compatible with the FIB system. An  anti-aliasing filter was appli


values. Thereby the bitmap does not only consist of high and low  values, but also grayscale values in between. These intermediate  values are calculated depending on how much of the pixel area is  part of a bright or a dark zone of the ideal ring structure. The  positive effect of this method can be seen in Fig. 19. The bitmap  on the right, created with anti-aliasing, shows a significant  reduction of the previously described artifacts. The same could  be observed in the dSIL structures created with this type of  bitmap. Using this dSIL in a laser scanning microscope again  showed an optically working dSIL, but not an aberration-free  image (Fig. 20).

As a conclusion from the six aspects modified during the  optimization of the FIB created dSILs, it can be said that the  process itself is quite robust regarding parameter variations. All  of the presented methods did create dSILs which worked  optically and could be brought into focus in some way. On the  other hand, the final step towards a truly aberration-free image  showed to be rather challenging. Both the SRP and BB blocks are designed to support a  multitude of experiments including FIB cut and via  resistance, etch rate determination, end point  characterization, and design for debug cuts/connections. Most  experiments are targeted on poly through M2 which are the  key layers utilized in the FIB. In the past all these types of  experiments required mechanical probing which is a tedious  process with low yield. The architecture designed for the PSD  block can support on-die mechanical probing but a new


technique was implemented to allow for package/board level  testing using the bi-directional analog pins.


An example of this is shown in the Fig. 6 schematic where  the pin1 signal is routed through a FIB option, then a contact, followed by M1 and M2. It is then routed out to pin2. Adjacent to these layers is a signal coming from pin3 routed  to ground.

It is obvious to see from Fig. 6 and 7 that the measured  dimensions start to approach the intended dimension only  towards the bottom of the milled via. Above this, the amount  of over etch increases towards the top of the vias. The  overetch may be attributed to some combination of the ion  beam’s profile and the ion beam’s material interaction. Given  the preliminary nature of these experiments and the limitations  of the prototype NIM platform, it is likely neon performance  could be further optimized.


Although the neon beam has a 1-2 nm probe size at 20 keV, the amount of over etch created by a 20 keV neon beam is  approximately 30 to 40 nm in Cu at the top of the via. In SiO2, the over etch is more significant. It was generally observed  that as the aspect ratio of the vias increases, the amount of  overetching becomes greater. In part, this may be due to the  increased sputter yield from the ion beam striking the via  sidewall at a glancing angle.


When vias with similar depth are compared, the amount of  overetch present in 15 nm, 25 nm, and 50 nm vias do not have  a significant difference. This tells us that overetch introduced  under a particular set of circumstances (ion beam mass, probe  current distribution, beam energy, substrate properties, via  depth, etc.) is independent of the intended dimension.


Instant Solid Immersion Lens creation in silicon with a Focused Ion Beam


- comparing refractive and diffractive methods


P. Scholz, U. Kerst, C. Boit


Berlin University of Technology, 10587 Berlin, Germany  philipp.scholz@tu-berlin.de, +49-30-314-26803


T. Kujawa, T. Lundquist


DCG Systems, Fremont, CA 94538, USA


The theoretical fundamentals of diffractive solid immersion  lenses (dSILs) where revised and adapted to a new application: the direct single-step chemistry-assisted creation of binary dSILs  in silicon with a focused ion beam (FIB). Current results were  able to prove the general functionality of this technique, but also  showed the limitations still present. These limitations were  identified; the underlying problems were analyzed and were  addressed by optimizing several aspects of the process.


The presented dSIL has a diameter of 150 μm and is created in  15 minutes of processing time. It is designed for a sample  thickness of 70 μm, which can be well adjusted if needed. For  this sample thickness, the theoretical numerical aperture is about


2.5, offering a significant improvement in resolution.

Comparing FIB created dSIL and rSIL


For a comparison of the FIB created dSIL and the rSIL as  described in [9], an rSIL is created on the same sample as the  dSILs presented earlier, but with a sample thickness of 55 μm to  match the lens design. Once again a chemistry-assisted FIB  process with an iodine-based chemistry is used, but this time an  ion beam current of over 8 nA and a processing time of 55


minutes is needed. A laser scanning microscope image of this  rSIL using a wavelength of 1300 nm and a 100x objective with  NA 0.5 (to better match the opening angle of the rSIL) can be  seen in Fig. 21. The smaller opening angle of the rSIL compared  to the presented dSIL achieves a lower resolution, but still a  significant improvement over the image without SIL. The image  shows an additional magnification of about 2.1x and less  aberrations, than the dSIL.


Figure 21:  1300 nm laser scanning images of a 55 μm thick  sample, without (left) and with (right) the FIB created rSIL  according to [9]. Image with rSIL is taken using a 100x objective (NA 0.5) matching the ideal opening angle of the rSIL. The  image is cropped and scaled to show similar features.


In order to have an efficient overview of the FIB created dSIL  and rSIL comparison, the parameters and properties of the lenses  are collected in Table 1. Apart from general aspects of dSILs and  rSILs, details of the designs presented in this work are compared. A similar table comparing common rSILs placed on the sample  surface, dSILs created out of silicon and regular liquid  immersion microscopy can be found in [11] on page 60.


Even though backside analysis through the bulk silicon is limited  by infrared wavelength and absorption, it also gives the  possibility to retrieve some of the thereby lost resolution by using  the silicon as the optical medium for creating solid immersion  lenses (SILs). These lenses when carved out of bulk silicon by a  focused ion beam can be shaped much smaller than usual SILs  mounted to the microscope objective and have a perfect coupling  to the sample, since they consist of bulk silicon. Two basic  concepts - the refractive and the diffractive SIL - are evaluated  and compared in this work. The main targets are an improvement  in image resolution and the usability of the presented SIL  concepts. Both concepts of this work can be realized using only a  single processing step in a FIB system. Furthermore, they can  easily be removed by polishing. The presented diffractive SIL (dSIL) method creates a lens of 150μm diameter in 15 minutes of  processing time. The dimensions are adjustable to a given  thickness. For the 70μm sample used here, the theoretical  numerical aperture is about 2.5, offering a significant  improvement in resolution. The process was optimized by  analyzing the influence of the not ideally shaped outer rings, by a  redesign of the bitmaps taking the ion beam diameter into


account, by starting at a low level in the center of the dSIL, and  by increasing the grayscale values towards the outside of the  dSIL. Furthermore using a smaller beam current with smaller  beam diameter and anti-aliasing during the creation of the FIB  bitmap were applied. Despite of all these efforts, an aberrationfree image has not yet been achieved. It has however shown that  the dSIL theory can be directly applied and that the process is  somewhat robust towards parameter variations.


Table 1:  Comparison of diffractive and refractive SILs created  with FIB and presented in this work.


The comparison of the FIB created dSIL and the rSIL showed  that once both methods are developed to their full potentials, the  rSIL has its advantages when it comes to broadband applications  and in unique situations where the available sample surface has  maximum restrictions (since the rSIL has a better ratio of field of  view over needed processing area). Currently the limitations of  the presented rSIL method are the ability to precisely meet the  ideal shape over the whole lens area and to create large lenses  giving large opening angles. Both of which restrict the image  quality and the maximum possible improvement in resolution. The dSIL on the other hand enables us to create large lens shapes  in a short processing time (15 min. in this case), because a much


smaller amount of material needs to be removed, and it can easily  be adjusted for various sample thicknesses.  It is thereby more  likely to make full use of the shortened wavelength in silicon. The dSIL, however, relies on monochromatic wavelength  applications and despite the presented efforts on optimization, it  did not yet create a fully aberration-free image.


In summary either method has its unique application, giving the  motivation to develop both methods to their full potential.

Circuit Edit and Optical Probe Development and Validation for Next Generation  Process Nodes


John A. Giacobbe


Intel Corporation, Folsom, CA


Patrick Pardy, Charles A. Peterson, David Shykind, Scot E. Zickel


Intel Corporation, Hillsboro, OR


Circuit Edit and Optical Probe technologies must scale with  Intel’s 2 year process cycle and the tick-tock design model. Geometry shrinking combined with revolutionary and  evolutionary process changes such-as high-k and metal gate, lower-k interlayer dielectrics, and non-planar devices, make  this very challenging. To develop new tools, analytical  processes, and validate if the current tool suite can analyze  next generation process node and architectures, a special  debug block has been designed into Intel’s process test  vehicle. In this paper the authors first provide an overview of  the Debug Block, we then provide an overview of the LADA, IREM, LVP, TRE, and FIB tools and their corresponding  technical challenges for Intel’s next generation  microprocessors. Finally we discuss the circuits, layout, and  32nm results.


Specifically designed physical debug circuitry allows tool  developers to perform critical experiments about 1 year before  1st Si arrives for the lead product. These circuits have been  included in the process test chip for the past 4 process  technology nodes and have evolved into a requirement for  Intel’s key debug development laboratories. In preparation for  the 32nm products a group of developers from across Intel  joined forces to design and implement a totally new and novel  set of circuits aimed to keep tool development on track for the  next few process generations. This new set of circuits is  referred to as the Physical Silicon Debug (PSD) Block. There  are 2 main categories of supported debug technologies, Focused Ion Beam (FIB) and Optical Probe.


Figure 1 shows a high level schematic of the block which  contains the input and output control, probe block, FIB block.  There are 4 digital input control pins for test selection, one  clock (CLK) input, and two digital output pins for test  observations. Direct (un-buffered) pin access (14 pins) are  also used for direct analog voltage and resistance  measurements. These package level control pins provide very  simple pin level decode enabling multi-site testing without  the need of a tester. Limiting the circuit complexity increases  functionality on developing process technologies. Timing


sensitive circuits (i.e. scan chains) can be challenging on very  early processes, we therefore decided not to use them.


Figure 1: Top Level Schematic of PSD Block showing major  functional blocks and interface pins.


FIB Block overview:


The FIB test features have 3 sections, ROC (Ring Oscillator), BB (Backbone), and SRP (Serpentine). The ROC block  contains ring oscillators used to test functional and timing  related FIB experiments. It also included many design for  debug features to simplify FIB connections and cuts. The BB  structure is a layout specific block built with layer by layer  experiments with graduated widths and spacing’s. The SRP is  also layout specific with experiments for lateral invasiveness (shorting and cutting) and direct FIB via measurement  structures.


Optical Probe Block overview:


The probe features have 3 sections: LVP (Laser Voltage  Probe), FI (Fault Isolation) and (EMR) Emission Microscopy  Resolution.


The LVP block contains circuits with graduated delays and  edge rates used to evaluate tool acquired waveform accuracy. Device spacing’s in layout are also critical to reduce signal to  noise ratios. The FI circuits comprise digitized delay blocks  used to evaluate tool acquired waveforms. Isolated transistors  are also included to calibrate signal to noise ratios. The ER  circuits are layout critical structures used to evaluate optical  properties including two dimensional graduated sizing and  spacing.


Debug Tool Overviews and Challenges


The FIB:


In addition to new technology development/characterization, the FIB PSD block is also heavily weighted towards providing  a medium for circuit edit recipe development; user process  training, and to gain experience on real silicon. So the special  designed circuits and layout are targeted to support both high  speed functional and low speed (or DC) activities. In addition  many non-electrical features are included for etch and  deposition characterization.


The process of performing backside Circuit Edit (CE) requires many tools and processes with the overall goal of  changing a circuits electrical characteristics or gain access to  nodes for observe-ability[1]. The FIB is the final and most  critical step in the process and is most impacted by process  scaling and material changes. It uses a focused Ga+ ion beam  to remove and add typical processing materials like SiO2 and  Tungsten. To obtain material etch selectivity a gas field (e.g. XeF2, Cl2) is introduced thus allowing the access and  removal of metal interconnects. For metal depositions a  metal-organic gas such as W(CO2)6 is used and for dielectric  a TEOS gas[2].


Aside from keeping up with x-y scaling and the thinner layers (z scaling) from generation to generation, more recent  material and layer changes have caused major inflection  points resulting in extreme challenges for FIB. The  introduction of Cu interconnects on 130nm products almost  eliminated milling selectivity of interconnects over ILD, then  low-k dielectrics on 90nm further exasperated this issue.


The LADA:


Laser Assisted Device Alteration (LADA), was developed  initially as a debug technique. A near-infrared laser is raster  scanned over a failing circuit while simultaneously running a  failing pattern on a Device Under Test (DUT) [3]. This pattern  is run as fast as possible and the Vcc and frequency of the  DUT are tuned to be on a shmoo edge. As the laser scans over  devices, it creates electron-hole pairs in the device channel  causing a speed up of both the P and N devices. Thus the  laser can be used to selectively advance various paths in a  speed path. This will induce changes in the pass or fail  behavior of the DUT. These changes are mapped over a  scanned image of the layout giving designers clues to design  fixes. It was later found that this technique was also effective  at fault isolation (FI) in cases of marginal fails.


Although LADA has proven to be an effective technique for  debug and FI since the 90nm process node, we did not have a  rigorous method of gauging the effect of the laser on devices. Specifically, we were looking for measurements of changes  induced in drive current at the device level and secondly, measurements of induced timing shifts resulting from the  drive current changes at the circuit level. This data is  important, not only to understand better how to predict what


the LADA signal implies about speed paths and defects, but  also to better predict how LADA performance trends with  each new process generation.


The LVP:


The Laser Voltage Probe (LVP) platform is another laserbased system that plays an important role in debug due to its  capability of acquiring high-speed voltage waveforms from  individual transistors on a processor.  The LVP system uses a  highly focused and short laser pulse (9ps) to sample the  voltage changes off a transistor’s drain during synchronous  operation of the DUT on a tester[4].  The wavelength, like  with LADA, is 1064 nm for maximal electro-optic  modulation on silicon and adequate transparency through the  substrate. It injects two laser sources into the DUT, cancels  the noise and detects/constructs waveforms using a softwarebased oscilloscope. The wavelength of the spectrally matched  lasers is 1064nm, allowing for transparency of the bulk  silicon. This enables raster imaging of the transistors through  the backside of the polished DUT so the device can be  localized and probed with an incident beam. Electo-optical  action modulation of the P/N junction, when the connected  node toggles, causes both absorption and polarization of the  injected laser light.  The reflected beam is modulated with  this information for detection and waveforms construction.


One issue of concern for the LVP is laser invasiveness and  the impact to circuits while measurements are made.   Although the impact is most likely post measurement due to  the speed in which the laser light interacts with the target  transistor


The TRE:


The Time-Resolved Emission (TRE) platform is a detectorbased non-invasive system that is used to detect and count  photons that are emitted during transistor switching events.  This tool detects these switching events and places a record of  this in a timeline (software-based oscilloscope) for debug and  speed path analysis[5].  The data is represented in histogram  Gaussian form. Imaging and isolation is difficult so shutters  are used to isolate single devices from nearest neighbors.


Some of the specific challenges with the TRE tools are  imaging and localization of small devices for data collection, cross-talk from nearest transistor neighbors, detection at low  VCC levels and bandwidth.  .


The IREM:


Infra-Red Emission Microscope (IREM), works by capturing  recombination radiation and is used to determine the logical  state of a transistor for fault isolation, or spot a defect by  looking for an emission “hot spot”. The IREM is based on a  conventional wide-field microscope with Near Infrared (NIR) optics and a sensitive array, and presents a NIR image of the  DUT[6]. The current IREM uses a 1.4 NA oil immersion  objective, future generations of the tool will employ a higherNA SIL (NA 2.45 like the LVP and TRE) to resolve  minimum pitch devices.


FIB Circuits and Results:


The FIB block contains 3 sub-blocks (ROC, SRP, BB)


designed to help develop FIB equipment and circuit edit  processes using both geometrical and electrical experiments. This block was also designed to allow for multiple  experiments and training exercises. The block contains  multiple bi-directional analog pins to enable package/board  level test, and has a series of control pins and one digital  output to monitor ring oscillator frequency.


M1FIB via


These results also provide us with resistance values that can


FIB jumper

M1山

Aside from the specific features described above the FIB


(+) ()


block also contains many design for debug features for  navigation and electrical/geometrical validation including  local and global fiducials, functional and non-functional  bonus logic cells, mechanical probe point and connection  cells. These cells are part of the standard library supplied to  all products so including these allows for early learning and  the ability to make changes before the leading project.


LADA Circuits and Results


One of the key features designed for the Probe Block is the  LADA Shift Timer (LST) which is used to help quantify the  LADA effect. Simplified in Fig. 11, it consists of an  intentional speed path with an output that can be easily  accessed. The PLL clock is divided once to create a 50% duty  cycle “signal”. The signal is launched by a “launch” latch  whose clock is also the PLL clock. The signal travels through  a configurable set of delay inverters and is then received by a “Catch” latch. The output of the two latches is compared by  XOR and that result is sent to a DUT pin.

x-talk (min separation)


Figure 12 shows results for 4 controllable speed paths. As the  delay is increased from delay 1 (small) to delay 4 (large) the  SHMOO boundary shifts to the upper right (less green  passing areas). When the LADA is focused on the target gates  the same effect occurs shifting the SHMOO boundaries as the  LASER speeds up or slows down critical gates in the variable  speed path.


Additional data has been collected on other target structures  showing a complex behavior depending on the target device’s  electrical relations and physical proximity to other structures.


TRE and LVP Circuits and Results


Some of the specific challenges with both the LVP and TRE  optical tools are imaging and localization of small devices for  data collection, cross-talk from nearest transistor neighbors, detection at low VCC levels and bandwidth. An additional  issue of concern is laser invasiveness and the impact to  circuits while measurements are made.

TRE data showing cross-talk obtained from the 32nm test  chip circuits described above is shown in Fig. 14. The white  circle highlights an emission for the minimum separated  devices (bottom signal) that is not seen in the devices with 2x  separation (top signal). IREM Circuits and Results


The major challenge facing the IREM microscope is optical  resolving power. As the IREM is upgraded with higher and  higher NA objectives, assessing resolution over the actual  emission wavelength band for a new process is a critical need  for both developers and tool owners.


The IREM emission targets are a set of combs spaced at  N*min process pitch and 2N*minimum process pitch from  process minimum to 8*minimum for both narrow and wide P  and N diffusions. An example of the diffusion layout is shown  in Fig. 15. This provides a realistic set of resolution targets  matched to the actual process emission wavelength band for  both testing new lenses and ensuring currently deployed tools  are performing near their theoretical (diffraction) limit.


Figure 15: Diffusion layout of IREM emission combs.


Figure 16: IREM emission from P and N combs(left) and line  intensity slice of N combs(right).


Figure 16 is an example of the resolution comb targets for  narrow and wide N diffusion combs at 4xmin and 8xmin. The  red dashed vertical line shows the contrast line slice through  the N pitch combs. The blue circle highlights that the IREM  can resolve the separation for the 8xmin N devices but not the  4xmin devices.


The debug block and its circuits have been instrumental for  characterizing capabilities for LADA, LVP, TRE, IREM, and


CE FIB, and has been used as part of Intel’s over debug  capability readiness flow for the last several process  generations. The most recent version of the PSD block was  successfully used on for 32nm process node readiness  demonstrating capability scaling and identifying critical  challenges for future process nodes (e.g. 22 nm Fin-FETs). Some of the key learning’s for the 32nm node include  quantifying impact of FIB depositions on circuit  modifications, validating the LADA effect, determining the  minimum separation without cross-talk for TRE and LVP, and the minimum diffusion spacing resolvable by the IREM.   Looking towards the future we plan on migrating the PSD  block into all new process test vehicles with the addition of  new circuits based on the challenges foreseen with 22nm and  14nm devices.

3DIC Fault Isolation Using the OBIRCH Approach


Ming-Sung Hsu, Yian-Liang Kuo, Yu-Ting Lin, Ru-Ying Huang, Min-Feng Ku and Chih-Horng Chang  Failure Analysis Division, Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company, Ltd., Hsin-Chu, Taiwan


mssheu@tsmc.com


This paper uses an interesting specific case study to highlight  the non-destructive fault isolation demonstration of 3DIC  stacked dies applied the optical beam induced resistance  change (OBIRCH) approach.


In the three-dimensional integrated circuit (3DIC) world, the  through silicon via (TSV) and micro-bump (u-bump) components function as the interconnection between two  stacking dies. [1,2] Since the 3DIC with TSV/u-bump is a new  technology, there are many challenges in the failure analysis (FA) field, especially in fault isolation. In this study, the nondestructive fault isolation of 3DIC stacked dies applied the  optical beam induced resistance change (OBIRCH) approach  is demonstrated.


In general FA cases of 3DIC, the traditional FA method (such  as C mode – scanning acoustic microscope (C-SAM) or Xray) is applied to isolate some 3DIC short failure conditions. Fig. 1 reveals an example of a 3DIC short circuit failure. The  bridged u-bumps are observed by X-ray, as shown in Fig. 1.


Figure 1: An x-ray image of the 3DIC u-bumps bridge short  failure condition.


Experiment


Traditional fault isolation


Because 3DIC sample preparation is more and more complex  and difficult due to stacked dies and warp issues as well as  smaller dimensions, it is better to isolate faults with nondestructive methods than destructive ways. However, due to  the small dimension (~ 20um u-bump v.s. ~ 100um Controlled  Collapse Chip Connection (C4) bump) of the stacked dies with  TSV structures, the non-destructive fault isolation for such  3DIC high resistance failure conditions is difficult to perform  by using current traditional FA skills, such as C-SAM and Xray. The current-voltage (I-V) curve of 3DIC high resistance  failure is demonstrated in Fig. 2a. The investigated case is  using daisy chain test vehicle , as shown in Fig. 2b, with TSV & u-bump and fail units suffer high resistance failure (the  resistance of fail unit is about 19K ohm, but normal unit  resistance is only about 31 ohm). In this case, the C-SAM and  X-ray images of that failed sample from an early stage 3DIC  tool vendor feasibility demonstration can not find any  abnormality, as shown in Figs. 2c and 2d.


Figure 2a: The I-V curve of the 3DIC high resistance failure  condition.


Figure 2b: The daisy chain test vehicle of this investigated  case.


Figure 2c: The C-SAM image of the 3DIC high resistance  failure condition shown in Fig. 2a.


Figure 2d: The x-ray image of the 3DIC high resistance  failure condition shown in Fig. 2a.


Solder bridge


Fault isolation using the OBIRCH approach


OBIRCH is a useful method for FCBGA fault isolation. By  tracing test vehicle chip current path, short or leakage failure  can be isolated by OBIRCH, as shown in Fig.3a ~ Fig.3c for  FCBGA C4 bump short failure condition and Fig.3d ~ Fig.3f  for leakage failure condition.

Figure 3f: The X-SEC SEM image of FCBGA leakaget failure  condition shown in Fig. 3d of which the fault is isolated using  the OBIRCH method. The image shows solder extrusion  between C4 bump.


Due to the thin die thickness (around 100um) and the common  ground structure feature of that 3DIC failed sample, the  OBIRCH approach successfully isolated that failure, as shown  in Fig. 4a. The cross-section (X-SEC) scanning electron  microscope (SEM) observation was performed at that failed  location, as shown in Fig. 4b, and a poor joint between the  TSV and the u-bump can be observed.


Figure 4a: The OBIRCH image of the 3DIC high resistance  failure condition shown in Figs. 2(a), (b),(c) and (d). The fault  can be isolated, as circled.


u-bump


Figure 4b: The X-SEC SEM image of high resistance 3DIC  failure condition shown in Fig. 4a of which the fault is  isolated using the OBIRCH approach. The image shows a  poor joint between the TSV and the u-bump.


Discussion


Because the test vehicle revealed the feature of specific  common ground, thus, the obvious electrical circuit pattern (bright/dark contrast image) in the normal area (TSV and ubump joint normally) can be seen since the bias current passes  the circuit during OBIRCH performing. However, the high  electrical resistance failure (such as poor joints between the  TSV and the u-bump) could significantly restrict current flow, the electrical circuit pattern of the abnormal area (such as with  the poor joint area) cannot be revealed by OBIRCH. Therefore, such failure can be isolated at the transition  location between obvious and unclear bright/dark pattern, as  shown in the circle of Fig. 4a. Fig. 4b demonstrates the poor  joint between the TSV and the u-bump at the transition  location through the X-SEC SEM check.


[3] The FA is facing many challenges in the 3DIC domain, such as sample preparation is more and more complex and  difficult due to stacked dies and warp issues as well as smaller  dimensions squeeze out the resolution limitation of current  inspection tools. Furthermore, the fault isolation is more and  more difficult to achieve due to stacked dies and smaller  dimensions. Fortunately, the current FA concept can be  applied and extended as well as helps to choose suitable  approaches to perform non-destructive fault isolation for  specific cases, such as common ground structure test vehicle  specimens with high resistance or open failure conditions. More and more new FA craft or instruments shall be  developed to isolate the 3DIC fault for various applications in  the future.


In conclusion, the OBIRCH approach is a useful technique to  isolate the high electrical resistance failure of some specific  3DIC cases.
