Improving Wire Sweep Performance by Measuring                                     Degree of Cure of Epoxy Mold Compounds


Sheila Liza B. Dal


ON Semiconductor Philippines Inc., Cavite, Philippines  she.dal@onsemi.com


The choice of epoxy mold compound (EMC) for an electronic  package is based mostly on how much protection it provides  to the active components in the package. But the choice is not  a straightforward process. Rather it is mostly trial and error  using different assembly parameters to find the most robust  material while assembly defects are monitored. One such  defect associated to EMC processing is wire sweep, and many  studies have shown that it is mainly caused by viscosity  changes in the EMC. In this study, samples of EMC in  various stages of shelf life and staging times were analyzed for  degree of cure using a method called differential scanning


plunger


calorimetry (DSC).  Samples are then processed at assembly


pellets


for wire sweep measurement. It was found out that degree of


cure increases with staging time at different rates for each


heaters


shelf life. It was also found out that wire sweep did not only  increase with degree of cure but it was also found to be  predictable with respect to the latter. Using this information,


ejector pins


the age and staging limit for each material was identified that  would not cause wire sweep issues.


Epoxy Mold Compound


Epoxy mold compounds (EMC) are encapsulants used in  microelectronic devices to protect the sensitive metal  components from harsh environments (Fig. 1).  Certain  properties of the material, which result from the particular  formulation used, are carefully chosen to fit a particular  packaging need. At the same time, a set of process parameters  is strictly followed in order to make sure that no assembly  defect is induced and that the cured material has the optimum  stress and moisture resistance.


Figure 1. Diagrams of molded wirebond packages


Transfer Molding Process


One of the common electronic encapsulation techniques is  transfer molding. Pellets of EMC are introduced by pressure  into a heated mold cavity. Heat liquefies the solid pellet and  cures it to its final shape, thereby encapsulating the contents  inside the mold (Fig. 2). The hard product is then removed  from the cavity by means of ejector pins. There is no single  temperature or time or pressure setting for all transfer molding  processes; the values vary for each combination of EMC  chemistry, cavity size and electronic component dimensions


[1].


Figure 2. Encapsulation by transfer molding.


One of the assembly defects that can be directly induced  during transfer molding process is wire sweep. Literally, the  wires in a bonded unit appear to sweep or sway in the same direction as the flow of molten mold material. Such defect at  its worst can result to wire-to-wire shorting [2]. A study had  shown that there is less than 1% probability of wire  deformation by actual filler collision in a filled molten  material. It concluded that wire sweep is induced mostly by  sheer viscous drag force [3]. Another study has also shown  that for each package size and cure temperature there is a


OOOOOO


Degree of Cure and Wire Sweep


Not much is known about another factor that is not found in  the TDS but that is also just as responsible in inducing wire  sweep. It is called degree of cure and it is a measurable  property, whose value is found to vary with storage and  environmental exposure. A freshly-manufactured material has  a degree of cure almost equal to zero before assembly use and  this value increases when the material ages or is exposed to  moisture and heat. As a consequence of this increase, viscosity  of the molten material also increases and this is what directly  impacts wire movement [5]. Figure 3 is an example of  different wire sweep severity when EMCs of different  manufacturing ages were processed.


Figure 3. Wire sweep measurements in SOIC using different


EMC Pellets: (A) 2.2% in a 1-month old pellet, and              (B) 28% in an 8-month old pellet.


Degree of Cure and Differential Scanning Calorimetry


Even though degree of cure is not found in the TDS, it is an  easily measurable relative value using differential scanning  calorimetry (DSC), a thermal analysis technique that measures  heat flow in response to temperature change [6]. In a DSC  sample chamber, 2 metal pans (reference and sample) are  heated simultaneously and the heat flow difference between  the 2 pans is measured (Fig. 4).


Figure 4. Schematic of a DSC sample chamber.


Due to the presence of sample, the sample pan will either have  a positive or a negative heat flow with respect to the reference  or empty pan. When EMC cures, heat is released and a  negative or exothermic heat flow is observed. The amount of  heat released at the cure temperature range of a material


corresponds directly to the amount of material that cures. A  fresh EMC therefore will undergo maximum amount of curing  during heating, but a partially cured one will only undergo  curing for the remaining unreacted material. Hence, relative to  the fresh one, the partially cured material will have a lesser  amount of heat released. The degree of cure of the partially  cured sample is computed based on the amount of heat  released by the sample and that of the fresh (unreacted) material (Eqn. 1).


In Leg 1, a pellet of EMC4 was analyzed for degree of cure at  different staging times from 1 to 3days. In Leg 2, the same  determination was done on all 4 EMC samples to show how  the samples vary in cure behavior. Finally, in Leg 3, an EMC  batch in varying shelf lives was analyzed and assembled in  SOIC packages for wire sweep matching.


4. Older EMC shelf lives induce higher degrees of wire  sweep than fresher ones under the same staging time. The information gathered is important in optimizing the mold  cure process at assembly. Since degree of cure is predictable  for every EMC material of any shelf life and the  corresponding wire sweep induced has been established, it  becomes easy to adjust process steps including staging time. Also, the general rule on floor life is now subject to the type  and age of EMC.


The author would like to thank Rommel Panghulan, Mold  process engineer, for providing the wire sweep information.

In Leg 3, a fresh and an expired pellet of EMC4 were tested  and degree of cure values were compared against wire sweep  data. Figure 8 shows very different cure behaviors between a  fresh and an old pellet of the same chemistry with respect to  wire sweep. With 10% as the passing value for wire sweep, it  appears that a 1-month old pellet will pass xray inspection  even after 96hrs. On the other hand, an expired (8-month old) sample will not pass inspection even within the first 12hrs  after thaw. Wire sweep varied from 21.5 to 26% within 96hrs.

The careful monitoring of degree of cure with staging time  and its matching against wire sweep has produced important  information:


1. The length of staging time impacts the degree of cure  of EMC in almost a predictable manner.


2. EMCs of different shelf lives undergo different rates  of partial curing during staging. The freshest has the  lowest degree of cure at any given staging time.


3. Different EMCs undergo partial curing at different  rates during staging.


Use of Lock-In Thermography for Non-Destructive 3D Defect Localization on System in  Package and Stacked-Die Technology


Rudolf Schlangen, Shinobu Motegi, Toshi Nagatomo


DCG Systems, Fremont, CA, USA  Rudolf_Schlangen@DCGSystems.com


Christian Schmidt, Frank Altmann


Fraunhofer Institute for Mechanics of Materials Halle, Germany


Hiroaki Murakami


Toshiba, Yokohama, Japan


Stewart Hollingshead, John West


Texas Instruments, TX, USA


Abstract:


With the growing variety, complexity and market share of  3D packaged devices, package level FA is also facing new  challenges and higher demand. This paper presents Lock-In  Thermography (LIT) for fully non-destructive 3D defect  localization of electrical active defects. After a short  introduction of the basic LIT theory, two slightly different  approaches of LIT based 3D localization will be discussed  based on two case studies. The first approach relies on  package internal reference heat sources (e.g. I/O-diodes) on  different die levels. The second approach makes use of  calibrated 3D simulation software to yield the differentiation  between die levels in 8 die SD technology.


Increasing interconnect complexity, component density and  number of stacked dies in 3 dimensional system in package (3D SIP) devices together with shrinking critical dimensions  result in increasing challenges for isolation and root cause  analysis of defects. In particular, new fault isolation  methods with 3D defect localization capability are required. Standard methods to localize electrical defects like OBIRCH


[1], emission microscopy or liquid crystal thermography [2] can not be applied, because optical access to the functional  structures in 3D SIP devices is mostly not available.


Lock-In-Thermography (LIT) is an established technique for  on-die defect localization, but based on its superior  sensitivity it also allows for completely non-destructive  localization of defects through covering package material. The first theoretical evaluation and proof of concept results  for using LIT in general and the ELITE specific phase data  in particular for non-destructive 3D localization of buried  defects were already shown in [3] & [4]. The applicability of  LIT for 3D defect localization on stacked die samples was  first shown and discussed in  [5].


This paper focuses on the practical implementation and  optimization of the 3D defect localization based on two case  studies, exemplary for the two most common product  types/applications for today’s 3D packaging; first, the  combination of one logic- and one memory die first and  second a multi-chip memory stack for SD technology.


Basic LIT theory


Key to successful localization of small heat sources, such as  defect related hot-spots, is the real-time graphical lock-in  methodology. Figure 1 shows the standard lock-in principal, used to detect a small signal buried in random noise of  higher magnitude.


Figure 1: Illustration of basic thermal lock-in principle


The Elite delivers the same results – amplitude and phase. However, there are two main differences:


a) The input is not a continuous- / analogue-signal, but a stream of IR-images, hence discrete data  points


b) The discrete lock-in algorithm has to be applied to  every image pixel separately


The SW performs all lock-in calculations (for 640 x 512  pixels) at real time, allowing to see immediate results and  offering to accumulate data as long as necessary; there is no  acquisition time limit. Acquisitions on hot-spots with  moderate power dissipation (> 1mW) only require seconds  up to a few minutes, but also extremely small heat sources  can be localized by simply spending more time; the record  so far is the clear detection of a 400nW source after


mouldA


18hours.

Figure 2 shows a scenario with two hot-spots, buried at  different depths in a package. Applying the lock-in  excitation voltage causes periodic heat generation at these  two spots. The heat starts diffusing in all directions, also  towards the package surface. Based on the individual depth  of the heat source, it takes the heat different times to reach  the surface, resulting in different phase shifts.


Since the ELITE directly measures the phase,  can be used  to calculate the depth of the defect.


Calculation of the defect depth requires either precise  knowledge of the thermal properties of the involved material (such information is usually not readily available) or a  calibration via reference measurements, which will be  subject of the first case study.


LIT 3D defect localization, based on reference  measurements


The first DUT is a fully encapsulated SIP with two dies  stacked on each other. The lower die is mounted face-down  in flip-chip technology. The upper die is glued onto the  lower one and connected to the package lead-frame via  bond-wires (see cross-section in Figure 3).

In a second step, we want to use the ELITE phase result to  determine the defect depth. The upper most row of images  in Figure 5 shows multiple such phase results of the failing  pin, measured at different lock-in frequencies. The extracted  phase values are plotted in Figure 6 (labelled fail). Since the  thermal properties of most involved materials (e.g. mould  compound and die attach tape) are not known precisely  enough, the use of a readily available 3D simulation SW is  not practical with such samples. The much faster approach  is to compare the thermal signature of the fail to reference  heat sources on known levels in the package. In many cases, IO diodes can serve as such reference heat sources by  simply biasing them in forward direction.

Figure 6 contains an additional set of reference  measurements (dashed lines), measured on a good / reference sample, showing very close match to the reference  results of the failing DUT, proving the consistency of the  phase data. This consistency allows using once measured  reference data for any sample of the same technology  simplifying and increasing the through-put of the proposed  technique. Furthermore, such reference data may be used to  extract the thermal properties of the involved materials  allowing the use of dedicated 3D SW, which will be  discussed in more detail with the second case study.

In contrast to the DUT of the first case study, most  commercially available stacked memory devices consist of 8  stacked memory dies and most recently even up to 16, with  ever shrinking die thicknesses of 30um an below.ever shrinking die thicknesses of 30m an below.


Initial experimental results and simulations regarding such  die stacks (published in  [5]) could show that the glue layers  in-between the stacked dies are poor thermal conductors and  therefore induce a clear difference in thermal time delay (measured as phase) going from one Si-die to the next.


Consequently, the same measurement principle as with the  first case study (compression to a local reference, on the

Fortunately, one fundamental requirement for such


60


aggressive 3D packaging is a very tight process control, ensuring minimum variation of layer dimensions and


therefore also providing very reproducible heat flow  properties within different samples of the same technology.


This allows yielding reliable identification of the defective  layer by comparing to the reference or calibration data,


previously measured on known defective devices.


Figure 7: Cross-sectional view of DUT-8+1 stack die uSDFigure 7: Cross-sectional view of DUT – 8+1 stack die SD  memory device


The results presented in this paper were mostly gathered on  8+1 stack die micro SD technology from Toshiba (a crosssectional sketch shown in Figure 7).


Multiple reference samples with known defective layers  were measured using similar measurement parameters (first  at a constant lock-in frequency of 5Hz) and the phase results  are plotted in Figure 8 as a function of the calculated defect  depth. (Note that the shown defect depth calculation is based  on a simple linear approximation)


Most results show a clear layer specific phase value which  are used to serve as reference when measuring unknown  devices. Using the reference samples based on the simple  linear model of phase vs. depth calculation, the exact layer  would have been localized correctly for 76% of the  measured samples whereby 100% of all samples would have  been correct within +/- 1 die of the defective one.


These results prove the basic capability of phase analysis for  layer detection, but there are already several ways to  improve the accuracy, usability and throughput of this  technique which will be discussed in the following.

Use of 3D SW


Besides the high level of process control, the limited number  of utilized materials in memory stacked-die packages makes  the use of dedicated phase simulation SW advantageous. With the shown example (8+1 die u-SD), the full stack  consists of the covering mould compound and a strictly  repetitive stack of dies and die-attach-tape (glue layer). Even-though the individual layer thicknesses may vary, there are only three different materials which need to be  characterized to correctly simulate the thermal behaviour of  the whole stack; mould compound, Si-die and die-attachtape.


The SW is based on an analytical solution of the 1-dimensional heat spread function. The procedure begins  with generating a layer model of the used sample including  all layers, their individual thicknesses and materials. The  simulation SW calculates and plots the expected phase  values vs. lock-in frequency for all relevant layers.


In case of dealing with unknown (not thermally  characterized) materials, the user starts with a set of default  parameters and can fine-tune / calibrate the model by  adjusting the used material parameters to match reference  measurements.


Figure 9 shows the results; the points are reference  measurement results and the solid lines are the simulated  phase shift based on a calibrated simulation model, both  plotted versus the lock-in frequency, showing excellent  agreement.


Due to the small number of materials to be accounted for  and the repetitive nature of the layer structure, 3-4 reference  devices with known heat-source in different layers are  considered sufficient for such model alignment on a given 8-stacked-die technology.


For the same samples shown in Figure 8, new data was  acquired now measuring at 2Hz and 5Hz and comparing to  the simulation model. The changed procedure allowed  correctly identifying 86% of all samples, and still 100% with +/- 1 die.

Optimized Measurement Procedure


The lock-in frequency is the most important measurement  parameter. If chosen too high, the resulting temperature  change visible on the surface decreases to a level where  sufficient SNR cannot be achieved in a reasonable time. This is especially critical for a defect in the lower layers, where there is more material for the heat to penetrate before  affecting the surface temperature. The other way around, if  the defect is located on one of the upper layers (close to the  surface), it’s advantageous to measure with increased lockin frequencies to achieve higher phase values, allowing  better separation between neighbouring layers.


Additionally, measuring at multiple frequencies improves  the accuracy and confidence level of the 3D localization.


To accommodate for the above, the proposed procedure  starts with one acquisition at a moderate frequency (at best  one where all layers deliver acceptable results, e.g. 5Hz as  indicated in Figure 9) and depending on this first phase  result the next measurements will be performed at either  higher or lower frequencies. In the shown example, for  phase below 150° at 5Hz, the next measurements would be  done at 7 and 10Hz, whereas for the lowest layer (die 1), with more than 200° at 5Hz, the next acquisition should be  at a lower frequency (e.g. 3Hz).


Depending on the desired level of confidence vs. the  required acquisition time, the procedure may end after 1, 2  or even more matching data-points between measured- and  simulated phase values.


One additional benefit of using multiple phase vs. frequency  data-points is that unforeseen influences like delamination  between layers or the presence of multiple defects in the  same x, y area but on different layers are likely to influence  the resulting phase values to not follow the expected square  root trend of the simulation data. This would allow  excluding those DUTs and not getting false localization.


Increased Throughput


The necessary acquisition time to achieve a certain SNR is  linearly dependent to the camera frame rate (number of  images captures per second). In standard operation, the  utilized 640x512 pixel focal plane array has 100Hz framerate.


Initially, the LIT acquisition should cover the full DUT to  localize the defect in x and y and also give the first (rough) z-localization to determine which frequencies to be


measured next. This often requires the use of the full pixelarray and a de-magnifying (wide-angle) lens.


After this point, the 3D localization only requires the phase  information on the centre of the hot-spot at different lock-in  frequencies, so continuing to measure with the full pixel  array is unnecessary.


To ensure stable and reproducible camera / system operation  we propose to use a pre-defined sub-array of 128x128 pixels  for the 3D (only) acquisition, allowing the camera to operate  at almost 9x higher frame rate, reducing the necessary  acquisition time by almost 9x.


Electrical Setup


Since the resistivity of shorts to be detected can vary by


orders of magnitude, it’s important to set the right voltage


levels for defect excitation; high enough to get sufficient SIP and high density memory products like uSD has provensignal (fast SNR) but still below power levels where the  temperature dependence of the thermal diffusion properties


of materials would have to be taken into consideration.


For example, with the used 8+1 SD devices excitation


power between 1 to 5mW is expected to be best and the


lock-in voltage should be selected accordingly.


Additionally, any kind of SIP is likely to show non-linear


IV-characteristics which may include current spikes due to


device initialization as shown in Figure 10 between 1 and


2.5V. Since such initialization procedures may cause nondefect related thermal activity it is beneficial to avoid this


voltage range, initialize the DUT before the LIT acquisition


and switch between two voltages above the initialization


limit (e.g. form VB low to VB high) during the LIT


measurement.


12.5 34.5 [V]


Figure 10: IV plot of DUT


Figure 11: ATE for LIT excitation


Especially with SIP, an increasing number of products  requires such initialization and is equipped with on-chip / inpackage voltage regulators, which often make simple  switching or toggling of Vdd inapplicable since the local  power dissipation of the defect does not change with the  outer modulation, therefore can’t be detected based on the  LIT principle anymore.


For such cases, a tester (ATE) may be used for controlled  defect activation whereby the LIT setup synchronizes to the  ATE operation (see Figure 11). A more detailed explanation  and multiple application examples were published in  [7].


Conclusion:


The 3D integration of multiple dies into one package as for  SIP and high density memory products like SD has proven  to be a clear trend and a fast growing market. The resulting  complexity of such 3D architectures and the shrinking  geometries of today’s packages with emerging SiFor example, with the used 8+1 uSD devices excitation


interposers, TSVs and micro-bump technology offer great  performance increase and cost reduction for various  applications, but also require new techniques and  methodologies for effective debug and FA.


Lock-In-Thermography is one of the most promising  techniques to deliver non-destructive 3D defect localization  through the still covering packaging material.


Based on the first case study it could be shown how 3D LIT  can distinguish between different die- and packaging layers  in a two-die SIP, using local reference points (e.g. IOdiodes) on each die for direct comparison.


This technique is fast and reliable, given access to the  individual die levels.

It was already demonstrated, that the method of Lock-in  Thermography (LIT) enables 3D localization of thermal active  defects, e.g. electrical shorts and resistive opens, on die level

and within fully packaged single and multichip devices [1,2]. The depth of a defect can be derived from phase shift  measurements of the defective compared to a reference device

For a general approach of this method, thermal modeling is

Die 1


used and verified by experimental data to investigate the  internal heat propagation under periodic stimulation in  correlation to the LIT measuring process. [3]. A basic  requirement for the successful application of the method is a  precise and reproducible measurement of both the thermal  material properties of each material layer and the phase shift  between the internal heat excitation and thermal response  measured by LIT. Significant influences from the material and  measurement setup to the detected phase shift have to be  identified and taken into account. However, to identify and  distinguish the relevant influences measurements with defined  internal heat sources are necessary which are presented in this  paper. First, the relationship between geometrical thickness of  a material layer and the resulting thermal parameters for both  homogeneous and heterogeneous materials are measured and  discussed. A new measurement setup generating a defined  point heat source will be presented to calibrate the LIT system  for quantitative phase shift measurements and to determine the  phase shift to thickness parameters of single material layers. In addition the variation of the phase shift caused by the  defect geometry and the defect environment will be  investigated. Finally, a case study is presented comparing the  experimental results to the obtained results from a real stacked  die device.


Failure analysis of modern system in package devices facing


more and more the limitations of standard localization


techniques for electrical defects like emission microscopy,


liquid crystal or OBIRCH [4,5]. The increasing 3D


complexity of modern devices leads to the circumstance that


defect localization must start on a fully packaged device in diffusion length (u) and defines the damping of the thermal order to locate the defect either at one of the ICs or on the


interconnect level and to identify the affected die. However,


different material layers, like mould compound (MC) or die


attach tape (DAT), are opaque to visible and IR light and


consequently block a direct optical access to the internal  structure, Fig 1.


Figure 1: Optical access at single chip devices and  limitations at System in Packages


For the defect localization of electrical defects the method of  Lock-in Thermography (LIT) can be used for none-destructive  defect localization at IC and package level. Due to the fact  that heat propagation from the origin defect position to the  device surface through several material layers is a time related  process, the measurable phase shift between electrical  stimulation and thermal response at the device surface can be  measured and used for defect depth determination.


Basic Theory of 3D Defect Localization Using  Lock-in Thermography (LIT)


Principle of spectral phase analysis for 3D defect  localization


LIT, which operational principle is described in detail in [6], can be applied for 3D defect depth localization by using the  effect that heat propagation is a time-depending process. After  stimulating a heat source which is covered by one or multiple  material layers, heat can propagate to the several layers and is  measured as a locally temperature signal on the device  surface. Heat propagation is a time-depending process and the  time delay of the underlying thermal diffusion process  determines the phase shift between excitation signal and  thermal response. The thermal diffusion process is not only a  function of time but also depends on the frequency of the  stimulation signal and the thermal parameters of the covering  material layer. The parameter which describes the influence of  the thermal properties of a material layer is so called thermal  diffusion length () and defines the damping of the thermal  wave inside of a bulk material, Fig. 2.

DB FIB X-section on Si backside without any mechanical / chemical thinning requires removing massive amounts of Si  material. By using a Gallium Liquid Metal Ion Source (GaLMIS), it provides a typical beam current range of 20-60 nA. This corresponds to a maximum material removal rate by  sputtering of ~ 103um3/min for silicon at 60nA [7]. Trenchingsputtering of ~ 103m3/min for silicon at 60nA [7]. Trenching  a 135um x 240um x 120um Si cavity will take about 128hrs  for 21nA beam current and 64 hr for 60nA. It is very time  consuming. Therefore, by using the combination of Si  backside local milling and the chemical etching process to


completely remove excess Si, it can reduce DB FIB X-section  time significantly. DB FIB X-section from the Si backside to  detect Polyimide (PI) cracking at the C4 bump was successful  and it only took about 11hrs by using 21nA beam current in  this particular case study.


Fig.6 SEM image of C4 bump PI crack after DB FIB Xsection


Locally milling


2. DB FIB X-section from organic substrate side


CSAM on the open failing sample showed die corner  delamination (see Fig.7), which suggested that the  delamination was inside the die. DB FIB was employed to  find the root cause. In order to avoid the mechanical stress  induced on the die, FIB X-section was performed from  organic substrate side. After parallel polishing to remove  substrate material until the C4 bumps were exposed, DB FIB  with 21nA current at 30kV was performed on the AOI.  Fig.8  shows the SEM images after the organic substrate was  removed and the C4 bump was exposed. The trench opening  was 160um x 160um x 80um and the same multi-pass  patterning with automated milling was selected. After 17 to  19hrs of milling, the trench was completed.  A SEM image  after the trench was completed is shown in Fig. 8.  The final  DB FIB X-section after cleaning is shown in Fig. 9.  It clearly  shows the ILD delamination as the root cause of the failure.


Fig.7 CSAM on die corner Delamination


Fig.8  SEM images (a) after removing organic package & C4  bump exposed; (b) DB FIB X-section trench


Fig.9 SEM image of die ILD delamination


By increasing the beam current, the X-section time will be  reduced significantly. High beam current (65nA) from the  upgraded system was used on the failed sample. The trench  opening and DB FIB X-section parameter setting was the  same as under 21nA beam current. Compared to 21nA, the  milling time for 65nA only took about 7hrs. It was about 2x  faster and the cross section time was significantly reduced.


Compared to DB FIB X-section from Si backside, X-section  from organic substrate had reduced the sample preparation


steps. It is very useful for die level delamination analysis as it   can avoid any external stress applied on the die.


Fig10: TDR curve on JTAG voltage spec failure


Fig.11 Optical image after parallel delayering above AOI


The exact location of failure can be calculated using material  constants and layout information. It suggested that the failure  location was in the I/O Cu interconnector layer of the  substrate. Based on the isolation, top-down polishing of the  substrate was performed and stopped just above the AOI  layer. Fig.11 shows the optical image after parallel de-layering  above the AOI. To avoid artifacts induced by mechanical  polishing on the Cu interconnector, DB FIB was used to  perform a X-section on the AOI buried by the organic  insulator layer. The substrate trench opening was 450um x  200um x 60um and the beam current used was 21nA. The Xsection took about 4hrs. Fig.12 shows that the failing signal  had a very thin copper trace as compared to the neighboring  good traces. No mechanical smearing was observed on the Cu  trace.  The


thinner area of the failing trace leads to higher resistive drop  thus causing the JTAG voltage spec failure. Corrective action  was taken based on this root cause analysis.


The work presented here showed the successful application of  DB FIB X-section in detecting failure mechanisms related to  die delamination, C4 bump PI crack, as well Cu trace defects  inside the organic substrate with no mechanical smearing or  artifacts.  Based on the failure signatures, two sample  preparation methods for DB FIB X-section were developed. For C4 bump related delamination failures, it is recommended  to perform the DB FIB X-section from Si die backside. By  combining local mechanical milling and chemical etching to  completely remove Si on SOI flip chip packages, it can reduce  DB FIB X-section time significantly.  For Si die delamination  related failures, it is recommended to perform the DB FIB Xsection from the bottom of the organic substrate side to avoid  external stress induced on the die during sample preparation. By increasing the beam current from 21nA to 65nA, the Xsection milling rate increased up to 2 times. DB FIB X-section  also can be applied to organic substrate defect analysis. This  work presented the Cu trace defect inside the organic substrate  determined by DB FIB X-section.

Logic simulation of defects is based on the observation  that, at the logic level, a static defect when excited will  manifest itself as a toggle of some logic values from 1 to 0 or vice-versa. This implies that any defect can be  simulated using a stuck-at fault simulator by defining the  defect excitation conditions, and the locations in the logic  model where stuck-at faults must be injected to simulate  the defect behavior. For example, a bridge defect between  two nets is excited when the nets are driven to opposite  logic values and under this condition the logic values at  either one or both of the logic gates driving the two nets  may be toggled. Hence, the bridge behavior can be  simulated by injecting stuck-at faults at the outputs of  these driving gates. This will produce a list of scan cells  expected to fail in the presence of the bridge for a  particular scan pattern, which is then compared with the  failures observed on ATE to determine how well the defect  explains them. In the end, the defect sites that best explain  the failing behavior are reported as diagnosis suspects.


Diagnosis suspects produced with logic diagnosis can  include bridges (specified by a net pair), opens (specified  by a net), and library cells. While logic diagnosis is very  effective in narrowing the actual defect to just a few  suspects, this localization is still at the level of an entity in  the logic model (i.e., net or library cell). This can be  enhanced by considering the design physical layout  information as discussed in the next sub-section.


2.2   Layout-aware Diagnosis


Logic diagnosis produces suspect defect locations in terms  of nets (opens) or net pairs (bridges). However, even a  single net can represent a considerable amount of physical  area that must be searched to identify the actual defect. Several previous studies have presented methods that use  design layout information during logic diagnosis to further  localize the defect within a single net or net pair. Here, we  provide a brief summary of how layout information can be  exploited to enhance open and bridge diagnosis.


For open defects, knowing the topology of a suspect net  allows identification of one or more physical segments  within the net that can contain the open defect. Consider a  net with one driver and several sink gates. Net topology  specifies which physical section of the net (which we refer  to as a net segment) drives which sink gates. This is  illustrated in Figure 1, which shows the physical layout of  an example net driven by a gate called instD with four sink  gates (instU, instV, inistW, and instX). The driver gate  along with the sink gates is the only information known in  the logic model. However, the layout allows us to  determine net topology, which in this example includes  seven net segments (numbered in Figure 1). Each net


segment drives a unique set of sink gates (e.g., segment 1  drives all the four sink gates, segment 3 drives instU and  instV, etc.).


This additional information from the design layout allows  more accurate modeling of open defects. As an example  from the net topology depicted in Figure 1, we know that  only branches A and B can never be affected by a single  open point on the net without branches C and I being  affected also. Hence, we can simulate the valid  combination of branches during diagnosis and determine  which combination best explains the observed failures. This result directly translates into a better localization of  the open point on the net. For example, if for some failing  die the branch combination B, C best explains the  observed failures, we can conclude the open point must be on net segment 3. This way, the area over which PFA must  look for a defect decreases from the entire net to just a few  segments of the net.


Layout information also enables diagnosis to distinguish  between a dominant bridge and an open defect. A  dominant bridge is a short between two nets such that one  net completely dominates the other net; in other words, when the bridge is excited, the same net always gets a  toggled value regardless of the test pattern. This can  happen if the one of the nets involved in the short is driven  by a much larger gate than the other. Since the dominant  net never assumes a toggled value, logic diagnosis can  only identify the victim net through the process of  matching simulation results with observed failures.


At that point, it becomes impossible to further distinguish  whether the identified net contains an open or is a victim


Figure 1: Example net showing the concept of net  topology.


in a dominant bridge. However, with layout information  available, the nets that are physically close to a potential  victim net can be determined; together, they form a list of  potential dominant bridge sites. This list is further  shortened by checking that the bridge is excited in all the  failing patterns (i.e., those net pairs that are driven to the  same logic value for some failing pattern cannot be the site  of a dominant bridge). As an example, consider the  scenario shown in Figure 2 which shows the physical  layout of a net called net1 and sections of three nets (net2, net3, and net4) that are in its physical neighborhood.


Consider the bridge defect shown in Figure 2 and assume


Blue= Logic Diagnosis


Metal3

it behaves as a dominant bridge, with net1 being the victim  net and net4 being the dominant net. In such a case, logic  diagnosis can only identify net1 as potentially having a  defect, because it is the only source of failures.  However, logic diagnosis cannot tell whether the failures are due to  an open on net1 or a dominant bridge with one of its  neighbors. Layout-aware diagnosis allows determination  of the nets physically close to net1 (i.e. net2, net3, and  net4) to be determined, enabling three net pairs to simulate  as dominant bridges. With a sufficient number of failing  patterns, diagnosis should be able to eliminate <net1, net2> and <net1, net3> as likely suspects.


2.3   Minimum Defect-enclosing Circle


In the first section we discussed how it is undesirable to  perform PFA on die whose diagnosis suspects are spread  over a large area. In the previous sub-section we saw how  layout-aware diagnosis achieves better defect localization,which leads to an increased number of failing die that are  candidates for PFA. However, before we can show the  veracity of such a claim, we need to define a quantifiable  measure of this “area over which diagnosis suspects are  spread” for a diagnosis report. Such a measure is the  diameter of the minimum defect-enclosing circle. This  sub-section describes the calculation of the minimum  defect enclosing circle.

3.  Industrial Case Study Results


In previous sections, we have seen how layout-aware  diagnosis can potentially reduce the minimum defectenclosing circle diameter and enable PFA for more die. In  this section, we use an industrial case study to show how  well this claim holds in real-world situations. The scenario  we used involved a set of failing die from two wafers in  volume manufacturing of a graphics chip. The goal was to  isolate defects through the process of diagnosis followed  by PFA in as many die as possible.


Since this was an ideal situation to test the claimed  benefits of layout-aware diagnosis, we performed a  comparison study in which we ran both logic and layoutaware diagnosis on all the failing dies and determined the  minimum defect-enclosing circle for each. Die were  classified based on whether the diameter of the minimum


demonstrates the minimum defect-enclosing circle is much  smaller for layout aware diagnosis than logic diagnosis,


especially for Die-2. In fact, Die-2 would not have been


chosen for PFA based on logic diagnosis alone because the  suspect net is too long.


The final example (Figure 6) is a die with a bridge defect  that behaves as a dominant bridge. The blue net shown in  Figure 6 is the victim net.


In this case, logic diagnosis was able only to identify the  blue net as a net with either an open defect or as a victim  net in a dominant bridge. Layout-aware diagnosis was able  to determine the defect was a dominant bridge with the

for two die with open defects.

neighboring net (shown in red) being the dominant net. This resulted in a significant reduction in the minimum  defect-enclosing circle and led to a successful PFA.


4.  Conclusions


This paper highlights the effectiveness of layout-aware  diagnosis in enabling efficient physical failure analysis. This is a direct result of layout-aware diagnosis being able  to localize defects to a much smaller minimum defectenclosing circle. We present results from an industrial case  study in which data gathered on a graphics chip shows the  number of die on which PFA is possible increases by more  than 60%. Layout-aware diagnosis is particularly effective  in cases in which a defect is on a very long net. We give  three specific examples in which layout-aware diagnosis  efficiently led to successful PFA.


Layout-aware diagnosis carries a cost in terms of increased  diagnosis run time. In the future, we look to reduce this  computational overhead. We are also looking into further  improving open diagnosis by accounting for the effect of  capacitive coupling from physically close nets.

Layout-aware Diagnosis Leads to Efficient and Effective Physical Failure Analysis


Manish Sharma, Sergej Schwarz, Juergen Schmerberg, Kathy Yang, and Ting-Pu Tai


Mentor Graphics Corporation, 8005 SW Boeckman Rd, Wilsonville OR 97070, USA


Yuan-Shih Chen, Cheng-Yiing Chuang, and Feng-Ming Kuo


Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company, 8, Li-Hsin Rd. 6, Hsinchu Science Park, Hsinchu, Taiwan 300-77, ROC


Mike Brennan, James Yeh, and Alan Ma


AMD, One AMD Place, Sunnyvale CA 94088, USA


Logic diagnosis analyzes scan test failures and produces a list of potential defect locations and types. This  information is often used as a starting point for a detailed  physical failure analysis (PFA) process that locates the  actual physical defect. One important criterion that dictates  whether PFA can be performed on a certain die is the  physical area of the die over which the potential defect  locations reported by diagnosis are spread. While logic  diagnosis works with a logic-level abstraction of the  design, in this paper we describe the use of additional  design layout information during diagnosis to lead to better  localization of defects and reduce the area over which  potential defect locations are spread. This directly results  in more die becoming suitable for PFA. We demonstrate  the effectiveness of such “layout-aware” diagnosis for PFA  using an industrial case study in which several die from  two wafers were diagnosed and 61% and 78% more die  became suitable for PFA using layout-aware diagnosis.


1.  Introduction


In a very common situation, a number of defective die that  are all failing scan test (also referred to as failing die) are  available, and the physical defect needs to be isolated in as  many die as possible. This situation can arise at various  stages of a product life cycle. For example, during early  yield ramp the set of defective die may come from several  wafers and the goal may be to determine the yield limiters. Similarly during volume production there may be a yield  excursion wherein the root cause will need to be  determined from the failing die on the excursion wafer. Finally the failing die may be customer returns that must  be analyzed to understand test escapes or reliability issues. Whatever the context from which the group of failing die  is derived, a typical analysis methodology is to first  perform logic diagnosis on all the dies in the group. Logic  diagnosis is a process that uses a logic-level model of the  design and performs detailed simulation-based analysis of  the test failures [1-3] [5-7]. It produces a diagnosis report  consisting of a small list of potential defect locations and  defect types (bridge, open, etc.) that best describes the  observed test failures. In this paper we refer to these as  diagnosis suspects.


Based on the diagnosis reports, some failing die are  selected for PFA. For a failing die to be suitable for PFA  based on diagnosis results alone, some conditions must be  met. The most important criterion is that the physical  layout area over which the diagnosis suspects are spread  must be less than a particular limit. This limit is dictated  by the PFA process in which top metal layers in the die are  removed mechanically so that features (e.g. vias, metal  lines, etc.) on lower metal layers can be visually examined  for defects. This de-layering step has to maintain strict  planarity to prevent destruction of the features in the layer  below the one being removed. Such planarity can be  achieved only over a small area on the die, and this  directly translates into a limit on the layout area over  which the diagnosis suspects are spread. Furthermore, even for a die on which this area is less than the limit, the  cost of performing PFA on that die is directly proportional  to it because more physical elements in the die need to be  examined.


From the above it is clear that reducing the physical layout  area over which diagnosis suspects are spread will be very  beneficial because more die can potentially become PFAworthy, and the PFA process itself will be more efficient. One way to achieve this is to use design layout  information during diagnosis (what we refer to as layoutaware diagnosis) to better localize defects [4, 8, 10, 11,  13, 14, 15]. In this paper we demonstrate the efficacy of  this technique by presenting data gathered on a graphics  chip design in which layout-aware diagnosis results in  many more die becoming amenable to PFA compared to  logic diagnosis.


In the rest of this paper, Section 2 provides background  information on logic and layout-aware diagnoses. It also  gives a formal quantitative definition of the concept of  layout area over which diagnosis suspects are spread. Section 3 details the real-world case study used in this  paper and its results are in presented in Section 4. Section  5 concludes the paper.


2.  Background


2.1   Logic Diagnosis


Logic diagnosis is the process that analyzes scan failures  observed on the ATE while testing a bad die to produce  some diagnosis suspects. Traditionally, logic diagnosis has


been performed based on a logic-level model of the  design. At a high level, this is done by simulating the  logical behavior of a defect site to produce a list of  expected failing scan cells, and then matching them  against the observed failures on the ATE for all the failing  scan patterns.


Certain net pairs cannot have a short between them  because they are too far apart from each other in the layout  or other physical structures between them preclude the  possibility of an exclusive bridge defect between the two. This knowledge can be used to eliminate many bridge  suspects identified by logic diagnosis based on logic defect  simulation alone.


First we identify all the regions in the layout that the PFA  process needs to examine to find the defect based on the  diagnosis results for a failing die. These regions are  referred to as defect-bounding boxes. Then a circle of  minimum diameter is determined that encloses all defectbounding boxes (i.e., all the corners of bounding boxes are  either inside or on the circle). This is the minimum defect  enclosing circle. The diameter of this circle is a direct  indicator of the possibility of performing PFA on the  corresponding failing die; hence, it becomes our measure  of the worthiness of a diagnosis report from the PFA point  of view.

Determination of Thermal Material Properties  Using Light Flash Analysis (LFA)


Physical Background of Thermal Parameter  Determination


Because thermal material parameters often unknown for  specific materials used in a microelectronic device or the  parameters of a thermally thin film can differ from bulk  behavior, they have to be determined experimentally. One  standard method for the determination of thermal parameters  is the light flash analysis (LFA). This method bases on the  principle stimulating the investigated sample with a defined  short light pulse from the backside and measuring the  resulting thermal response over time on the front side. Figure  4 shows a measured signal curve of a LFA measurement.

The needle is  contacted to VDD and the silicon layer to GND resulting in a  local high resistive contact at the needle tip. As a next step, the system response can be determined by  measuring the phase shift at different lock-in frequencies and  detector frames. Figure 9 shows the determined phase shifts  and the linear fit function for different detector frames  depending on the lock-in frequency.

Using the determined slopes as a correction function, a  quantitative phase shift analysis is enabled. One disadvantage  of needle probing at front side is that heat propagation of  single material layers can be not be investigated, directly. Therefore, the setup is modified resulting in backside probing  which can be realized in two different ways.  In one setup the  sample under investigation has to be coated on the backside  with a thin metal layer and is placed on a metal-based support  layer for electrical connection to GND. The needle probes the  sample directly creating a point-like heat source in the  metallization layer of the sample. This setup can be used to  investigate single material layers or material stacks by external  stimulation. As an alternative, the investigated sample can be  stimulated indirectly. In this case, the support layer is replaced  by a metal-coated silicon layer (thickness ~280μm). Figure 10  shows a sketch of the two different setups.

The advantage of using indirect stimulation is the  circumstance that un- or low-doped silicon is transparent to  IR-light. Therefore, using the indirect stimulation without a  sample, the LIT setup can be calibrated easily. Furthermore, it  can be used specifically to investigate the influence of contact  resistances between two layers to the phase signal.

Within this paper, the method of 3D defect localization at


fully packaged device using Lock-in Thermography was


350 μm


demonstrated. Influences resulting from internal interfaces,


615 μm


used lock-in frequencies and thermal parameters to the phase


shift behavior were investigated. A specific test setup was  presented which can be applied to calibrate the LIT system for  quantitative phase shift measurements, to investigate the


behavior of single material layer and their phase shift to  thickness correlation as well as the separation of interface  influences to the resulting phase shift. In addition, thermal  material parameters and their dependence to thickness have  been characterized using light flash analysis (LFA) in  combination with a correction model for heterogeneous  material layers. For mould compound, the results show a  general match to the applied one dimensional analytical  solution for the thermal wave propagation, however reveal  local increase for certain thicknesses. Finally, a case study was  presented using a stacked die device with internal heat  sources. The resulting phase shift values are compared to  reference measurements using the new calibration setup as  well as to the calculated behavior. All three approaches to  determine the phase shift to frequency behavior are consistent. It was shown that accurate measurements of the thermal  parameters can be used to easily calculate the phase shift to  frequency behavior saving time for calibration measurements


by LIT. In the future, further investigations are focusing on  the microscopic relationship between filler size and –concentration to the resulting thermal parameters. Additional  measurements on stacked die devices with a higher number of  dies as well as the influence investigation of the lateral defect  position are scheduled.

Failure Analysis of Flip Chip C4 Package   Using Focused Ion Beam Milling Technique


Lihong Cao, Loc Tran, Wallace Donna


Advanced Micro Devices, Inc


Austin, Texas, USA


Lihong.cao@amd.com, Tel: 512-602-4272


This article describes how Focused Ion Beam (FIB) milling  methodology enhances the capability of package-level failure  analysis on flip-chip packages by eliminating the artifacts  induced by using conventional mechanical techniques. DualBeam Focused Ion Beam (DB FIB) cross sections were  successful in detecting failure mechanisms related either to the  die/C4 bump or package defect inside the organic substrate. This paper outlines detailed sample preparation techniques  prior to performing the DB FIB cross-sections, along with  case studies of DB FIB cross-sections.


IC packages are becoming increasingly complex due to the  increasing size, I/O numbers, and application of lead-free  solder bumps [1, 2]. Package level reliability and failure  analysis has become an area of more concern and challenge. Effectively detecting failure mechanisms has become very  critical. Conventional mechanical cross-section technique is  the most common methodology to determine failure modes in  the semiconductor industry. However, the typical problem of  mechanical cross-sections is the smearing of the soft material  into the cracks and delamination, as well as easily inducing  artifacts. Thus, it is difficult to determine failure mechanisms.  Focused ion beam milling technology is widely used in the  semiconductor industry for the purposes of circuit edit, fault  isolation, Si- level cross-sectioning, Transmission Electron  Microscope (TEM) analysis preparation, and material ablation [3, 4, 5]. By precisely monitoring the beam etch patterning  and the area of exposure, very detailed characteristics of the  failing structure, such as manufacturing defects, can be  detected without inducing any artifacts. Instead of using FIB  for Si-level analysis, this paper presents the DB FIB technique  to perform package level cross-sections on flip chip packages  to determine the failure mechanisms. The detailed process  with high precision and artifact free access to the defective  buried interconnection and microstructure is also discussed in  this paper.


1. Fault isolation technique for package failure analysis  The electrical characterization by using a parametric analyzer  is the first step of the fault isolation. Time Domain  Reflectometry (TDR) has been successfully used to isolate  Open/Short package-level failures by generating a step signal  which is transmitted through the device under test (DUT) [6]. When a discontinuity in the DUT is encountered, a part of the  transmitted signal is reflected.  In this paper, a comparative  TDR method is used to quickly determine whether an  Open/Short failure is within the package or within the silicon. The impedance profile of a failing signal is compared to the  impedance profile of a “golden” unit and a bare package (without die attachment).  Another non-destructive technique  used to detect package level failures is C-Mode Scanning  Acoustic Microscopy (CSAM). CSAM is widely used in flipchip devices to evaluate the chip-to-bump interface, the solder  joint through the entire thickness of the solder ball, and the  solder-to-substrate interface.  In this work, CSAM with a  230MHz high frequency transducer is selected to perform  scanning on the samples to identify the failure.


2. Dual Beam FIB-SEM milling and imaging system


Focused ion beam milling can provide detailed and specific  information about structures without inducing artifacts by  precisely monitoring the beam etch patterning and the area of  exposure. FIB systems generally use a Gallium Liquid Metal  Ion Source (Ga-LMIS) as the source of the ions, providing a  typical beam current range of 1 pA to 20-60 nA [7]. In this  study two systems are used; one is a FEI Strada400 with a  maximum beam current of 21nA and another is the upgraded  Strada400 with a maximum beam current of 65nA. The effect  of beam current was studied.


3. Sample preparation


The samples in this study are flip chip Organic Pin Grid Array (OPGA) packages and the Si device is Silicon On Insulator (SOI). The Si die with a thickness of 750um is attached to an  organic substrate with a thickness of 1.4mm through lead free  solder bumps. In order to find the root cause of package  failure, an extensive sample preparation is needed to expose


the region of interest buried within the substrate, solder joints,


tocaly milling Sthacks de


or Si die. Using mechanical processes to section and polish  the sample can introduce stresses and smearing of materials.  In this work, DB FIB is employed to perform cross sections  for package related failures. It is well known that the ablation


Lead free 04 bumps


rate of a FIB on silicon is typically as low as 2.7um3/s for a


and underfill


beam current of 10nA[8].The time required for the FIB  preparation is the limiting factor and it will take a very long  time to perform a cross section through a Si die with the full


Organic package


thickness of 750um. Two sample preparation methods for DB  FIB X-section were developed in this study. One started from  the die backside and another was from the bottom of the


organic substrate side.


(1) Sample preparation from Si die backside


The mechanical milling from Si backside to remove excess Si  material was employed. A local milling technique using the  ASAP-1S from Ultra Tec was performed on the failed  samples.  In order to reduce the milling time, a selective area  milling was applied only over the Area Of Interest (AOI). In  this study, the ASAP1-S was used to mill a 7mm x 7mm  opening in the silicon over the AOI to be cross sectioned. A  1mm diameter bit was used for milling with a rotational speed  setting of 4.  Silicon thickness was monitored manually  throughout the milling process and milling was stopped when  the remaining silicon thickness was approximately 50 – 60um’s.  The total process time was less than 30 minutes. Fig.1 shows the schematic picture of local milling from Si  backside to remove excess Si material.  In order to further  reduce DB FIB X-section time from Si die into the C4 solder  joint, a chemical etchant was subsequently applied on the  samples that had been mechanically thinned down to 60um. Tetramethylammonium Hydroxide (TMAH) with 24.9% aqueous solution at 83°C was used to completely etch away  the remaining Si and stop at the SOI layer as TMAH only  reacted with Si and didn’t etch SiO2. By then the sample was  ready for DB FIB X-section from the Si die backside.


Once the type of analysis has been chosen, the next step is to  generate the signature Pareto for each of the three zones  independently.  The left and center Paretos look similar to the  combined ’Failing Chains’ Pareto.  The right Pareto has a  feature that shows higher fallout relative to the left and center  Paretos.  This is a systematic yield detractor.  The wafermap at  the bottom of the figure shows only devices that contain Chain #4.  Evident from the bottom wafer is a strong dependence at  the edge of the wafer.  The edge dependence of Chain #4 is also  contained in the wafermap at the top of the figure; however the  random yield loss served to mask the systematic yield detractor.


Failing Chains


Chain #4


Figure 1: With diagnosis-driven yield-analysis manufacturers analyze a feature type (a scan chain, in this  example) in each zone of a wafer.


Zonal analysis for chain fails uses the results from chain  diagnosis to generate signatures.  The signatures that are  generated can be which chain is failing, which scan cell is  failing, the behavior of the chain fail, the type of scan cell that  is failing, the clock domain of the scan cell, the location of the  scan cell on the chip, etc.  In order to do zonal analysis for  chain fails each of the signatures must be analyzed for each of  the different kinds of zones.


Once zonal analysis has identified a systematic yield detractor  the devices are chosen for FA.  The devices are chosen by:


1) Devices that contain the signature


2) Devices that exist in the zone that is identified as being  an outlier in the zonal analysis


3) Devices that have the best resolution.


Devices that meet the criteria above have two main advantages  over the traditional approach.  First, these devices are very  likely to contain the systematic defect.  This reduces the  likelihood that random defects will be found resulting in wasted  FA resources or worse, the wrong root cause will be attributed.  The second advantage over the traditional approach is that by  including chain diagnosis as part of the process the failure  analyst can work on devices that have the best diagnosis  resolution, thus reducing the time spent in FA.


4 Case Study #1: Isolating Systematic Chain Fails  at the Center of the Wafers


由


4.1 Design and Yield  Characteristics


The design used in this case study is an automotive chip that is  manufactured in the 90nm technology node.  The device has ~800k equivalent gates.  The data used in this case study comes  from 1,472 devices on multiple wafers.  More wafers than this  were affected by this yield detractor; however this is all the data  that is required for successful for zonal analysis on this  material.  For each of the 1,472 devices, up to 20,000 cycles  worth of data are collected from the ATE on a compressed  pattern.  Compressed mode chain diagnosis is used in order to  avoid having to recollect the failing cycles in bypass mode [4]. The yield detractor in this case study is described by the  product engineer as systematic chain fails at the center of the


1010Failing ChainsFailing Chainsfailing Chains


wafer. The yield impact of the chain fails is ~66% (970 devices  out of 1,472) of the total scan fails on the wafers that are  analyzed.  Figure 2 shows the stacked wafermap of all scan  fallout and it can be seen that there is elevated fallout in the  center of the wafer.


Figure 2: Stacked wafermap showing yield detractor that  has heavier fallout at the center of wafer


4.2 Results of zonal analysis, population  separation, and device selection


For this population of devices the zonal analysis indicates a  sensitivity of the failures at the center of the wafer to a certain  type of flip-flop cell, FF-Type-A, failing as a Stuck-at-1.  A  stacked wafermap of devices that contain FF-Type-A in their  diagnosis result is shown in Figure 3a.  Figure 3b shows the  stacked wafermap when the devices that contain the systematic  failing flip-flop type are removed from the stackmap.  Unlike  Figure 2, the remaining die on the right wafermap doesn’t show  a significant center to edge correlation.


Figure 3: a) Wafer stackmap showing devices that contain  the systematic yield detractor FF-Type-A Stuck-at-1.  The  wafermap demonstrates a significant correlation of this  yield detractor to the center of the wafer. b) Wafer  stackmap showing devices remaining after FF-Type-A Stuck-at-1 yield detractors are removed from the  population.


By using zonal analysis for device selection, the number of  potential candidates from FA is reduced from 307 (chain fails  at the center of the wafer) to 159 (FF-Type-A Stuck-at-1 chain  fails at the center of the wafer).  Note that almost half of the  devices on this wafer that would be submitted to FA using the  traditional approach do not contain the systematic defect.


4.3 Failure Analysis results on devices selected  using zonal analysis


Diagnosis results for devices that are identified by zonal  analysis to contain a systematic defect were submitted to FA.  Ultimately devices that only had a single failing location were  submitted to FA along with the information that the defect is in  the cell enclosure (as opposed to in the metal layers).  The  defect is known to be in the cell because the signature is the cell  type (FF-Type-A).


Once the failure analyst had a device in his lab with only a  single potentially bad cell he began the FA.  These devices  were first check for abnormal emissions using photon emission  microscopy, however none were observed.  Knowing that the  defect was inside the cell the analyst acknowledges that there  are many cell internal defects that would not cause emissions.  For this reason he uses the front-side nanoprobing techniques to  isolate the faulty transistors.  This technique is made possible  by the fact that the localization is already down to a single cell.  The nanoprobing revealed that some instances of series  transistors exhibited abnormally weak drive strength.  An IV  curve of a pair of series transistors with weak drive strength is  shown in Figure 4.


The transistors that showed the weak drive strength had a rather  specific topology: they are series n-channel transistors that are  constructed as minimum width.  An example of this topology is  shown in Figure 5 where solid is polysilicon and striped is the  active n+ region.  Since this problem is merely a sensitivity  rather than a hard violation it only happens occasionally and  effects random instances of cells each time.


Figure 4: IV curve showing drive strength of defect free  transistor pair (top, blue) and transistor pair showing weak  drive strength (bottom, red).


Figure 5: Example topology that shows the sensitive  transistor configuration that is responsible for the center of  wafer yield detractor. Solid red is polysilicon and striped  green is active area.


After realizing that the defect which caused the center of wafer  yield loss resulted in weak drive strength the device engineers  discovered that a certain piece of equipment used in the wafer  manufacturing was slightly out of spec, which caused the  sensitivity to the minimum width series transistor topology.  The FF-Type-A cell occurs often in the scan chains, and this is  why it shows up as systematic scan chain fails.


Multiple cross sections were imaged in FA, but no physical root  cause could be identified with 100% certainty. This is in spite  of the fact that the defect has been localized to a single pair of  transistors within a cell.   A potentially malformed gate was  observed on the transistor pair, but was not conclusive enough  to directly assign a root cause.  In order to get definitive proof  that malformed gate was the cause of the yield loss the fab was  required to run expensive and costly process split experiments. Because of the essentially invisible nature of the root cause, the  closest thing the failure analyst could provide as certain  evidence of the defect is the weak drive strength voltage curves  shown in Figure 4.  The lack of an observable defect seems to  be evidence that supports the claim from the ITRS Roadmap  that as circuit design becomes more complex there will be an  increase in the number of non-visual defects [3].  The fact that  the non-visual defect is the result of a design-process sensitivity  underscores why it is imperative to use diagnosis driven yield  analysis for device selection and not just pick devices at  random.

A bar chart that looks at only devices that contain a Fast-toRise feature on FF-Type-A is shown in Figure 6b.  By  comparing Figure 6b to Figure 6a it can be seen that Fast-toRise FF-Type-A is a much better way to discriminate Lot 1  from Lots 2 and 3. 5.3 Failure Analysis results on devices selected  using zonal analysis


All of the devices that were submitted to FA were selected  using diagnosis driven yield analysis.  The feature that does the  best job of separating the random defects from the systematic  defects is Fast-to-Rise on FF-Type-A.


The first question that was considered by the FA team is why  there would be a fast-to-rise fault.  The initial assumption made  by the failure analysis team was that a hold time fault was  responsible for the Fast-to-Rise diagnosis result.  Timing  measurements were taken inside a defective chip that was  provided by DDYA to verify this behavior.  In order to collect  the timing measurements the chip was thinned from the  backside and time resolved emissions (TRE) were used to  identify transitions on the flip-flop outputs.  Figure 7 shows a


Location


waveform representation of the transitions that were measured  where FF(n) is the defective scan cell and FF(n-1) is the scan  cell upstream from the defective scan cell.  As shown in Figure  8, OUT represents the output of the flip-flop and IN represents  the input to the flip-flop.  By combining the schematic and the  waveform it can be understood that the defect exists inside the  multiplexer of the faulty scan-flip flop.


Figure 7: Waveform representation of signals that were  measured using time resolved emissions.  The shaded area  shows the defective timing.


INOUT


TIFigure 8: Simple representation of a scan flip-flop.  An  anomalous signal can be observed at the 'IN' node for faulty  flip-flops with a Fast-to-Rise chain diagnosis signature.


Six die were submitted to FA, each containing the signature  Fast-to-Rise and only containing a single suspect scan flip-flop.  All six devices submitted to FA were verified by TRE to  contain a defect at the output of the multiplexer inside the scan  cell.


Timing measurements taken inside a defective chip provided by  DDYA were used to verify a hold-time behavior in the flipflop, which is consistent with a fast-to-rise behavior.  In order  to collect the timing measurements the chip was thinned from  the backside and time resolved emissions (TRE) were used to  identify transitions on the flip-flop outputs.  By combining the  schematic and the timing measurements the failure analysis  team understood that the defect exists inside the multiplexer of  the faulty scan-flip flop.


After physical failure analysis found that the systematic Fastto-Rise defects were due to Si dislocations, the process and  design engineers began to hypothesize what may have been  systematic about this particular transistor configuration.  The  transistor layout is shown in Figure 10 where the green striped  layer is the diffusion region, light blue are contacts, and dark  red is polysilicon.  Possible explanations for the cause of the  dislocations are the T-shaped diffusion region or the offset  contacts.  To date, conclusive evidence has not been found to  prove one way or another.  The defect was fixed by modifying  the process rather than the design due to the fact that it would  have been more expensive to build new masks.


The process change was implemented based on the results from  the failure analysis which indicated Si dislocation defects.  Three wafers worth of data were collected and the same  analysis was run to see if the process change affected the chain  fallout.  Out of all three wafers there is only one device with a  Fast-to-Rise FF-Type-A signature.  The elimination of this  feature is a very good indication that the process change has  fixed the systematic Si dislocations.  The relative fail rate of  chain fails compared to logic fails on the two wafers following  the process change is 51%.  This is not outside of the  expectation for an appropriate level of chain fails.


5.4 Case Study #2 Summary


The systematic signature presented in Case Study #2 is found  by using zonal analysis to compare lots with differing fallout of  chain fails.  The signature that shows the most sensitivity is
