Device Selection for Failure Analysis of Chain Fails Using Diagnosis Driven Yield  Analysis


Chris Schuermyer1, Brady Benware1, Graham Rhodes2


Mentor Graphics Corp., Wilsonville, Oregon, USA1  Mentor Graphics Corp., Grenoble, France2


Davide Appello3, Vincenzo Tancorre4, Olivia Riewer5


STMicroelectronics, Agrate Brianza, Italy3 STMicroelectronics, Rousset, France4 STMicroelectronics, Grenoble, France5


This work presents the first application of a diagnosis driven  approach for identifying systematic chain fail defects in order  to reduce the time spent in failure analysis.  The zonal analysis  methodology that is applied separates devices into systematic  and random populations of chain fails in order to prevent  submitting random defects for failure analysis. Two silicon case  studies are presented to validate the production worthiness of  diagnosis driven yield analysis for chain fails.  The defects  uncovered in these case studies are very subtle and would be  difficult to identify with any other methodology.


1 Introduction


Yield enhancement is the process of identifying and  understanding yield detractors and making manufacturing or  design improvements in order to eliminate them.  This process  involves the following steps:


1. Yield analysis - identification, quantification and  prioritization of yield detractor signatures by analyzing  for example spatial failure patterns on wafermaps.


2. Device selection - selection of a few failing devices that  are expected to contain the highest priority yield  detractor.


3. Failure analysis (FA) - determination of the failure root  cause on the selected devices.


4. Corrective action - implementation of a design or  process change that is intended to eliminate the yield  detractor.


5. Verification – analysis of the yield after implementing  the corrective action to ensure the original yield detractor  is eliminated without creating new issues.


A rapid yield ramp is accomplished by minimizing the time  required to complete each of these steps and ensuring that each  step is done correctly.  While successful execution of all these  steps are necessary for yield enhancement, the device selection  step is often the most critical because incorrect device selection  may result in wasted FA or the wrong correct action.


Yield enhancement cycle times are lengthening.  Specifically, three trends in the industry are resulting in longer cycle times, which increases the importance of proper device selection.


1. As geometries continue to shrink, performing FA is getting  more expensive and time consuming for each process  node.  The feature dimensions are becoming so small that  physical fault isolation is reaching its practical limit [1].


The number of metal layers along with the widespread  adoption of flip-chip packaging has driven the need for  backside processing techniques, requiring additional  deprocessing steps and specialized hardware setups [2].


2. Non-visual defects are becoming a larger percentage of the  yield loss as the circuit design and manufacturing  processes become more complex [3].  A non-visual defect  manifests itself as an electrical failure, however is not  readily observable in the physical construction of the  device. The increasing occurrence of these subtle defects  introduces additional analysis steps and forces the FA  process to proceed more cautiously, increasing the overall  FA cycle time.


3. In nanometer technology nodes, many of the yield  detractors are the result of an interaction between a specific  design feature and the manufacturing, which require  domain specific expertise about both the design and  manufacturing process [13].


The key to an effective yield enhancement is the ability to  correctly separate the yield detractors and select small groups  of devices that represent each yield detractor.  While there are  many types of failures that can occur, this device selection is  particularly challenging for scan chain failures. Scan chain  failures (or chain fails for short) are also referred to as chain  integrity, chain flush, or broken chain failures.  The biggest  challenge in device selection for chain fails is that oftentimes  the only information available is which chain failed, and this  isn’t usually a meaningful failure classification for effective  device selection.  A large percentage of the chip may be  covered by logic that would cause a chain fail if it was  defective.


The following section will present related previous work and discuss remaining challenges.  Section 3 focuses on the  methodology applied for device selection.  Sections 4 and 5  each present a silicon case study in which the proposed  methodology was successfully used to improve device selection  on scan chain fails. Section 6 will summarize the paper.


2 Previous work and limitations


For many years people have been using test data to better  understand yield loss in the logic portion of ICs [7].  This  concept has been used for clustering similar devices together  using fail signatures for the purpose of identifying systematic  defects [9].  This idea has been extended by using logic  diagnosis to provide a richer set of data for the analysis [8].  Identification of even more subtle systematic defects is demonstrated in [6] in which the authors bring together logic  test data and process data.  In [11], the authors present a case  study with 20 devices that systematically fail the chain test. In  this example chain diagnosis was employed, but since the  systematic defects makes up such a high percentage of the  failures diagnosis driven yield analysis is not required for  device selection.  In [12], the authors identify a systematic  defect as being a zonally sensitive chain fail, but the zonal data  is not leveraged to improve the accuracy of the device  selection.  Additionally the classification is limited to attributes  about the diagnosis location and doesn’t leverage the true  defect behavior.


3 Proposed Methodology


3.1 Overview


The methodology for device selection proposed in this paper is  made up of the following steps:


Data Collection: The failing cycles and chip pin data is  collected from the ATE for the subsequent processing.


Classification: The fail data is classified by signature where  signature can be derived from the fail data or the diagnosis  process.


Zonal Analysis: Zonal analysis is performed on the population  in order to label the systematic yield detractor according to a  classification and zone.


Population Filtering: The zonal analysis results are then used  to filter out the part of the population that doesn’t contain the  systematic yield detractor identified by zonal analysis.


Diagnosis Ranking: Finally, the devices in the systematic  population are ranked by diagnosis resolution and score.


3.2 Chain Fail Diagnosis


Diagnosis is the process of combining ATE fail data with  design data in order to derive defect locations and behaviors.


The design data that is used in the diagnosis process is the  verilog design, and the ATPG patterns.  Chain fail diagnosis is  the application of diagnosis to chain fails [5].  Locations for  chain fail diagnosis are generally considered to be a scan cell  on the chain and the nets that the scan cell is attached to.  Chain


diagnosis may be able to resolve down to a single scan cell for  some devices and to a range of scan cells for other devices.  With chain diagnosis a failure analyst is able to narrow down  the possible defect locations to small number of suspect scan  cells and nets.


3.3 Identifying Systematic Yield Detractors Using  Zonal analysis


Identification of yield detractors with diagnosis driven yield  analysis uses a technique called zonal analysis.  Zonal analysis  is a technique that is widely used in the industry, but this paper  presents the first application of zonal analysis to chain fails.  A basic example of zonal analysis, adapted from [10], is shown in  Figure 1.  In this example the first step is to choose the  population that is to be analyzed (wafer at top of figure), the  type of signature that is to be analyzed, and the zone that is to  be analyzed. The signature that will be analyzed in Figure 1 is  failing scan chain.  A signature is made up of a collection of  features.  Each feature, represented as a vertical bar in the ‘Failing Chain’ Pareto, represents one of the five scan chains in  the design.  The height of the feature is the count of devices on  the wafer that include that particular scan chain in their datalog.  In the Figure 2 example the zone chosen for analysis is wafer  radius.  The zone to be analyzed could also be regions on a  wafer, stepper field, different wafers, different lots, different  processing, etc.


4.4 Results from traditional approach


At the time this yield issue was initially affecting the  manufacturing line, the concept of diagnosis driven yield


analysis was not widely adopted.  Prior to the device selection  using zonal analysis that is discussed in Section 4.3 this yield  detractor was addressed using the traditional approach.  Six  devices were submitted to FA using the following criteria.


1) Chain fails


2) Devices in the center of the wafer.


Several weeks were spent getting the hardware ready for doing  backside photon emission microscopy.  Abnormal emissions  were discovered on 4 of the 6 devices and these were further  explored.  The other 2 devices were considered No Defect  Found (NDF) because no abnormal emissions were observed.  Of the four devices with abnormal emissions, different defects  were discovered on the corresponding circuitry.  The defects  that were found were a missing via, a bad contact, missing  polysilicon, and bad oxide.


After seeing four different defects for four successful FA jobs  the analysts eventually hypothesized that systematic defect may  actually be in the devices that were considered NDF and the ‘successful’ FAs may all be baseline defects.  The analyst  decided to further explore the NDF devices by nanoprobing the  transistors to see if there is a parametric defect that was causing  the behavior.  After switching to the nanoprobing technique it was discovered that a pair of series transistors had unexpectedly  weak drive strength.  Because of improper device selection the  failure analyst had to perform 6 FAs before finding the defect.

5.2 Results of zonal analysis, population  separation, and device selection


Data was collected on twenty-one wafers from three lots that  were manufactured after instituting corrective action for the  yield problem presented in Case Study #1.   The fallout for  chain fails on these wafers is shown in Figure 6.  Note that Lot  1 has lower fallout than the other 2 lots.  Lot 1 can be compared  against the other 2 lots using diagnosis driven yield analysis to  see if there are any systematic defects that are causing higher  fallout in Lot 2 and Lot 3. With this type of analysis each lot  will represent a zone for the zonal analysis.


Similar to Case Study #1, many chain fail signatures were  analyzed on this material while comparing Lot 1 to the Lots 2  and 3. The signatures that are analyzed are shown in Table 1 sorted descended by best discriminator to worst discriminator.


The best way to separate systematic chain fails from random  chain fails is the signature Diagnosis Type – Cell Type – Value.


The systematic feature of signature ‘Diagnosis Type – Cell  Type – Value’ that is returned by zonal analysis of chain fails is  Fast – FF-Type-A – 1.  A Fast fault with a 1 value means it is a  Fast-to-Rise fault.


Table 1: Types of analysis of chain fail data ranked by ability to discriminate random and systematic defects.


A single defective transistor was localized using voltage curves  collected by nanoprobing.   The transistor was cross sectioned  but no defect was observable in the scanning electron  microscope (SEM).  In order to image the defect the failure  analyst had to use a transmission electron microscope (TEM).  TEM imaging revealed that the cause of the Fast-to-Rise  signature was due to silicon dislocation defects shown in Figure


9.    If a failure analysis lab did not have access to a TEM this  defect would most likely be invisible.


Figure 9: Cross section of the defective transistor identified  by DDYA, TRE, and nanoprobing.  Darker blue arrow is  pointing at a large Si dislocation defect.  Lighter red arrow  is pointing at the location of the Si dislocation defect  responsible for the Fast-to-Rise fault.


TE


Figure 10: Layout of transistor in FF-Type-A that


contained systematic Si dislocation defects which were  describe as Fast-to-Rise in chain diagnosis.


The proper device selection in this case study is critical to being  able to successfully get to root cause.  Because of the very  subtle nature of Si dislocation defects, a failure analyst will  only go to the expense of nanoprobing and TEM imaging if he  is very confident that the defect is a true systematic yield  limiter.  Diagnosis driven yield analysis was used in production  in this case to provide that confidence to the analyst.  The result  was 6 successful FA jobs out of 6 FA jobs attempted.  The  systematic signature Fast-to-Rise on FF-Type-A is only present  on about 20% of chain failing devices and the other 80% of the  fallout is presumably random.  If the traditional approach for  device selection had been pursued (picking any device that is a  chain fail), than 4 of every 5 FA jobs would be focused on a  non-systematic defect.


One other important result that comes from this case study is  the dependence of yield on the design.  The Si dislocation  defects were distributed randomly on the wafers and randomly  in the devices.  Additionally, the fabrication process used to  manufacture these devices was initially qualified using a library  validation test chip.  This test chip did not show any of the  silicon dislocation issues that were later discovered in the  product.  This highlights the fact that even though test chips can  provide a lot of insight on how the design is interacting with the  process, it is not complete.  The only cost-effective way to  monitor all possible design-process interactions is to make  them observable in volume production.


6 Conclusion


This paper presents a case study which demonstrates that  improper device selection has the potential of creating months  of delay in realizing yield improvement.  Proper device  selection for scan chain fails is dramatically improved by  incorporating scan chain diagnosis results in the yield analysis  and device selection procedure.  The zonal analysis of chain  diagnosis data can effectively enhance the failure analysis  process by separating devices with a systematic yield detractor  from devices containing random defects.  One silicon case  study was presented in which diagnosis driven yield analysis  could have been used to reduce the number of FAs on random  defects from 4 down to 0 by focusing on the devices which  contained the true, albeit invisible, systematic defect. A second  case study is presented that shows where DDYA is used in  production to identify an design-process dependent defect that  is detectable in FA only when using very expensive techniques.

Debugging an Invisible Flaky Scan Chain Defect


Rahul Shukla1, Richard Billings2, Anurag Bakhshi2, John Schulze2, Atchyuth Gorti2, and Nagesh Tamarapalli1 1AMD India Private Limited, 28 Cubbon Road, Shivajinagar, Bangalore INDIA 560001  2  Advanced Micro Devices Inc., 7171 Southwest Pkwy, Austin TX 78735, USA


In general, scan shift failures are difficult to debug. Usually we use the compressed-mode chain test or scan  capture-based chain diagnosis to ascertain the small chain  segment or the position of the sequential element in the chain  that is the cause of the failure. This method of diagnosis works  well when the failures are static and limited to a chain segment, but fails to give results when the failure is caused by intra chain  or inter chain segment interactions . This paper presents a  scenario in which the root cause of the chain failures was due  to interaction between  chains. We call it the “invisible flaky  chain defect” because -- although we were able to replicate  failure at a test point (voltage-frequency) on the Shmoo -- the  failing cycles changed from run to run.


Traditionally, scan chain shift failures are diagnosed using  the compressed chain-check vectors to ascertain a short chain  segment that is causing scan shift failures. A scan capturebased chain diagnosis can also be used. In this technique, failing scan chain(s) capture responses from other scan chains  using scan capture, and this captured response is shifted out to  locate the position of the failing sequential cell in the failing  scan chain. These methods of diagnosis work well when the  failure is limited to a small chain segment [1-11], but fail to  give results when the failure is caused by interactions among  scan chains.


In this paper, we illustrate  debug of a scan chain shift  failure caused by a combination of two issues. We call the  defect an invisible flaky chain defect because the defect was  due to signal integrity and the failure could be replicated only  at the same test point (voltage-frequency) in the Shmoo, but the  failing cycle varied across different runs. We made two  observations:


1. The Shmoo for the scan chain failures exhibited flaky (non-uniform) failing and passing regions.


2. The scan chain-check Shmoo had a dependency on the  value chosen for PLL multipliers. Extra failing spots were  observed when the PLL multipliers were increased.


This paper discusses the techniques and experiments used  to debug the invisible flaky chain check defect. Given that the  traditional scheme of things did not help identify the root cause  of the failure, we describe the different experiments  carried out  to root cause the failure.


The flakiness also limited the shift frequency for scan test. The reduced shift frequency increased the test time by 3x compared to initial estimates.


In this paper, we start by describing the observed symptoms  and the data collection that helped us narrow the possible  reasons for the failure. We then discuss fault isolation and rootcause analysis, followed by our conclusions and references.


Symptoms


The scan chain check failures were observed on multiple  units, predominantly at low voltages and sporadically at higher  voltages.


The failures were observed for most IPs in the design, and  had sensitivity to the temperature and duty cycle of the clock. The scan chain check failures were limited to a few compare  cycles during shift (ruling out a stuck-at defect in the chain, which would have caused the alternative two compares to fail  for a 0011 chain-check pattern).


Multiple runs of scan chain test for a part at the same  voltage and frequency caused the same scan chains to fail,although the failing cycles varied across different runs.


The Shmoo in Figure 1 depicts the flaky chain failure issue.


The Shmoo does not point to set-up or hold issues in the scan  chains, because those would have caused consistent failures at  greater than a given frequency or a given voltage, respectively.


Figure 1: Flaky chain check


The Shmoo in Figure 2 illustrates the dependency of failures on  the variation of PLL multipliers.


Figure 2: Dependency on PLL multiplier


Data Collection


The design under consideration employed a MUX-D style  of scan; however, to simplify timing closure, we used two  different scan-enable signals. As illustrated in Figure 3, one  signal for switching the data was propagated to all scan cells, and the other for switching the clock from externally supplied  shift clock to a capture clock. The initial experiment was  designed to check the timing of these clock- and data muxingenable signals.


Shift Clock


Figure 3: Clock and data mux select line sequencing


This order of events ensures that there are no spurious scan  captures. During a chain test, the automatic test pattern  generation (ATPG) tool applies a dummy capture window with  no capture clocks, as depicted in the waveform in Figure 3. To confirm the failures were not occurring due to spurious capture  clocks, the scan data and clock selects were kept constantly high (essentially, in shift mode) during the capture window.


To isolate the failures caused by PLL multiplier change,


-10C


75C


we turned off the REFCLK (Reference Clock) to the PLL. The  resulting Shmoo, shown in Figure 4, illustrates that -- even  with PLL REFCLK turned off, which in turn would turn off  any PLL clock, and data mux/clock mux selects kept high in  shift mode -- flaky (inconsistent) failures remained. Further  efforts were dedicated to isolating the reason for the flakiness  with PLL REFCLK turned off and the dependency of failures  on PLL multiplier would not be discussed here

The first step was to isolate the region where the scan  chains were failing. The design under consideration has  pipeline stages along the external scan channel paths to achieve  better shift frequency. By implementation, these pipeline  stages, which belong to different scan channels, were routed in  close proximity to each other as depicted in Figure 5. To  determine whether the failures were caused due to these  pipeline stages, we ran logic built-in self-test (LBIST), which  does not use these pipeline stages.


Figure 5: Scan channel pipeline stages


The results from LBIST still depicted spots, indicating the  defect was contained inside the tile (physical module).


To check the dependency of failures on timing, we obtained  Shmoos by varying temperature (Figure 6) and duty cycle (Figure 7).

To look at different possible causes of timing failures, we


applied experiments including utilization of alternative clock to


shift, changing I/O voltages, and different chain-check patterns


with reduced toggle. None of these experiments provided


conclusive results.


Our next step was to check for signal integrity. For this, we


quieted chains other than the failing ones (scan input set to


constant 0 during the chain test), which removed the flaky


failures. This experiment indicated that the failure was caused


either by IR drop in the region where the failing chain was


located or by failing chains affected by a coupling defect.


Iterative analysis to isolate the chains that interacted with each


other narrowed the failing chains to a small sub-set of short


chains.


We conducted further experiments to isolate the victim and Masking All Memory Chains


Masking Chainon ControlLogic


aggressor flops in these interacting short chain segments. These  experiments, described in Section 5, utilized LBIST, which  accelerated turn-around time.


Defect Isolation


Our first experiment was to load the scan chain test at the


passing test point (voltage-frequency) in the Shmoo for all Masking Chain on DataInput


Masking Chain on Data Output


cycles except the last five. The last five shifts were carried out  at the failing test point. The scan chain unload was carried out  at a passing test point in the Shmoo.


This experiment yielded results that limited the failing  cycles to five, which helped identify the location of the failing  flop in the scan chain. The results from this experiment pointed  to the flops located inside scan wrappers of memories.


In parallel to this experiment, we conducted CTP analysis  on the chip. The results for fault site depicted in Figure 8, indicated the failure was located inside the memory, corroborating the results obtained from the last-five-shifts  experiment.


The scan wrappers for memory were stitched in three scan  chain segments:


1. Scan wrappers on data inputs of the memory


2. Scan wrappers on the data outputs of the memory


3. Scan wrapper on the control pins like address, write, enable of the memory


Figure 8: CTP results indicating sites that the caused failing  part to pass {memory array}


Further isolation of the chain segments based on these three  categories was carried out by masking selective chains at a time  and running LBIST. Masking the chains containing the scan  wrappers on the data inputs of the memory resolved the fails  indicating that the scan chains containing the scan wrappers on  the data input of the memory were the affected, the Shmoo  results for which are depicted in Figure 9.


Figure 9: Shmoos for different memory scan wrappers


To isolate the failing chain and the aggressor further, using  carefully crafted uncompressed custom chain test patterns; we  carried out a binary search experiment. From previous  experimental results, we knew the chains that served as victim  and aggressor, but the relation between the two remained  elusive. To gain better insight, we carried out the following  process:


1. A pattern of 1100 was shifted inside the failing chain until  the position of data input scan wrappers in the chain with  all other chains shifted with the regular chain-check pattern (0011).


2. This pattern did not fail. When closely analyzed, we observed the regular chain-check pattern was 0011 and the


position of 11 in the victim chain was not the same as in a  regular chain-check pattern.


3. Changing the bit position of 11 to match the regular chain  check showed the failure at scan wrappers on data input  pins of the memory as depicted in Figure 10, where  chain25 depicts the victim chain.


Figure 10: Results for different chain test patterns


4. The victim and aggressor chains were now isolated, with  the chain-check pattern shifted only into the aggressor  chain and the 0011 pattern shifted into the victim chain as  depicted in Figure 11 where chain 24 is the aggressor chain  and chain25 is the victim chain.


Figure 11: Chain test that manifested the fails


5. To isolate the aggressor flops, a small portion of the chaincheck pattern was shifted into the aggressor chain, with  0011 shifted into the victim chain and a binary search done  on the aggressor chain to find the flops that affected the  victim chains as depicted in Figure 12 where chain 24 is  the aggressor chain and chain25 is the victim chain


Figure 12: Aggressor/victim scan cell isolation


Those results were conclusive in identifying the victim and  aggressor flops in the two scan chains under consideration. Once the aggressor and victims were short-listed, we carried  out analysis to find the correlation between the two to identify  the root cause of the failure.


The correlation between the aggressor and victims  depicted that the aggressor flops were part of the chain utilized  to shift in the repair data. Functionally, the paths from these flops to the memory were asynchronous and therefore untimed. A one-hot decoder , as shown in Figure 13 ,  was used to  get the data from the repair flops to generate the address that  needed repair. Because the paths from these flops to the


memory were not timed, the decoder resulted in multiple bits at  the memory to go high at the same time causing contention.


Figure 13: Redundancy decoder and memory wrapper  chain


We attributed the invisibility and cycle variation of the  failures to the fact that contention was dependent on time of  signal propagation through the one-hot decode logic to create  multiple repair bits to turn high. This timing varied from run to  run, so different compare cycles failed in different runs. For the  same reason, the failure also did not depict any consistent  pattern.


Figure 14: Memory architecture (depicting right shift  repair mechanism)


By design, as shown in Figure 14, memory repair works on  the principle of right/left shift. Whenever the right/left shift is  done, the present bitline has a weak pull-down to keep the  bitline from floating.


When we were shifting in the data in scan, because the  logic from the repair bits to the memory was untimed, multiple  repair bits were set as high, which caused contention. This  contention corrupted the data, which was shifted through the  scan wrappers connected to the data input pin of the memory.


The wrappers are made up of a master-slave latch, as  depicted in Figure 15. When the data was shifted through the  aggressor chain, causing contention in the victim chain, the  master latch closed capturing the contentious value. The duty  cycle increase helped allow more set-up time for the repair bits and the settled value of those bits to get captured in the master  latch of the wrapper flops.


Figure 15: Master Slave


With this hypothesis satisfying the different observations, further revisions of the chip were fixed to stop the flops from  toggling in scan shift, and this issue was verified fixed in  silicon.


There were many lessons from the experiments we  conducted for debugging the invisible flaky chain defect. The  experiments we conducted had very fast turn-around times  because of the utilization of LBIST. For LBIST at ATE first a  set of chains were masked, using a small change in the  initialization sequence, and subsequently signature was  calculated. Once the signature was known for a given setup  Shmoo was observed for existence of flakiness. These set of  debug patterns generated on the fly at ATE saved time as  compared to generating ATPG chain test patterns which  required high runtimes.


The fix we took up was to turn off the clock to the  aggressor flops, and we modified the pattern generation flow to  account for the same. For compressed vectors, we utilized the  short-chain mask feature provided by the ATPG tool; for  uncompressed vectors, we used a short-chain bypass feature  that was implemented in the design. This separate chain  segment for analog IPs that can be bypassed will help in any  future debug.

diagnosis of defects in scan chain " Proc. Of Intl. test


If a signal on clock tree is slower than expected due to  either a design error or a manufacturing defect, it may  cause complicated fault behaviors during scan-based  testing. It makes the diagnosis of such defect especially  difficult if the defective clock signal is used for both shift  and capture operations during the scan testing, because


(1) the defect induces hold time faults on scan chains  during shift cycles, and (2) hold-time faults may also be  introduced during capture cycles in the functional logic  paths. In this paper we illustrate the failure behaviors of  such clock defects and propose an algorithm to diagnose  it.


I.


Scan-based testing has proven to be a cost-effective  method to achieve good test coverage in digital circuits, and hence has been widely adopted in most designs. Advances in semiconductor technologies and increasing  complexity of designs are rendering Physical Failure  Analysis (PFA) and yield learning techniques inadequate  for finding the root cause without knowing where to look. Scan-based diagnosis can help to guide PFA and speedup  yield ramp-up.


Prior scan-based diagnosis research can be roughly  divided into three categories:


(1) System logic diagnosis, such as in [WAI89] [RED00] [BAR01] [VEN01] [LI02] [WAN03] [WAN04] [CHE04] [MAJ06] [HUA08]. In system logic  diagnosis, it was always assumed scan chains are  working correctly.


(2) Scan chain diagnosis, such as in [KUN93] [GUO01] [HUA03] [YAN05] [HUA06] [HSU06] [TZE07] [HWA08] [LEE08] [HUA09]. Similarly, in software  based scan chain defect diagnosis, it was typically  assumed that the system logic is defect-free.


(3) Compound defect diagnosis [HUA04] [HUA07] [WAN08]. Compound defects mean that both the  scan chain(s) and the system logic have defects on  one die.


Testing and diagnosing hold time faults in the system  logic were investigated in [RED00] [WAN04] and [MAJ06] [HUA08]. Hold-time fault models were built  around source Flip-Flops, sink Flip-Flops, the paths  connecting the sources and sinks or clock fault models. However, these fault models cannot be used for scan  chain hold time fault diagnosis and hence are not suitable  for compound hold time fault diagnosis either.


Scan chain hold-time diagnosis has been studied in [HUA03] [HSU06] [TZE07] and [HWA08]. All of these  prior algorithms tried to locate the scan cell(s) that suffer  hold-fault(s) during scan chain shift. However, they  cannot tell if the real root cause is on the scan chain or  clock. Shift clock diagnosis has been studied in [HUA06] and [LEE08]. In [HUA06], scan chain diagnosis is run at  first by using stuck-at-X model. However, it may only  identify the boundaries of two ends of a faulty chain if  using this model. After chain diagnosis, analysis on scan  enable / clock is performed based on the scan cell suspect  range(s). In [LEE08] there is one more step compared to [HUA06]. The additional step uses serial simulation per  shift cycle to further validate and rank the suspects. Both  of these prior works can only diagnose clock defect  caused scan chain stuck-at faults. However, they cannot  be applied directly to diagnose slow clock defects.


When compound defects exist on one die, the chain  defects and system logic defects could be either correlated  or uncorrelated. In [HUA04], a special defect called  DACS was discussed. DACS stands for Defects that  Affect Chain and System logic. It means one defect that  could impact on both chain and system logic. However, the method proposed in [HUA04] cannot diagnose  uncorrelated defects that impact chain and system logic  independently. In addition, the method proposed in [HUA04] has to use per-shift-cycle simulation, which is  very slow and impractical to be applied to very large  designs as we mentioned earlier. In [HUA07], the authors  proposed a diagnosis algorithm and a diagnosis flow that  can be used to diagnose the compound defects by  partitioning the failures into logic-defect-caused and  chain-defect-caused failures. They then mask the faulty  scan chain(s) to diagnose system logic defects and mask  the system logic defects to diagnose scan chain(s) defects. In [WAN08], an ATPG based methodology was applied  to diagnose compound defects. In reality, we often find  that the root cause of compound defects is in the global  control signals such as the clock, which cannot be located  by simply running any compound diagnosis algorithms  proposed earlier.

Yu Huang,     Wu-Tung Cheng, Ting-Pu Tai, Liyang Lai, Ruifeng Guo

The objective of this paper is to investigate the fault behavior  of slow clocks caused by spot delay defects in the clock tree  and propose an algorithm to identify which clock signal has  the defect. The paper is organized in the following manner.  In  Section II, we study the impact of a slow clock on scan chains  and logic. In Section III, we review the background  knowledge about signal profiling based scan chain diagnosis. A new diagnosis flow is proposed in Section IV. Experimental  results on simulated clock failures and case study on industrial  failing die are presented in Section V, followed by the  conclusions in Section VI.


In this study we investigated various possible causes for  the formation of Cu whiskers including decomposition of  additives, residual stress, and impurities or  contamination on the surface. Through the experiment, we could find reproducible factors for whisker growth  and suggest the mechanism of the Cu whisker growth.


We set the experimental conditions similar to those used  in commercial Cu electroplating lines to determine


factors that affect whisker growth.  Test plates were  prepared by using copper clad laminate (CCL) 90mm x  50mm in size.  The process flow for electroplating is  summarized in Figure 3.  A thin layer of Cu was  chemically plated on the CCL to a thickness of 1.5±0.5  m. For convenience, this test plates will be referred to  as ‘CP plates’ (Chemically Plated plates).  Each CP  plate was pre-cleaned using Atotech’s proprietary acidic  cleaner, CupraPro, at 35±3 , rinsed in warm water at  40±5 , rinsed in water and dipped in aqueous H2SO4  before Cu electroplating.  The Cu electroplating  solution consisted of H2SO4 (200±20g/l), Cu (19.5±3g/l) and Enthone’s proprietary CUPROSTAR ST-2000  additives.  Cu was electroplated in a 267ml Hull cell  operated at 24±3  for 20 min.  The DC-current  density was 2.0 A/dm2. After Cu electroplating, we  washed the plates with de-ionized water and dried them  with air.


Effect of residual stress


Stress, especially compressive stress, is an important  factor in other metal whisker growth in a dry  environment.  During Cu electroplating, surface stress  can change.  Stress can also be concentrated on certain  points of the plates.  In this experiment, we prepared  four test plates with different processes intending to  cause a difference in the residual stress of each test plate.  The stress was then measured using an X-ray  diffractometer with the side inclination method.  Table  2 shows the type of test plates and the residual stress of  each plate.

Through this experiment, we conclude that the stress in  the Cu deposit does not change much during PWB


fabrication and therefore can not affect the Cu whisker  growth. But surface impurities may play an important  role in Cu whisker formation.


Effect of contamination by process chemicals


Contaminants can be introduced in pre-cleaning  processes and Cu electroplating processes.  Since  whiskers begin to grow on electroplated Cu grains as  shown in Fig. 2(b), it can be assumed that contaminants  transferred to the Cu electroplating bath matter. Contamination may happen from pre-cleaning processes  through residue left on products or in the environment.


We considered three potential sources of surface  contamination in the manufacturing process: (a) residual  cleaner on the product, (b) DFR ingredients dissolved in  the acidic cleaner, and (c) DFR ingredients dissolved in  the H2SO4 solution.  In order to cause residual  impurities to remain in CP plates, we soaked the plates in  undiluted acidic cleaner, diluted cleaner in which DFR  was dissolved, and DFR dissolved-H2SO4 solution, respectively.  To maximize the amount of impurities, non-cured DFR was put directly into each solution.  The CP plate soaking was done just before the Cu  electroplating, after all of the pre-cleaning process had  been finished.  In this experiment, no whiskers were  found.  Instead, a group of nodules was found  especially when the CP plates were contaminated with  DFR dissolved cleaner as shown in Fig. 6. Fig. 6(c) shows small whisker-like horns on the top side, but no  actual whisker growth was observed after an additional  50min. of plating as shown in (d), only the size became  larger.


II. Fault Behaviors of Slow Clock


A. Fault behaviors on scan chain


Using the example shown in [HUA03], the timing  diagram for one flip-flop is illustrated in Figure 1, from  which we know that the data at the input D of a flip-flop  can be correctly registered to its output Q only if it is  maintained for at least a specified hold-time (tH) after the  clock is active.  If this hold-time requirement is not  satisfied, the registered data may be incorrect.


One simple example is shown in Figure 2, where a  slow clock (CLK1) drives one scan cell (C1) and a  normal clock (CLK2) drives C1’s immediate  upstream scan cell (C2). Without losing generality, the cell connected to scan-output is numbered 0 and


the cells in the chain are numbered incrementally


from scan-output to scan-input sequentially.


Figure 1. Timing diagram for a single Flip-Flop


Figure 2. Timing diagram for a pair of scan cells on a  scan chain


Based on the timing diagram shown in Figure 2, it is easy  to understand that a hold-time fault could be introduced if  tSK + tH - tCQ > td, where tSK is the clock skew between  CLK1 and CLK2, tH is the required hold time, tCQ is the  delay from activating clock to registering data for the  driving flip-flop (C2) and td is the propagation delay from  the output of C2 (Q2) to the input of C1 (D1).


Therefore, when a clock signal is delayed enough to  satisfy the above inequality, it will introduce a hold-time  fault on the scan chain between two neighboring scan  cells such that the downstream scan cell is driven by a


slow clock and the upstream scan cell is driven by a  normal clock.


Next in Figure 3, we show an example in which  a slow  clock signal (A) drives a segment of consecutive scan  cells from Cell M to Cell (N+1) (in shaded range). All  other scan cells in the white range are driven by the  normal clock signals.


Note that from Cell (N+1) to Cell N, there will be no  hold-time fault, but a potential setup time fault may  happen if the clock shifts in high speed. In this paper, we  do not explicitly consider this potential setup time fault in  diagnosis. However, as explained later, our diagnosis  algorithm should be robust enough to accommodate such  noise.


It is easy to extend our prior analysis to a scenario where  a slow clock drives N (N>1) segments of scan cells on a  chain. In that case, the scan chain may have up to N holdtime faults. It has nothing to do with the number of  consecutive scan cells in each segment. Diagnosing  multiple segments of scan hold-time faults is difficult. However, as we propose later, using profiling-based chain


diagnosis algorithm, we can at least find one segment of  faulty scan cells, and run clock diagnosis from them. In  the rest of the paper, we use single segment as an  example. The algorithm can be expanded to multiple  segments.


B. Fault behaviors on system logic


Similar to scan chain hold time illustrated in Figure 2, if the  hold-time requirement is violated in system logic path(s), incorrect logic values may be registered in the sink cells driven  by the slow clock during the capture mode. As an example  shown in Figure 4 below, assume clk1 is a slow clock signal, while clk2 and are clk3 normal clock signals.


Figure 4. An example of system logic hold-time faults


During the first capture cycle, C1 may capture an incorrect  value due to hold time fault caused by its slow clock, if some  of its source cells have transitions at their Q that can be  propagated to data input of C1. For multiple capture cycle  patterns, during the remaining capture cycles, the incorrect  value captured at C1 may be propagated to cells that may or  may not driven by the slow clock. Since multiple sink cells  could exist due to a slow clock and each sink cell may be  driven by multiple source cells, using the traditional  simulation-based hold-time fault diagnosis may not be an  effective solution. In our proposed diagnosis, we use profiling –based chain diagnosis, which can tolerate such noise if they  are relatively small compared to chain hold-time profiling.


C. Compound Fault behaviors


If a slow clock is used for both shift and capture, as we see in  many real cases, it will cause compound hold-time faults. Taking both scan chain hold-time faults and system logic holdtime faults into consideration, the compound fault behavior is  even more complicated. In capture mode, a sink cell could be  driven by some source cells that may be on the faulty chain  themselves. In shift mode, the captured values may change its  sensitivity to the hold-time faults on scan chains if the captured  value has been changed. Prior compound diagnosis is difficult  to locate chain and logic hold-time faults, let alone to identify  the problematic slow clock signal.

III. Profiling-based chain diagnosis


As explained in the previous section, it is hard to directly  model the slow clock defect that may cause the compound  hold-time faults. So it is feasible to start from chain  diagnosis first and locate the defective clock signals from  identified faulty scan cells. However, the traditional  model-based chain diagnosis cannot be applied to this  scenario because it is difficult to model compound holdtime faults.


Fortunately, recent developments in profiling-based chain  diagnosis algorithms [YAN05] [HUA09] are promising  because they can handle “noise” that may be introduced  into a software-based diagnosis system. The sources of  the noise include, but not limited to:


(1) Discrepancy between a fault model and un-modeled  realistic defects


(2) Compound defects that cannot be easily modeled.


(3) Errors introduced into fail log, during fail log  generation / translation / ATE truncation


(4) Imprecision / Inaccuracy of simulations procedures in  EDA tools


(5) Unrepeatable test results due to transient effect / testing environment changes


In [YAN05], functional patterns or special diagnostic  ATPG patterns must be used to run chain diagnosis. In a  practical chain diagnosis application, existing  manufacturing ATPG patterns are preferred for diagnosis. If the diagnosis results are not satisfactory, additional  special diagnostic patterns may be created. However, it  will result in extra logistic overhead to apply the  iterations. Under this circumstance, in [HUA09], the  authors proposed a solution that can use data-driven  techniques with manufacturing ATPG scan patterns. The  overall algorithm is described in 5 steps as follows.


Step 1: Use chain patterns to identify fault chain(s) and  fault model of each faulty chain. This step is same as prior  model-based chain diagnosis algorithms. It can tell if a  scan chain has hold-time fault(s) by using chain patterns.


Step 2: For each scan pattern, X-mask all the sensitive  bits in the loading values. The sensitive bits are the cell  values that may be changed during scan chain shift  operations. For different fault models, the sensitive values  are also different. For example, suppose a scan pattern has  good machine loaded value 001110011010 on the faulty  chain.  If a hold-time fault is identified in step 1, all  transition bits will become sensitive bits, and the loaded  values will be modified as X0X11X0X1XXX.


Step 3: Simulate all X-masked scan patterns. For each  cell i on the faulty chain, statistically count two numbers.


(1) Sen(i) is the number of scan patterns that capture a  sensitive bit on cell i on the faulty chain.  For circuit with  embedded compactors, Sen(i) is the number of scan  patterns that capture a sensitive bit on cell i on the faulty  chain and have a binary value at cycle i on the faulty  channel after compaction.


(2) Fail(i) is the number of times that Sen(i) failed on  ATE.


intersection as I and  I = ∩  (all range r on all  faulty chains) Ur.


Step 4: We order the nodes in set I based on its depth in  the clock tree, in decreasing order. Next we perform a  forward trace from each node in set I to the scan cells. The forward trace is performed one node at a time  according to its order in I. If a node also drives at least  one scan cell on a good chain, it is not a suspect. So we  drop this node from I. Meanwhile, we drop all its parent  and ancestor nodes on the clock tree if any of them are in  I as well. The searching order of I avoids any unnecessary  forward trace from higher level nodes. After we apply this  procedure to all nodes remained in set I, we will find all  suspect clock signals that satisfy the conditions (a) drive  at least one scan cell in each range and (b) don’t drive any  cell on good chains. These remaining nodes in I will be  reported as suspect defect locations in the clock tree.


V. Experimental Results


In this section, we will present experimental results based  on some simulated cases. We also give a case study from  TSMC.


We create simulated fail logs by using two different  designs to test the proposed diagnosis flow. Design A is a  big industrial design, which 57M gates. Design B is a  small toy design, which has 8.2K gates.


For design A, we select 17 different locations to “inject” a  slow clock fault one at a time by Modelsim to create 17  failure logs. Diagnosing 10 failure logs did not report any  clock fault. Instead it only reported the fault on scan  chains that were caused by the injected clock. This is  because the injected fault did not trigger system logic  hold time faults or the system logic hold time fault has  been masked by the chain hold time faults. In that case, it  will not behave like compound hold-time faults and it will  not run clock diagnosis. The remaining 7 cases that ran  clock diagnosis are showed in Table 1.


Figure 10. Case study – defective clock voltage vs. current characteristics


VI.  Conclusions


In this paper, we proposed an algorithm to diagnose a  special kind of defect – slow clock defect that caused  compound hold-time faults on both scan chain and system  logic. We first analyzed the compound hold-time behavior  and identify a pattern in the failure ratio profiling. We  then proposed a diagnosis flow and validation flow to  locate such defects. Simulated cases and a real case study  showed good accuracy and resolution of the new  diagnosis flow. Although the PFA hit rate for the  currently diagnosed failing die is 100%, based on our  simulated experiments, it is likely some clock defects will  not trigger the clock diagnosis if the system logic holdtime faults are not triggered or masked by chain hold-time  fault. In such cases, only scan cells that are driven by the  defective clock will be reported, and PFA process will not  be accurately guided. We will investigate such cases in  future research.

2001.

With the shrinking of gate oxide thickness in advanced  semiconductor technologies, quality of the gate oxide layer is  of prime importance for the reliability of IC devices. Gate  oxide integrity (GOI) failures are often caused by metallic  contaminations (e.g. Na, K, Ca and Fe) and physical defects (e.g. pinholes, surface irregularity) [1, 2]. While the physical  defects could be readily investigated by the microscopy


techniques, the identification and investigation of  contamination induced gate oxide failure is challenging and  complicated due to the very low concentration level of  contaminant. In our previous study [3], we reported an analysis  flow for GOI failures, which generally contains four steps: (1) electrical fault isolation, (2) failure mode identification (contamination-related or plasma-induced) utilizing Wright  etch, (3) contamination elemental survey, and (4) contamination elemental determination. Such a systematic FA  flow or approach enables fast identification of failure mode  and root cause. However, for the GOI failure caused by very  low level of contamination, the element survey technique in  our previous FA flow (Time-of-flight Secondary Ion Mass  Spectrometry, TOF-SIMS) may not be able to effectively  determine the possible contamination elements, which  increases the difficulty and time of the subsequent analyses.


On the basis of our previous study, we present in this paper a  more comprehensive FA flow for analyzing contaminationinduced GOI failure. This new flow, which is schematically  shown in Figure 1, improves the success rate for identification  of metallic contamination, . The FA flow 1 in Figure 1 is for  investigation of affected product wafers, while the FA flow 2  is for investigation of GOI failure with known suspected  machines as contamination source. More specifically, in FA  flow 2, we propose to use Vapor Phase Decomposition, Inductively Coupled Plasma Mass Spectrometry (VPD-ICPMS) for contamination elements survey. Contaminants from  the suspected equipments are collected by processing dummy  wafers (dummy wafers refer to blanket Si wafers with or  without thin oxide layer), and the elements are analyzed by  ICP-MS after sample preparation by VPD. The ICP-MS  technique is highly sensitive and capable of simultaneously  measuring a large number of elements at very low  contamination level in the ppb-ppt range, which is more  sensitive than the TOF-SIMS technique utilized in our  previous analysis flow for GOI failure [3]. Therefore, the  contamination elements could be more effectively determined  by the current analysis flow, enabling quick and accurate  subsequent elemental survey and analysis. In addition, we will  present a case study of contamination caused GOI failure, which happened right after the preventive maintenance of a  machine. We have conducted failure analysis by the FA flow 2


proposed in this paper. The FA results enable process  engineers to quickly identify the root cause.


By combining the FA flow 1 and 2, the current method is  applicable to a wide range of GOI failure cases: both cases  with plenty of failed product wafers and ones with known  failed process machines could be readily analyzed for quick  investigation of root causes.


Figure 1. A comprehensive FA flow for GOI failure. The FA  flow 1 is for investigation on affected product wafer; the FA  flow 2 is for investigation with known suspected tool.


Failure Analysis Techniques in the method


The method strategically combines several FA techniques to  identify the possible root cause of gate oxide failure, as  schematically shown in Figure 2. The details of the FA  techniques are introduced in the following sections.


Figure 2. FA Flow of current method


Electrical Fault Localization


In common GOI failure caused by contamination or physical  defects, the defects are normally concentrated at certain  location of the gate oxide layer. This results in paths of  leakage current once the gate oxide layer is under electric  field. Therefore, the knowledge of the location of the failure  site at the gate oxide is of prime importance for the  investigation of root cause.


Thermal Induced Voltage Alteration (TIVA) is a laser-based  defect localization technique and is widely used for localizing


and detecting temperature-sensitive defects in semiconductor  devices. During the TIVA analysis, the devices are biased with  constant current, and a focused 1340-nm near-infrared laser  beam is used to scan across the whole test structure.  During  the scanning, the laser heating changes the resistance of  breakdown site, resulting in voltage variation which is  recorded by the TIVA system.  By measuring this voltage  variation as a function of laser positions, the defects could be  localized to an area of micro-meter range. Figure 3 shows an  example of defect localization by TIVA, where a defect at the  edge of gate oxide was found (indicated by the arrow).


Figure 3. Optical image of the failed device with the failed  location indicated by TIVA scanning (the arrowed site).


Failure Mode identification


As discussed above, both physical defects (e.g. pinholes, surface irregularity) and contaminations (e.g. Na, K) could  result in gate oxide failure, which are difficult to be  differentiated by electrical testing alone. For different failure  mode, the crystal structure of the Si below gate oxide is  different. Pinholes and voids generally does not change the  crystal structure of Si layer, while process-induced Si crystal  defects such as  point or dislocation defects in the Si layer  create weak points in gate oxide [4]. On the other hand, metal  contaminations in gate oxide layer can diffuse into Si layer  during high tempertaure processes, thus resulting in  concentrated Si lattice defects.


Because of the stress variation and weakened Si bonds  around  the defects in Si lattice, the difference of the defect area and  normal Si crystal can be readily delineated by chemical  etching. Wright etch is a well-accepted etchant for delineation  of Si defects. Wright etch is applicable to various type of Si  wafers, and the defect shapes from Wright etch delineation are  distinct for identifcation of different defect types [5].


As schematically shown in Figure 4, the sample is first  deprocessed to active layer by HF, followed by defects  deleanation by Wright etch. The failure modes can be  identified based on the shape and orientations of the defects  after Wright etch. Si defects delineated by Wright etch can be  generally classified into 3 categories as shown in Figure 5. From the signatures of the Si defects delineated by Wright  etch, the failure mode of the GOI could be identified.  In the


case of “Combined-pit” defects (i.e. contamination related) delineated by Wright etch, elemental analysis will be  performed by VPD ICP-MS and Dynamic-SIMS in subsequent  steps.


Figure 4. Process flow of chemical FA for failure mode  identification


Figure 5. Category of defects delineated by Wright etch: (a)


"Single-pit" Si crystalline defect caused by intrinsic gate


oxide/substrate crystal weak point, (b) "Combined-pit" defect  caused by contamination,  and (c) Plasma induced defect


(PID).


Contamination Survey


Vapor phase decomposition (VPD) combined with Inductively  Coupled Plasma Mass Spectrometry (ICP-MS) was developed  for the measurement of trace metal contaminants on a silicon  wafer surface. Advantages of VPD ICP-MS include accurate  analysis of up to 40 elements in a single droplet, excellent  detection limits (part per trillion and sub part per trillion), and  a quick cycle time (tens of minutes).


In this FA flow, possible machines that potentially introduce  the contaminants are first identified. The suspected machines  may include rapid thermal oxidation furnace, cleaning bath, etc. Dummy Si wafers are processed in the suspected machines  using the standard recipes for production to collect the  contaminants. The processed dummy wafers are then  transferred to the VPD machine, where the contaminants and  surface SiO2 layer on dummy wafers are extracted to a HF  droplet. The extracted droplet is then piped from the wafer  surface and analyzed by ICP-MS. The sample preparation  process by VPD is schematically shown in Figure 6.


This contamination survey step could identify several possible  contaminants that potentially induced the gate oxide failure.


Figure 6. Process of VPD sample preparation for ICP-MS


Contamination determination


After identified possible contaminant elements, Dynamic  SIMS analysis is conducted on the product wafers to  investigate the difference of contaminant element  concentration in normal and affected wafers. DSIMS analysis  is a powerful mass spectrometric technique for the chemical  microanalysis in microelectronics. It offers nanometer depth  resolution, parts per billion sensitivity and high lateral  resolution. By using DSIMS analysis, the contaminant  elements found in contamination survey step can be confirmed  in the affected wafers.


A Case Study of GOI failure analysis


In this case study, gate oxide failure was found in some  LVPMOS and LVNMOS BV devices. The failure of devices  was happened right after the prevention maintenance of a  machine in diffusion module. The E-test failure signature is  that the breakdown voltage of thin Gate PMOS & NMOS is  out of specification.


Electrical fault localization was performed on the test  structures of the affected wafer by a TIVA system. Hot-spots  were observed in the internal bulk structure, as shown in  Figure 7. Several samples with identified locations of electric  failure were prepared for the subsequent analysis.


Figure 7. Optical image of a test strucutre with a hotspot  found from TIVA scan


Failure Mode Identification


One sample with known location of electric failure was chosen  for failure mode identification. The sample was de-processed  to active layer by HF solution, followed by Wright etch for  defect delineation. The location of electrical failure was  examined by SEM. As present in Figure 8, defects in the shape  of “Combined-pit” were observed at the electrical failure  location. According to the defect categories shown in Figure 5, the failure mode was identified as contamination-related.


Figure 8. SEM image of the failed site after Wright etch. The  shape of the defects belongs to category (b) of Figure 3, indicating that the failure is contamination-related.


Since the gate oxide failure was happened right after the  prevention maintenance of a machine in diffusion module, it is  highly suspected that the process from that machine induced  contaminations in the affected product wafers. To investigate  the process induced contaminants, a bare wafer was processed  in the suspected machine, while a reference wafer was  processed in a machine which is similar to the suspected one  but known in good condition. The processed bare and  reference wafer together with a new bare wafer without any  processing were analyzed by VPD ICP-MS, and as many as 18  elements of the samples were compared in one analysis.


The concentrations of 18 metal elements of the wafers from  ICP-MS analysis are shown in Table 1. It was found that the  sample wafer showed high level of Al, Fe, Mo and Sn  compared to the reference wafer and the new bare wafer. Figure 9 shows the detailed comparison of the element  concentrations. Based on the ICP-MS results, Al, Fe, Mo and  Sn are suspected to be the contaminants which induced gate  oxide failure in the affected product wafers.


Due to the optic design induced beam size limitation in typical

D-SIMS, the minimum analysis area is 50 μm without  significant sacrifice of detection sensitivity. Therefore, for  contamination determination, the samples need to have a  continuous pattern of gate oxide in the size of larger than 50


μm, and the sample with failed site in the center of the pattern  is preferred.


The failed samples were deprocessed to the poly-Si layer on  top of the interested gate oxide, and D-SIMS analysis was  conducted to determine the contamination elements and their  doses. As shown in Figure 10, compared to the good sample, the failed sample presented abnormally high Mo and Fe at the  interface of gate oxide and Si. The content of Mo and Fe of the  failed sample are almost 5 times as high as that of the good  sample. This confirms that the high Mo and Fe exist in the gate  oxide layer which induced the GOI failure.


Figure 10. Composition depth profiles of failed and reference  samples analyzed by D-SIMS: (a) comparison of Mo content, and (b) comparison of Fe content.


Contamination source investigation


After confirming that the suspected machine is the  contamination source, we carefully checked the machine parts, and found an H2 gas flow valve with an abnormally stained  area (refer to Figure 11). To study the elemental information  of the stain, we used HF solution to dissolve the stain and  transfer the solution for ICP-MS testing. In addition, fresh HF  solution and a reference solution sample prepared from a clean  valve were also analyzed by ICP-MS for comparison. As  presented in Table 2, the sample from stained area showed  significantly higher Fe, Cr, Mo, confirming the valve is the  source for the observed contaminations.


Figure 11.A photo of the H2 valve with the contamination  source. At the inner surface of the valve, there is a stained  area containing the contamination elements (indicated by a  arrow)


Table 2 ICP-MS results of the valve with stain (unit: 1E9


atom/cm2).


In this paper, we propose a fast and reliable method for the  gate oxide integrity failure analysis. The method includes a  series of techniques for fault isolation, failure mode  identification and possible contamination determination. Most  importantly, the technique of VPD ICP-MS is used for the  possible contamination elements survey. ICP-MS is highly  sensitive and capable of simultaneously measuring a large  number of elements of a range of part-per-billion level. The  utilization of VPD ICP-MS makes the current method very fast  and reliable for gate oxide integrity analysis. Detailed FA  results of a case study were presented to show the  effectiveness of the new FA flow. By combining the current  FA flow and with the one we previous reported, a wide range  of GOI failure cases could be analyzed to identify the root  causes.

Whisker Formation in Copper Electroplating


Theresa Han, Eunin Cho, Jinwoo Heo, Seoung Jae Lee


Samsung Electro-Mechanics. Co., Ltd.


Chungnam-Do, Republic of Korea


In the manufacture of Printed Wiring Boards (PWB), unwelcome structures, such as nodules and whiskers can  be formed during copper electroplating with copper  sulfate. Copper (Cu) whiskers with lengths of up to a few  hundred micrometers can lead to electrical shorts  between layers or patterns.  In this paper, we document  factors that can affect the growth of Cu whiskers; decomposition of additives in the Cu electroplating  solution, surface stress, acidic cleaner, and the  ingredients of a dry film.  Contaminants from outside of  the electroplating bath and the ingredients of the dry film  were shown as key components in the formation of Cu  whiskers.


The shape of the Cu whisker is similar to stringy  filaments rather than single crystal whiskers as shown in  Figure 1. It is not straight and the diameter changes  irregularly from bottom to top.  Long whiskers can  grow up to hundreds of micrometers and may cause  electrical shorts between layers or patterns.


Figure 1: (a) An example of Cu whisker and nodule  formation.  (b) A Cu whisker has grown to reach  adjacent patterns.


Figure 2 shows the cross-sectional view of a Cu whisker. Cu whiskers show a distinctively fine grained structure  compared to normal electroplated Cu.


Whisker formation in the electroplating of copper has  been known since the 1950s [1-3].  Meulen stated that  two types of whiskers grew in acid copper sulfate cells  operated at low current densities [3].  One was a  straight needle-like whisker that grew in the presence of  chlorides. The other was a whisker resembling twisting


hair of varying diameter that grew in the presence of  cationic surface-active agents.


Although Cu whiskers are not commonly observed in  well-controlled commercial processes, these unexpected  structures are often detected during the PWB Cu  electroplating process and disappear without any reason. The difficulty with Cu whiskers in a commercial line is  that too many factors can be related to the whisker  growth and none are reproducible.  Dietz reported some  mechanisms that can affect the whisker formation in a  commercial line as follows [4]; first, the chemical  interactions between dry film photo-resist (DFR) and  pre-plate cleaner, second, the stresses in the Cu deposit  arising during Cu electroplating, and third, microbial  contaminants in the brightener. Various efforts were  made to remove these mechanisms but no methods could  guarantee a perfectly whisker free process.


Figure 2: (a) A Cu whisker grown on a pattern. (b) Cross-sectional image of whisker obtained from the line  marked in (a). The Cu layer above the dotted line is the  whisker and the Cu layer below the line is the  electroplated Cu. Unusually small grains which are  characteristic to the structure of Cu whiskers, were  observed on the electroplated Cu layer (FIB image).


Figure 3: Process flow for electroplating Cu.


The number of whiskers was counted using an optical  microscope.  Residual stress and crystal structure were  analyzed with an X-ray diffractometer (Rigaku D/MAX2500). Surface analysis was carried out by X-ray  photoelectron spectroscopy (PHI Quantera).  A  scanning electron microscope (Hitachi S4800) equipped  with X-ray energy dispersive spectrometer (EDS) was  used to study the shape of whiskers and to analyze  elemental composition. We employed focused ion beam (FEI Nova) to obtain cross sectional images of whiskers.


Effect of additive decomposition


A Cu electroplating solution typically contains additives  such as MPS (3-Mercaptopropane sulfonic acid), SPS (Bis-(3-sulfopropyl)-disulfide, disodium salt), and  polymers such as PEG (polyethylene glycols), PPG (polypropylene glycols), PVA (poly vinyl alcohols), etc. These additives act as accelerators or suppressors during  Cu electroplating. The concentration of the additives  should be accurately controlled.  Otherwise, unexpected  crystal growth may occur.  It has been reported that  PEG decomposes during Cu electroplating [5].  Gabrielli described that decomposition of additives


increased strongly when an insoluble anode was used  compared to a soluble anode [6].


Although the roles of additives are relatively well known, it has been rarely understood how decomposition  products of additives work during electroplating of Cu. In order to see the effect of additive decomposition, we  investigated whisker growth over plating time. Table 1  shows the percentage of remaining additives which were  100% at zero plating time. There are two additives in  solution and number of whiskers tend to increase over  plating time. The needle like whiskers are shown in  Figure 4.

All the plates showed tensile stress and the stress  difference among test plates was negligible, contrary to  our expectations.


However, CCL still showed more whiskers than the CP  plates. For example, CCL showed ten whiskers in a  90x50 mm2 area while the Chemically Plated Cu sample  showed only one whisker under the same conditions. It  turned out that there were impurities such as Cl and Zn  on the CCL surface as summarized in Table 3.  The Cu  whisker growth on bare CCL might be explained by the  existence of Cl, because it has been known as a factor for  Cu whisker growth [2, 3].

Figure 3. One slow clock drives consecutive scan cells


In the above example, the scan chain hold time fault may  only happen between Cell (M+1) and Cell M because Cell  M has a delayed clock compared to the clock driving Cell (M+1). Nevertheless for all the cells from Cell M to Cell (N+1), since they share the same "delayed clock", there  are no hold-time faults between these cells on the faulty  chain. That is to say there is only one hold-time fault on  this faulty chain, although multiple cells are affected. This  behavior is quite different from a clock defect causing  scan cell stuck-at faults, in which every scan cell will be  impacted by the defective clock.


Step 4: For each cell we can calculate a failing  probability, denoted as Pfail(i) = Fail(i) / Sen(i), when  Sen(i) is not 0. In case Sen(i) is 0, Pfail(i) will be  interpolated based on the value of Pfail(i-1) and Pfail(i+1). We expected to see a drastic change of Pfail between the  downstream cells of the defective cell and the upstream  cells of the defective cell. Therefore, for each cell, except  cell 0, we calculate a differential failing probability, denoted as Difffail(i) = | Pfail(i)- Pfail(i-1) |. Our job is to  find the cell location where Difffail(i) is maximum.


Step 5: Ranking. Identify the defect location by finding  maximum Difffail(i) at cell i such that the Pfail has  maximum jump from cell (i-1) to cell (i). However, due to  noise as we mentioned earlier, an adaptive feedback noise  filtering system was adopted to estimate the maximum  Difffail among cells and used for ranking.


The reason the profiling-based algorithm works is that we  masked out all failures introduced during the scan chain  loading. Profiling is only on the possible failure bits  during unloading, which will largely depend on the defect  location.


An example of an ideal profiling plot for a permanent  chain fault is illustrated in Figure 5A, in which the cells in  the downstream of cell 1000 has 0 failing probability and  the cells in the upstream of cell 1000 has 100% failing  probability. In this case, cell 1000 is the defect location  and can be easily diagnosed. In the example profiling of  Figure 5B, it shows some small “noise” in the failing  probability. When the “noise” is small, the profiling  algorithm based on some advanced filtering techniques [HUA09] can locate the defective cell with accuracy. Figure 5C illustrated an example with “large” noise of  varying failing ratios from 10%-90% in one middle range  between 0 and 100% failing ratios. This is hard to be  explained by the prior chain defect and diagnosis down to  scan cell is difficult to achieve good resolution. However, during our industrial practice, we found such profiling is  an indication of a defect with slow-clock causing  compound hold-time faults. The large “noise” in the  middle range is very likely to be caused by the hold-time


faults during the capture mode. In the next section, we  will propose anew flow to diagnose such defects.


Figure 5. Different chain failing probability profiling


IV. Proposed Diagnosis Flow


The overall diagnosis flow is depicted in Figure 6. Our  objective is to identify if a chain hold-time failing ratio  profiling has similar pattern as depicted in figure 5C. We  start from the normal profiling based chain diagnosis as in [HUA09]. Even if the slow clock defect causes compound  hold-time faults, the profiling-based chain diagnosis can  tolerate certain “noise” and may still identify the sharpest  failing ratio edge at cell N, which is at the lower bound of  a consecutive range of cells that suffered compound holdtime faults. Next, if the failing ratios in the range between  cell 0 and N are not small (in our heuristic > 10%), it does  not match the pattern in Figure 5C, and we only report  chain hold-time fault between cell (N+1) and cell N. Otherwise, we continue to identify another sharpest edge  of failing ratios between cell (N+1) and scan-input cell. Assuming the second edge is at cell M, which should be  at the upper bound of a consecutive range of cells that  suffered compound hold-time faults. If the failing ratios in  the range between cell (M+1) and scan-input cell are not  large (in our heuristic < 90%), it does not match the  pattern of Figure 5C, and we only report a chain holdtime fault between cell (N+1) and cell N. Otherwise, we  validate whether the cells between cell (N+1) and cell M  are suffering compound hold-time faults. The validation  procedure is illustrated in Figure 7, and explained later. If  the validation passes, we conclude that the fail log  indicates compound hold-time faults caused by a slow  clock defect, and backtrace the clock signals that drive the  cells between (N+1) to M to identify the defective clocks,


as illustrated later. If the validation fails, we report a chain  hold-time fault between cell (N+1) and cell N. Note that it  is possible the first edge is the upper bound and the  second edge is the lower bound, which should be handled  in the similar way as mentioned earlier.


Figure 6. Proposed Diagnosis Flow


In Figure 7, we show a proposed flow to validate if the  cells in the middle range (from cell (N+1) to cell M) have  failing ratios that match the compound hold-time  assumption. It is based on signal probability calculation


rather than deterministic simulation since the nature of  compound hold-time makes it difficult to run simulation  based on fault models.


We first mask the sink cells from Cell 0 to Cell M to X  for each scan pattern, since these cells’ loading values  may be corrupted during the shift. For each sink cell, between cell M and cell (N+1), we find its all potential  source cells by critical path tracing. Then we calculate the  transitional signal probability of each potential source  cell, and derive the transitional signal probability of the  sink cell by assuming each source cell’s transition has the  same contribution to the sink cell. After we get the failing  probability distribution, we compare it against the  observed failing probability in the failure log. If the  correlation is large enough, (in our heuristic we set the  threshold to 80%), we think it passed the compound holdtime verification. Otherwise, it fails the verification.


Figure 7. Proposed Validation Flow


At this point, we obtained one range of scan cells per  faulty chain that suffered from compound hold-time  faults. If we assume there is one defect on the clock tree  that causes the defective chains, the defective clock must


drive all scan cells in that range. We locate this faulty  clock signal by the following four steps:


Step 1: We perform backward trace on the clock tree  from the scan cells in each range. For scan cell i, we find  a set of signals, denoted as Ci, on the clock tree, which  drives cell i. Obviously, if a node drives a scan cell, all its  driving nodes on the clock tree are the drivers of this scan  cell as well.


Step 2: Find the union of the suspect nodes on clock tree  of all cells in the same range. For a range r, we denote


this union as Ur and Ur = ∪(all cell i in range r) Ci.


Step 3: Find the intersection among all the unions  identified in the previous step. We denote this

It can be seen from the Table 2 that design B has 8% misdiagnosed cases. The average resolution is 2.28. Since  the run time is very fast (<1s) for this case, we do not  report the performance overhead in Table 2. Note the  reason for the misdiagnosed cases came from the fact that  we may not accurately diagnose > 2 faults on one chain. If  the chain diagnosis results are not accurate themselves, the clock diagnosis step will be impacted.


We also run the proposed diagnosis flow on two real  industrial cases. The design has 45M gates that were  manufactured with 28nm technology. Both cases  identified the real defective clocks. In one case, it  reported 3 suspect locations on the clock tree, and another  case has 5 suspect locations on the clock tree. The  accuracy and resolution are good for the experiments at  hand.


We take one case as example, its layout is shown in  Figure 8. The red circle highlights the defect location on  layout.


Figure 8. Case study – Layout View


Figure 9. SEM Image


Using Scanning Electronic Microscopy (SEM), PFA  engineers found a resistive open at a contact. The SEM  image is shown in Figure 9. In SEM, darker image circled  by a red circle means higher resistance than normal.


Further PFA results proved that defect existed in the  contact of the interface of a via and a metal layer. It  contributed a high resistance to clock signal so making


the clock signal slower than expected. This defective  clock is driving dozens of scan cells, and exhibit  compound hold-time behaviors.


The defective clock signal’s “voltage vs. current” curves  are illustrated in Figure 10. The X-axis is voltage, and Yaxis is current. The curve labeled “R” indicates the  normal curve, and the curve labeled “T” is the defective  clock signal’s abnormal behavior. It is clearly showed that  the defective clock has a larger resistance, which caused a  slow clock signal. The simulated cases and the real case  study proved that the proposed diagnosis flow can  identify the slow clocks, which caused compound holdtime faults that are not easily diagnosed by prior  methodologies.
