A Comprehensive Analysis Methodology for Gate Oxide Integrity Failure Using  Combined FA Techniques


Hua Younan, Nistala Ramesh Rao, Chen Shuting, Zhu Lei,


Chia Chin Ning, Zhang Jianming, Huang Yanhua and Neo Soh Ping


Material Failure Analysis, QRA Dept


GLOBALFOUNDRIES Singapore Pte Ltd


60 Woodlands Industrial Park D, Street 2, Singapore 738406   Tel: +(65) 6360-3458, E-mail: huayounan@globalfoundries.com


In this paper, a comprehensive analysis methodology for gate  oxide integrity (GOI) failure using combined FA techniques is  proposed. The current method integrates the failure analysis  flow we previously reported with a new flow proposed in this  paper. The method is applicable to a wide range of GOI failure  cases and has been used in analyzing many product wafers  with GOI failure. In particular, there is one wafer with GOI  failure that results from known failed process machines. This  wafer could be readily analyzed with this new method to  identify the root causes.


The newly proposed flow is based on our previous report on  GOI failure analysis, but the detection limit of contamination  elements was significantly improved. The enhancement of  detection limit is mainly attributable to the utilization of Vapor  Phase Decomposition and Inductively Coupled Plasma Mass  Spectrometry (VPD ICP-MS). The ICP-MS technique is  highly sensitive and capable of simultaneously measuring a  large number of elements at very low concentration level in the  range of ppb (part per billion) to ppt (part to trillion). This  enhanced sensitivity enables effective investigation of  contamination caused by specific machines. A case study of  GOI failure investigated by the proposed new method will be  discussed in detail. In the study, Al, Fe, Mo and Sn  contamination from a suspected tool were detected by ICPMS, followed by confirmation by Secondary Ion Mass  Spectrometry (SIMS) on the affected product wafers. Failurepart isolation investigations of the affected diffusion furnace  revealed that the root cause of the failure is due to a defective  gas flow valve.


Figure 6: Optical micrographs of nodules formed on CP  plates. The CP plates were contaminated by DFR  dissolved cleaner. (a) , (c) Electroplating for 20 min. (b) Electroplating for 70 min. (d) Further electroplating of  the CP plate in (a) for 50 min.


Effect of inorganic contaminants


We suggest a mechanism of whisker growth. Considering  the similarity between zeolites and the AlXSiYOZ  compounds, transition metals and noble metals may be  immobilized in the inter-lamellar spaces or the small gap  between thin fibers of the AlXSiYOz materials. These  kinds of metal immobilized AlXSiYOz compounds are  expected to have the same shape selectivity as metal  immobilized zeolites. It is well known that metal  immobilized zeolites show catalytic properties because  of the shape selectivity originating from their porous  structure [7, 8]. Therefore, the whisker formation by  AlXSiYOZ compounds can be explained by catalytic  reaction.  Cu ions can be trapped in the pores of the  AlXSiYOZ compounds, which induce whisker growth  during Cu electroplating.  Then current density is  concentrated in the precipitate at the beginning stage of  plating.  It then grows to form whisker as depicted in  Fig. 10.


If the porous structure of the AlXSiYOZ compounds is  broken as a Cu whisker grows, it may be very difficult to  observe the AlXSiYOZ compounds in the whisker.


Figure 10:  Step 1: H+ ion is surrounded AlO4- to keep  the electroneutrality.  Step2: Cu2+ ion concentrates  replacing the H+ from Cu2+.  Step3: Cu2+ accepts the  electron at the surface of the CP plates, forming Cu  precipitate [9].


We investigated the factors that can affect the growth of  Cu whiskers; decomposition of the additives in Cu  electroplating solution, surface stress, acidic cleaner, and  the components of a dry film. We found that the  components of a dry film and impurities are critical in  Cu whisker formation and also proposed a mechanism  for its growth.  It was also shown that AlXSiYOZ  compounds perfectly reproduced Cu whisker growth.

Comprehensive nano-structural approach of SSRM nanocontact on silicon  through TEM-STEM study


B. Domengès


LAMIPS, CRISMAT – NXP semiconductors - Presto-Engineering laboratory, CNRS-UMR6508, ENSICAEN, UCBN, Presto-Engineering Europe, 2 rue de la Girafe, 14000 Caen, France


bernadette.domenges@ensicaen.fr


T. Delaroque, K. Danilo, A. Colder


Presto-Engineering Europe, 2 rue de la Girafe, 14000 Caen, France


[4]. An explanation of the observed nanometric resolution of the  SSRM technique is that the -tin pocket created under the probe  below the surface, can be considered as a virtual SSRM probe, which size is about 3 times smaller than the actual probe radius  for tips varying from 5 to 50nm [5, 6]. The Si(II) phase is  metastable and stabilize after unloading of the nanoprobe as an  amorphous phase. Whereas the structure of tips was  characterized even at a nano-scale, that of sample surface was  only studied after micro-indentation experiments [7, 8, 9]. In  order to further understand the influence of SSRM measurement  parameters, it appeared interesting to characterize through  Transmission Electron Microscopy (TEM) study the nanostructure of sample surface after SSRM measurements. We  performed several experiments, playing on the scanning  parameters, that is, either the load on plain scanning windows or  the distance between scanning lines.


Experimental


AFM measurements


In order to evaluate our experimental procedure for testing the  influence of the load on the electrical measurements, we first  obtained calibration curves, based on Clarysse et al. methodology [4]. Our calibration samples were built up from the  stacking of several 800 nm-thick epitaxial layers exhibiting  different doping. We chose to increment load value during a  single scan.


Then, we studied a boron doped silicon (111) substrate (1.37×1018 at.cm-3), scanning as well its surface as a crosssection but also played on the geometry of the scanning. For the  first trials, we used a constant load, high enough to measure a  significant SSRM signal, and scanned square areas following a  zigzag shape as shown in Scanning Electron Microscopy image (Fig. 1a). Though the use of a rather high load and a tilt of  sample for SEM observations, it appears very difficult to  distinguish the trace of the probe. Also, the bright speckles on  the image are related to material residues due to the high contact  mode of SSRM measurements. A second series of experiments  were performed in a similar way to the measurements of the  calibration sample, that is, we chose to increment the load in a  single scan, in several steps (Fig. 1b).


b


Figure 1: a) SEM image of a zigzag scan ; b) topographic  AFM image of a four-step scan, load is increased from B to E, note accumulation of material for higher loads on the side of  the scanned area.


Topographic and electrical measurements were performed with a  Digital Instruments Dimension 3000 equipped with a SSRM  module. The surface state of the sample being crucial for  reliability of SSRM measurements [10], cross-sections were  classically grinded and polished in order to get a smooth area (Rq<1.3nm). Ohmic back-contact was realized through a  gold/palladium metal deposition prior gluing the back-face of  the samples with conductive silver paint on an AFM homemade  holder.


SSRM measurements were performed using contact highresonance frequency silicon AFM probes (NCHR-Nanoworld), characterized by 125 μm-long - 30 μm wide - 42 N/m-averaged  spring constant cantilevers. Tips are made of antimony-doped  silicon, coated with 100 nm of boron-doped diamond, exhibit  anisotropic geometry and their height is in the range 10 to 15 μm, with a radius of curvature between 35 nm and 50 nm.


Tip shape control


The shape of the SSRM tip was analyzed using a calibration  sample TGT1 from NT-MDT®, that is composed of an array of  very sharp Si tips (about 700nm high). Topographically scanning  of TGT1 with the SSRM tip returns 3-D visualization of its top  surface. Moreover, using a deconvolution program “Deconvo2” (NT-MDT), it allows the quantification of the tip sharpness  parameters (radius of curvature), tip degradation and  contamination control. Besides Scanning Electron Microscopy  observations were performed in a XL-40 FEG scanning electron  microscope.


TEM study


Because of the size of SSRM scanned areas, the TEM lamellae  were FIB prepared, in a single beam FEI 200XP system (30kV  Ga+ ion beam and Pt deposition chemistry) adapted to lift-out in  situ preparation technique [11, 12, 13]. In order to avoid Ga+ irradiation damages, the sample surface was protected with an  evaporated thin Au-Pd layer (at least five tens of nanometers, otherwise the subsequent procedures for thin lamellae  preparation generates irradiation damages at the surface of the  substrate). The common protective Pt line was deposited in the  FIB but in two steps : large area deposit (20 μm x 20 μm x 0.2 μm) using very fast scanning conditions and slowly increasing  the ion beam current from 4 pA to 350 pA ; 2 μm thick  protective line with 350 pA ion beam current. Whatever the


condition of SSRM scanning was, the orientation of the TEM  lamella was always chosen perpendicular to the direction of  scanning (dashed arrow on fig 1b). Also, the TEM lamella was  always long enough to contain an un-scanned area in order to be  able to check that the FIB preparation had not introduced any  artifact.


TEM observations were performed in a JEM 2010 TEM-STEMFEG, operating at 200kV, equipped with a high-resolution  objective lens (Cs = 1 mm), JEOL High-Angle-Aperture-DarkField detector, EDS PHOENIX system, and tilting-rotating  sample holder allowing the orientation of layer interfaces toward  the electron beam.


In commercial PWB fabrication, there are many sources  of surface contamination from process equipment and  environment.  Among many processes, the hot air dry  process is the most suspect step in terms of  contamination that may cause the growth of Cu whiskers.  This is because the hot air dry process can contaminate  the surface of the CP plate before electroplating of Cu.  The purpose of the hot air dry is to remove any humidity  from the surface of a PWB panel after the DFR  developing process.


We inspected the hot air dryer in the production line and  the most suspicious part was an insulating material in a  pipe through which hot air passes.  The insulating  material was a composite of inorganic fibers in a matrix  of silicone.  We found that some fragments from the  insulating material could be easily transferred to the  PWB surface.  Figs. 7(a) and (b) show the fragments  and their compositions.  The fragment consisted of Al, Si, and O. Possible candidates for this kind of AlXSiYOZ  compounds are asbestos, mica, and zeolite.  When we  applied these fragments on the CP plate, Cu whiskers  grew as shown in Fig. 7 (c).


Figure 7: (a) EDS image of fragments from the  insulating material in a hot air dry pipe. (b) EDS results  obtained from the rectangle marked in (a). (c) Optical  micrograph of a whisker reproduced by the insulating  material of pipe. (d) EDS image of fragments from the  home hair dryer. (e) EDS results obtained from the  rectangle marked in (d). (f) Optical micrograph of  whisker reproduced by the insulating material of the  hair-dryer.


Interestingly, Cu whiskers grew when a CP plate was  dried using a home hair-dryer then electroplated as  shown in Figure 7(f).  We could also find an insulating  material in the hair-dryer and the composition was the  same as that of inorganic fragmentation in the hot air dry  process in the production line as shown in Figures 7(d) and (e).  Since we could collect enough samples from  the hair-dryer, we performed crystal structure analysis  using an XRD as shown in Figure 7. The insulating  material (AlXSiYOZ) was identified as a kind of zeolite  which had a preferred orientation of (00L).


Figure 8. X-ray diffraction pattern of AlXSiYOZ, insulating material in a hair-dryer.  The pink lines are  peaks based on JCPDS 44-0696 (Silicalite). (XRD, 2 = 3-60°, scan speed 2°/min, step size 0.01°, 40 kV, 300  mA.)


We tried to determine whether there were AlxSiyOz  coupounds at the root of Cu whiskers but, we could not  find any foreign material at the boundary of the whisker  and the electroplated Cu as shown in Fig. 9(b).


Figure 9: (a) SEM micrograph of a whisker reproduced  by AlXSiYOZ (from a hair dryer) in CP plates. (b) FIB  micrograph obtained from the line marked in (a). The  upper part of Cu with fine grains is the whisker. The  lower part is electroplated Cu.


Scanning Spreading Resistance Microscopy electro-mechanical  nanocontacts are nowadays well understood and numerous  influent parameters have been identified (Bias, load, surface  state of the sample, radius of curvature of the tip). Despite  several simulation and modelization possibilities, calibration  curves are required to ensure reliable electrical characterizations. In this paper, we bring, through nano-structural studies (Scanning Electron Microscopy, Transmission Electron  Microscopy) of surface state of both SSRM tips and doped  silicon surface a new understanding of tip-sample interaction  during SSRM measurements. As a result of load, a nanometric  residual amorphous silicon layer was observed which thickness  depends on applied force and might be due to as well the plastic  transformation (Si to -tin phase) as plough-effect residues  resulting from the tip indentation into the sample. It appears thus  in a failure analysis process to find the best compromise between  stable electrical SSRM response and sample/tip surface  degradation.


As the downscaling in semiconductor industry continues, the  need for analysis technique capable of measuring the spatial and  electrical carrier profiles at a nanoscale becomes crucial. Among  electrical modes of Atomic Force Microscopy (AFM), Scanning  Spreading Resistance Microscopy (SSRM) appears a powerful  tool for such characterization, ensuring high spatial resolution as  well as high sensitivity and large dynamic range [1]. Physics of  SSRM have been widely studied, leading to a better  understanding of the electrical and mechanical properties of  SSRM nanocontacts [2]. To ensure stable electrical signals, high  contact pressure between the nanoprobe and the sample are  necessary, inducing on silicon substrates elasto-plastic or even  fully plastic deformation leading to the so-called -tin Si(II) phase, which is metallic. The Schottky-like nanocontact nature


[3], the Surface States at the surface and the probe resistance, all  influent parameters on spreading resistance measurements, make  quantification more reliable when based on calibration curves


Results


The obtained I/V curves [14] agree well with those of the  literature [10, 15]. In the same way, calibration curves of fig. 2  show that trying to fit the resistance dependence on resistivity  with a Maxwell-based formula, which should follow a linear  dependence, is not satisfying for p-doped silicon or returns a  contact radius of 6nm for n-doped sample, much smaller than  that expected from the tip characteristics. Indeed, the radius of  curvature of the SSRM tip was evaluated close to 40 nm and  evolves during SSRM measurements (Fig. 3). AFM  reconstructed images (through deconvolution) as well as SEM  ones give evidence of the strong erosion of the summit of the  SSRM tip. Indeed, the latter exhibits, as new, an almost  spherical shape, and becomes strongly eroded with a badly  flattened summit after several SSRM measurements.


Since, it has been shown that erosion of that type of tips leaves  individual diamond grains at the top surface and most of all, one  should distinguish contact radius and electrical radius [2, 4]. Indeed, the effective radius of nanocontact is directly related to  the volume of the -tin silicon pocket under the sample surface  because of load. Though this silicon phase transformation is  metastable, the high values of load do introduce irreversible  indentation of the sample surface, as well as most probably


Figure 2: Calibration curves of p-doped (Vbias=600mV) and  n-doped (Vbias=-500mV) samples for four different loads. Dotted lines correspond to ideal Maxwell based R=f() for two  tip Radius.


material residues (see Fig. 1). Indentation was observed to  increase with load, for instance in the sample of fig. 1b, it was  measured ranging from almost negligible to 5 nm (at 8.7μN  load), and to 10 nm for the last step (10.9μN load). But it  appeared to vary from one sample to the other and might also  depend on the orientation of the sample. That is whether load is  applied on the surface or on a cross-section, corresponding thus  to different orientation of the silicon framework. Besides, surface roughness was also evaluated and shown to be most  often stable or to slightly increase with load before stabilizing to  value as low as 10nm (RMS). TEM measurements were then  performed in an attempt to characterize the nanostructural  modification of sample surface under different load.


TEM observations


The first observations confirmed how important it was, to check  that the FIB preparation of the TEM lamella had not introduced  any artifact on the surface of the sample. Indeed, the main result  is the presence of an amorphous layer (Fig. 4). Such an  amorphization could as well be due to Ga+ irradiation damages.


The High Resolution Transmission Electron Microscopy (HRTEM) image of the surface of a scanned area with a 4.4μN


load, shows at the interface between Au-Pd protective layer and  silicon substrate, a light grey layer, which contrast is typical of  amorphous material. It is about ten nanometers thick. In  between, a slightly brighter layer can be distinguished (small  blue-grey arrow). It is about 1 to 2 nm thick. Also, the surface of  silicon substrate appears quite corrugated with darker contrast, probably related to strain phenomena (empty arrow in fig. 4).


The TEM nanostructural characterization of the surface state was  performed for each SSRM experiment on n-doped (111) silicon  substrate. Those correspond to:


1. Line scanning on the Si surface, as presented in fig. 1, with a


constant load of 11.4μN and lines spaced of 1μm (Fig. 5).


2. Scanning area with load steps applied on a cross section, i.e.


load parallel to <11-2>Si (Fig. 6).


3. Scanning area with load steps applied on the surface, i.e. load


parallel to <111>Si (Fig. 7).


On all images, the big plain arrows point the amorphous layer  due to heavy contact SSRM scan, the empty arrows point the  strained silicon substrate surface and the small plain arrows  point the thin amorphous layer identified as silicon native oxide.


The interpretation of the effect of applied load with line  scanning is quite difficult though interesting (Fig. 5). Indeed, the  amorphous layer exhibits an almost round shape, as if imaging  the trace of the probe, with an apparent radius of 30nm. But the


Figure 3: Images of new (resp. used) SSRM tips : a) (resp. b) AFM topographic TGT1; c) (resp. d) deconvoluted images  return the true shape of the tip; e) (resp. f) SEM images. Note the strong erosion of the tip summit.


Figure 4: HRTEM showing an amorphous layer (plain arrow)


at the surface of a scanned area with 4.4μN load; strain at the


corrugated surface are observed (empty arrow).


Figure 5: Medium magnification TEM image of a line scanning  load.


lines should be 1μm spaced and not as close as they appear. An  explanation might probably due to the fact that the control of the  path of the probe on our AFM system is not accurate enough for  such experiment, that is single line scan, and would require a  better control of the reaction loop. Area scanning is better  adapted to our system.


Figure 6 shows TEM images of the sample surface for different  loads applied to a cross-section. Confirming that our procedure  for preparing the TEM lamellae does not induce any artifact, the  unscanned area exhibits a very thin layer (almost not  distinguishable) at the interface between Au-Pd protective layer  and silicon substrate, typical of silicon native oxide. Besides, the  scanned areas are characterized by an interface layer which light  grey contrast is typical of amorphous material, above the Si  substrate. The latter exhibits a corrugated surface and appears  with a darker contrast typical of strains (empty arrows).


Figure 6: Medium magnification TEM images of scanned area  with different load a) End (10μN), b) 10μN, c). 5μN (applied  load parallel to <11-2> silicon axis).


Though measurements were made difficult by the surface state  of the sample, the increase of the indentation depth with load  was confirmed, from negligible to about 8.5 nm for each of the


last two steps (7.5 and 10μN). The thickness of the amorphous  layer also evolves with the load. In the case of studied crosssection, the lower the load, the thicker the amorphous layer and  the more corrugated the silicon substrate surface underneath, imaging-like a deformed replica of the tip summit.


When the applied load is parallel to <111> silicon axis, the  thickness of the amorphous layer increases with it (Fig. 7). This  different behavior may be explained by the fact that, in that case, the applied load is perpendicular to the easy plane of shearing in  silicon. These experiments should be confirmed by new  observations. In the same way, some new experiments of applied  load on (001) silicon substrate are under progress.


Complementary EM characterization were performed to precise  the nano-structure of the amorphous layer, such as nanodiffraction to confirm the amorphous state of this layer, EDS for  elemental composition and HAADF STEM imaging.


Figure 7: Medium magnification TEM images of scanned area  with different load a) End 10.9μN, b) 8.7 μN, c) 4.4 μN (applied load parallel to <111> silicon axis).


Figure 8 gives an example of HAADF images of a scanned  sample with applied load parallel to <11-2> silicon axis. The  amorphous layer exhibits a slightly darker contrast than Si  substrate suggesting a lower mean Z atomic number or a less  dense material. The latter interpretation was confirmed by XEDS complementary analyses, which showed that this  amorphous layer mainly contains silicon and generates a lower  count X-ray signal. Thus, this layer should definitely be  distinguished from 1-2nm thick native oxide.


Figure 8: TEM and corresponding HAADF-STEM images of  a 5μN load area. Note the darker contrast of the interface  layer of silicon substrate on HAADF image.


Discussion-Conclusion


Though these experiments cannot be directly compared to nanoindentation ones [7-9], where indenter stays at the same point, it  appears in a similar way that a consequence of the load is the  amorphization of silicon. The morphology and thickness of the  formed amorphous layer depends on the load and on the  orientation of the applied load with respect to silicon framework. Some further experiments are under progress to better  understand this phenomenon. Furthermore, as the tip scan the  surface, increasing the load will cause this amorphous layer to be  pushed away perpendicularly to the tip movement, in a sort of  plough-effect, to the borders of the scanned area. Indeed, SEM  and topographical AFM images showed such a material  accumulation, and especially for higher load (arrow on Fig. 1).


In order to get a stable SSRM response, it is necessary to apply  high load, inducing tip and sample erosion. As a consequence, local amorphization of sample is observed, leaving a wavy  aspect of the surface of crystalline silicon bulk underneath. Both  phenomena change its electrical behavior permanently. Indeed, applying a second scanning on the same area showed a clear  increase of local resistivity [14]. Besides the complementary  view this study brings to modelization works, these  nanostructural modifications should be kept in mind when  preparing SSRM measurements parameters.

[9]  Le Bourhis E. and Patriarche G., "TEM-nanoindentation  studies of semiconducting structures", Micron 38 (2007) pp  377-389.


[10]  Eyben P., Denis S., Clarysse T. and Vandervorst W., "Progress towards a physical contact model for scanning  spreading resistance microscopy", Mater. Sci. Eng. B  102 (2003), pp 132-137.

Highly Automated Transmission Electron Microscopy Tomography for Defect  Understanding


James J. Demarest & Hong-Ying Zhai


IBM, Albany, NY USA


Imaging tomography by transmission electron microscopy (TEM) is a technique which has been growing in popularity in  recent years, yet it has not been widely applied to  semiconductor defect studies and root cause determination [1-3]. In part this is due to the complex equipment, computing  needs, and microscope time required to generate the various  images which ultimately compose the data set. However, the  latest generation of TEMs—with their high level of stability  and automation—are greatly reducing the resource needs to  create high quality and informative movies of defects rotating  about a central axis. One significant advance is the reduction  in time required to fabricate a sample and perform the data  acquisition by TEM. Today’s microscopes allow for sample  fabrication to take place in a few hours or less and can acquire  more than 100 images in about an hour at different sample tilt  conditions with minimal analyst intervention.


This paper describes using automated TEM sample  preparation with dual beam focused ion beams (previously  reported [4]) in conjunction with automated tomography  software on a state-of-the-art TEM. By using an advanced  tomography holder ±70° of tilt can be obtained. This is a  powerful way to view defects as the failure can be viewed  through more than 90° of rotation. Consequently a more  complete understanding of the failure site can be obtained over  a typical single projection TEM image. This can greatly  facilitate root cause determination in a timely manner.


Discussion: Sample Preparation


Sample preparation is fundamentally important to any failure  analysis technique. For TEM tomography, a number of critical  items need to be considered. The failure site itself cannot be  disturbed by the sample preparation process. The sample needs  to be electron transparent even at high tilt angles and the  sample cannot be so thin that the advantage of performing  tomography is eliminated. This limits the sample thickness to  about 200nm or less. Fortunately small defect (or feature) size  works to the analyst’s advantage for imaging tomography. Semiconductor technology now has metal interconnects which  are 50nm in diameter and smaller. Three dimensional devices  like finFETs and conventional planar gate stacks are around  20nm in width and only slightly larger in height when looking  at them in cross section. As a result the entire structure (or


device) of interest can be captured in a conventional TEM  sample on the order of 100nm thick. Normally the sample  would only be viewed when aligned along the crystallographic  zone axis to minimize projection effects and to allow for  critical dimension measurements. What if we could routinely  tilt the sample through 140°, take an image every degree or  less and then stitch all the two dimensional images together to  render a movie? This kind of data might enable greater insight  into the root cause of the defect leading to improved process  fixes, better yield, and device performance.


An automated TEM sample fabrication process was reported  previously at ISTFA 2009 [1]. A version of this technique was  used for the TEM sample preparation discussed in this paper. The major difference is the automated process discussed  previously was for ex-situ sample extraction while the process  here is for in-situ preparation.


The basic process flow is the same. A wafer is manufactured  and inspected. At the time of inspection (visual or electrical) a  determination is made to obtain TEM analysis. The wafer then  receives an encapsulation layer to protect the region of interest  during the sample fabrication process. As the wafer will no  longer continue through the fabrication process it is scrapped  from the line and submitted for analysis (Fig. 1).


Figure 1: Flow chart of wafer submission for STEM analysis  at ANT. Overview of entire sequence (left) and detailed  automated script process (right).


Sample preparation then occurs at the region of interest. The  first steps in this process are the creation of fiducial marks, the  deposition of a Platinum (Pt) protection layer, and toblerone  fabrication (Fig. 2). This process is automated in a script and  takes about 12 minutes to complete.


Figure 2: “X” fiducial marks and Pt protection layer on  extracted toblerone containing the region of interest for  analysis.


Next the toblerone is mounted to a three finger grid within the  dual beam focused ion beam (DBFIB). A second automated  script is run to thin the sample down to electron transparency (Fig. 3).


Figure 3: mounted and thinned toblerone ready for TEM  analysis.


The final thickness of the sample can be controlled based upon  the design of the script. Three principle thicknesses have been  developed: 50nm, 100nm, and 200nm. This allows the sample


thickness to be adjusted as appropriate to the size of the defect. The second script takes approximately 20 minutes to run. The  total time to manufacture a sample through this method ranges  from 90 to 120 minutes. This time is greatly governed by the  skill of the operator manipulating the sample with the  mechanized transfer needle.


Discussion: Tomography


Imaging tomography was performed on a state-of-the-art  TEM. An advanced tomography holder was used which allows  for ±70° of rotation about a central axis. During the  experiment it was found that ±50° of rotation was more than  adequate to fully render the observed defects. The critical  visualization issue is the inclusion of at least 90° of tilt.


Normally failures are imaged along a crystallographic zone  axis. The crystallographic zone axis is typically of the <100> for Silicon. This serves to minimize projection effects of the  fabricated structure as patterned structures are constructed  along these directions as well. Failures, however, do not  necessarily follow these rules and often have a three  dimensional quality which needs to be understood by the  analyst to be able to appropriately determine root cause (Fig. 4). The circled area in Fig. 4 shows high density metallic  particles located between two nitride layers. This is the feature  of interest over the next few figures (Fig. 4-6).


Figure 4: Bright field TEM image of metallic defects (circled  area) in a test structure taken near a crystallographic zone  axis for Si.


Initially the defect appears to consist of three discrete  particles. As the sample is tilted off the zone axis position to  about 10°, five separate particles can be observed (Fig. 5).


Figure 5: Bright field TEM image of metallic defects (circled  area) in a test structure taken near about 10° off a  crystallographic zone axis for Si.


At 20° tilt from the zone axis, seven different metallic particles  can be seen in the image (Fig. 6). The majority of the particles  lie along the same location within the sample


Figure 6: Bright field TEM image of metallic defects (circled  area) in a test structure taken near about 20° off a  crystallographic zone axis for Si.


101 separate images were acquired on the region of interest  shown in Fig. 4-6 at 1° tilt steps. These images were then  automatically aligned with respect to each other and used to


form a 6 second movie. Data acquisition of the 101 images  took about one hour and was entirely automated.


Other detectors in the microscope can be used as well. In  addition to the BF TEM images, high angle annular dark field (HAADF) TEM images were also generated. As before 101  images taken at 1° increments were obtained and automatically  joined to create a movie.


Fig. 7 shows a similar test structure to Figs. 4-6. The region of  interest in Figs. 7-9 is the metallic interconnect which  electrically connects the first metal level to the active Si. The  circled area in Fig. 7 indicates a void in the metallic  interconnect.


Figure 7: HAADF TEM image of metallic interconnect void (circled area) in a test structure taken near the  crystallographic zone axis of Si.


As the sample is rotated about a central axis, the extent of  voiding becomes apparent. In Fig. 8 additional voids can be  observed in the interconnects on the right side of the image.


Figure 8: HAADF TEM image of metallic interconnect void (circled area) in a test structure taken near about 10° off the  crystallographic zone axis of Si.


When larger tilt angles are achieved it can be observed that  there are four interconnects contained within the sample  volume (Fig. 9). The partial vias “in front” the first two in the  plane of projection were obscuring the degree of voiding  within the interconnect contained completely within the  lamella.


Figure 9: HAADF TEM image of metallic interconnect void (circled area) in a test structure taken near about 20° off the  crystallographic zone axis of Si.


The 101 images obtained automatically for the HAADF  imaging condition were also combined together to create a 6  second movie. The acquisition time for the HAADF images  are significantly longer (one the order of 30-45 seconds each) than obtaining BF TEM images. This increases the required  microscopy time to about two and a half hours.


With sample preparation taking about two hours and the bright  field TEM microscopy time taking approximately two hours, a  tomography movie can be generated in three to four hours  from wafer to finished file. HAADF tomography movies can  be generated with an additional hour or two of time. This is  predominately due to the additional acquisition time required  by the HAADF images. The time savings achieved through  this highly automated process greatly reduce the time  investment of the analyst who previously had to align and  stitch all the image files together one at a time. As a result, it is  now feasible to perform imaging tomography on key failures  in a manufacturing environment to enable root cause  determination of defects and failures in less that a day after the  defect has been localized.


[1] P. A. Ercius. L. Gignac, C. K. Hu, D. A. Muller, “ThreeDimensional Measurement of Line Edge Roughness in  Copper Wires Using Electron Tomography,” Microscopy  and Microanalysis, vol. 15, 2009, pp. 244-250


[2] H. Bender, O. Richard, A. Kalio, E. Sourty, “3D-analysis  of Semiconductor Structures by Electron Tomography,” Microelectronic Engineering, Volume 84, Issue 11, Materials for Advanced Metallization 2007, 16th  European Workshop on Materials for Advanced  Metallization 2007, November 2007, pp. 2707-2713


[3] Ercius, P., Weyland, M., Muller, D.A. & Gignac, L.M. ~2006. Three-dimensional imaging of nanovoids in  copper interconnects using incoherent bright field  tomography. Appl Phys Lett. 88, 243116.


[4] J. Demarest and Z. DeSouza. “Start Up and Evolution of a  FEI DA 300 DBFIB for Automatic STEM Sample  Preparation,” Proc 33rd International Symposium for  Testing and Failure Analysis, San Jose, CA, November  2009, pp. 334-338


Understanding the 3D Structure by 2D Imaging Technique


Yu Zhu, Jemima Gonsalves


IBM Thomas J Watson Research Center, Yorktown Heights, NY, USA  yuzhu@us.ibm.com


For 22nm technology node and beyond, fully depleted devices  such as FinFET and ETSOI are leading candidates. Certain  critical dimensions of such devices are well below 10nm, and  only transmission electron microscopy (TEM) has the  resolution to provide measurement with sub-nanometer  accuracy. Due to the projection effect of TEM technique, comprehensive understanding of the 3D structure from 2D  images is needed for process development of FinFET. This  paper will address sample preparations and TEM imaging  techniques for FinFET device at sub-100nm pitch.


Future technology nodes require scaling gate lengths to 25 nm  and below [1].  Fully-depleted devices such as FinFET and  trigate [2] are leading candidates to enable such gate-length  scaling and density scaling. The transmission electron  microscope (TEM) can provide the resolution to characterize  structures at sub-nanometer scale, but it only provides images  in 2D due to the projection effect. However, FinFET is a 3D  structure, so 2D imaging techniques need to be optimized for  characterizing FinFET. This paper will address the challenges  with using the TEM to characterize the 3D FinFET structure. FinFET structures characterized in this paper are fabricated at  IBM Albany Nanotech [3].  There are several processing  challenges during FinFET integrations require accurate  structural analysis. For instance, the dimensions of the Fin are  30nm tall and around 10nm wide at 40nm pitch, so only TEM  has the resolution to reveal the accurate size of a single Fin at  this scale. Gate patterning is the next module for FinFET, the  step coverage of the HKMG gate over the Fin, the gate etch  profile, the removal of residual HKMG from the Fin sidewall  at between the gate region, all requires TEM analysis. For  22nm technology, the gate pitch (CPP) is below 100nm [4], which post additional difficulties for TEM sample preparation  and analysis. Another process challenge is to form a silicon  nitride spacer on gate sidewall, but remove the spacer on the  Fin sidewall. This requires a precise slice of TEM sample  taken from between the gate at less than 100nm pitch in the


across-Fin direction. In addition, conventional doping  techniques are not capable of forming uniform junctions on  the three dimensional fin structures. The epitaxial films were  used for conformal doping of the devices, reducing the  resistance and improving the performance of the device  significantly [5]. TEM is a powerful technique to analysis the  crystalline quality of the epitaxial layers. In summary, TEM  analysis is critical for most of the major integration processing  steps for FinFET. It require technique development


The TEM used in this study is a JEOL 3000F operated at  300kV. The TEM samples were prepared using focused ion  beam tool (FEI HELIOS 4000 dual beam FIB).


Fin cross-section measurement


The profile of the Fin is critical for charge transfer as well as  for process integration. A perfect straight Fin sidewall will  help the charge transfer along the perpendicular surface. A  slightly tapered Fin profile will help the process integration, in  terms of HKMG and spacer removal in following process  steps. In order to accurately measure the profile, the Fin needs  to be edge on with the electron beam in the TEM. SOI wafers  are used to start the Fin formation process. Due to the  misalignment of the SOI with the bulk Si of the wafer, and the  bending of the TEM sample, the Si substrate can not be used  to accurately align the sample for TEM imaging. Convergent  beam diffraction pattern (CBDF) on the Fin (10x30nm) is  needed to obtain an edge on condition for imaging. Figure 1  shows an example HRTEM image of a Fin cross-section and  gate stack taken after CBDF alignment. Fin width is around  10nm, and height is 30nm. HK is conformal around the Fin, and MG deposition is not conformal. Also, the hard mask on  top of the Fin is intact. Fin dimensions, HKMG thickness and  profile can be accurately measured. The oxide hard mask will  be consumed during the later gate patterning and spacer etch, and completed before the epitaxial merge of the Fins.


Gate patterning of FinFET


Patterning the gate on the Fin is one of the biggest challenges  for FinFET fabrication. After the Fin formation, HKMG stack  and Si are deposited over the Fin. The gate stack will wrap


around the Fin from the top and the sides. Then, the gate stack  at source and drain region is removed by reactive ion etch (RIE). To complete remove the a-Si, HKMG on the Fin


Figure 1: TEM of a Fin cross-section with HKMG and gate  wraps around


sidewall by etch without damage the Fin requires a lot of  development work. It is a 3D nanoscale structure and it is hard   to characterize the sidewall of the Fin in the TEM after the  gate patterning because of the projection artifact. It is more  difficult when the gate pitch is less than 100nm. TEM samples  prepared from all three directions, i.e. across the Fin, across  the gate, and parallel to the wafer surface, are needed to reveal  the details of the FinFET. Figure 2a shows the TEM image of  a sample made from the across-gate direction to show the gate  profile above the Fin. The wafer was further coated with  silicon nitride spacer and amorphous silicon as FIB sample  preparation protection layer. Figure 2a shows 4 gate at 100nm  pitch, from top to bottom are: poly silicon; HKMG stack; projection of Si/HKMG on Fin sidewall; and the HKMG stack  at bottom of on recessed BOX (circle). Around the gate is the  SiN spacer. The Fin is the horizontal band across the image. It  clear shows the recess of Fin in between the gate during the  gate (arrow). However, the gate profile on the Fin sidewall  was not clear and the Fin profile was not shown. Also, this


image doesn’t reveal information about if there is any HKMG  stack residual left on Fin sidewall.


Figure 2b shows the TEM image of a sample made from the  across-fin direction (90 degree from the x-gate direction) of  the same structure shown in Fig. 2a. In order to show the Fin  cross-section at the source/drain region without the gate  projection effect, the TEM sample needs to be thinner than  70nm. To ensure the TEM sample includes the region of S/D  only, sample was prepared with a slight tilted angle away from  the gate direction. Because of the tight pitch, the projection of  the gate wrapping around the Fin is seen in most of the area of  the TEM sample. The middle few Fins in the image, without  the projection, are the region of the Fin that is in between the  gates, since there is no HKMG wrapping around. Still there is  a dark feature on the sidewall of the Fin. These dark features  are residual HKMG on the Fin sidewall at source/drain region. Also, Fig 2b shows the Fin recess at S/D region (arrow), which agrees with the TEM images from another angle.


Last, a plane-view TEM sample was prepared in FIB/SEM  dual beam system to review the HKMG profile on the  sidewall of the fin (Fig 2c). TEM sample needs to be thinned  from both top and bottom, which require precisely remove  part of the gate and remove most of the BOX. Conventional  polishing approach doesn’t have the accuracy for this. To  make the plane-view sample, a large chunk of material was  cut and released from the sample surface by in-situ lift out. Then, the sample was attached to the sample grid on a flip  stage at 90 degree with respect to the surface in DB-FIB. The  grid was then placed on the standard bulk stage in DB-FIB, which will allow SEM on both surfaces during the FIB  thinning. Figure 2C shows TEM image taken from this plan  view sample. The sample is aligned so that the Fin shows a  dark diffraction contrast, and poly gate is the white band from  bottom left


Figure 2a: TEM images of the FinFET from across gate  direction


Figure 2b: TEM images of the FinFET from across Fin  direction


corner to top left. It clearly shows the HK residual (The dark  line pointed by an arrow) on the Fin sidewall outside the gate; the poly-Si gate is narrower than the HKMG; and the  uniformity of the Fin needs to be improved.


Figure 2c: TEM sample made parallel to the wafer surface of  the same sample from Fig. 2a and 2b.


Though the bright-field TEM from three different directions  shows the structure, still the gate profile on the Fin sidewall is  not clear. High angle annual dark field scanning transmission  electron microscopy (HAADF-STEM) has been used to  determine the HKMG profile. Figure 3a shows the STEM  image from the same TEM sample as shown in Fig 2. Z  contrast clearly shows the HKMG profile as well as the  residual on outside the gate (arrow) To distinguish the high-k  material from the metal gate material in the DF-STEM, EDX  maps were obtained which show residual material on the Fin  sidewall at the S/D region are mainly metal gates. As shown  in Fig 3b, the map was taken from the red square in Fig 3a. the


x-ray signal from the metal component of HK and MG was  extracted and composed into a 2D map. HAADF-STEM  image from the across-gate direction is more efficient  technique to assess the HKMG residual than TEM and crosssection from the other 2 angle, and it less constrained by the  gate pitch.


Figure 3a: HAADF-STEM image from Fig 2a shows the  HKMG profile on the fin sidewall


Figure 3b: EDX map of Hf and Ti shows the gate profile and  residual on fin is mainly HK


Spacer and epitaxial of FinFET


For 22nm node and beyond, the gate pitch is100 nm or  smaller, which means the feature of interest approaches the  physical limit of a TEM sample. By cutting the TEM sample  with a slight tilt, it increases the chances that the TEM sample  contains some area of the fin at source/drain region. In Fig 2b, the fins on both ends has some HKMG wrap around the Fin, this is due to the projection effect from the gate. In the middle  of the image, the Fins don’t have any HKMG wrap around, suggesting that this is the region of S/D without projection  effect from the gate. This is especially critical for asses the Fin  hard mask loss after etch, process development of the spacer  etch and epitaxial merge for FinFET.


Recently, we demonstrated TEM analysis of FinFET at 70nm  gate pitch after epitaxial merge of Fins as shown in Fig 4.


Figure 4: TEM image of sample made from 70nm cpp FinFET  from across-Fin direction


The image on top is TEM taken at a lower magnification, showing the gate stack wrap around the Fin at both sides and  no gate projection effect at the middle 4~5 Fins. This confirms  the sample is made from between the gate. The high  magnification image in the bottom shows the epitaxial layer is  almost crystalline defect free. The image also shows the  residual silicon nitride spacer on Fin sidewall (arrow pointing) blocked the epitaxial growth from the bottom half of the Fin. All of these information provide insight for device process  and integration development.


Fully integrated FinFET device


Once we understand the structure of the FinFET and how to  interpret the TEM images from different angles from each  process development step, detailed physical failure analysis of  FinFET is possible. Figure 4 shows two devices from the  same wafer, which have very different ON resistance as well  as current according to the data from electric test. Device  shown in Fig. 5a has about half of the ON resistance than the  device from Fig. 5b. TEM shows the Ron difference is most  likely due to the poor contact between CA to silicide, which is  caused by the un-merged epitaxial of the Fin.  The arrow is  pointing at a region, where the epi merge was no complete, and the contact etch punch though the epi and silicide layer  and causing the poor contact.


Figure 5: FinFET devices from x-fin direction on 2 device  which has different Ron


Advanced TEM analytical technique such as holographic


interferometry or nano-beam diffraction can be used to  measure the strain of Fin along certain directions. However, TEM is a destructive technique. FinFET manufacture requires  non-destructive metrology such as x-ray scattering, optical  scatterometry, CD-SEM, AFM. Most of these techniques are  based on modeling/fitting. TEM can provide the direct and  accurate measurement for development of all these  metrologies.


We have demonstrated the detailed TEM characterization of  FinFET integration at different stages from Fin formation, to  gate patterning, spacer formation at 100nm and 70nm pitch, and device failure analysis. Comprehensive understanding of  the 3D structure in sub 100nm pitch has been achieved. Innovation in sample prep and data interpretation is needed to  fully understand the 3D structure. HAADF-STEM combined  with EDX mapping can provide additional insight.


This work was performed by the Resrarch Alliance Teams at  various IBM Research and Development Facilities. The  authors would like to thank V. Basker and S. Kanakasabapathy and the FinFET team from IBM Albany  Nanotech for helpful discussion and providing the hardware.

[2] J. Kavalieros et al., Symp. VLSI Tech.,p. 50, 2006.


[3] V.S. Basker et al., Symp. VLSI Tech., p. 19, 2010


[4] B. Haran et al. Proc. IEDM 2008

A Comparison of Lock-in Thermography and Magnetic Current Imaging                   for Localizing Buried Short-Circuits


David P. Vallett


IBM Systems & Technology Group, Essex Junction, VT, USA


The relative effectiveness of lock-in thermography and  magnetic current imaging for identifying defects in packaged  ICs was studied by directly comparing results on the same  three devices. One known (in-lab fabricated) and two unknown (field return) defects were studied in organic flip-chip and  wirebond configurations. Both methods succeeded in  identifying the defective area but significant differences were  observed in the qualitative nature of the signals, XY  localization resolution, and sensitivity. Depth estimates were  obtained where possible which aided in localization.


Lock-in thermography (LIT) and magnetic current imaging (MCI) are common methods for non-destructive electrical  isolation of short circuits in ICs (MCI also being known as  Scanning SQUID Microscopy or SSM). [1, 2]  Both  techniques are especially well-suited for imaging through the  relatively thick opaque layers used in IC packaging and will  become critical as 3D packaging technologies emerge. [3] However the nature of the signals acquired and the respective  imaging techniques operate according to drastically different  principles as shown in Fig. 1. Accordingly the approaches  taken to calculate signal depth are likewise quite dissimilar.


Lock-in thermography relies on an indium antimonide array to  image heat transmission through overlying materials from high  I2R power densities typical at short circuit sites. Pulsing the  applied bias to the DUT allows the use of lock-in amplification  for increased signal-to-noise ratio (vs. steady-state  thermography) as well as phase-delay calculation to determine  the absolute heat transfer time (and therefore distance) between the heat source and the surface, or the relative heat  transfer from a known reference depth. [4]


Magnetic current imaging for packaged devices is  accomplished using a high-temperature (72K) superconducting  quantum interference device (SQUID) to measure the vertical  components of the magnetic fields surrounding conductors  carrying current to, from, and through short circuits. An AC  bias is utilized on the sample to enable lock-in amplification  for noise reduction. Magnetic field data are transformed into  current density images [5] which typically show higher  gradients inherent in localized narrow current paths or  dissolution of current density where narrower paths dissipate  to much broader conductors or power planes at short circuit  sites. Depth can be estimated using the well-established


relationship between magnetic field strength, current, and  distance. [6, 7] Current direction is also easily determined as  the fields surrounding active conductors are bipolar.


A previous comparison of the two techniques focused on LIT’s  value as a follow-on to MCI on one sample, localizing strong  thermal gradients within current paths. [8] The purpose of this


Figure 1: Fundamental operating principles of LIT (top) and MCI (bottom).


work is to directly assess LIT and MCI performance factors on  the same samples with each investigator blind to the other’s  results, adding an important evaluation of the relative depth  estimation capabilities of the techniques.


Experiment Description


Samples


A PBGA wirebond mixed-signal device and a flip-chip  processor were selected for examination to assess LIT and  MCI capabilities through plastic overmold and substrate  silicon materials, respectively. Both devices had low resistance  short circuit failures between a power supply or input pin and  ground electrodes. A second PBGA wirebond device was  intentionally shorted in a predetermined location to simulate a  buried defect in an organic laminate.


Method


Imaging was performed at separate locations and by different  individuals (LIT: vendor laboratory; MCI: author’s  laboratory), neither of whom were aware of the other’s results  at the time of testing. The only information known at the start  of the experiments was the failing pins. Initial analysis of LIT  and MCI results was performed without the aid of design data. Arbitrary starting voltage and current levels were selected then  adjusted up or down to examine the sensitivity of each  technique. Finally, physical failure analysis was performed on  the two unknown defect samples to determine the actual  locations and nature of the short circuit mechanisms for  comparison with LIT and MCI outcomes.


Measurement Details and Results


PBGA Wirebond Sample


An 8.4-ohm short was measured between a power supply  terminal and ground. LIT data were acquired with a 0.5 Hz  pulsed power source supplying 230.0 uW (46 mV; 5 mA) and


930.0 uW (93 mV; 10mA). Acquisitions were taken at 2, 10, and 30 minutes using a 640 x 512 array behind a wide-angle  and a 1X lens. Measurements at the higher bias point were  repeated at frequencies of 0.3, 0.5, 0.7 and 0.9 Hz with a faster  frame rate 320 x 256 array and 1X lens for depth localization. MCI measurements were obtained using a 5.3 KHz stimulus  supplying 8 uA, 50 uA, and 500 uA with acquisition times of  approximately 25 minutes each.


Figures 2 and 3 show the results obtained with both techniques  indicating a fairly well-localized problem in the same region. The local shape and distribution of the signals differs  considerably however owing to the fundamental differences in  the mechanisms involved (magnetic field derived current  density vs. thermal gradient). Figure 4 shows the relative  locations of both signals after scaling and alignment using the  respective background optical images of the device as  references.


Figure 2: MCI (left) and LIT (right) results on power to  ground short circuit failure in PBGA wirebond device.


Figure 3: 3D surface rendering of MCI (left) and LIT (right) signals.


Figure 4: Scaled and aligned overlay of MCI and LIT  signals using optical background images as references.


Estimating the depth of the signal source below the surface of  the device is done using the phase delay in the LIT data. Lacking an accurate value for the thermal diffusion length of  the plastic overmold, a reference heat source was created by  powering a nearby ESD protect device located in the die at the  transistor source/drain level. The results in Fig. 6 indicate that  the LIT signal from the failing pin is also originating in the die  at or near the silicon transistor level. Depth estimation using  MCI requires a well-isolated current path having two  symmetrical bipolar field peaks. The suspect defect area on  this device is where current diverges and as such provides no  such isolated path.


Physical failure analysis identified an electrical overload at the  location identified in Fig. 5, where an SEM image of the  damage observed at the silicon level can also be seen, verifying the LIT depth estimate.


The device failed with a 5.9 ohm short between an input pin  and ground. LIT data were taken with a 5.0 Hz pulsed power  source at a bias point of 1.7 mW (100 mV; 17 mA) and  acquisition times of 30 sec. using a wide-angle objective, 20  sec. with a 1X objective, 1 min. at 5X, and 1 min. and 3 min. at 10X. Once the suspect site was identified, LIT was repeated  at 1X for 90 min over the full frame of 640 x 512 pixels to  examine sensitivity. Under these conditions a signal was  visible down to 10.4 uW (8 mV; 1.3 mA) at 5.0 Hz. Reducing  the pixel field to 128 x 128 the spot could be observed at 5.0  uW (5.5 mV; 0.9 mA) albeit requiring a 12 hr acquisition. MCI data were acquired at 5.3 KHz with 1.5 mA and again at  10 uA to assess sensitivity. Each scan took 42 minutes.


Artificial Defect


A defect was created by abrading a small portion of the solder  mask and reflowing a BGA solder ball to the underlying  ground plane. The result was a very large-area low-resistance  short circuit (0.8 ohms) at the bottom of the laminate, as far as  possible from both sensors.


Initial LIT data at 0.1 Hz with 19.2 mW (120 mV; 160 mA) applied power over 3 min. and 73.5 mW (245 mV; 300 mA)


over 5 min. showed no signal at the known defect location  using a wide angle lens. After focusing on the known defect  region however with a bias point of 84.0 mW (280 mV; 300  mA) and acquisition time of 35 min. a generally localized  region of heat was observed. MCI data were taken at 5.3 KHz


Figure 9: High-magnification views of LIT signal seen in  Figures 7 and 8 taken through the silicon backside.


LIT and MCI differed considerably in the shape and  distribution of their respective signals on each of the three  devices. On two samples (Fig’s 3 and 7) LIT shows mostly  symmetrical thermal peaks. On the flip-chip device in  particular (Fig’s 7-9 and 11) the peak is quite well resolved  because unlike on the plastic encapsulated PBGA part, the  radiative component of the signal is able to pass through the  silicon with relatively little lateral diffusion. On a third device  having a much larger-area defect (Fig’s 12 and 13) LIT data  indicate a relatively widespread less well-localized heat  distribution.


In contrast MCI signals on two of the samples are more  representative of the current path to and through the defect  sites. Using current directional analysis MCI clearly showed  regions of current divergence where relatively narrow  conductors shorted to larger planes. On a third sample (Fig. 13) a current density peak was well-resolved at the site of the  defect.


Sensitivity


The minimum detectable signal with a discernible signal-tonoise ratio and acquisition time less than 90 min. (arbitrarily  chosen) was observed for each sample (for the flip-chip device  the LIT sensitivity measurement was made over a relatively  small field-of-view only after a suspect region had been  identified at lower magnification). The results are shown in  Table 1 with the respective figures of merit for each technique (power for LIT; current for MCI).


The data clearly show MCI to be the more sensitive technique  in each case owing to the unusually high responsiveness of the   SQUID detector. (It should be noted that the sensitivity of both  techniques was assessed using the total applied power or  current to the device, not the power or current only at the


Figure 12: MCI (left) and LIT (right) results on artificially  created defect (circled area). MCI current direction is  indicated (black arrowheads).


Figure 13: LIT (upper left), MCI (upper right), and both  results (lower image) taken from a smaller field-of-view and  overlaid on X-ray image. Outlined area is approximate  location of artificially created solder ball to ground plane  laminate short circuit.


defect site. Series power division and parallel current paths  may have reduced the LIT or MCI signal strength, respectively. As such the detectable power or current at the  actual defect site may be lower.).


Resolution is defined here as ‘localization resolution’, the  smallest area of signal indicative of a defect (as determined by  a well-experienced and knowledgeable operator of the  instrumentation). The results show that it is highly sampledependent. For the PBGA sample shown in Fig. 5 the  approximate area identified is comparable for both methods. For the flip-chip device data in Fig’s 7-9 and 11 it is clear that  LIT has a distinct advantage, once again due to the radiative  nature of the LIT signal which is easily resolved optically. Finally, for the case of the fabricated artificial defect Fig. 13  indicates better MCI resolution in localizing current density  directly to the area of the fabricated ground plane short.


Depth Estimation


Absolute depth estimation using LIT requires known thermal  diffusion lengths for materials overlying the heat source. These  data were not readily available for the plastic overmold and  organic laminates present in the devices used. Alternatively  reference heat sources were used for phase-delay comparison  with the unknown defect heat sources. This approach allowed  resolution of signal depth to the only reference heat source  available which was an ESD protection device at the silicon  level, ultimately confirmed in both cases by physical failure  analysis.


MCI depth estimation requires straight, sufficiently narrow  conductors isolated from surrounding current paths where an  ideal line scan containing both field peaks can be obtained. This condition was available only on the flip-chip device  where accurate estimation of the absolute current depth of the  laminate trace carrying current to the defect beneath the  surface of the die was made, supporting the conclusion that  current was dissipating where the laminate conductor meets  the die.


A careful comparison of lock-in thermography and SQUIDbased magnetic current imaging has been made using identical  samples. The study focused on qualitative signal  characteristics, sensitivity, localization resolution, and  effectiveness of depth estimation.


This evaluation was limited in scope to buried short-circuit  failures in three packaging configurations. Further, only two  fundamental defect types were assessed – a relatively narrow  conductor shorting to a much larger plane, and a very large  area short circuit. Finally all defects were less than 10 ohms in  resistance. Other defect types and resistances in different  package formats, especially deep three-dimensional  assemblies, should be subjects of possible future study.

In this paper, we demonstrate that lock-in thermography (LIT) appears as a key and complementary technique for Failure  Analysis across different use cases. Even if the failure requires  a complex emulation setup, thanks to a specific capability of  our thermal system, this kind of failure can be addressed. In  our FA case study, we will show that LIT is a most efficient  solution to address a bridge defect located inside a complex  logic area, and furthermore that LIT highlights the defect itself  and not only the consequences of the defect.


Lock-In Thermography has been established as a key  technique in the failure analysis flow to address defects. The  aim of this paper is to present a full case study related to a scan  chain stuck-at failure, where the EDA diagnosis tool was not  successful. Since common failure analysis methods, such as  emission microscopy [1] or laser stimulation techniques [2] were not efficient enough to highlight the X-Y location of the  failure and thus determine root cause. We demonstrated that  Dynamic Lock-In Thermography is able to pinpoint a bridging  resistance between 2 internal signal lines inside the logic  section of the device.


Lock-In Thermography


Lock-In Thermography (LIT) was introduced a few years ago  to overcome the limitations of the MCT thermal camera and  Liquid Crystal (LC), which have been used to localize  thermally active defects in microelectronic devices. The three  main benefits of LIT over previous static thermal detection  are:


1. Sensitivity: due to latest generation of the highly  sensitive InSb camera, and the specific lock-in  methodology to drastically improve the fault  detection efficiency (μW range) [3]


2. Resolution: due to an advanced optical Solid  Immersion Lens which open the door to sub-micron  resolution in the thermal domain [4]


3. Ease of use.


The most common usage of LIT is Vdd-GND short  localization. In this configuration, we connected the failing  pins of the Device under Test (DUT) to the Lock-In  thermography system, which activated (voltage => Vdd) and  deactivated (voltage => GND) them at a given frequency. Then, the system applied the lock-in method based on the  same known frequency on resulting infrared images. This lockin process is applied to each pixel of the thermal detector in  real time (Figure 1).


EMMI and Laser Based Approach:


The first set of acquisitions was acquired on a high  performance emission tool (EMMI). Unfortunately, in this  case, EMMI highlighted only defect consequences but it was  not able to pinpoint the source of the defect. This is a wellknown limitation of the EMMI technique, such as when  dealing with a bridge defect located inside a logic area [6]. Also, Laser stimulation technique like OBIRCH [7] was tried  in pseudo static mode due to electrical setup (stopping on  failing vector) without a valid signature at low (1x) and midmagnifications (20x). This can be explained by the fact that the  defect had a relatively low resistance value (few tens of ohms) and its geometric dimension was small compared to the laser  beam size at these magnifications. Thus, resistance change of  the defect was not sufficient to induce a detectable  consumption variation.


Specific Test Pattern Generation for LIT Analysis:


To analyze this device using the LIT tool, the first step is to  setup specific test pattern to be adapted to the external trigger  setup of the camera. This part of the process can be tricky  when dealing with more complex failure activation. Let’s add  that we can use various techniques to meet this electrical  activation requirement.


In this case study, we used a 10MHz clock (nominal  frequency), we send 25000 ‘0’ followed by 25000 ‘1’. At the  same time, in the beginning of each test loop we generated a  trigger signal to the system using a dedicated output of the  ATE. As a result, the failure is ‘activated’ during the first half  of the test pattern, and ‘not activated’ during the second part of  the test pattern (Figure 3).


Figure 3: Simplify test pattern used for the LIT analysis.


Lock-In Thermography Analysis:


As a common defect localization approach, the first step was  to analyze the entire failing die at a low magnification (1x  Field Of View = 9.6mm x 7.68mm). Figure 4 shows 1x LIT  images, performed on a golden device and on the failing  device.


Figure 4: LIT amplitude images from a golden device (left) and a failing device (right). LIT frequency 20Hz. Mag.: 1x. Acq. time: ~1min.


We note that a huge thermal spot is localized, in the center of  the failing die, inside the logic area. Using additional  information on the chip design, the area was spatially  correlated with the failing scan chain. Moreover, we can add  that the golden device image was not mandatory in this case, since it was obvious that this thermal spot was coming from  the failing circuit.


Figure 5: LIT amplitude image (superimposed on pattern  image of the DUT) from the failing device (dotted box area in  Figure 4). LIT frequency 20Hz. Mag.: 10x.Acq. time:  ~1min.


Figure 5 shows a zoomed in 10x acquisition done on the  failing device, in the logic area, where the defective thermal  signature was found using the low magnification lens. We can  resolve at least 5 thermal spots which were linked to the  defect, since they were only visible on the failing die. One  more point, these thermal spots seemed really interesting since  they are spatially located near the failing scan chain  implementation.


Next step was to get higher magnifications using a Thermal  Solid Immersion Lens [4]. Related images are presented in  Figure 6.


Figure 6: LIT amplitude (overlaid on pattern image) images  performed on the failing device (cropped). LIT frequency  20Hz. Mag.: 17x (SIL). Acq. time: ~2min.


These high resolution images allow easy alignment to CAD  information to identify which cells and nets are implicated in  the failure mechanism (Figure 6). Physical Net Trace [8] analysis was performed using the CAD database.


Using the same alignment method, instead with an X-ray  image as reference, the MCI and accompanying directional  data in Fig. 5 show current traveling down a package power  plane, through a wirebond, and diverging in the area where the  wirebond terminates at the die (the strong peak in the  wirebond portion of the MCI signal is an artifact of the current  rising closer to the SQUID at the apex of the wirebond loop, causing a stronger field). Also seen in Fig. 5 is the LIT signal  located in this same wirebond termination area, confirming the  likelihood of a short circuit defect.


Flip-chip Device


Comparing the two techniques, similar results to those  obtained on the PBGA device are seen in Fig. 7, with MCI  showing a localized current path that terminates abruptly at the  edge of the die and rapidly disperses, and LIT indicating a  point heat source in that region. In Fig. 8, overlaying the MCI  and LIT results, it is clear that the heat source is once again at  the termination point. Finally, Fig. 9 presents highmagnification views of the spot taken with LIT through the  silicon die backside showing a very well localized heat source. For estimating defect depth a reference ESD protect diode was  once again used with LIT having a phase-delay matching that  of the defect, indicating that it is again on the die level.


With MCI data indicating another current dissipation situation  it is possible in this case to estimate the absolute current depth  of the path leading to the defect. Figure 10 shows a section of  the magnetic field image where a sufficiently straight and  isolated current path exists. The line-scan obtained, best-fit  model, and resulting depth calculated from the horizontal

separation in the z-component peaks of the magnetic field are  also seen. These data indicate that the path to the dissipation  point is approximately 738 microns below the scanned sample  surface (the back side of the die) consistent with current in the  top layer of the laminate leading to the failing C4 pin between  it and the die.


Physical failure analysis results are highlighted in Fig. 11. No  defects were observed at any level at the failing pin or the  circuits connected to it. However a large damaged area on the  upper wiring levels with an apparent short circuit between C4  balls was noted adjacent to the failing pin, in the region where  the MCI signal dissipates. The LIT signal is seen immediately  to the right of this area, apparently arising from heat generated  in the I/O circuits at silicon level affected by the observed  defect, matching the LIT depth estimate.

using currents of 99 uA, 1.4 mA, and 3.0 mA and acquisition  times of 38 min. each with a more well-defined region. Results  are depicted in Fig’s 12 and 13. It should be noted that the  actual power imaged by LIT at the defect site is lower because  of power division between the wire attachment point and the  defect region, verified by the two LIT peaks in Fig. 12.


With no reference heat source in the laminate, relative depth  estimation using LIT was not possible. Likewise thermal  diffusion data were not available obviating calculation of  absolute depth. Similarly the lack of a straight sufficiently


narrow and isolated current path to or through the defect site  prevented MCI depth estimation.


Unlike the previous two samples very different outcomes are  apparent between MCI and LIT on this sample. The signal in  the lower right-hand corner in the images of Fig. 12 can be  ignored as it is coming from one of the two wires supplying  power to the device. MCI indicates a current path directly  from this wire connection at the lower right, to the defect  where it terminates as current dissipates in the ground plane (the diagonal current path through the center region of the  device is from the second wire lying well beneath the sample). LIT shows a relatively large area of signal adjacent to the  defect. Figure 13 highlights results taken in the region of  interest around the defect site now overlaid with an X-ray  image with the artificially created defect outlined. Here it is  apparent that the MCI signal peaks at the defect location  whereby the LIT activity is somewhat off the defect and  adjacent to it.


Qualitative Signal Characteristics


From the analysis it is observed that each technique provided  data with fundamentally different characteristics. LIT  indicated single regions of high thermal gradient where MCI  imaged current density paths leading to, through, and from the  defect sites. For the signal buried under opaque plastic  package overmold the qualitative localization resolution was  comparable. On the flip chip device where the only material  covering the signal source was silicon, LIT showed  exceptionally better resolution. And the defect location under  the organic laminate was localized and resolved more  precisely with MCI.


Sensitivity was three orders of magnitude better with MCI than  LIT in all three cases. Depth estimation was demonstrated with  both methods where LIT resolved signal depth relative to the  silicon level on two samples and MCI approximated absolute  depth with an isolated current path on one of the samples. Both  approaches were constrained in this regard without detailed  design and/or material parameters.

The Signal to Noise Ratio (SNR) of the resulting  lock-in thermography image improves with the acquisition  time {SNR  ln(tacquisition)}.


Dynamic Lock-In Thermography


In this paper, the LIT technique is used in a dynamic  environment [5] because of the failure type. In fact, this defect  cannot be activated with a simple “2 wires connection” VddGND. It was detected during the scan chain test, using a  dynamic ATE to activate it, and which classified it as a broken  scan chain.


To amend the LIT method to this specific case study  configuration (dynamic emulation needed), we built a specific  test setup adapted to the lock-in thermography technique. As  we already described, the basic idea is to be able to enable and  disable the failure at a given frequency (lock-in frequency). First, we develop a specific test pattern revised to the previous  need (discussed in detail hereafter). Second, to ensure correct  lock-in methodology, the thermal camera frame rate needed to  be synchronous with the defect activation. This was easily  achieved on our system (ELITE) through external triggering of  the thermal camera. Thus, using this specific capability, we  sent a trigger signal, which occurs at the beginning of each test  loop, to synchronize the thermal camera with our dynamic  defect activation (Figure 2).


Figure 2: Dynamic Lock-In Thermography.


Failure description


This work has been performed on a 0.15 μm process device, designed for Digital TV applications, packaged in SBGA 256. The device failed during the scan chain integrity test. During  this first level of test, the scan chain is setup in “serial mode” and exercised as a simple serial register. We send a known  data stream in the first flip flop of the serial register (SCAN  IN) and we expect to detect the same data stream at the output  of the last flip flop (SCAN OUT). In our case, the SCAN OUT  information was always stuck at ‘1’ and the EDA diagnosis  tool did not give any useful prediction. It just classified this  failure as a broken scan chain issue, and gave us the  approximate failing flip flop.


Failure Localization
