The channel of metal-oxide-semiconductor (MOS) transistors  at different modes of operation has been characterized using  dynamic backside laser reflectance modulation technique for  different NMOS and PMOS transistors with different channel  lengths. The reflectance modulations contain a primary peak  near the drain-end when the MOS transistor is in saturation  mode. Comparison studies with a Pseudo-Two-Dimensional  analytical model support the hypothesis that the observed peak  corresponds to the pinch-off point.


When a laser beam is incident on a material or device, the  optical reflectivity may be changed due to temperature [1] or  electro-optical effects [2] resulting in a modulation of the  reflected laser beam. Laser reflectance modulation has been  used to measure the temperature changes of microscale metal  thin films [1], silicon (Si) integrated circuits [3] and to acquire  timing signals from Si devices [4].


Laser reflectance modulation based on temperature effects  using frontside and backside techniques have been used to  characterize the temperature profile of metal layers [1, 5]. As  semiconductor technology advances, frontside techniques face  severe limitations due to the use of increasing metallization  layers and flip-chip packages.


Backside techniques use near infrared (NIR) wavelengths to  penetrate through Si substrates and directly probe the  underside metal layers and active layers. Devices with ball grid  array (BGA) and flip-chip packages can be characterized with  backside probing


In a study of various parameters affecting the laser reflectance  signals on isolated complementary MOS transistors with 10 μm gate length and width, it has been shown that the channel  due to inversion while operating in the pinch-off and saturation  mode can be observed by mapping the laser reflectance using a  spectrum analyzer [6]. These results indicate the potential of  using backside laser reflectance modulation to characterize  electrically active devices.


This work focuses on characterizing the channel of MOS  transistor at linear, pinch-off and saturation modes of  operation [7] using backside laser reflectance modulation. Nchannel MOS (NMOS) and p-channel MOS (PMOS) devices  with various channel lengths are investigated. The reflectance  modulations of the channel contain a primary peak near the  drain-end when the MOS transistor is in saturation mode. The  location of the primary peak is compared with the pinch-off  point from the drain-end based on the Pseudo-TwoDimensional Model proposed by Ko [8] and found to be in  good agreement. The backside laser reflectance modulation  technique is therefore able to characterize the MOS device  operations.


Theory


When a voltage VGS is applied to the gate G of the NMOS  transistor, with drain D, substrate S and bulk B grounded, causing an inversion, a conducting channel is formed between  S and D. If a small drain voltage VDS is subsequently applied, a  current flows through the conducting channel, with the drain  current IDS proportional to the applied VDS. This is the linear  mode. As VDS increases further, the electron density at the  drain-end is depleted and eventually reaches pinch-off. With  further increase in VDS, the transistor is in saturation mode as  the pinch-off point moves towards the source and a velocity  saturation region VSR is formed. The length of VSR L is the  distance between the pinch-off point from the drain-end [9]. The voltage at the pinch-off point is VDSAT with voltage drop  DSAT


DS VV −  across L [7]. Fig. 1 shows an overview of the  MOS device in saturation mode.


Fig.1: MOS Device in Saturation Mode


The VSR is the most resistive section of the channel [10], with  corresponding heat dissipation profile that peaks in the VSR


[11]. Numerical simulations have indicated that the location  with the highest electron temperature corresponds to where the  electrical field is the highest, which is at the drain-end of the  VSR. Published experimental data showed similar trends  within experimental limits, but the peak temperature location  could not be resolved accurately [12].


The intensity modulation of a reflected laser beam is affected  by temperature [1] or electro-optical effects [2]. A study on  backside reflectance modulation due to temperature effect  concluded that the peak reflectance modulation resulted from  maximum temperature change of the device [5]. Due to high  electric field [13] and maximum temperature change [10] in  the VSR, the reflectance modulation of the VSR should be  more significant as compared to the rest of the channel.


The discontinuity in carrier density along the channel is  apparent at the pinch-off point which acts as the boundary  between two distinct regions: VSR and the source section (LL). In fact, the Reddi-Sah model [10] had predicted  discontinuity in the electric field at the pinch-off point [8, 14].  Subsequent analytical models have evolved and assumed  electric field continuity at the pinch-off point. This is generally  achieved with analytical solution of the electric field ES at the  pinch-off point for the source section [14]. ES is then applied  as the boundary condition for the VSR with the assumption of  electric field continuity at the pinch-off point [15]. The general  approach to calculate L is to solve Poisson’s equation in the  VSR [8] as follows:


Si


In the first step, wafer was diced and test structures   of interest were glued and bonded in the 256-pins SBGA  packages. Next, the bonded samples were encapsulated   and packages were opened from the backside. The final step   of pre-preparation was mechanical die thinning to desired  thickness with the limit of approximately 25-30 μm thickness  of remaining Si substrate. The sample for SPEM using InGaAs  detector was processed down to approximately 100 μm as the  InGaAs spectral sensitivity region has only very small overlap  with absorption spectrum of the Si material. The sample for  SPEM using Si-CCD has been mechanically thinned down to  25 μm but needed to be further thinned down to very thin  layers of Si.


The ultimate backside substrate thinning has been  performed using OptiFIB system manufactured by DCG  Systems. Samples were thinned down to ultimately even less  than 0.5 μm (STI level) using methodologies presented  elsewhere [3, 4]. During FIB milling process, device operation  was monitored continuously to track the impact of the FIB  process on the device functionality. Even when approaching  thicknesses lower than 1 μm the devices were still fully  functional. Several transistors have been chosen for this  investigation. Trenches have been created under the devices  from the backside of the die. To demonstrate the influence of


the Si substrate thinning on the SPEM performance, samples  of various thicknesses of remaining silicon have been prepared  ranging from 25 μm down to STI level. Great attention was  always paid to trench floor co-planarity - a very important  parameter for ultra thin silicon (UTS) FIB processes.


3) Experimental Setups


The core of the setup was the well known and widely  used Phemos-1000 PEM/LS system manufactured by  Hamamatsu Photonics. Both detectors: Si-CCD and InGaAs  could be installed. In addition, we used two different methods  to spectrally resolve photon emissions. Together with Si-CCD  detector we implemented a set of interference band pass filters  to obtain discrete spectrum of the light. The filters had the  FWHM of 40-70 nm and several central wavelengths to cover  the entire spectral range of the detector. Together with InGaAs  detector, we have implemented a prism as a spectrally  resolving unit. Therefore, when using InGaAs detector, the PE  could be resolved spectrally at very high spectral resolution, limited only by the pixel size of the detector.


The DUTs were activated using external semiconductor  characterization system. Experimental setups are presented  schematically in figure 2 and 3.


Fig.2: Experimental setup for discrete PE spectrum  acquisition using Si-CCD detector.


Fig.3: Experimental setup for continuous PE spectrum  acquisition using InGaAs detector.


[7].

For most simulations the thickness of the well  was set to 2 μm and the thickness of the bulk was a variable. To simulate the ultra thin Si cases also the well thickness was  reduced to the desired value (between 0-2 μm). The doping  concentration levels were chosen to correspond to technology  parameters: NWELL = 21017 cm-3 and NSUB = 51015 cm-3. After

In order to prove the consistency of the experimental data  with modeling & simulation results, the experimental data  presented in fig. 4 has been used to check the validity of the  simulations. Namely, the PE spectrum obtained for 25 μm of  remaining Si has been corrected with regard for the absorption  in Si using absorption simulations for Si thickness being the  difference between the 25 μm and the other two thicknesses  presented in fig.4 (14 μm and 1μm). For example, to obtain  the PE spectrum for 14 μm of remaining Si (blue), the PE  spectrum acquired at 25 μm (green) of Si has been corrected  using the simulated absorption data for 11 μm of bulk Si  material. The results of this procedure are presented in fig.11. To summarize, generally, the PE spectrum for a certain Si  thickness D1 obtained by correcting the PE spectrum at a Si  thickness D2, using the simulation data calculated for the Si  thickness D = D1 – D2, stays in very good agreement with the  experimental PE spectral data for the targeted thickness D1. Ultimately one can use the simulation data calculated for the Si  thickness corresponding to the thickness of Si during PE  spectrum acquisition, and therefore obtain the pure devicerelated PE spectrum (black).


Figure 7: NetTrace results of CAD analysis


Interpretation


As we see in Figures 6 and 7, all thermal spots can be linked to  two different nets (blue net for spots 1,3a, 3b, 4b and 4c; red  net for spots 2, 4a and 5).  Also, through this analysis we  notice that all spots are spatially correlated with standard cell  locations, except spot 2. Figure 8 shows a high magnification (30x) view of spots 1 and 2:


Figure 8: LIT amplitude image (superimposed on pattern  image) for the failing device. LIT frequency 20Hz. Mag.: 30x (SIL). Acq. time: ~2min.


We note from this high magnification image analysis that spot  2 is not located over standard cell area ( black area), and the  CAD analysis shows that this specific location corresponds to  an area where the two suspect nets are in close proximity (Figure 9).


4c


3b


4a 4b


3a


100μm


10μm


25μm


Figure 9: Closer view of CAD at the thermal spot 2 location  showing the close proximity of M3 to M2 via connection.


All information collected during this analysis (test, fault  localization, CAD analysis and schematic analysis) converged  to a resistive bridging defect at the spot 2 location, between the  two nets which are highlighted by the LIT analysis.


All other thermal spots which are present on Figure 5 can now  be explained through this fail hypothesis. In fact, this resistive  bridge would induce an incorrect voltage state of the two  highlighted nets. Consequently, cells that are connected to  these nets must be in a wrong polarity state (for gate  connected) or a wrong charging (for drivers). Consequently, additional currents would have passed through these cells and  heated them up. That is why we observed thermal spots on  gates linked to the bridge defect.


This hypothesis was confirmed using a micro-probing  approach on the output of the failing flip flop (blue net). Results are described in Figure 10.


Figure 10: Oscillograph from micro-probing experiment


performed on the output of the suspected flip flop.


From this experiment, we note that the lower state of the  output of this flip flop is offset by 0.9V. This result is in line  with a bridge defect between two nets, when the red net is set  to Vdd. Moreover, we understand why this scan chain was  failing during the scan chain integrity test, since this failure  will result in a stuck at ‘1’ test result on the output of this scan  chain.


We severed this net to disconnect the two consecutive flip  flops in that scan chain, and we were able to verify that these  two independent sections of that particular scan chain was  working as expected.


Finally, we can also explain the broken scan chain result that  was obtained during first electrical testing. Hereafter, is  presented the schematic representation of the failing area (Figure 11):


Figure 11: Schematic of the failing area of the defective scan chain.


Flip-flops used in our case study are positive edge triggered  multiplexed scan D-flip-flops. The scan control input (SC) selects either the data input (D) when SC is low, or the scan  data input (SD) when SC is high. The selected data is  transferred to the Q output on the rising edge of the clock CP. Thus, in scan mode, since the bridge defect is located directly  on the net which connects the two flip-flops of interest, it was  detected as a scan chain integrity issue during the electrical  test.


Physical characterization


The final step of this study was to perform a physical failure  analysis at the spot 2 location to find the physical cause of the  failure. Results are revealed in Figure 12:


Figure 12:  SEM, top view images shows barrier short  between two M2 metal lines.


Physical analysis reveals a barrier short at the bottom of two  M2 lines which induced a low resistive bridge defect. This  issue was solved by design rule modification and this close  proximity prohibition of M2 to M3 double vias was added to  the design rules datalog (Figure 13).

Figure 13: Closer view of CAD showing the design fix.


In this paper, we have addressed a successful failure analysis  case study, on a dynamically activated device, using the lockin thermography technique. We demonstrated that this thermal  based technique enabled the localization of a resistive bridge  inside a digital logic area, where EMMI and laser stimulation  techniques were not conclusive. The main benefit is that this  approach allows a direct localization of the root cause, since  other classical techniques (EMMI and pseudo dynamic  OBIRCH) will generally pinpoint only defect consequences.


This specific external trigger setup capability on the LIT tool  opens the doors to a wide range of applications, where a defect  cannot be easily emulated with a two probe setup (Vdd-GND).


All lock-in thermography experiments have been performed on  CIMPACA Characterization platform. This lab is a mutualized  platform between different local partners located in Rousset, France.

Practical Implementation of Soft Defect Localization (SDL)  in Mixed Signal and Analog ICs


Eric Barbian, Gretchen Crow, Win Thandar Swe, Mark C. Phillips


ON Semiconductor, Phoenix, Arizona   Eric.Barbian@onsemi.com


Dynamic Laser Stimulation (DLS) techniques for Soft Defect  Localization (SDL) have been well documented for logic  devices [1][2]. More recent literature has broadened the  traditional SDL pass/fail mapping by employing multiple  device parameters including power analysis [3], spectrum  response [4], and other analog variations [5]. A practical and  efficient implementation of SDL without the use of  synchronization or traditional Automatic Test Equipment (ATE) hardware is presented. A dynamic way of analyzing  many parameters of mixed signal and analog ICs can be  obtained through the use of a high waveform rate oscilloscope, feedback loop, or discrete comparator. Multiple case studies  are shown to illustrate the methodology.


Dynamic Laser Stimulation (DLS) techniques are commonly  used in failure analysis of soft defects. Soft defects are defined  as those which allow devices to operate within specifications  at some operating condition but result in failure when one or  more operating condition is changed (i.e. voltage, temperature, timing, etc.). SDL using DLS techniques are typically  performed using an ATE to provide the operating conditions  necessary to put the device on the edge of the pass/fail criteria. A scanning near infrared (NIR) laser is then used to locally  heat the die to modify the DUTs pass/fail response. The  pass/fail information is then fed back to the scanning optical  microscope (SOM) to provide physical mapping of areas that  modulate the pass/fail state.


More recently, several papers have extended the use of SDL to  include variation mapping. Instead of only monitoring a digital  pass/fail criteria, any electrical parameter can be measured and  the variation caused by the IR laser scanning can be mapped. This analog approach to SDL can provide additional insight  into the failure mechanism and origin. This paper illustrates a practical and low cost implementation of SDL using an SOM. Four case studies will be examined utilizing different mapping  techniques on analog and mixed signal ICs.


Methodology


Typical SDL implementation utilizes an ATE to provide  pass/fail data and synchronization of laser movement with the  SOM system. Given a global manufacturing environment, it is  not always practical or possible to have all ATE systems and load boards available for failure analysis. In the case of analog  and mixed-signal analysis, discrete bench setups and  application board testing of failing devices are more common. Given these limitations, implementing SDL across all product  types can be a difficult task. Several different techniques are  illustrated to overcome these limitations to detect the laser  perturbations of device characteristics.


The challenge is to provide the SOM an appropriate input that  responds to the laser. The Dynamic Analysis by Laser  Stimulation (DALS) function kit is used with the PHEMOS1000 to provide the input to the SOM. Only the 50 ohm  TESTER SIGNAL input signal is utilized with this kit as no  external synchronization is necessary with this methodology. The DALS software allows selection of whether to run in  latch, counter, or analog acquisition mode. The latch mode is  a traditional binary pass/fail mode. The existence of a  rising/falling edge, selectable in software, within a pixel dwell  time results in a failing pixel. Counter mode is similar to latch  mode excepting that the number of rising/falling edges per  pixel are counted. Analog mode captures a voltage value per  pixel.


The predominant method utilized consists of a high waveform  rate oscilloscope to provide the feedback to the SOM system  using the latch or counter acquisition modes. The high  waveform rate oscilloscope provides a repeatable and fast  means of measuring multiple device parameters for feedback  into the SOM. Essentially, if a trigger can be acquired on some  aspect of the device’s failure mode, the trigger output can be  used to drive the SOM for SDL. As long as the DUT has the  potential for at least one trigger within each pixel dwell time  of the SOM, no synchronization signal is necessary [6]. The  oscilloscope utilized has a maximum waveform rate of  100,000 waveforms/s. The actual waveform rate will depend  on the frequency at which any potential trigger occurs as well  as the amount of time the oscilloscope is set to acquire as  illustrated in Figure 1.


The oscilloscope waveform update rate and dead time are key  elements in obtaining a successful SDL result.  A hardware  triggered oscilloscope typically has a more deterministic dead  time, the time between oscilloscope acquisitions where  processing of the previous waveform occurs, than do software  triggered oscilloscopes. While software triggered  oscilloscopes can be used for SDL, the slower waveform  update rate requires longer SDL acquisition times and the nondeterministic dead times create additional noise in the  acquired image.


Figure 1: Hardware triggered oscilloscope maximum output  trigger frequency versus time/div setting of oscilloscope. These output frequencies can only be obtained if the input  signal’s rate is greater than or equal to the maximum output  trigger frequency.


Using the trigger out of an oscilloscope allows a familiar and  simple means of adjusting the pass/fail threshold for many  device parameters to fine tune the SDL response without  resorting to modifying ATE code. When running in this  manner, only a single coaxial cable is connected between the


oscilloscope’s trigger out and the DALS kit TESTER


SIGNAL input. To successfully run SDL in this manner  requires the following setup steps.


The failure mode must be setup to loop. Depending  on the failure mode, this can range from simple to  complex setups.


Verify that temperature changes modify the  parameter being observed (frequency, rise time, duty  cycle, etc.). Typically, any change that can be  observed on an oscilloscope will provide some SDL  response.


Adjust the operating parameters and/or oscilloscope  settings to achieve a near zero probability of a  trigger.  These settings can be adjusted during DLS to  attain the sensitivity desired.


Set the laser scan rate such that the test loop period of  the DUT will allow for at least one potential trigger


to occur within each pixel’s dwell time.


The second method employed to achieve SDL consists of  amplifying some device parameter, converting the parameter


into a voltage, and providing that voltage to the SOM using  the analog acquisition mode. Case study 3 provides an  example using a feedback loop on an operational amplifier to  convert the input bias currents into voltages and then  amplifying the difference.  This result is then buffered and fed  to the SOM for SDL in analog acquisition mode.  Traditional  current amplifiers and voltage amplifiers have also been  successfully used for SDL.


Finally, a third methodology has been utilized that is similar to  the oscilloscope method. This involves the use of a discrete  operational amplifier or comparator to create a rising edge at  an SDL site that is input to the SOM in latch or counter  acquisition mode. While this methodology is not inherently  different than utilizing an oscilloscope trigger out, it provides  another low cost means of digitizing a device parameter for  SDL.


Case Study One


Case study one involves a bi-directional level shifter  exhibiting an abnormally slow falling edge on one output. The  block diagram of the bi-directional level shifter is shown in  Figure 2 and the abnormal falling edge in Figure 3.


Figure 2: Block diagram of one I/O channel of the bidirectional level shifter.


Bench testing revealed that the fall time would decrease when  the device was heated as shown in Figure 4.  Therefore the  failure was considered a good candidate for SDL.


In order to loop the test, a 10kHz square wave was used as the  input to the low side level shifter input. The oscilloscope was  set to 10μs per division to ensure that every falling edge  would result in a possible trigger event. The trigger output of  the oscilloscope was connected to the TESTER SIGNAL input  of the SOM DALS kit. Initially the oscilloscope was  configured to trigger when the pulse width of the failing  output was less than a pulse width of 60μs. The laser scan  speed was set at 36 seconds allowing for approximately 130μs  dwell time per pixel (512x512 resolution). Latch acquisition  mode was selected. Under these conditions at least one trigger  was indicated in each pixel resulting in a completely white,failing, response. Then while the laser was scanning, the pulse  width trigger value was dynamically lowered in order to set


the trigger pulse width just above the heated pulse width. When the optimum pulse width trigger was achieved, a trigger (FAIL) was detected only when the laser scanned over the  thermally sensitive area as shown in the Figure 5 SDL overlay  image.


Figure 3: Slow falling edge observed on level shifter output.


Figure 4: The DUTs abnormal fall time was observed to  decrease with heat.


Figure 5: SDL overlay image obtained using latch acquisition  mode. The signal was over a double via1.


A localized signal was detected over a double Via1. Subsequent deprocessing revealed over-etched metal 2 at the  edge of a double via which etched the underlying metal 1 trace. The metal 1 trace was not completely severed, resulting  in the resistive trace connection that was sensitive to thermal  stimulation as shown in Figure 6.


Figure 6: SEM image of the etched Metal 1 trace to the right  of a double Via.


Case Study Two


Case study two involves an emitter coupled logic (ECL) programmable PLL synthesized clock generator device that  failed output frequency at high temperature (~70°C). When  the device was heated, the output frequency was observed to  transition from its desired 50MHz programmed frequency to a  higher frequency of 120MHz. Figure 7 illustrates the block  diagram of PLL clock generator. The voltage controlled  oscillator (VCO) within the PLL operates at a frequency range  of 400MHz to 800MHz. The external quartz crystal is used to  create the frequency reference. The output of the crystal is  divided by 16 and sent to the phase frequency detector (PFD) as the reference frequency. The PFD compares the reference  frequency to the VCO divided by M quotient to provide  dynamic control of the VCO. The configurable M and N dividers provide an output frequency in a range of 50MHz to  800MHz.


Since the failure mode was temperature sensitive and could be  triggered by raising the temperature through laser scanning, SDL was chosen as an appropriate fault localization technique. The system was set up as shown in Figure 8 for SDL. The  device’s Fout signal was connected to the oscilloscope which  was setup to trigger on a pulse width of <5ns. With this setup, trigger pulses would only be created if the output frequency  transitioned to the failing 120MHz.


The SOM was run with a frame rate of 128 seconds using  counter acquisition mode in which the image is created by


counting the number of oscilloscope triggers per pixel. The  SDL results are shown in Figure 9. The locations of the SDL  sites were observed to be in the PFD block.


Figure 7: Block diagram of the PLL Clock Generator device.


Figure 8: Block diagram of DUT to SOM connection through  oscilloscope.


Figure 9: SDL overlay image obtained using counter mode.


Figure 10 depicts a simplified, single ended I/Os only, block  diagram of the PFD with the SDL sites highlighted. A PFD  has two inputs and two outputs. The R input is the reference


frequency signal while FB is the VCO output through the M  counter. The PFD compares the phase difference between the  two inputs. The difference in phase results in pulses generated  at either the U or D outputs of the PFD calling for the VCO to  PUMP UP or PUMP DOWN in frequency.


Figure 10: Simplified representation of PFD block. Green  dots represent the location of the SDL sites.


Figure 11: Waveform view of the outputs (U, D) and inputs (R, FB) of the PFD block in the failing high temperature state. Many false U pulses were observed (top trace).


A progressive Focused Ion Beam (FIB) cross section, followed by TEM analysis of the resistive emitter revealed a  void inside the poly emitter contact as shown in Figure 12.


The emitter poly was pinched off as a result of an insufficient  opening between the poly spacers. This poly voiding was  observed to be systematic and seen on all the 0.3μm drawn  devices to differing degrees.


Figure 12: TEM image of poly silicon void in the emitter  contact.


Case Study Three


Case study three involves a voltage mode controller device  that marginally failed input offset current on one error  amplifier. Input offset current measured 52nA as compared to  typical values of <5nA. No leakage was observed on the input  pins in question down to 1pA. Neither photoemission analysis  nor Optical Beam Induced Resistance CHange (OBIRCH) testing revealed any abnormalities. Using large feedback  resistors, a circuit was constructed to transform the input bias  currents to a voltage and then amplify the difference as  illustrated in Figure 13.


The output voltage was buffered and then fed directly into the  TESTER SIGNAL input of the SOM. SDL was run using the  analog acquisition mode to essentially give variation mapping  of the input offset current. The SDL result is shown in Figure


14. Subsequent deprocessing revealed mechanical damage to  the substrate that traversed one of the input differential pair  PNP transistors.


A similar approach could be taken in the case of an input  offset voltage failure.  In this case a servo loop could be  employed to amplify the input offset voltage for SDL.


Figure 13: Representative schematic of test circuit. The output  voltage is proportional to the difference in input bias currents. Vout = 2.5V + IB2*R2 – IB1*R1


Figure 14: SDL overlay image obtained using analog  acquisition mode.


Case Study Four


Case study four involves a 5V, 100mA, Low Drop Out (LDO) voltage regulator with reset and enable which failed for low  out-of-specification (4.84V) output voltage at elevated  temperature. No current leakage was noted on any of the  external pins. Neither photoemission analysis nor OBIRCH  testing revealed any abnormalities.  Bench testing revealed  that at high temperature a small AC oscillation was present on  the low DC output.

Photon Emission Microscopy (PEM) is one of the most  commonly used non-destructive functional analysis and defect  localization failure analysis (FA) techniques. Technological  advancements of the microelectronic industry like multi-level  metallization and flip-chip packaging resulted in an increased  demand for FA to be performed from the chip backside, through the silicon substrate. The detector typically used   for frontside applications, namely Si-CCD could also be used   for the backside analysis but its applicability was strongly  limited by strong electron-hole related absorption of light

The model is widely used due to its simplicity but it  underestimates the maximum electric field Em and L [13]. In  addition, the model does not account for different channel  lengths. Empirically, L is found to depend on junction depth


xj, gate oxide thickness tox, effective channel length Leff [16] and VDS [7].

A more elaborate pseudo-two-dimensional model was  originally proposed by Elmansy [15]. An enhanced version  that incorporates the effects of velocity saturation and junction  depth was subsequently introduced by Ko [8]. The  determination of

Ko’s model is used to compare with experimental data  presented in a later section as it takes into account variation in  channel lengths which affected ES. In the analysis, a simple  linear approximation ES=VDSAT/L is used with reference data  ES=4x103 Vcm-1 at Leff=1.15μm [13] to determine the ES values at other channel lengths.


Experimental Procedure


Fig. 2 shows the experimental setup of the scanning optical  microscope (SOM) using a non-coherent 1340 nm wavelength  laser for the reflectance measurements in this paper. Laser  scanning confocal microscopy system minimizes unwanted  surface reflection commonly seen in backside reflectance  systems. The use of a non-coherent 1340 nm wavelength laser  is minimally invasive and mitigates interference issues [17].


Fig.2: Experimental Setup


NMOS and PMOS transistors from 0.18 μm process  technology node with channel width W of 10 μm and various  mask channel lengths Lmask are backside prepared with  substrate thickness of 350 μm. Fig. 3 shows the backside  image of an NMOS device with Lmask = 4 μm.


Fig.3: Experimental Setup


S and B are grounded. DC bias VGS is applied to G. Square  waves at frequency Vref of 7.33 kHz with 50% duty cycle is  applied across VDS, Lock-in amplification is used to enhance  the sensitivity of the response with a lock-in time constant of 2  ms. The laser dwell time at each pixel is set at 2 ms. The  acquisition time for Area D with 100 pixels by 100 pixels  takes less than a minute. The experimental spatial error is ±0.25 μm. Lower spatial error is possible with the use of solidimmersion-lens [18].


The output Vl of the lock-in amplifier is differential in nature, enhancing the signal at the reference frequency and  discriminating unwanted signals at other frequencies. This  technique relies on the dynamic response of VDS, and is  appropriately termed dynamic backside laser reflectance  modulation technique. The dynamic laser reflectance  modulations are investigated for NMOS and PMOS transistors  biased at linear, pinch-off and saturation regions for different  VGS and channel lengths.

Fig.1: Spectral sensitivity characteristics of Si-CCD detector  and InGaAs detector.


2) Devices and Sample Preparation


The devices under test (DUT) used in this study were  manufactured by Infineon Technologies AG in 120 nm process  technology. The nominal voltage for n-type and p-type FETs   was ±1.2V. he FETs were surrounded by STI (shallow trench  isolation). In addition there were also STI and metal fills (dummy fills) for mechanical stability. The thickness of gate  oxide was 3 nm, n-well thickness - 2 μm and n+ and p+ diffusions - 100 nm. Doping levels of most important   Si regions were as follows: substrate doping concentration – NSUB = 51015 cm-3, well doping concentration – NWELL = 21017 cm-3 and diffusion doping concentration – NDIFF = 21020 cm-3. Here, in this work we present the results for 0.12 μm x 10 μm n-type MOSFET.


4) Experimental Results


The samples used to obtain the results presented further in  the text were ~93 μm thick (for experiments with InGaAs  detector) and ~25 μm to ~1 μm thick (for experiments with SiCCD detector). First of all, using the set of band-pass  interference filters the spectral mode of the PEM could be  investigated in 400 nm – 1100 nm spectral regime (Si-CCD). The spectrum of PE has been recorded for several bulk Si  thicknesses and the results are summarized in fig. 4.


Fig. 4: PE spectra acquired through backside of the die for


0.12 μm channel length n-type MOSFET using Si-CCD  camera. Analysis has been performed for various thicknesses  of the silicon substrate, as obtained by FIB milling.


One can clearly see that for thicker remaining silicon layers   of silicon the slope of the spectral characteristics is higher   as the overall absorption increases with the decrease   of the light wavelength and with the increase of the thickness  of the silicon layer that the light is travelling through. Thinning  down the sample decreases the overall absorption and enables  the detection of emissions at shorter wavelengths.


Typical parameter which is usually associated with the  exponential region of the PE spectrum is the electron  temperature Te which can be extracted from the ln(I)=f(Eph) characteristics. Extracted Te values for several silicon  thicknesses are summarized in table 1. It is obvious that the  interaction of light with Si substrate significantly affects the  correctness of electron temperature extraction from the  acquired PE spectrum. Only for very thin layers of remaining  Si material the extracted electron temperatures are close to the  real temperature of electrons in the channel. For this particular  process technology this statement is true for thicknesses lower  than ~3μm. It has been experimentally validated - the  extracted electron temperatures for spectra acquired through  thinner Si layer did not differ significantly from the 3-μm-Sithickness case. Also the modelling and simulations, presented  later in the text, support this statement. This clearly shows that  to reliably extract the temperature of electrons in a particular  device, operated in a particular electrical condition, it is  necessary either to thin the substrate under the device down to  ultra low thicknesses, or correct the spectrum obtained at  greater thicknesses using the absorption data characteristic to  particular thickness and doping concentration condition. Absorption data can be obtained from literature and via simple


simulations, as presented in the next section. Although the  corrective action can be undertaken to obtain device-related  spectrum even for thicker samples, the issue of strongly  limited spectral range will remain. To extend the delectability  spectral range further into visible light regime, ultimate  thinning is the only solution.


Table 1: Extracted electron temperatures (Te) for various  silicon substrate thicknesses.


5) Silicon Absorption Modeling & Experimental  Results Calibration


In order to be able to correct the measurement results   with regard for the interaction of light with the remaining Si  material, we carried out simple simulations of e-h generationrelated and free-carrier-related absorption in silicon being  dependent on temperature, light wavelength (photon energy) and doping concentration. The chosen spectral range for  modeling was 200 nm – 1800 nm to cover spectral range of  both detectors with extra margin of 200 nm on both ends. Absorption coefficient as a function of the wavelength for two  different doping concentrations is presented in fig.5.


Fig.5: Si absorption coefficient as function of light  wavelength. Coefficient includes contributions from e-h pair  generation and absorption on free carriers. Intrinsic silicon  absorption data was taken from M.A. Green work [5], while  free carrier absorption was simulated using Green’s model


Fig.11: Demonstration of consistency of experimental & simulation data. The PE spectrum data acquired at the  thickest Si layer (case A) has been corrected for the  absorption in Si to reflect the PE spectra acquired at thinner  Si layer (case B & C).


To reconstruct the complete PE spectrum, PE spectral  experimental data has been acquired using InGaAs detector  through 93 μm of remaining Si, and using Si-CCD detector  through 1 μm of remaining Si. The measured experimental  data has been corrected with regard for the absorption in Si  using simulation data for appropriate Si thicknesses. The final  combined, calibrated PE spectrum is demonstrated in fig. 12. It shows continuous exponential increase of PE intensity as a  function of wavelength and exhibits no specific transitions. The temperature of electrons in a MOSFET at this particular


operating condition (VG=VD=1.2V) could be easily extracted  and is close to 1100 K.


Fig.12: Combined spectrum of PE acquired for 0,12x10 (μm x μm) n-type MOSFET biased with nominal voltages. Measured  spectra have been corrected with regard for optical path  transmission properties as well as for the absorption in  remaining Si material.


Summary and Conclusions


We have demonstrated spectrally resolved measurements   of photon emission from a 0.12 μm x 10 μm, n-type MOSFET  operated in saturation. Si-CCD and InGaAs detectors have  been used to obtain wider PE spectrum. In order to increase  the signal-to-noise ratio and to be able to detect the spectrum  in a wider spectral regime, especially when using Si-CCD  detector, the silicon substrate was thinned down to even ultra  low thicknesses (300 nm corresponding to STI level). The  measured PE spectra were corrected with regard for the optical  system transmission and detector spectral sensitivity. Simulations of the absorption in Si have been performed to  additionally correct the spectra with regard for the absorption  in the remaining Si substrate material. The fully corrected PE  spectrum covering wide wavelength range has been  demonstrated. The result suggests that the hypothesis that the  PE is fully electrical field related is probably true. No specific  transitions have been observed and electron temperature could  be easily extracted. Some work still needs to be done about  final standardization of PE results but the findings presented in  this document are certainly not far away from it.


In this work we have also shown that Si-CCD detector  can still be applied to backside photon emission analysis of  recent technologies, when assisted by some additional sample  preparation like mechanical or FIB substrate thinning. In FA  laboratories where PEM systems are not equipped with  InGaAs detectors, backside analysis can still be performed  provided that samples are properly prepared. Moreover, the  spectral analysis of photon emission can also be performed  using the Si-CCD detector. It can be very important in cases  when spectral information is required for defect identification. However, in case of Si-CCD additional correction with regard  for the absorption in the remaining silicon is a must. The  ability to record PE spectrum through the backside using SiCCD detector enables to obtain more complete spectral  fingerprints of up-to-date devices in various modes of


operation, related device defects, as well as fingerprints of  device abnormal functional behavior.


Micro-probing in the failing mode revealed that the PFD  provided many false U pulses causing the VCO to track higher  in frequency. Figure 11 shows the inputs and outputs of the  PFD in the failing state. Further micro-probing revealed a  difference in emitter resistance on the differential input of the  U NAND gate. This difference in emitter resistance reduced  the slew rate resulting in a longer propagation delay along the  signal path highlighted by SDL creating a race condition. At  higher temperature, the increased propagation delay of the  input would result in a momentary incorrect output of the  NAND gate that would latch causing false U pulses. These  false U pulses resulted in a higher VCO output frequency. The  SDL results indicated the signal path that would latch at high  temperature to create the higher VCO frequency.


A discrete comparator was used to monitor the output voltage  of the device. The inverting input was biased with a divide-by2 resistor divider fed from output of the DUT and a 2.5V  reference voltage was fed to the non-inverting input as

illustrated in Figure 15. The reference voltage was adjusted  during SDL to make the output of the comparator low while  the input (from the DUT) was at 5V. When the input to the  negative terminal dropped below 4.8V (DUT failing) the  comparator output would switch high creating the necessary  transition edge for the TESTER SIGNAL input.


Figure 15: Representative schematic used to drive the DALS  input. When the output from the DUT falls below 5 volts, the  output of the test circuit transitions to drive the SOM for SDL.


The comparator output voltage was buffered and then fed  directly into the TESTER SIGNAL input of the SOM. SDL  was run using the counter acquisition mode, providing  pass/fail probability for each pixel of the background image. The SDL results are shown in Figure 16. A PN junction  capacitor tub elicited a response in the test circuit and thus  failure signals when stimulated by the laser.


Figure 16: SDL overlay image highlighting the tub of a PN  junction capacitor.


The PN junction capacitor indicated by SDL was electrically  isolated and curve trace analysis indicated a resistive short  between the tub diffusion and emitter diffusion of the PN  junction capacitor. Seebeck Effect Imaging (SEI) analysis (refer to Figure 17) revealed the defect in the capacitor and  subsequent deprocessing found the defects to be emitter pipes.


Figure 17: SEI overlay image after isolating the capacitor tub  identified by SDL analysis.


A practical approach to implementing SDL on analog and  mixed signal ICs is described. By utilizing an oscilloscope, feedback loop, or discrete comparator one can easily provide  the device parameters for the SOM during SDL. Much like  photoemission and OBIRCH analysis on mixed-signal and  analog devices, SDL does not always highlight the failure, but  provides additional insight to focus the failure analysis efforts.


In this work we present spectrally resolved photon  emission microscopy (SPEM) measurements originating from  short-channel MOSFETs acquired through the backside   of the silicon substrate. Two commonly used detectors have  been chosen for the detection of electroluminescence (EL) in  the visible and near-infrared spectral regime, namely Si-CCD   and InGaAs. As the backside photon emission (PE) inspection   is strongly influenced by the absorption of light in a substrate  material, the SPEM experiments have been carried out through  thinned silicon layers as obtained by mechanical grinding   and local focused-ion-beam (FIB) assisted Si material  removal. Intrinsic Si absorption (generation of electron-hole  pairs) and absorption on free carriers have been modeled   to be able to calibrate experimental results and obtain devicerelated PE spectra. The results show no evidences of specific  transitions and lead to a conclusion that photon emission   from MOSFETs is fully electrical field related.


1) Introduction


in the Si substrate with the amount of photons absorbed being  dependent on the light wavelength, sample thickness   and doping concentration. The latter one, increasing from  technology to technology further complicated the analysis   as it also lead to the increase of free-carrier-related absorption. Other detectors, working in near-infrared (NIR) spectral  regime (like InGaAs and MCT) were more often applied  taking advantage of better transparency of Si to NIR  wavelengths. But even these detectors suffered from increased  free-carrier absorption resulting from increased doping  concentration necessary to reduce short-channel effects in the  first place. In addition, these detectors are characterized by  much worse SNR properties. Thinning down the Si substrate  material was the only solution for optical techniques like PEM  to remain applicable to backside analysis.


Spectral mode of PEM is of interest since the PEM tools  are in the market with the first results being published more  than two decades ago [1, 2]. Over years several approaches  have been made to acquire wide PE spectra from MOSFETs  and other devices but the results that have been published are  very often inconsistent with each other and usually are limited  to narrow spectrum as obtained using only single detector. In  this study we would like to present the results of the SPEM  experiments performed on 120 nm process technology devices  acquired through the backside of the Si substrate using both  Si-CCD and InGaAs detector to cover wider range of radiation  spectrum (see also fig.1). We also demonstrate how thinning  of Si substrate material from the backside, performed using  FIB process, improves the SPEM acquisition, especially while  using Si-CCD detector. In addition, we also present basic  modelling of absorption in Si and demonstrate how these  simulations can be used to calibrate the experimental results,

Variation in VGS


Fig. 7 shows the differential reflectance for the same NMOS  device with VGS  reduced to 3V at the bias  Q1 to Q5 in Fig. 4. Resulting voltage drop across the VSR is significantly higher at  Q4 and Q5 than P4 and P5, so a wider portion of the channel  is expected to have significant reflectance modulation at Q4 and Q5.


Fig.7: Differential Reflectance across Channel AA’ at VGS=3V   for NMOS transistor with, Lmask= 4 μm


A distinct peak is again observed when the NMOS transistor is  in saturation mode Q2 to Q5. Lpeak values at Q2 and Q3 are  similar to those obtained in Fig. 6 for P3 and P4. Lpeak at Q5 is visibly larger than at P5. This may be attributed to wider  VSR at Q5 as compared to P5. Q4 is seen to have slightly  larger differential reflectance than Q5. This is unexpected and  may be due to saturation of Em at Q4 and Q5.


To compare the experimental results with the analytical model, it is necessary to make a distinction between Lmask and Leff [19]. Lmask depends on the mask alignment and is optically measured  using the SOM. Leff is an electrical parameter that determines  the electrical characteristics of the MOS. It cannot be optically  measured and is used in the analytical model as given by [8]:

Fig.8: NMOS, Lmask=4μm, Different VGS


VDS-VDSAT accounts for the difference in VGS which result in  different VDSAT, and allows a better comparison to be made  between the experimental and analytical data. Within  experimental limits, the experimental data and analytical  values agree well. Simulated values of LKo increase with  larger VDS-VDSAT as a result of VSR expansion. A similar trend  is also observed for the experimental values of Leff. The  consistency in trend is observed for Leff values at different  VGS. Leff correlates well with LKo, suggesting the observed  peak to be the pinch-off point.


Variation in Channel Length


Additional experiments are carried out on two other NMOS  devices with Lmask= 2 μm and 1.4 μm respectively to  investigate the variation in channel length. The IDS-VDS characteristics of the two devices are similar to Fig. 4, except  IDS currents are larger for shorter Lmask. VDSAT at  the same VGS are comparable for different Lmask.


Fig. 9 shows the differential reflectance across the channel  AA’ at VGS = 5V and VDSAT=2.4V for NMOS with Lmask=2 μm. At VDS=3V, the NMOS transistor is in saturation and a distinct  peak near the drain-end is observed. With increased VDS, this  peak becomes more prominent, and Lpeak is seen to remain at


0.65 μm. A secondary peak near the source-end appears when  VDS= 4V and 5V, possibly due to localized heating as a result  of non-uniform doping in the channel.


Fig.9: Differential Reflectance across Channel AA’ at VGS=5V  for NMOS transistor with Lmask= 2 μm


Fig. 10 shows the differential reflectance of the channel AA’ at    VGS=5V and VDSAT=2.4V for NMOS with Lmask=1.4 μm. A  primary peak is similarly observed when the NMOS is in  saturation mode. When VDS= 3V, Lpeak is 0.22 μm. With  increased VDS, Lpeak moves towards the source. Compared to Lmask= 2 μm in Fig. 9, the secondary peak appears earlier when  VDS = 3V. This may be attributed to increased IDS current at the  shorter channel length which enabled localized heating to be  detected earlier.


Fig.10: Differential Reflectance across Channel AA’ at


VGS=5V for NMOS transistor with Lmask= 1.4 μm


Leff derived from the primary peaks in Fig. 6, 9 and 10 are  compared to analytical values in Fig. 11. The analytical model  shows L increase with larger channel length as ES is smaller  due to a larger source section and the pinch-off point  remaining at VDSAT. The experimental values for different  channel lengths follow the same trend and are in good  agreement with the analytical model.


Fig.11: Comparison of Leff with LKo for NMOS transistor at  different Channel Length


As VDS-VDSAT increases, Leff also increases, with the exception  of the experimental data at Lmask= 2.0 μm where Leff remains  constant at 0.4μm. This exception is reasonable within the  experimental limits, although a smaller error delta is desired. In general, the same trend as shown in Fig. 8 is observed. This  study further supports the hypothesis that the observed primary  peak is the pinch-off point.


Variation in MOS Type


A PMOS device with Lmask=1.4μm is studied and compared to  NMOS device with similar Lmask dimension to determine the


effect of the MOS type on dynamic reflectance modulation. Fig. 12 shows the dynamic reflectance across the channel at  VGS= -3V and VDSAT= -0.8V for PMOS device with Lmask= 1.4 μm.


Fig.12: Differential Reflectance across Channel AA’ at VGS= -3V for PMOS transistor with  Lmask = 1.4 μm


A slight shift of the primary peak away from the drain-end is  observed when VDS -2V. The PMOS transistor is in saturation  when VDS= -1.0V and -1.5V, but the primary peak was clearly  absent. This may be due to weaker optical reflectivity changes  at these bias points for PMOS transistors. A secondary peak is  also observed when VDS  -2V. Comparatively, the secondary  peak in Fig. 12 is significantly weaker. The IDS current for the  PMOS device is a fraction of those observed in Fig. 9 and 10, and possibly result in less severe localized heating.


Leff derived from NMOS and PMOS devices at Lmask =1.4 μm  are plotted in the same graph with analytical values obtained  using Ko’s model, as shown in Fig. 13. It is noted the  analytical model makes no distinction between NMOS and  PMOS.


Fig.13: NMOS versus PMOS, Lmask=1.4μm


The experimental data and simulation values agree well. The  primary peak positions for different MOS types do not appear  to differ significantly, further supporting the analytical model, and confirming the hypothesis that the primary peak is the  pinch-off point.

Effective Fault Isolation using Memory BIST and Logic BIST  Diagnostic Techniques


Rakesh Kinger, Chuck Tong, Ayyaz Chaudhry, Vijay Chowdhury*,Wenzhen Sun*, Hongqi Deng*, Steve Smith*


Broadcom Corporation


3151 Zanker Road, San Jose 95134

As device technologies are shrinking, developing  new techniques for fault isolation and defect  localization is becoming a crucial challenge. In  this paper, we have shown that by using memory  and logic BIST diagnostic techniques, we are able  to isolate the defect to two suspected nets. Thirdparty electronic design automation (EDA) tools  were used for both MBIST and LBIST hardware  implementation and for running diagnostics on the  failing part. Using experienced and sophisticated  failure analysis support, we were able to confirm  with confidence that the stuck-at zero type of  defect was caused by a signal line short at metal-5  to the ground line at metal-6.


Currently, a common theme in the industry is to  find creative ways of analyzing devices that are  becoming as complex in design as they are in  fabrication. Electrical fault isolation is essential in  the production of workable products. Techniques  such as emission microscopy and e-beam and  laser voltage probing [1,2] can tremendously help  with a certain genre of failures. The use of more  automated test program development, however, is  crucial for gaining an understanding of the  failures when the fault localization tools  mentioned earlier do not provide clear diagnostics [3,4]. In this paper, we discuss a customer return


part for which the failure associated with memory  and logic was narrowed down further through the  use of MBIST and LBIST diagnostics. Two dataout nets from the memory macro layout  examination indicated that the defect site would  involve about 500 μm of interconnect and up to  five vias. A combination of microprobing and  deprocessing techniques was considered to  localize the failing net.


Electrical Fault Isolation Procedure


Because the part failure manifested itself as a  failure in both memory itself and the shadow logic  around the memory, the first step in isolating the  failure was to run memory BIST diagnostics.


Section 1 : Memory BIST Diagnostics


The MBIST diagnostics run clearly showed that  for one of the memory instances, there was a  mismatch on 3 data bits (#131, 125, 124). The  expected data from the memory was “1” but  actual value sampled by the controller logic was“0”. Figure 1 shows the detailed results of the  MBIST diagnostics run that helped us to pin point  to the three failing nets. In order to confirm that  the nature of the failure was not speed related and  was actually stuck-at type defect, we ran MBIST  diagnostics using very slow speed (@1MHz) JTAG clock.

Section 2 : LBIST Diagnostics


Because these suspected failing nets were also  captured in the pipeline flops on the output of the  failing memory, we also ran logic BIST (LBIST) diagnostics to get more failure data. LBIST  diagnostics on the failing part showed that for the  same memory instance that was failing MBIST:


1. There was a stuck-at-0 defect on the cone of the  logic feeding to din[124]. This defect manifested  itself as stuck-at-0 on the scan flop (BR_reg_124) present inside the memory. Because the cone of  logic feeding to din[124] was huge, it was hard to  pinpoint the exact location. Therefore, this net  was not a good candidate for failure analysis (FA).


The good correlation between the diagnostic  results from both memory BIST and logic BIST  techniques gave us sufficient confidence in the  accuracy of the results to further conduct failure  analyses on these two suspected nets. Based on  the diagnostic results shown earlier,

Type of defect: stuck-at-0


Failure Analysis Approach:


Through the use of MBIST and LBIST  diagnostics, the cause of the memory and logic  failure of the customer return part was further  narrowed down to two data-out nets from the  memory macro. The original failure analysis plan  was to use probing techniques to verify the stuckat-0 fault and then to use fault isolation techniques  to isolate where the faults are. Upon decapsulation  of the device, however, we noticed some  impressionable scratches (Fig. 3) in the region of  interest (Fig. 4). Profilometry was used to  determine the depth of the scratches (Fig. 5–7). The measured depth of the scratches was <0.1 nm. The fact that, during the decapping process, the  chemical seeped through and eroded metal-7 lines  underneath the passivation scratches suggested that what profilometer measured was the depth of  the indent caused by the scratches, not the depth  of the cracking caused by the scratches. After  decapsulation, the sample was retested, and now it  failed at even more vectors, which was not  surprising given the nature of cracks and  inadvertent seepage of chemicals during  decapsulation. A scanning electron microscope (SEM) inspection of the scratch surface showed  eroded metal-7 lines underneath the cracked  passivation. Fig. 8 shows the region where one  surface scratch


intersected with the failing net dout[131] at metal5. This area was selected for precision cross  sectioning in a dual focused ion beam (DFIB) system, and a short was found between the metal6 VSS line and the failing net dout[131] at metal5.

we concluded that performing failure analyses on  the two potential failing nodes shown below can  help isolate the defect:


1. dout[131] ->


coreInstance_mmu_mmu_pp_mem_ram1_pmmu_pp_mem1_LV_dout_reg_ 131/d


2. dout[125] ->


coreInstance_mmu_mmu_pp_mem_ram1_pmmu_pp_mem1_LV_dout_reg_ 125/d


A novel fault isolation technique using memory  BIST and logic BIST diagnostics, combined with a  judicious use of failure analysis techniques, was  effectively employed in localizing a defect on a  very critical customer return part. The results of  these diagnostics, which correctly identified the  defect location in the silicon, enabled us to  determine the root cause of the problem and take  necessary corrective action to avoid experiencing  the same problems in the future.

DRAM Static Refresh Weak Cell Characterization and Structure Analysis


Sung Ho Lee, Yong Ho Yoo, Tae Jung Park, Jin Choi, Ju Hyeon Ahn, Seok Sik Kim,


Chang-Jin Kang,  Seok Woo Nam, Joo Young Lee, Gyo Young Jin


Semiconductor R&D Center Samsung Electronics Co., Ltd.


San #16, Banwol-Dong, Hwaseong-City, Gyeonggi-Do 445-701, Korea


Tel : +82-31-208-3347, Fax : +82-31-208-0699, E-mail : lsh26@samsung.com


Data retention characteristic is one of the most critical issues  in low power DRAMs because it determines idle currents of  self-refresh operation. Compared to normal healthy cells, a few  ppm orders of cells in a tail distribution have much higher  leakage currents.  The origin of the leaky cells (so called weak  cells or tail cells) has been quite arguable for the past decades [1, 2], but it should be scrutinized in order to achieve long data  retention time.  In this paper, we have thoroughly investigated  the behavior of the retention weak cells using a newly  generated combination program and TEM analysis so as to  discover and explain their origins


Conventional methods [ 3,4 ] use a sequential analysis to  investigate the connection between electrical failures and  structural abnormalities by using both electrical test and  following TEM analysis for the characteristic failure cells.  These methods are comprised of time-consuming works and  interpretational difficulties since the results cannot be clearly  explained or matched the electrical characteristics.


Therefore, we devised a combination program (multi pattern  test program) for more precise analytical method, which  provides exact addresses of tested cells.  In the process of  electrical test, we can keep track of exact addresses for the  failure cells (weak or tail cells for the retention characteristics  in this paper) by using a time-sweep-test pattern in a DRAM  tester (Mosaid MS4205).


A new analysis method of tracing and   analyzing data retention weak cells


The electrical nodes in a DRAM cell are defined in Fig. 1 and  the flowchart of the combination program including the timeauthorized-test-pattern is described in Fig. 2.  A DRAM cell  has a Gate, a Body (an Active), a Bit Line, a Capacitor, and  Plate Poly. A bias condition to each node is different.


Figure 1: Schematic view describing each electrical node in a  DRAM.


For the retention characteristic analysis in this work, all cells  are first classified as either passed cells or failed cells at a  specific data retention time. After saving the address  information of each failure cell, the bias condition applied to  each cell node is varied [or stepped] from 0.3V to 1.0V for the  cell plate, from 0.4V to 0.8V for the bit-line, -0.4V to -1.0V to  the body, and -0.1V to -0.5V to the negative gate, respectively  while repeating the test.   The cells that are changed according  to the differently applied voltage (Body-Bias Voltage, Negative Gate-Bias Voltage, Plate Poly Voltage, Bit Line  Voltage) are counted, which is described in Fail Bit Density  Graph of Fig. 3 that explains the trends of the weak cells.


We classified all weak cell by a new analysis. If the weak cells  either passed or failed by change of a negative-gate biased  voltage, we called the weak cells to negative-gate-biasdependant weak cells (NBDC). If the weak cells either passed  or failed by change of a body biased voltage, we called the  weak cells to body-bias-dependant cells (BBDC). In the same  way, we classified plate-poly-bias-dependant cells (PPDC) and  bit-line-bias-dependant cells (BLDC).


We applied a new method to real analysis. We can get  information of leakage components.  The major component is


NBDC.  Most of these weak cells changed according to the  negative gate-bias voltage.  Second component is BBDC,which changed according to the body-bias voltage.  We have  classified each weak cell to each biased voltage condition.


There are few PPDC and BLDC. Weak cells were composed  of three major components which are NBDC, BBDC, and etc.  Etc cells are not related with any biased-voltage.


Figure 2: The flowchart of a combination program for tracing  and analyzing data retention weak  cells .


Figure 3: The cumulative data retention curve (failure  probability) and histogram classified by leakage components  through the analysis proposed in this paper.


Results and Discussions


Variation in VDS


The dynamic laser reflectance modulations of an NMOS  device with Lmask = 4μm is studied for different VDS. The IDSVDS characteristic of the device is experimentally measured  using a parametric analyzer, as shown in Fig. 4. VDSAT is  empirically determined from the electrical measurements.


Fig.4: IDS-VDS Characteristics of NMOS Transistor with Lmask= 4μm


The bias points of VDS at VGS= 5V are selected to cover the full  operating modes: P0: unbiased, P1: linear, P2: pinch-off, P3-P5: saturation of the NMOS transistor. The dynamic laser  reflectance modulation of Area D at each state of bias, P0 to  P5 is transformed in pseudo-color and shown in Fig. 5.


Fig.5: Pseudo-color Image of  Area D for NMOS with Lmask=4 μm


Negligible reflectance modulation is observed when the  NMOS device is unbiased P0 or in linear mode P1. At pinchoff P2, a faint reflectance modulation is detected near the  drain-end. In saturation mode, P3 to P5, a strong reflectance  modulation of the channel near the drain-end is clearly  observed, increasing in intensity with increased VDS. The  observations support the hypothesis that reflectance  modulation is significant when VSR is present.


Characterization of the MOS channel at different modes of  operation has been successfully carried out using dynamic  backside laser reflectance modulation technique for variations  in VGS, channel lengths and MOS device types. The reflectance  modulations are distinct at different operating modes, with a  primary peak observed near the drain-end of the channel when  the MOS devices are in saturation mode.


The Pseudo-Two-Dimensional Model is used to compare the  length of the velocity saturation region with the experimental  data based on the hypothesis that the primary peak is the  pinch-off point. The experimental and analytical values are in  good agreement and supports the hypothesis that the primary  peaks observed in dynamic reflectance modulation  experiments is the pinch-off point. The backside laser  reflectance modulation technique is therefore able to  characterize the MOS device operations.

Atom probe tomography (APT) has the required threedimensional nanoscale compositional measurement  capability  and has been used in materials characterization and materials  science for more than 40 years [1]. Using a laser to assist field  evaporation of materials in the atom probe was demonstrated ~30 years ago [2], but only recently has APT begun to see  widespread usage in the areas of semiconductors [3-6] and  ceramics [7-11]. Recent work covering different individual  aspects of semiconductor devices (i.e., doping  concentrations/profiles, high-κ gate stacks, various silicides, etc.), and often well correlated with transmission electron  microscopy (TEM), scanning electron microscopy (SEM) or  secondary ion mass spectroscopy (SIMS), has been very  successful, especially for blanket structures (for a recent  review see [5]).


APT has also been applied to transistor and FINFET-type  structures [12-14]; however, these structures usually have been  stopped at some point in the fabrication process in order to  accommodate the analysis. There are many opportunities in  semiconductor processing where APT analysis of a finished  product is desirable; competitive analysis and failure analysis  are two good examples, and only recently have APT results  been obtained from fully processed “off-the-shelf” transistor  structures that are part of a finished product [15, 16]. This  paper explores the feasibility of APT analysis for fully  packaged integrated-circuit microelectronic devices by  detailing the various options available in specimen preparation  and the resulting analyses.


Specimen preparation is a key aspect of many analytical  techniques, and APT is no exception.  Advances in focusedion-beam (FIB) preparation techniques, primarily to drive fast  and reliable TEM sample preparation, have been an enabling  technology for APT as well.  In this work, specimen


preparation was carried out in an FEI Novalab dual-beam™FIB instrument with an Omniprobe AP200™micromanipulator. STEM observations were performed along  the [110] orientation of the Si substrate with a JEM ARM  200F™ operating at 200 kV equipped with a Schottky field  emission gun. APT data collection was performed on a LEAP 4000X HR from Cameca Instruments, Inc. APT was  performed in 200 kHz pulsed-laser mode with a pulse energy  of 100 pJ and spot size (four sigma) at the specimen of ~3 m. The specimen base temperature was 50 K and the ion detection  rate was 0.20% (an average of 1 ion detected for every 500  laser pulses).


Figure 1.  (a) The general layout of the chip ready for  depackaging where the red circle marks a potential region of  interest. (b) SEM image after depackaging, and (c) SEM  image showing specific region to be prepared for APT  analysis using FIB lift-out and sharpening methods.


The specimen examined in this work is a device from a  commercial 32-nm technology chip (Intel i5-650) which was  purchased at a retail outlet. Following depackaging to (approximately) metal-1 (performed by SVT Technologies


[17]), the main portion of the specimen preparation was  accomplished using FIB milling with a gallium source [18-21],which has become a standard methodology for APT  preparation over the past decade and has revolutionized atom  probe tomography specimen preparation (for a review, see


[22]). Figure 1 shows a sequence of images from the  depackaging procedure, including (a) the general layout of the  chip, (b) and (c) SEM images showing a specific region to be  prepared for APT analysis.


Specimen Preparation


The goal of this work is to take an off-the-shelf  microelectronics product and perform APT analysis on various  device-level components. In order to remove individual  devices from the product, suitable gross deprocessing (depackaging, dry and wet electrochemistry for selective  material etching, etc.) must first be performed to place the  devices near the wafer surface. Then, FIB-based preparation  can be used to isolate <50 nm heterogeneous devices, place the  devices in a geometry suitable for APT, and finally, perform  the analysis.


APT specimens are required to take the form of very sharp  needles (<200 nm diameter at the region of interest) in order to  achieve the electric fields necessary for field-evaporation of  surface material.  Standard FIB-based specimen preparation is  summarized in Fig. 2 and is used to remove material from a  planar specimen, attach the material to carrier microposts, and  sharpen the material into a suitable dimension for APT  analysis. These steps are relatively straightforward and are  reviewed in detail elsewhere [5]. For optimal preparation of  microelectronics devices, however, more complex methods  have been developed, and these methods are presented below. Gross deprocessing often results in device-level targets that  reside below some undesirable inter-layer dielectric, SiN  spacers or other dielectric materials found at or above gate  level.  This issue can be overcome by use of further FIB-based


Figure 2.  .  Illustration of the basic steps involved in standard  FIB-based preparation. (a) The first three FIB milling cuts  produce a wedge-shaped cantilever of material for attachment  to a micromanipulator. (b) A piece of the wedge shown in (a) has been attached to a silicon microtip using Pt. (c) and (d) show selected shapes of the specimen during the annular  milling process used to sharpen the specimen.  Note that the  specimen is not entirely finished in (d) as the desired final  radius of curvature must be <100 nm.


Figure 3. Illustration of the basic steps involved during FIBbased device deprocessing.  (a) and (b) A wedge containing  features some depth below the wedge surface is oriented so  that the ion beam can progressively remove material from the  top surface using the cleaning cross-section method. (c) The  original wedge surface is shown before deprocessing, and (d) after exposing the device elements below the original surface.


(e) The original wedge surface is shown in cross-section with  the bright device structures located some 100 nm below the  top of the image.  (f) The overall objective with this process is  to remove the dielectric material in the red region and replace  it with a desirable capping material.

ring.  It is possible that Phosphorus out-gassed from the V-ring  during high temperature processing and moved upward into  the Argon anneal boat, resulting in N-type contamination. This  contamination caused resistivity changes at the wafer edge, subsequently causing leakage failures in these areas.  Single  lot yield analysis showed that wafers located near the V-ring  during Argon anneal had consistently higher yield loss due to  leakage, which verified the failure analysis findings.


Figure 6: SIMS analysis on dummy wafer observed the  presence of Phosphorus.


This paper described a doping related low yield case with  symptoms of prime die pin leakage failures near the wafer  edge.  A systematic problem solving approach was used based  on the application of a variety of FA techniques such as  electrical curve tracing, SRP, SIMS, and Chemical Analysis  coupled with extensive Fab investigations.  These techniques  transformed an invisible defect into a visible one and led to the  identification of the root cause and understanding of the  failure mechanism. The wafer edge leakage failure was found  to be the result of N-type contamination of the substrate due to  Phosphorus outgassing from the V-ring during the high  temperature Argon anneal process.


The authors would like to thank our fellow colleagues from  Fab 7 Production Engineering Group, Process Integration  Engineering Group, and our wafer supplier for fruitful  discussions during this investigation.
