From the Store Shelf to Device-Level Atom Probe Analysis:  An Exercise in Feasibility


D. J. Larson, D. Lawrence, D. Olson, T. J. Prosa, R. M. Ulfig, D. A. Reinhard, P. H. Clifton and T. F. Kelly


Cameca Instruments Inc., 5500 Nobel Drive, Madison, WI 53711 USA


W Lefebvre


Université de Rouen, Saint Etienne du Rouvray 76801  FRANCE


Resolving semiconductor device features and defects over the  past few decades has been primarily achieved through use of  electron microscopy, ion microscopy, various X-ray  technologies, as well as some light optical techniques. As the  industry has moved further into the nanoelectronics realm, the  use of these methods has become more challenging due to the  scale of the devices of interest and limitations of what have  become standard techniques within the industry.


A structural analysis by using TEM


Conventional methods use a simple analysis to make a  conclusion. After we got an address of weak cells by tester, we  made a sample by TEM. A new analysis method use a  combination program by tester, and then the each class of the  weak cells in Fig. 3 was analyzed structurally by TEM.  The  structural analysis by TEM makes the failure modeling more


efficient, because we already knew the electrical characteristic  of the weak cells.


Fig. 3 shows both a cumulative curve and histogram for the  retention failure probability, where the histogram is obtained  by classification of the leakage components for the retention  weak cells.  The classification is completed by implementing a  novel combination program to analyze the specific behavior  depending on each bias condition.  That combination program  with a well-made macro file provides perfect bit addresses at  each stage during stepping-up or -down the applied bias in a  body, gate, plate poly, and bit line as summarized in Figure 2.


With an interpretation of the results, the failure cells can be  classified as either strong body-bias-dependant cells (BBDC) or strong negative-gate-bias-dependant cells (NBDC) and  something else through analyzing the behavior involved in  each bias condition.


In a Vertical TEM Analysis, We get a TEM image of a weak  cell (a fail address cell).  At the same time, we get four TEM  images of the two left and right adjacent cells (pass cell).


Finally, we get the 5 cell TEM image.  Each TEM image gives  us much information.  For example, we got the value of Gate  CD, Gate Trench Depth, Spacer SiN thickness, Gate Oxide  thickness, Contact CD, Contact Recess Value, and so on.


We analyzed a larger group of each class of weak cells.  We  compared many values of weak cells with pass cells.


Surprisingly, we got the result that weak cells related to the  body-biased voltage have a relation with a value of a contact  recess (the red text in Fig. 4).  Every weak cell has a high  contact recess value.  But weak cells related to the negativegate biased voltage have no relation with the value of contact  recess.


Figure 4: Two types of cells showing major dependence on  either body bias or negative gate bias are classified by storage  node contact recess depth.


Next, we tried to make a plane TEM analysis, so we got a value of Active CD, the distance of each active, an overlap  dimension of contact with active, an angle of an active, and so  on.  We got the result that weak cells related to the negativegate biased voltage have a relation with a value of an angle of  an active.  Every weak cell has a small value of an angle of an active.


Figure 5: The BBDCs and NBDCs are classified by active  island corner angle of storage node by using plane TEM  analysis.


We further investigated the classified cells in order to verify  root-causes by using TEM.  In Vertical TEM analysis, we can  know the recess value of a cell is related with a junction  leakage.  BBDC have a high recess value among the TEM  sample. We already know that the distance between body and contact is very important factor to a junction leakage.  In Plane  TEM analysis, we can know the active angle of fail cells is  related with NBDC.  NBDC have a small value of active  angle. An electrical field concentrated the edge of an active. As small as the value of angle, the leakage will be enhanced by  a thin gate oxide thickness. As consequences discovered  interesting results as followings.


1.        BBDC, considered as junction leakage dominant cells  in general, majorly shows deeper recess of the storage node  contact as shown in Fig. 4 [5].


2.        NBDC, considered as GIDL dominant cells in general, characteristically shows sharper corner angles for the active as  shown in Fig. 5 [6].


The results indicate that major portion of retention weak cells  is still either BBDC or NBDC.  In addition, the storage node  contact recess or the sharpness of active is considerably


responsible for the true origin of the junction leakage or GIDL, respectively.


After we have thoroughly investigated and categorized the  behavior of the retention weak cells using a newly generated  combination program, we did analysis on weak cells by TEM  cross section methods.  The BBDC sensitive cells are related  to the value of the contact recess and the NBCD sensitive cells  are related to the angle of the active.  So, The BBDC failure  rate was improved by reduction of the contact recess and the  NBDC failure rate was improved by increasing the thickness  of the gate oxide and the active angle sharpness reduction, which decreased the number of refresh weak cells dramatically  and enhanced the yield.


[1] Kinam Kim and Jooyoung Lee, Electron Device Letters,vol. 30, No. 8, 2009


[2] T. Hamamoto, S. Sugiura, and S. Sawada, IEEE Transaction on Electron Devices, vol. 45, No. 6, 1998.


[3] Kinam Kim and J.Y. Lee “A New Investigation of Data  Retention Time in Truly Nanoscaled DRAMs,” IEEE  Device Letters, vol. 30, no. 8, AUG 2009


[4] Onishi, S “TEM Analysis of failed bits and improvement  of data retention properties in megabitDRAMS,” Reliability Physics Symposium, 1990. 28th Annual Proceedings., International


[5] Sze, Physics of Semiconductor Devices. 3rd. 83page.


[6] Hori, Gate Dielectrics and MOS ULSIs, 111page.


A Systematic Failure Analysis to Reveal the Mystery of Lower N-well Resistance


David Zhu, Soh Ping Neo, Alfred Quah, Ghim Boon Ang, Lei Zhu, Yanhua Huang,   Hong Tak Koo, Moi Kian Yau, Ma Hninhnin, and Nagalingam Dayanand


GLOBALFOUNDRIES, Singapore Pte. Ltd.


Failure Analysis Group, QRA


60 Woodlands Industrial Park D Street2, Singapore  DavidZhu@globalfoundries.com


In this paper, we will describe a low yield case which revealed  itself as leakage failures near the wafer edge.  A systematic  problem solving approach was used based on the application  of a variety of FA techniques such as electrical curve tracing, Spreading Resistance Probing (SRP), Secondary Ion Mass  Spectrometry (SIMS), and Chemical Analysis coupled with  extensive Fab investigations.  These techniques transformed  an invisible defect into a visible one, leading to a full  resolution of the issue with good understanding of the failure  mechanism and the root cause.  We will show that the wafer  edge leakage was the result of N-type contamination of the  substrate due to Phosphorus outgassing from the V-ring during  the high temperature Argon anneal process.


As the CMOS technology minimum feature size continues to  shrink and the transistor densities steadily increase, the defects  that cause device failures are also decreasing in size.  These  defects are frequently invisible.  For example, a subtle defect  or process anomaly can cause leakage through the gate oxide, poly line edge roughness can cause short channel effects in  minimally sized devices, and residual interfacial films can  result in high resistance vias or contacts.  Device level doping  related defects in particular are some of the most difficult nonvisible defects (NVD) to detect.  Doping profiles, though  rather subtle, are critical for normal device function.  Therefore, identification of doping issues is very important for  failure analysis as it drives yield improvement.  Currently  there are two analysis methods used for failures related to  doping issues.  One method uses a traditional wet chemical  stain combined with TEM/SEM inspection to reveal dopant  profiles, and another recently developed method is TEM  electron holography to delineate junction profile with no  chemical treatment required.


We will also present a doping related low yield case with  symptoms of prime die pin leakage failures and corresponding  Electrical Test failures with lower N-well resistance. A


systematic problem solving approach based on the application  of a variety of FA techniques led to the identification of a  previously invisible defect.  In the process, we also gained an  understanding of the failure mechanism and its root cause.  First, electrical failure analysis coupled with extensive Fab  investigations showed that the lower N-well resistance is  caused by an incoming substrate issue.  Second, additional  analysis using Spreading Resistance Probing (SRP), Secondary Ion Mass Spectrometry (SIMS) and Chemical  Analysis revealed higher substrate resistivity due to  Phosphorus contamination resulting from compensation  effects.  Finally, we identified that outgassing from V-ring  during high temperature Argon anneal process was the source  of the Phosphorus contamination.


We encountered a low yield issue due to leakage failures.  All  of these low yield wafers exhibited high failure density near  the wafer edge.  Looking at inline process control data, we  noticed that Electrical Test (ET) maps of N-well resistor  structures showed regions of abnormally low resistance  correlating to the maps of the leakage failures.  For this  reason, failure analysis was performed on the abnormal ET  structure instead of the much more complex prime die, allowing us to avoid extensive electrical analysis and fault  isolation on the prime die.


Electrical failure analysis was performed on the ET structure   of the N-well resistor using two curve tracer probes.  These  measurements confirmed a much lower resistance for failed  sites compared to good sites as shown in Figure 1.  After this  discovery, we needed to determine whether the lower N-well  resistance was caused by the N-well implant itself or by some  other kind of defect.


After looking through the raw I-V curve trace data we found  that the current flowing out from Pad4 (I_pad4) was much  smaller than the current flowing into Pad5 (I_pad5).  This  indicated the remaining portion of the Pad5 current was  flowing out of the structure through some other path.  A layout  study showed the N-well resistor sits in a P-type substrate, so  current leaking to P-sub was the only possibility.


Figure 1:  I-V curve tracing confirmed abnormally low N-well  resistance for failing sites compared to good sites.


In order to verify this hypothesis, we repeated curve trace  testing on this N-well resistor, but added a third probe to  monitor the P-sub current (I_sub).  The results of these  additional tests are shown in Figure 2 and Table 1.  It can be  seen that current flow from Pad5 to P-sub is very large for bad  dice (>30μA) compared to good dice (<8nA).  Further I-V  curve tracing while probing Pad5 with respect to P-sub  showed N-well to P-sub junction leakage as shown in Figure


3.  In order to identify whether the leakage was caused by the  N-well or the P substrate, electrical analysis on another similar  P+/N-well/P-sub test structure was performed, These results  also showed N-well to P-sub leakage, and the junction of P+ to  N-well exhibited a normal diode curve with no leakage  detected.  From these analyses, we came to the conclusion that  the leakage was caused by a substrate doping issue instead of  the N-well implant.


Figure 2:  The cross sectional structure of the N-well resistor.


To better understand why N-well to P-sub leakage will cause  lower N-well resistance, let’s review the methods used in  inline resistance measurements. Generally the inline resistance


measurement method uses a constant current If that is forced  into one pad of the test structure, and voltage drop Vm across  the test structure is measured.  The resistance Rm can be  calculated from Vm/If.  For this case as some portion of the  current If leaks into the substrate, the measured voltage


Table 1:  The curve trace test current distribution for the Nwell resistor with I_pad5 biased to 1.2 V and I_pad4 and Isub  at 0 V.  Note that I_sub for bad sites is much larger than for  good sites.


Figure 3: I-V curve tracing showed abnormally high N-well to  P-sub leakage in the failing sites.


drop V’m across the test structure is Rm(If-ILeak ), thus the  calculated N-well resistance is Rm(If-ILeak)/If, which indicates  the measured resistance is always smaller than the actual value  by the factor (If-ILeak)/If..  This factor is smaller than unity if  the leakage exists between the N-well and P-sub even though  the N-well resistance itself remains normal. With more wafers  found showing yield loss due to wafer edge leakage failures, Fab inline commonality analysis was done.  This revealed that  all of the affected wafers came from the same supplier.  Further investigation showed that all affected wafers were  processed in the same Argon anneal boat.  This strongly  suggested that this low yield issue was caused by the substrate.

characterization of the failing bit through examination of both  the forward and reverse bitcell characteristics [5]. A distinct  difference was observed in the forward vs. reverse bitcell  characteristics of the fail bit as compared to adjacent normally  behaving bitcells, as shown in Fig. 4.


Figure 4: AFP forward (solid) Id-Vg characteristics shown for  a fail bit (red), compared to an adjacent good bit (green) were  consistent with expected results, but reverse (dashed) Id-Vg  characteristics showed a much larger difference.


Given the lack of a physical defect on a failing bit, and the  notable difference in bitcell forward and reverse Id-Vg  characteristics as obtained by AFP, a hypothesis was  developed in an attempt to explain the failure in view of the  known notable characteristics, namely--


1. Write/erase cycling provides a method to build negative  charge. Writing the bitcell consists of electron hot carrier  injection (HCI) drain-side programming. Either “stray” or “misdirected” hot electrons could potentially build into a  significant negative charge, near the drain side of the bit.


2. A pocket of negative charge could explain the lack of any  notable physical issue at the fail bit. The lack of a physical  issue being observed, however, raises the question of how and  where charge is being stored.


3. A pocket of negative charge stored at the drain side of the  fail bit could explain the anomalous difference between  forward and reverse Id-Vg curves. Such a behavior is  purposely used in reading flash bitcells implementing a local  charge storage medium [6,7,8].


Figure 5: AFP fail bit forward (solid) and reverse (dashed) Id-Vg characteristics as initially collected (red), following  additional UV-erase and a four hour 125C bake (blue), and  after a 1 hour 250C bake (black). The characteristics defined  in green highlight the changes introduced by the 250C bake, and are compared to a good bit in Table 1.


Table 1: A comparison of parameters for a fail bit and a good  bit, as shown in Fig. 5, shows the notable effect of the 1 hour  250C bake. While a good bit was affected by the bake as well, the impact of the bake was greater on the failing bit, affecting  even the difference in bitcell forward-reverse behavior.


With the notable response of the fail bit to the 1 hour 250C  bake, where both the anomalous transconductance and the  forward-reverse Vg delta characteristics were affected, it was  clear that an anomalous charge “defect” was at fault. Analysis  work thus pivoted based on this data to understand the


behavior of charge at the failing bit, and the response of the  bitcell to the bake was explored.


Continued 250C baking and “readouts” of the Id-Vg response  of the failing bit and correlation bits were taken to ~504 hours. As expected based on the 1 hour 250C bake response of the  fail bit, further improvement was consistently observed in the  fail bitcell Id-Vg characteristics. Figure 6 shows the Vg delta  between forward and reverse bitcell Id-Vg characteristics for the fail bit and an adjacent good bit across the bake time  evaluation. Figure 7 shows the final bitcell Id-Vg curves  collected after 504 hours of 250C bake, at the same scale as  Fig. 4; comparison shows the significant improvement in  bitcell characteristics.


Figure 6: The delta between forward and reverse Id-Vg  characteristics continued to diminish over the 250C bake time  for the fail bit (red), while a minimal delta was observed for a  good bit (green).


Figure 7: AFP forward (solid) and reversed (dashed) Id-Vg  characteristics following ~504 hrs of 250C bake, shown for  the fail bit (red) and an adjacent good bit (green). Minimal  difference now exists between the bitcells.


Fail bit


Good bit


During the continued AFP data collection and 250C bake  evaluation, further refinement of the anomalous charge pocket  hypothesis was considered. An open question remained where  the anomalous charge storage was occurring, with no  physically evident anomalous structure apparent. A likely  candidate for storage of anomalous charge was considered to  be sidewall spacer nitride, as nitride is used as a localized  storage medium in various forms of flash memory, with nonvolatile devices that use such a structure, even with HCI  programming [6,7,9,10]. Retention characteristics of such  devices have been explored as well [7,8,9,11,12], and share  similarities with the observed characteristics collected on the  fail bit with AFP. The most logical explanation of the results  incorporated anomalous charge storage on the spacer nitride, with the likely cause to be an issue with the lateral edge of the  implant at the drain side of the fail bit which did not extend far  enough past the spacer nitride. Such an issue would enhance


the probability of “stray” electrons during programming being


captured on the spacer nitride, and building over cycling on  just a single bit.


To confirm this hypothesis, staining analysis was performed on  the initial TEM cross section shown previously in Fig. 3 in  order to delineate junction locations. TEM results, shown in  Fig. 8 (fail bit), support the experimental results and  hypothesized fail cause, with the fail bit lateral drain-side  junction appearing retracted in a TEM sample including 8-10 other normally-behaving bits appearing as shown in the TEM  image of Fig. 9.


Figure 8: TEM cross section of fail bit after junction stain  performed, showing a retracted drain diffusion edge more  aligned with the fail bit spacer nitride than with floating gate  poly edge (indicated by dashed yellow lines).


Figure 9: TEM cross section of a good bit after junction stain  performed, showing a drain diffusion edge aligned with the  bitcell floating gate poly edge (indicated by dashed yellow  lines).


The authors would like to express their thanks and  appreciation to all of the individuals and groups at Freescale  who helped make the aspects of this analysis possible, including product test mode accessibility, TEM sample  preparation, bake setup access, TEM staining analysis  techniques, and many helpful discussions with technology  R&D teams.

Identification of Extension Implant Defect in Sub-Micron CMOS ICs - Analysis Technique, Model, and Solution


Yuk L. Tsang1*, Giri Nallapati2, Ron Skarupa1, and Brian Anthony1


1Freescale Semiconductor Inc., Austin, TX


* ra0149@freescale.com


2Now with Qualcomm, San Diego, CA


A major failure mode known as ROMBIST (logic and  memory) that topped the yield loss pareto and plagued a


0.13 m technology chipset production was analyzed. This  MCUROM fail exhibited a unique failing pattern where every  4th row failed. A detailed electrical microprobing analysis had  led to an exact model that was able to explain the failure  mechanism. This model pinpointed a PFET extension implant  blockage.  Subsequent silicon etch analysis directed by the  model prediction brought out the non visual defect (NVD). Inline inspection at the extension implant photo step identified  the root cause. A greater than 45% reduction in die cost was  realized in lots with photo resist fixes implemented.


Flash memory is exceedingly more pervasive and expansive in  its use, thanks in large part to both the flexibility it provides to  store and update contents, and the nonvolatile aspect of the  memory. As it expands in prevalence and size in certain  markets, some aspects of the memory are critical for the end  user—typically those involving write/erase cycling endurance  and data retention. Great effort in understanding and modeling  interrelated and complex fail behaviors in flash memory is  often made; for example, much work has focused on the low  temperature data retention effects on flash reliability [1,2]. An  understanding of these aspects of flash memory failures down  to root cause determination is critical so that defects can be  eliminated during manufacturing, and verified as eliminated  during product testing prior to customer delivery and use.


The complexity of embedded flash memory—in process  integration, in device structure, in device operational  characteristics, and in overall system architecture—often  provides many challenges during failure analysis. Successful  root-cause analysis often depends on the electrical  understanding of the failure. Extensive electrical  characterization of flash failures in combination with carefully  orchestrated physical analysis techniques must be employed to  find the root cause [3]. Often, the defect causing the failure can  be novel and requires substantial electrical characterization  data to substantiate the finding and place it into the context of  the failure [4]. With this consideration, failure analysis of flash  memory write/erase cycling or data retention failures may be  quite complex.


Failure and Initial Analysis


During room temperature flash write/erase cycling, a small  number of units were observed to fail for exceeding the


number of erase pulses allowed in a single cycle of a block. The observed erase pulse count (EPC) behavior for affected  fail unit blocks is shown in Fig. 1, and contrasts with the  expected typical cycling EPC behavior of unaffected fail unit  blocks. Evaluation of each failure showed the anomalous EPC  to be caused by a single bit in the flash block which did not  erase as quickly as the rest of the bits in the block.


Figure 1: EPC behavior across write/erase cycle, showing  failing (red) and typical (green) EPC behavior by flash block.


An analog test mode allowing external control over a specified  wordline and bitline in the flash module was utilized to collect  Id-Vg data. Id-Vg data for failing bits and adjacent passing  bits in the low-Vt erased state, are shown in Fig. 2.


Figure 2: Test mode Id-Vg characteristics shown for two  failing single bits (red/red dash), compared to adjacent  passing bits (green/green dash).


Fail bits


Passing bits Fail blocks


Typical blocks


The test mode Id-Vg data shown in Fig. 2 provides insight into  the slow to erase behavior of the bits. The lower  transconductance (gm) caused the bits to fail erase verification, leading to the application of additional erase pulses. Given the  observed EPC trend vs. cycle count shown in Fig. 1, it was  apparent that the gm of failing single bits diminished during  write-erase cycling.


The physical locations of the few failing bits was determined  and examined for any similarity in the physical layout that  might provide insight. No physical commonality to the fail bit  locations was apparent. Given the observed anomalous gm,TEM cross sections were performed on two failing single bits, both parallel and perpendicular to the control gate, to  determine if any subtle physical anomalies were present to account for the failure. No notable physical difference was  observed to account for the lower gm or the diminished gm  with cycling, despite a detailed comparison of the physical  structure of the failing single bits to both neighboring good  bits and prior reference bitcells. Figure 3 shows a fail bit TEM  cross section view perpendicular to the control gate.


Figure 3: TEM cross section analysis of a failing single bit  revealed no notable physical anomalies which would account  for the lower gm of the bit.


Electrical Analysis


Following unsuccessful physical analysis, remaining single bit  failures were top down deprocessed to contacts to allow  electrical characterization with Atomic Force Probing (AFP). Following delayering, the die underwent 30 minutes of  ultraviolet (UV) erase to allow evaluation of the bitcells  independent of effects of any residual charge stored on the  floating gates. Initial AFP electrical analysis verified the  failing bit locations and bit behavior, but also enabled further


Figure 4. Illustration of the basic steps involved in lamellar  encapsulation. (a) A standard wedge is mounted to a microtip  post and thinned to <30 nm (b) capturing devices of interest  as shown in the wedge cross-section (c). The thinned wedge is  coated with polysilicon (d), propagated to additional  microtips (e) and sharpened for atom probe analysis (f).


Data Analysis


The first step in the interrogation of APT data is to view and  identify all peaks in the mass spectrum. Figure 5 shows the  spectrum obtained from one specimen prepared from the  gate/channel region using the methods outlined above. Peaks  are readily identified for C, B, Si, O, Ge and the molecular  ions SiO and SiC. High mass resolving power, and low  background are important parameters for any mass  spectrometric method (1:1100 and 11 ppm/nsec at 28 Da  achieved in this dataset) because they allow simple  identifications of ionic species with high analytical sensitivity (< 10 appm is typical for high quality APT data).


The higher mass range portion of the specimen’s mass  spectrum is shown in Fig. 6. We observe ready differentiation  of the As and Ge peaks in the plus-one charge state, as well as  several complex molecules. All of the Hf is observed to be  detected as part of the HfO molecular ion and several different  varieties of Si-C-based molecular ions are observed (including  SiC, SiC2 and Si2C). Note the high level of carbon which is


discussed further below. Both the Ge and the Hf isotope  distributions are in good agreement with the expected values (within ~1% of the expected relative abundances).


Once all of the elements are identified, we can visualize the  data volume by assigning different colors to each element and  creating an atom map, an example of which is shown in Fig. 7. The atom map shows clearly that the APT analysis contains at  least a portion of the gate oxide region of a transistor-type  structure. The red source/drain regions of Si+Ge are  distributed on either side of the channel, while the B and As atoms are distributed throughout the source-channel-drain  regions. Further details of the structure are shown in Fig. 8,which separates the image shown in Fig. 7 into separate atoms  maps in Fig. 8a. A concentration profile across the nearchannel region is then presented in Fig. 8b. The B is clearly  diffusing into the near channel region from the SiGe  source/drain regions, while the As appears to be uniformly  distributed throughout the channel. The apparent Ge in the  middle of the channel region is actually background noise.


Figure 5. APT mass spectrum from 0 to 46 Da for a region of the commercial logic-device sample.  The ions of the major constituents  in the material analyzed are easily identifiable (C, B, Si, O, Ge).


Figure 6. APT mass spectrum from 50 to 100 Da for a region of the commercial logic-device sample. The As and Ge peaks in the  plus-one charge state are well separated from the background noise level.  All of the Hf is detected as part of the HfO molecular ion. Several different varieties of Si-C-based molecules are observed (including SiC, SiC2 and Si2C).


This is evidenced by the fact that a mass spectrum extracted  from just the channel region does not show any signal above  the noise level at the Ge-isotope mass-to-charge positions.


The distribution of carbon atoms appears particularly nonrandom in atoms maps of the transistor, as shown in Fig. 9a. We can estimate this degree of non-randomness using a cluster  detection method based on a parameter known as d-max [23]. This value is used to compute the number of carbon atoms that  are positioned within or less than this distance, d-max, from  other carbon atoms. It is often beneficial to compare the  number of detected clusters for randomized data to the number  detected in the actual data over a range of d-max values, Fig. 9b. This helps the user to choose a d-max value that  preferentially identifies real clusters versus randomly  associated atoms. Applying standard cluster analysis methods


Figure 9. Evidence for carbon clustering in the channel


region of the transistor. (a) carbon (or carbon-containing


species) map. (b) Cluster analysis shows the distribution of  identified clusters in this channel volume where only those  which are clearly beyond the scope of a random distribution  of atoms are counted. (c) The resultant 29 clusters only.


Figure 10. (a) Bright field and (b) high-angle annular-darkfield STEM image of the device structure.  Note that this is a  different device than that shown in the APT data, Fig. 7.


Ge isoconcentration surface may be calculated that delineates  the source/drain regions from the channel region and has a  shape which resembles the undercut shape observed in TEM  images, Fig. 10 [15].  This is especially evident in the high  angle annular dark field image shown in Fig. 10b.


This Ge shape correlation between Fig. 7 and Fig. 10a may be  used to create composite images of the STEM and APT data, where the APT image is scaled to match the STEM image. Solute atoms from the APT data may be overlaid onto this  image to form composite images containing information from both types of microscopy. Figure 11 shows the results of  overlaying atom maps of Ge (including a 12 at. % Ge  isoconcentration surface), C, B and As onto a portion of the  TEM image shown in Fig. 10.


Although the data shown in Fig. 11 are not from exactly the  same volume of material (something which has been done very  few times to date in the literature [28]), the exercise is still


useful. It provides us with information on the accuracy of the  APT data reconstruction as well as perhaps a glimpse of the  future of correlative microscopy. Indeed, TEM and APT are  complementary techniques that will likely develop a closer  relationship in the future where the spatial fidelity of TEM is  combined with the analytical sensitivity and three  dimensionality of APT. Projects that combine these abilities  into a single instrument are currently being pursued by  combining LEAP and STEM [29, 30]. This work demonstrates  that a wealth of high quality information may be obtained from  site-specific APT analysis of post-production microelectronic  devices. The present work has not included enough specimens  to fairly assess the yield of useful APT results. Adequate  yields (>50%) need to be realized and APT reconstruction  methods must be improved going forward, but certainly at this  time, feasibility has been demonstrated.


Figure 11.  Composite images of STEM (high angle annular dark field) overlaid with APT data.  In the Ge image, the isoconcentration  surface was calculated for 12 at. % Ge and used to scale the APT data to match the STEM image.


The feasibility of analyzing post-production microelectronic  devices with APT has been demonstrated in this work. The  yield of useful results from such analyses has not yet been  determined, but the small number of specimens analyses (four) yielded quality results in the first attempt. No serious  impediments to routine APT analysis of post-production  devices were encountered in this process. This capability can  have important impact on the development and assessment of  integrated circuits.

Non-visible defect analysis of OTP device


C.Q. Chen, G.B. Ang, Z.X. Xing, Y.N. Hua, Z.Q. Mo, RAO. Ramesh, Y. Li


Failure Analysis Group, QRA Department, GLOBALFOUNDRIES Singapore  60 Woodlands Industrial Park D, Street 2, Singapore 738406


Several product lots were found to suffer from data retention  failures in OTP (one time program) devices. PFA (physical  failure analysis) was performed on these devices, but nothing  abnormal was observed. Cross-sectional TEM (transmission  electron microscopy) revealed no physical defects or abnormal  CDs (critical dimensions). In order to isolate the failed layer or  location, electrical analysis was conducted. Several electrical  simulation experiments, designed to test the data retention  properties of OTP devices, were preformed. Meilke's method


[1] was also used to differentiate between mobile ion  contamination and charge trap centers. Besides Meilke's  method, a new electrical analysis method was used to verify  the analysis results. The results of our analysis suggests that  SiN charge trap centers are the root cause for the data retention  failures, and the ratio of Si/N is the key to charge trap center  formation. Auger analysis was used to physically check the  Si/N ratio of OTP devices. The results support our hypothesis. Subsequent DOE (Design Of Experiment) experiments also  confirm our analysis results.


Key Words: OTP, data retention, Non-visible defect, AFP, charge trap center, mobile ion.


Data retention (DR) is an important property of electronic (e-) memory and is one of its main reliability concerns.


Traditionally, mobile ions have been found to be the root  cause of the majority of data retention failures in this type of  memory. As a result, the control of mobile ions is very critical  in the e-memory fabrication process, especially in the FEOL  steps [1]. Normally, the defect that causes data retention  failure is not visible, so indirect methods must be used to  simulate and verify the defect. These include EFA simulations  and SIMS (secondary ion mass spectroscopy) elemental analysis. DOE experiments has also been found to be a useful  methodology for identifying the root cause of failures.


The OTP devices, a type of e-memory, discussed in this paper  were found to fail data retention in several lots of different  products. The process sequence in our Fab for these devices is:


Bump UV Erase KGD(Know Good Die) Normal Sort (Write/Read).


All the wafers passed the Normal Sort step. During packaging, the subsequent process steps are:


Die saw  Soldering Molding Read.


The defective OTP devices failed the Read step.


FA procedure


Initially, we suspected a packaging issue because all the units  we received were from the same package house. After more  investigation, however, it was found that product from  different packaging houses also failed OTP data retention. This made packaging issues unlikely as a cause for the failures.  Next, we suspected a physical defect in the failed OTP  structure. Cross-sectional TEM was performed on both failed  and reference devices, as shown in Fig. 1. The TEM images  revealed no anomalies on the floating Poly gate, spacer and  CESL (Contact Etch Stop Layer), and all of the CDs were  comparable between good and bad units. These results made  physical defects unlikely as a cause of the failures.


Based on our previous experience, most data retention issues   in e-memory are related to mobile ion contamination. To  investigate this possibility, we performed DOE experiments on  the impact of mobile ion contamination.


As shown in Fig. 2, the top area of a wafer was purposely  contaminated with mobile Na+ ions. The units from this area  all exhibited data retention failure. Time-of-flight (TOF)-SIMS  measurements, Fig. 3, showed the increased concentration of  Na+ on the BPSG (Boron, Phosphorus, Silicon Glass) interface, which correlated with the electrical failure results.


Based on these results, mobile ion contamination became our  key focus. We performed elemental analysis of both bad and  reference units. SIMS analysis showed that Na+ was present in  both, but indicated no obvious elemental differences, so no  conclusions could be drawn.


Concurrently with the elemental analysis, EFA simulations  were performed in our EFA lab. Failed and reference units

The mechanism shown in Fig. 4 indicates that the charge trap  unit reverts to its initial state after UV erasing. But the other  units improve after baking twice, which is shown in Table. 4. It shows why there is no difference in data retention property  between the trap charges and mobile ions if the UV erase step  is not employed, and why, if the UV erase step is employed, it can differentiated between the mobile ion and trapped charge  mechanisms.


For further verification, elemental analysis using Auger  spectroscopy was performed on the bad and reference units to  check their Si/N ratio, which can be translated to charge trap  center Si-H bond [2]. The results are shown below in Fig. 5.

Hypothesis Evaluation


To test the hypothesis, failing bitcell behavior was examined  with additional work targeted to investigate the response to  non-electrical charge-modification stimuli, which included  additional UV-erase, 125C bake, and 250C bake (in that  order). No significant effects were observed on the fail bit IdVg curves, threshold voltage, or forward and reverse Id-Vg  characteristics after an additional UV-erase, or after a 125C  bake. However, a 1 hour 250C bake was found to have a  significant impact on all aspects of the fail bit characteristics  of note. Figure 5 shows the forward and reverse Id-Vg  characteristics as initially collected, after a 4 hour 125C bake, and after a 1 hour 250C bake for a fail bit.  Table 1 tabulates  the parametric impact of the 250C bake.

This paper is good reference for the invisible defect analysis. Nanoprobing using the AFP was employed for the electrical  simulation at the contact level. Two simulation methods were  used to differentiate between mobile ion contamination and  Si/N charge trap centers. Successful and comparable result was  obtained by the two methods. A mechanism was proposed for  the simulation method. Finally, the Auger Si/N ratio analysis  was employed to verify the simulation result. The elemental analysis results correlated with our electrical simulation  results.

Addressing Stress-Memorization-Technology (SMT) Induced Defects


IBM Systems and Technology Group, Hopewell Junction, New York, USA


High performance source/drain (S/D) stress-memorization  technology (SMT) has been previously demonstrated to  enhance electron mobility in leading edge SRAM NMOS  designs. Dislocations initiating from SMT induced stacking  faults cause electrical fails in the device. Transmission  electron microscopy (TEM) results show that these dislocations can be reduced by controlling certain processing  steps following SMT processing.


SMT induced dislocations as a consequence of enhanced  electron mobility of NMOS devices have previously been  reported [1,2].


Figure 1 is an example of the dislocations reported in SMT


devices [1]


Figure 1: TEM image of source/drain SMT induced mask edge  dislocation (1).


Deep pre-amorphization-implant (PAI) for SMT creates  multiple mask edge dislocations under source/drain regions  while enhancing short-channel mobility by 40 to 60% [1,2].


The stresses associated with these dislocations were modelled  by simulation and the gate poly removal process controlled by  solid phase-epitaxial anneal was thought to minimize the  formation of these dislocations for this gate last process.


A case study is presented here where a gate first SMT NMOS  device assocated with a SRAM failing signature is identified  with dislocations initiating from SMT induced stacking faults. The movement of the dislocation along 111 crystallographic


axis effectively disrupted the source/drain junction  documented by atomic force probing  electrical measurements.


By employment of certain process implant step as well as  controlled thermal processing, the level of stacking faults was  reduced and dislocations minimized, thereby preventing  dislocation movement that can disrupt the source drain  junction area causing device failure..


Failure signature


Bit cell mapping of SRAM arrays employing SMT process  showed a voltage “droop” issue that manifested itself in an  eight bit line failure signature within the SRAM array.


Figure 2 shows the bit fail wafer map with characteristic eight  bit line failure signatures . The different colors in this bit fail  map are intended to highlight different fail signatures.


The eight bit line signature is circled below and extends eight  bit lines within the SRAM array itself.


One characteristic associated with this eight bit line signature  is the SRAM array voltage fluctation or ‘droop’.


Figure 2: Bit Fail Wafer Map showing 8 bit line failure  signature.


Microelectronics Failure Analysis Desk Reference, Sixth Edition


R.J. Ross, editor


Product code: 09110Z


Figure 3: Detail of Bit Fail map showing eight bit line  signature


Fault Isolation and Failure Analysis


Backside infrared laser localization called Critical Parameter  Analysis (CPA) was used to identify the failure location.  A  tester was employed to exercise the SRAM array in a marginal  passing/failing schmoo configuration, while a laser beam with  infrared (IR) wavelength was inputted from the wafer  backside. IR signatures showed up around the failure locations  and detected by CPA.  The failing peripheral circuit  responsible for  the observed eight bit line array failure  signature was then highlighted. This CPA IR signature  in the  peripheral circuit is shown in Figures 4 and 5.


Figure 4. CPA localized site in peripheral circuitry adjacent  to SRAM 8 bit line signature


Figure 5: Detail of CPA localized site in peripheral circuitry  shown in Figure 4


Based on the CPA localization and an analysis of the  peripheral circuitry that would account for a failure to write a  zero, a specific SMT NFET device was identified (Figure 8)


Figure 6: Circuit layout showing CPA site (circled) in  peripheral circuit responsible for eight bit line SRAM array  failure


Figure 7:  Circuit schematic highlighting NOR2 circuit  causing failure to write a “ zero”


Figure 8: Specific failing NFET in circuit causing failure to


write a “zero” that was atomic force probed


This SMT NFET device was atomic force probed. Atomic  force probing is a well documented method for identifying  failures in discrete devices especially useful in finding


“ invisible defects” [3,4,5]. The failing device (colored coded


blue) is compared to a non failing device (color coded red) as  shown in Figure 10, plotting gate voltage Vg versus the log of  the drain current Id


Figure 9: Atomic Force Probe electrical characterization of  SMT NFET shown in Figure 8 plotting gate voltage, Vg versus  log of drain current Id


Following the electrical characterization of this discrete SMT  NFET device, transmission electron microscopy (TEM) analysis was performed first in planview followed by cross  sectioning of the same device.


Figures 10 and 11 describe these TEM images


Figure 10: Plainview TEM image of SMT NFET that atomic  force probing identified with the fail.  Note dislocation near  surface and dislocation at bottom of shallow trench isolation (STI)


Figure 11: Cross sectional TEM of dislocations shown in  Figure 10. Note dislocation at bottom of STI aligned along  111 crystallographic plane.


The dislocation observed at the bottom of the STI in Figure 11  actually initiated from stacking faults near the device surface. Subsequent processing steps caused this dislocation to form at


the SMT induced stacking fault and “glide” along the 111


crystallographic axis to the bottom of the STI.


In the course of this movement along the 111 crystallographic  plane, the source/drain junction region was disrupted causing  the electrical failure measured by atomic force probing and  shown in Figure 9.


Figure 12: Cross sectional TEM of SMT NMOS devices  following process steps designed to minimize stacking faults.


Note lessened degree of crystallographic defects (dark region)


By controlling certain processing steps following SMT, the  initiation of dislocations at SMT induced stacking faults was  appreciably reduced. This is shown in Figure 12.


In contrast, Figure 13 shows a higher density of stacking faults  that serve as dislocation initiation sites leading to device  failure.


Figure 13: Cross sectional TEM of SMT NMOS devices  without corrective processing steps. Note higher density of  stacking faults in SMT NMOS devices (highlighted by grey  regions)


TheTEM analysis disclosed that dislocation movement along  the 111 plane disrupted the SMT source/drain region resulting  in the elevated Ioff  characteristics and the measured  source to  drain leakage.


Implementation of SMT for optimum device performance with  pre-amorphization-implantation (PAI) that enhances shortchannel mobility must be carefully controlled by specific  processing steps to minimize stress and prevent dislocation  formation/movement. The dislocation movement along the  111 crystallographic plane disrupted the source drain junction  itself in the failing device, causing failure. These failing


devices are typical of “invisible” type of defects and would


not be seen unless the specific device was atomic force probed  and the specific device structure TEM sectioned.


In the case study described, the characteristic eight bit line  signature caused by failure in a SMT NMOS device was  eliminated through  these  processing steps.

Flash Single Bit Cycling Fail Due to Charge Accumulation from   Incorrect Lateral Drain Junction formation


Keith Harber, Sam Subramanian, Tony Chrastecky, Khiem Ly, and Fuchen Mu


Freescale Semiconductor, Inc, Austin, Texas, United States of America


This paper outlines the analysis of a flash single bit failure  caused by bitcell degradation over write/erase cycling. With no  physical anomaly present at the failing single bit, Atomic  Force Probing (AFP) characterization was utilized in  conjunction with thermal response characterization to direct  analysis towards a particular non-visible defect as the root  cause. Existence of the hypothesized non-visible defect  causing the single bit cycling failure was proven through  Transmission Electron Microscopy (TEM) stained sample  analysis, which highlighted an anomalous lateral drain junction  formation at the single bit that caused the cycling failure.


deprocessing as illustrated in Fig. 3. Here, standard FIB-based  lift-out produces a wedge of material from the die containing  the devices of interest (schematically shown in Fig. 3a).  The  wedge is oriented such that the original surface is parallel to  the ion beam, but visible to the SEM column (Fig. 3b). Before  removal of the dielectric material (Fig. 3c), only a few of the  device features can be seen on the surface at the bottom of the  image.  By carefully aligning the wedge and using successive  line scan mills typical of TEM specimen processing, very  precise removal of material via visual end-pointing with the


SEM allows the user to “deprocess” to the desired depth. Fig.


3d shows the surface of the wedge after the deprocessing has  been terminated. The middle surface regions contain gate  exposed devices, while regions below and above are  deprocessed to a greater or lesser degree.  This variety of  finishing enables the user to choose the uppermost point of the  APT experiment by selecting an appropriately finished device  for analysis.


Because some dielectric materials are difficult to successfully  analyze with APT, it is highly desirable to remove that  material and replace it with a capping material.  Proper  capping becomes very important when near-surface features  are to be analyzed. Desirable capping properties include:  1) relatively low temperature deposition (to avoid inducing  undesired diffusion), 2) matching of evaporation field with  target material (to minimize APT reconstruction artifacts), 3) matching of sputtering rate with target material (to minimize  FIB-induced milling irregularities), 4) good adhesion to  neighboring surfaces, and 5) minimization of isotopic overlaps  between capping material and the material to be analyzed (to  minimize ambiguities in APT mass peak assignments during  reconstruction). For this deprocessing example, the original  device locations can be seen in the cross-section image Fig. 3e. After deprocessing, the removed dielectric material region is  to be replaced with a suitable capping material (sputtered  silicon in this case—red box region of Fig. 3f).


The lamellar encapsulation preparation technique may also be  employed to minimize dielectric materials within an APT  specimen. In this case, both dielectric material covering the  device and any dielectric material surrounding the device can  be controllably removed by first capturing the device volume  in a TEM-like lamella. After this is accomplished, all the  surfaces are covered with a desirable capping material to  enable reshaping the lamella into an APT specimen of  appropriate size and shape. The general steps of this technique  are shown in Fig. 4.


First, a standard, normal orientation lift-out wedge is  transferred and mounted to a microtip post (Fig. 4a). The  wedge is then carefully thinned to <30 nm using standard TEM


lamella preparation techniques described elsewhere (Fig. 4b)


[5]. The captured device(s) of interest are clearly visible in  cross-section shown in Fig. 4c. The microtip array carrying the  thinned wedge is then removed from the FIB and capped with ~300 nm of conformal CVD polysilicon. The encapsulated  lamella is then returned to the FIB (Fig. 4d). Sections of the  lamella are next transferred to microtip posts (Fig. 4e).  Finally, each mounted section is sharpened with an annular  milling pattern so that the specimen tip is <200 nm at the  region of interest.


In Fig. 4f, the original lamella (bright) can be seen embedded  in the center of the polysilicon tip. In this case, replacing the  surrounding dielectric material with polysilicon enhances  analysis yield by better matching the evaporation fields of the  remaining device material and removing several material  interfaces that were not of strong analytical interest.

The EFA results were unexpected: Charge loss was found to  be very fast for the failed units. Even with only several minutes  of baking, charge loss reached more than 90%. These results  suggested a new possibility to use: SiN charge trap centers, because the charge loss rate is much faster for charge trap  centers than it is for mobile ion contamination [2][3][4].


Because physical defects were ruled out, charge trap centers  and mobile ions were the only possible root causes. How to  differentiate between the two became our key focus. We redesigned our EFA simulation experiment and employed  Meilke’s method [1], which is commonly used in e-memory  data retention analysis.


For Meilke’s method, a 5X5 array was chosen, as shown  below in Table 1. For the “unbiased cell”, all 25 cells in the  5X5 array were programmed. For the “biased cell” only the  cell in the middle was programmed.


Table1: Experiment design of Meilke’s method


According to Meike’s method [1], the charge loss rate is  expected to be different for the “unbiased” versus “biased” cells in the presence of mobile ion contamination. For the SiN  charge trap center mechanism, on the other hand, the charge  loss rate is expected to be the same (see Table 2).

After baking for varying periods of time, the data was read out  for the “biased” and “unbiased” cells. The results are shown in  Table 3. The charge loss rate was found to be almost the same  for both types of cells. Based on this result, charge loss was  most likely induced by SiN charge trap centers.


Table 3: Charge loss of the biased and unbiased cells.


In addition to Meilke's method, we also employed a new  method combining baking and UV erasing to further verify our  EFA analysis and to test a new hypothesis and failure  mechanism.


For this method, the units were de-layered to the contact level, and we performed the following AFP simulation experiment: ProgramRead 1st Bake Read UV Erase Program Read 2nd Bake Read


Based on our analysis and simulation results, data retention  should recover after the second bake for ion contamination, with or without the UV erase step. For a charge trap center  mechanism, however, data retention should not recover with  the UV erase step, but should recover without it. This expected  behavior is summarized in table 4.


Our reasoning is as follows: UV does not impact mobile ions,but can neutralize charge trap centers. So mobile ions and  charge traps without UV will both improve after baking, but  charge traps will revert back to their initial states after UV  erasing.

VI. Model verification


To verify the model, the bad and good driver PFET’s were


FIB cross-sectioned and junction delineated using various  staining techniques for seconds. In the bad PFET, the  extension junction was not delineated under the poly gate edge  whereas that in the good PFET was as shown in Figure 11.


This physical evidence clearly confirmed the model.  The wet  etch technique had highlighted the non visual implant defect  without invoking the complicated and sample preparation  intensive SCM or TEM holography dopant mapping  techniques.

VII. Inline defect identification


Immediately after the model verification, inline inspection  identified that UV210 resist residue was indeed observed at  the PFET extension implant patterning step in the MCUROM  row driver area as shown in Figure 12. Note that the driver


ROM Array Row Drivers


Pictures taken in-line @ Post P-Extension Photo


Affected Area


Thin film of resist present


Good Row Driver Area


Figure 12. ROM driver inspection revealed resist residue, indicated by red arrows, at the PFET extension photo step


Row#1 is immediately next to a huge island of resist and is  expected to be hit the worst by the defects.


Detailed electrical microprobing of the failing MCUROM  Wordline driver components had enabled development of an  accurate model to explain the signature failure mechanism and  offered clue to solving the problem. This theory was  immediately confirmed by wet delineation techniques and  inline defect inspection data. Source/Drain non-link up due to  missing extension implant was caused by photo resist residue.  It was through extremely well coordinated yield enhancement  team effort that the root cause was identified inline and the  problem solved effectively by converting UV210 to UV110  resist. A greater than 45% reduction in die cost was realized.

The electrical data supported gate oxide leakage as  the root cause failure mechanism. However, longitudinal  and cross-sectional TEM images of the n-channel  transistors did not reveal any physical defects or  evidence of gate oxide leakage. The lack of physical  evidence created a need to develop an alternative


method for performing root cause analysis of these  samples. If the gate oxide surface can be exposed, conducting atomic force microscopy (CAFM) could be  used to scan the oxide surface for leakage.


CAFM has been proven to be a powerful technique in  isolating electrical defects in semiconductors [1],[2],[3]. Although CAFM is widely used in failure analysis for  isolating locations of gate opens or shorts at the contact  level, there have been persistent challenges in the area of  sample preparation when making the argument that  CAFM would be a viable technique for isolating leakage  defects at the gate oxide level in SOI devices [4, 5]. The next section demonstrates a repeatable sample  preparation technique of exposing polysilicon and gate  oxide tailored from the proposed sample preparation  process in reference 3. CAFM can be used for  successfully scanning both surfaces for gate oxide  defects after the sample preparation.


CAFM Sample Preparation


Figure 4: Optical image of a sample mechanically  polished to broken polysilicon. (Note: The image shown  is the memory cache and not polysilicon)


Figure 5: SEM image showing the disconnection of the  polysilicon between active and the gate contact.


The first step in isolating the defect in the failing gate  using AFM is to scan a reference gate with varying  sample bias. This would achieve the optimum scanning  bias without inducing damage. Once the bias was  established, the failing gate was scanned to localize the  defect. It was observed that different samples had  different operating voltages. Figure 6 is the current  image of a sample scanned at the broken polysilicon  level, showing what appeared to be a leakage current  from a gate oxide defect.


surface, the next step was to design a sample preparation  technique for exposing the gate oxide to localize the  defect on the surface [8].


To scan the gate oxide, the polysilicon needed to be  removed. Due to its high selectivity, cesium hydroxide  was used to etch the polysilicon and expose the gate  oxide [9, 10]. It was observed that dendrites grew on the  tungsten contacts during the etching process, which  inhibited the ability for the AFM tip to scan the sample  surface as seen in Figure 8. Elemental analysis  confirmed the presence of copper dendrites. Since CsOH  had high selectivity in etching polysilicon, removing the  copper dendrites was explored first. It has been  theorized that an electrochemical reaction is taking place  during the CsOH etch of polysilicon where free Copper  radicals found elsewhere on the sample in the form of  metal lines, attach themselves to the Pt ground contact  and to the Tungsten contacts in the rows which are  grounded.


Figure 8: SEM image showing growth of copper  dendrites after CsOH etch.


Through various experiments, it was found that red  fuming Nitric acid, etches the Copper dendrites without  damaging the gate oxide or the remaining Tungsten  Contacts. Figure 9 shows the top-down image of a  sample after etching the dendrites in which the gate  oxide is still intact. Figure 10 shows the sample was still  conductive after performing an AFM scan at the gate  oxide. If there were defects in the gate oxide, the CAFM  technique could have identified the defect. The sample  was imaged using TEM to confirm that the AFM tip was  scanning over the gate oxide. Figure 11 shows the TEM  image of the sample.


Figure 9: SEM image of a sample after a chemical etch, showing the removal of the dendrites.


Figure 10: Conduction of sample was confirmed on


AFM after red-fuming nitric acid etch.


Figure 11: TEM cross-section of failing gate after the


removal of polysilicon, confirming the presence of gate  oxide.


The results obtained from several samples, exhibiting similar Single-bit failures in the memory  arrays, indicate that gate-oxide leakage was not the  probable root-cause of the failure. Only one sample  among ten showed a gate-oxide defect as shown in Figure 7.  Although the majority of the samples did not  show any leakage sites at polysilicon level and gate  oxide level, the CAFM scans show that the sample  surface was still conductive as evidenced from the  current through the tungsten contacts. The TEM images  proved that the polysilicon and the gate oxide were  intact at each step of the sample preparation.


It has been proven through the results presented in  this paper that this sample preparation process for  exposing polysilicon and gate oxide is a viable  technique for identifying gate oxide defects using  CAFM. Through this process, a unique advantage to the  technique has been demonstrated, in the ability to scan  both polysilicon and gate oxide surfaces.

With ever shrinking device dimensions of CMOS integrated  circuit technology, dopant related failures in a single device  remains a challenging non-visual defect (NVD) for even ultra  high resolution microscopy techniques such as SCM [1, 2] ,SMM [3], TEM holography [4, 5], and SSRM [6, 7].


Complicated sample preparation and imaging optimization are  some of the obstacles, although a handful of such successful  analyses have been reported on both single crystalline silicon  and poly silicon [8, 9, 10, 11]. To increase the success rate of  any physical analysis, particularly the subtle NVD types, a detailed electrical analysis to pinpoint exactly where and what  to look for in the sample is critical. As a result of an accurate  model, and with its guidance, sophisticated and time  consuming techniques could be avoided and simpler and more  efficient methods can be utilized instead. This quick  turnaround feedback is essential for wafer fabrication lines to  make corrective actions to address critical yield issues timely.


In this work, detail electrical microprobing analysis leading to  an accurate physical model and simple wet etch techniques to  bring out the NVD will be described.


The first part is focused on the row driver characterization  using electrical microprobing technique. A row driver PFET  Source-Drain non-link up theory was then developed based on  the device electrical behavior. The second part is focused on  junction delineation techniques and inline inspection that  verified the model. The problem was finally solved with a photoresist change. The ROMBIST fallout was drastically


reduced to less than 10% and the lots yielded 70-80% representing a yield delta of >50%.


I. Yield loss pareto


The yield loss pareto over 15 lots is summarized in Figure 1a.


MCUROM fail had 26.5% die killed and was by far the worst. The normalized MCUROM fail rate was typically in the 30% range for the baseline material whereas counterpart foundry  material was only 8% as shown in Figure 1b.  The failure  pattern will be discussed next.

the worst

The MCUROM fail exhibited a unique every 4th row failing  pattern as illustrated in Figure 2.  This pattern can best be  explained by the ROM Row Decoder layout design placement.   The Wordline driver relative locations that access the rows  will be discussed next.

There are four parallel rows of drivers, namely driver row #1, #2, #3 and #4. Drivers in driver row#1 control ROM rows #1,#5,#9…every 4th rows; Drivers in driver row#2 control  ROM rows #2,#6,#10…every 4th rows; Drivers in driver  row#3 control ROM rows #3,#7,#11…every 4th rows; Drivers  in driver row#4 control ROM rows #4,#8,#12…every 4th rows. The majority of the row fail patterns are related to driver  row#1 that is placed immediately next to the ROM array.


IV. Electrical microprobing technique and procedures


(a) Sample Preparation


Since the Wordline drivers are the devices directly feeding the  rows, the focus is to characterize these drivers. The sample  was unlayered by mechanical polishing to the Via1 level and  then probe pads put on the Vias for electrical measurements.  The probe pads were achieved with Platinum deposition using  focused ion beam tool. The ion current density and current  level are each critical for making good pads with minimized  beam damage and shorting.  The beam current chosen was  1pA for a 2 m x 0.3 m pad size resulting in a current density  of less than 2pA/ m2.  The deposition time was about 5  minutes for a thickness of 0.7 m.  A cross (+) pattern was  selected for probe tip anchorage. The deposition was preceded  by a short ion sputtering clean of the Via surface to improve  contact quality. A clean up step to remove the Platinum spray  was followed. More than one pad on the same line was made  for probe tip to pad electrical continuity check.  Since multiple  terminal measurements will be made, probe pads at Vdd, Vss, P-Well, N-Well, Source and Drain for both of the PFET and  the NFET were prepared.


(b) Wordline driver inverter electrical characterization


A HP Semiconductor Parametric Analyzer 4145B was used  for the measurements. The first step was to investigate  whether the Wordline driver was functioning in the failing  rows. A simple configuration was investigated with the gates  grounded. Although all the drivers in the same driver row  shared common Vdd by design at Metal 1, and were not  isolated in this study, the inverter probed was a single stage as  illustrated in the CAD layout shown in figure 4a.  The Vdd  node was swept with Vss at ground while sensing the Vout.  A

It should be emphasized that all the data collected to this point  did not offer a single clue as to why the bad driver PFET did  not exhibit any transistor action.  The next task was to search  for the cause.


(e) The driver Source/Drain diodes


The various transistor parametric components were next  investigated. The vias and contacts were found to be normal. The S/D and body to gate leakage was in the nAmp range that  was about the same in both good and bad transistors. With thin  gate oxide in the range of 18A, Fowler-Nordheim tunneling  through the gate oxide is expected. The forward Source/Drain  to body junctions for both NFET and PFET in the driver were  then characterized with a gate modulation potential varying  from -0.5v to 0.5v. A cross-section of the device structures in  the inverter under various biases is shown in figure 8.  With


N+ P+

Figure 8. Driver inverter Source and Drain diode measurement  configuration for both PFET and NFET


both Vdd and Vss grounded, the Vout node was swept from a  negative to positive potential.  The positive and negative  potential at Vout will forward bias the PFET and the NFET  S/D to Well diodes respectively. It was found that the NFET  diodes were normal and the forward current can be modulated  effectively by the gate potential as shown in Figure 9a.


Figure 9a. Source/Drain junction forward diode   characteristics of good driver


However, the PFET S/D diodes in the bad PFET were found  insensitive to the gate potential and the forward diode turn-on  voltage was also higher than the good PFET as shown in


figure 9b. Based on the forward diode characteristics, a failure  mechanism model was formulated next.


Figure 9b. Source/Drain junction forward diode characteristics  of failing driver


V.  Failure mechanism model


The good driver NFET and PFET forward diode  characteristics indicated that the surface junction component  dominated.  The bad driver PFET diode characteristics  indicated the followings:


(a) The gate poly did not overlap the Source and Drain pn  junctions since there was no gate modulation effect [12, 13].


(b) The higher diode turn-on voltage suggested that the  background dopant concentration was higher than the good  PFET as the junction build-in potential, b, was related  according to b ~lnCb/ni [12,13], where Cb is the  concentration under the spacer nitride and gate overlap region,ni is the intrinsic concentration.


Based on these diode characteristics, a Source/Drain non-link  up theory was proposed to account for the failure mechanism.


This model required that the extension implant that linked the  Soure/Drain to the channel region was missing. This non-link  up structure would be able to explain the absence of both  transistor action and gate potential modulation to the diode  characteristics.  With the extension implant missing, the  background concentration (N-type) under the spacer nitride  was therefore effectively higher. It was further postulated that the missing implant was most likely blocked by a resist  residue. The non-link up model is schematically illustrated in  Figure 10.


Nwell

VIII. Problem fixes and yield impact


The root cause of the ROMBIST failure was identified as  UV210 photoresist residual occurred at the PFET source/drain  extension photo. UV210 photo resist was replaced with  UV110 or extended rinse after UV210 develop. The  ROMBIST fall out in the first three lots with UV110 was  reduced to about 10% as shown in Figure 13.   Lots with the  resist fix had resulted in record yield in the 70-80% range  representing a yield delta of 50%.

Memory Arrays Using Conducting Atomic Force Microscopy


Lakshminarayanan Lakshmanan, Lowell Herlinger

Shrinking gate lengths have led to increased challenges  in isolating defects using conventional physical failure  analysis methods. Conducting atomic force microscopy (CAFM) has been proven to be a powerful tool to isolate  gate oxide defects in silicon-on-insulator devices. Some  sample preparation techniques of exposing polysilicon  and gate oxide, which were critical to perform CAFM  scan, are discussed in this paper.


Case Study Introduction


Samples identified during device testing experienced single-bit failures in the memory array. These samples  were pulled for routine physical failure analysis (PFA) to  inspect and identify the root cause failure mechanism. The suspected failure mechanism was the breakdown of  gate oxide produced by high-temperature annealing. Nanoprobing data collected at the contact level  identified the defect as a gate-source or gate-drain short  in one of the n-channel transistors,


Figure 1: Gate-to-Source/Drain I-V measurements  acquired from nanoprobing.


as shown in Figure 1.


Traditional planar de-processing methods of de-layering  the sample to the contact level were used. Since the units  were SOI devices, each sample needed to be grounded to  achieve conductivity for CAFM scanning. The dualbeam focused ion beam (DB-FIB) was used to short the  active silicon to the substrate silicon through the  insulator. Figure 2 shows an image of a sample that had  been milled through insulator and then filled with  platinum, creating an electrical pathway from the nactive down to the substrate silicon [6]. The e-beam  deposited platinum seen directly right of the post was  deposited for protecting the surface from ion beam  damage [7]. Through a series of experiments, it was  determined that grounding the samples 5 μm from the  failing bitcell was the optimal distance for reducing  resistance while limiting the horizontal field of view to  prevent inducing damaging on the failing gate when  using the ion beam during the milling process. The  sample was scanned at the contact level using AFM to  identify the failing n-channel gate contact. Figure 3 is  an example of a current scan in which the failing gate  contact was identified within the bitcell. The high level  of leakage corresponds to the darkest color.


Figure 2: Top side SEM image at contact level of Pt  contact between the n-active and substrate silicon. A Pt  protective coating has been deposited to the right of the  post.


Figure 3: CAFM scan at contact level indicating gate


leakage found on the failing n-channel gate contact.


CAFM at Polysilicon


In an attempt to isolate the defect location on the gate, the samples were de-layered to the polysilicon level.


This leaves the gate oxide intact, as it was suspected that  gate oxide leakage can be observed through the  polysilicon. To ensure the polysilicon-to-contact  connection was broken and there was no remaining  silicide, a polysilicon thickness (cross-section height) of  5nm to 10nm was targeted. Several experiments  characterized the capability of the dimpler in achieving  this target thickness, and our work found it a reliable, repeatable, and time-saving process. Figure 4 shows the  result of de-layering using the dimpler. Figure 5 is a  SEM image used to confirm the samples were delayered to the target polysilicon thickness. We then  scanned the samples using AFM.


Figure 6: Conductive AFM image showing the defect  localization at the polysilicon level within the width of  the transistors and the current through the Source-Drain  contacts.


Figure 7: TEM cross-section image of the failing gate, which shows the defect in the gate oxide.


The localization of the defect was vital for preparing a  cross-section lamella for TEM imaging. Figure 7 shows  the TEM image of the failing gate, along with the  remaining polysilicon. A defect in the gate oxide was  found at the leakage site identified by the CAFM.


CAFM at Gate Oxide


Some of the samples did not show any leakage sites  when scanned at the polysilicon level, and reasons for  this are still under investigation. Knowing the resolution  of AFM offered the capability to scan the gate oxide

Failure Analysis on Power Trench MOSFET Devices with  Copper Wire Bonds


Huixian Wu, Arthur Chiang, David Le, and Win Pratchayakun  Vishay Siliconix


2201 Laurelwood Road, Santa Clara, CA, USA


Phone: 408-970-5369, Email: Huixian.Wu@vishay.com


Abstract: With gold prices steadily going up in recent


years, copper wire has gained popularity as a means to


reduce cost of manufacturing microelectronic


components. Performance tradeoff aside, there is an


urgent need to thoroughly study the new technology to


allay any fear of reliability compromise. Evaluation


and optimization of copper wire bonding process is


critical. In this paper, novel failure analysis and


analytical techniques are applied to the evaluation of


copper wire bonding process. Several FA/analytical


techniques and FA procedures will be discussed in


detail, including novel laser/chemical/plasma


decapsulation, FIB, wet chemical etching, reactive ion


etching (RIE), cross-section, CSAM, SEM, EDS, and


a combination of these techniques. Two case studies


will be given to demonstrate the use of these


techniques in copper wire bonded devices.


1. Introduction


With the semiconductor industry moving towards low  manufacturing cost solutions, there is demand for  copper wire bond boosted as a low-cost, highperformance manufacturing solution for package  bonding. Compared to gold wires, copper wires have  lower electrical conductivity, lower wire sagging and  sweep during molding and much lower cost. However  the challenge of copper wires is the reliability, which  lies primarily in the wire bonding process. The  optimized wire bonding process is essential and  critical to the performance and reliability of copper  wire products. Analysis of copper wire products  becomes critical to help and assist the evaluation and  optimization of the copper wire bonding process [1]. In this paper, we describe some analytical techniques  that has contributed to the successful development of  copper


wire


technology,

Copper Wires


The attack of the copper wires and bonds by the  etching chemicals can complicate the interpretation of  the result of any follow on analysis. A better technique  is clearly in need. Besides a corrosion-free  decapsulation method, we will also describe a few  other techniques for different purposes associated with  copper wire bonding technology.


3.1 Novel Decapsulation Methods for Copper Wire  Bonded Products


After numerous experimentations, we found using  laser decapulation to partially remove the molding  compound first and followed by an optimized wet  chemical etching/jet etch to expose the die surface to


be the best recipe to minimize the attack on copper  wires and bonds.


Figure 4 shows the optical images of copper wire part  with a combination of laser and chemical etch  decapsulation. The integrity of the copper wires and  bonds are clearly preserved. However, the use of wet  chemicals, though minimal, will wash away any  foreign substances that could happen to be the root  cause of the failure.


Figure 4: Laser/chemical Decapsulation of Copper  Wire Part


In order to examine lifted wire bonds, die attach  overflow, die surface contamination, etc., the best  method is a combination of laser decapsulation and  plasma dry etching. Figure 5 shows an example of  laser/plasma decapsulated part where the surface of  the die, wires and bonds is well preserved for further  analysis.


Figure 5: Laser/Plasma Dry Decapsulation of  Copper Wire Part


3.2 Combined Mechanical Parallel Polishing / FIB  Cross-section Analysis


Another need for studying copper wire bonding is to  determine the thickness of leftover die metallization  after the wire bonding process. The minimum  thickness of aluminum layer remaining under copper  wire bonds is a critical factor for the long term  reliability of copper wire bonded products [5]. Focused ion beam (FIB) has been an essential FA tool  for characterization of copper bonding process [6].


We developed the combination of parallel polishing  and FIB cross-section analysis, which offers an FA  process without the involvement of wet-chemical.  Figure 6 shows the parallel polishing of the molding  compound to expose the bare die surface.  A thin layer  of molding compound still covers the lower right half  of the die because of subtle die tilt in the original  device.


Figure 6: Parallel Polishing of Molding Compound


Figures 7 and 8 show photos of FIB cross-sectioning  through a copper wire bond, indicating the minimum  remaining Al thickness to be 0.125 um. A bond like  this is certain to be avoided. However, without a good analysis technique the process engineer would not  have known that.


Figure 7: FIB cross-section through a copper wire  bond


Figure 8: FIB cross-section through a copper wire  bond – a close-up

We used 63% Nitric at 30 C to remove only the  copper wire bond without attacking the underneath  aluminum layer and followed by FIB cross-section  analysis to investigate the minimum remaining Al  layer under copper wire bonds. Figure 17 shows the  remaining Al thickness underneath the copper bond is  around 2.06 um on Leg A1.  Figure 18 the remaining  Al thickness underneath the copper bond is around


3.97 um on Leg A4. By using the minimum remained  Al thickness as an indicator of safety margin for overbonding, we found that the decreased base power  dramatically decreased the risk of over-bonding of  copper wires. An optimized set of bonding parameters  should provide good bond integrity with no overbonding.
