Infrared Thermography Developments for III-V Transistors and MMICs


Dominique Carisetti, Mohsine Bouya, Odile Bezencenet, Bernard Servet, Jean-Claude Clément


Thales Research and Technology, 91767 Palaiseau, France.


Benoit Lambert, Laurent Caille


United Monolithic Semiconductors, Domaine de Corbeville, 91404 Orsay Cedex, France


Nathalie Malbert, Nathalie Labat


IMS Laboratory, University Bordeaux 1, 33405 Talence, France


The III-V circuits and transistors have received much attention  for their ability to operate at high-power levels and low noise  in high frequencies. They are very useful components for civil, defense and space radar applications.


In the present work, we will focus on IR thermography  capabilities on III-V components for thermal measurements  applications and failure analysis. At first, we will discuss the  thermal mapping on InGaAs/AlGaAs PHEMT structure and  compare IR thermal measurement with the well known  techniques as Raman [1] and SThM [2].


Infrared Thermography is based on emissivity of a material at


a given temperature in relation with Planck’s law. The system


uses algorithms and proprietary software to measure and  compensate for the emissivity of the material being tested in  order to generate an accurate, calibrated temperature map  image with a 3μm spatial resolution.


On the other hand, temperature mapping microscopy addresses  an important aspect of managing device reliability. The  temperature mapping software performs automated emissivity  correction to produce a true temperature map with high spatial  resolution. We have used this technique also to verify thermal  design rules on the HPA III-V component.


In the second part, we will discuss about the IR thermography  on challenging FA for hot spot detection on the most popular  type of capacitor for III-V MMICs as the MIM (metalinsulator-metal) capacitor. In this case, the thin film capacitor  is composed of two Gold metal plates separated by a dielectric  material. In our case, the SiN dielectric is typically 0.1 to


0.25μm thick. The yield of good capacitors on a wafer is an  important issue. As the MMICs (Monolithic Microwave  Integrated Circuits) use a lot of capacitors, one defect which  ruins one capacitor also ruins the entire chip. The major yield  limiting factor for MIM capacitors is shorts caused by defects  in the dielectric or sharp points on the metal plates [3]. In this  case study, we will show how IR thermography can easily


localize very small pinholes in SiN, where liquid crystal and  OBIRCH techniques are not well adapted.

After a complete comparison of these techniques on different  power dissipated, the IR thermography is a practical (easy to  use), quick for junction temperature measurement on MMICs  from the same technology.


Especially on MMICs, the control of thermal design rules is  important for the IIIV HPA reliability. The IR cartography (Fig  3) shows quickly the temperature difference between transistor  of the same stage and between stage and confirms with a good  agreement the thermal simulation. The higher magnification (x12) on transistors gives a more precise temperature value (high spatial resolution) and allows seeing quite easily the  difference between gates.


This last result is very important for the designer and indicates  the stability and current flow inside the transistor.

3.3 Combined Chemical Etching and FIB Analysis


An alternative, time-effective way to measure the  minimum thickness of remaining Al layer under  copper wire bonds is to develop a wet chemical  etching method that only etches away copper wires and bonds while preserving the Al layer underneath. To that end, we developed and optimized an FA  method that removes only the copper wires and bonds and is followed by FIB cross-section analysis. The  advantage of this method is that one can have an  overview of all the Cu wire bonds across the whole  die surface before selecting the appropriate wire bond  for FIB cross-section analysis.


Figure 9 shows an overview of die surface after wet  chemical etch.  Figure 10 shows the FIB crosssectioning through one of the wire bonded areas. Note  that the grain boundary of the Al top metallization and  the push-out of Al at the perimeter by wire bonding  can be clearly seen, indicating that the top metal  surface had not been altered


Figure 9: Overview of Die Surface after Removal  of Copper Wire Bonds


Figure 10: FIB Cross-section through the Copper  Wire Bond Area


3.4 Combined Mechanical Cross-section/ FIB Fine  Polishing Analysis


Traditional mechanical cross-section method through  copper wire bonds often introduces copper smearing  at the interface between copper bond and under bond  metal. Local FIB fine polishing after mechanical  grinding can solve this problem. Figure 11 and 12  shows the image after mechanical /FIB cross section.


Figure 11: Mechanical Grinding and FIB Fine  Polishing


Figure 12: Enlarged Picture of Copper Bond and  Al interface


4.  Case Studies


Case Study 1


The part failed high Rds,on after 1000 cycles of  Condition C temperature cycle on a MOSFET bonded  with copper wires. Source wire bonds and wedge  bonds needed to be carefully examined. First, we


examined the copper wedge bonds by using only laser  decapsulation as copper wedge bonds could be easily  damaged by traditional wet decapsulation.


Figure13 and 14 show the pictures of the exposed  copper wedge bonds after laser decapsulation. SEM  inspection revealed cracks on wedge bonds of the  MOSFET, which could be the main contribution for  high Rds,on.


Figure 13: Laser Decapsulation to Expose the  Copper Wedge Bonds


Figure 14: Enlarged Picture of Copper Wedge  Bond Cracks


Next, we examined the copper wire bond and top  metal interface by using mechanical cross-section  though the center of the copper wire bonds, followed  by FIB fine polishing to get rid of the copper  smearing.  SEM analysis revealed normal interface  between Cu wire and Al top metal. Figure 15 and 16  show the picture of the copper wire bond and Al  interface.


After examining both copper wire bonds and wedge  bonds, we concluded that the high Rdson failure is  attributed to the cracks of the copper wedge bonds.


Figure 15: Overview of the Copper wire bond and  Al interface


Figure 16: Enlarged Picture of Copper Bond and  Al interface


Case Study 2


During the development and optimization of


Vishay’s high reliability copper wire bonding


process, failure analysis had played a critical role in  characterizing the bonding process parameters. This  case study helps to illustrate how FA techniques  can help to characterize the minimum remaining Al thickness under copper wire bonds as a function of  various bonding parameters. Table 1 shows an  example of such experiment where wire bonding  parameters were adjusted to cover some range  around a set of nominal values.


5. Conclusion


Failure analysis has played an important role in the  development of a high reliability copper wire bonding  technology. In this paper, detailed analytical  techniques and strategies are applied to analyze  copper wire products. Several FA/analytical novel  techniques and FA procedures are discussed in details. Two specific case studies are given to demonstrate the  effectiveness of these FA techniques.

Thermal mapping and measurements by IR  Thermography


The junction temperature measurement is a critical point for  the reliability of the devices and need to be checked for all  designs and technologies in the military and space  applications.


Many tools are available for junction thermal measurement, especially micro-Raman spectroscopy and Scanning Thermal  microscopy. These techniques are very sensitive with high  spatial resolution but lack speed for cartography and thermal  values extraction depending on the type of sample. Also, SThM measurements could induce important mechanical  damage by electrostatic attraction on probes when the  component is biased under high voltage conditions.


The purpose of this work is to apply IR Thermography on a  0,25μm InGaAs/AlGaAs PHEMT transistor and compare it  with Raman and SThM techniques in order to transpose and  extrapolate the results for MMICs application in the same  technology.


Micro-Raman spectroscopy is performed on a Renishaw  system using a 514,5nm Argon ion laser probe to measure the  frequency of vibrational modes in semiconductor materials. The frequency is temperature dependent and therefore can be  used to determine the local temperature of the material at the  focal spot of the laser beam [4,5].


This type of measurement requires a calibration curve around  the temperature expected (see Fig.1) and the accurate  measurement of the shift of the GaAs Stokes lines. This can be  achieved by a linear approximation in the temperature range of  300-500K according to the formula :

Localization of defect in MIM capacitor by IR  Thermography


MIM capacitor is a passive element  necessary for MMIC  applications in the IIIV technology. The localization and  identification of these defects allows screening/corrective  action thus preventing these defects from entering the field.  Reliability risk in the field is reduced


In this case study, some MIM capacitors present abnormal  leakage current after accelerated stress, IR thermography is  used with synchronous detection at 5Hz for defect localization


[7]. Thermal mapping shows a very local increase of radiance  inside the upper electrolytic Gold electrodre (Fig.5) and  indicates the presence of a hot spot inside the MIM capacitor. This result is remarkable with the very low emissivity of  electrolytic Gold (0.2) and the missing of defect in the upper  electrode of the MIM (Fig.6).


The physical analysis, after BCB (BenzoCycloButene) removal by plasma and top electrodre removal by chemical, shows the presence of a pinhole defect in the dielectric layer (Fig. 7, 8) and confirmed by FIB cross-section (Fig 9).


The local breakdown area is due to particles inside the SiN  probably at deposition step [8].


The Pinhole defects on MIM capacitors are almost impossible  to eliminate completely, but can be minimized by good  cleaning and deposition process (dust on the wafer, or particles  formed by contaminant gases in a plasma deposition reactor  can cause pinholes in the dielectric film). The problem will be  worse for thinner dielectrics; yet thinner dielectrics are  desirable to decrease capacitor size for a given capacitance  value.

Certification of a Replacement Source


Yong Hong


Harman International Automotive Division, Farmington Hills, Michigan, USA   Yong.Hong@Harman.com, (248) 994-2903


Harman Automotive is the world’s leading supplier of branded  automotive audio and navigation systems. The quality and  reliability of our electronic systems, along with other vehicle  features, are extremely important for our customers to stay  ahead of their competitors in a fiercely competitive global  automobile market. A large portion of quality and reliability  issues have been related to supplier quality of electronic  components. The counterfeiting of semiconductor devices, a relatively new type of supplier quality issue, has become an  important contributor as more components are used in the  increasingly sophisticated audio and navigation systems while  more suppliers are moving manufacturing plants off-shore. This paper presents a case study on how we were able to  identify a counterfeit device and certify a replacement source.


Background


During Q1 2006, Harman experienced an unusually high reject  rate in plant testing and higher field returns of a subwoofer. Board level analyses by design engineering traced the problem  to an Intersil CA3080 Operational Transductance Amplifier (ODA) IC.


Device history investigations at sourcing department indicated  that this device was obsolete at Intersil and the recent  purchases were from a 2nd source as recommended by Intersil. Communications with Intersil indicated some suspect lot codes  did not match Intersil’s manufacturing lot history, preventing  the detailed analyses of suspect devices at Intersil.


A request was made by management for ECAFA lab (Electronic Component, Assembly and Failure Analysis Lab) to check the suspect date codes and see if they are consistent  with those date codes received directly from Intersil, in order  to determine the next step sourcing strategy for this device.


Suspect Samples and Analyses


Intersil CA3080 Operational Transductance Amplifier (OTA) is a high performance semiconductor device that outputs a  current proportional to the voltage difference at its differential  input terminals when triggered by a control signal [1, 2]. It was  used inside the subwoofer for clipped signal processing [3]. The Pin layout, circuit schematic and die surface of the Intersil  CA3080M96 OTA IC are illustrated in Figure 1.


Figure 1: OTA IC: Pin layout, schematic and die surface.


The submitted samples included suspect lot codes G413AFN, G418APK from 2nd source; P509BXR directly from Intersil  and P432BRG from 1st source, removed from a known good  PCBA in a working subwoofer. For the lot codes from the 2nd source, Intersil indicated a match for Lot code G413AFN in  their production lot codes, but not for Lot code G418APK. Table 1 summarizes the background information.


The devices from 4 date codes were soldered on PCBAs, no  obvious functional difference was observed in the beginning.


The devices were then examined under microscope. The  markings on the top and bottom sides and pin 1 designation  marks are compared in Figure 2. The following differences  were observed between the Lot codes from the 2nd source and  the lot codes directly from Intersil and the 1st source [4]:


1. The font;


2. The space between the lines;


3. Pin 1 designation mark;


4. Marking on the bottom side.


This paper presented a case study where failed devices from  factory testing and field returns in suspect lot codes were  purchased from a 2nd source, due to the unavailability of the  obsolete device from the regular 1st source. Many pieces of  physical evidence (exterior markings, pin designation dent, lead frame, bond wire routing, markings and traces on die  surface etc) suggested that the failed devices were not  consistent with genuine devices directly from manufacturer.  The suspect lot codes that were not recognizable by the  manufacturer were determined to be counterfeit devices.


In addition, the same comparative techniques were used to  verify that the replacement devices in a manufacturer  recognizable lot code were consistent with the genuine devices  directly from the manufacturer.


Figure 7: Comparison of a 3rd source Lot Code P509BXL  and the Lot Code P509BXR directly from Intersil.


Discussions


In general, to purchase electronic devices directly from  original manufacturers is the most reliable sourcing strategy.


However, this may not always be possible and distributors  need to be used. The devices from reputable distributors are  normally reliable in its manufacturer origin. The vulnerable  time for counterfeit devices to make their way to an OEM is  either when a device is obsolete or when there is a device  shortfall that the OEM has to look for alternative sources [6].


In this case, comparative analysis of perspective components is  needed in order to prevent a potential counterfeit device from  making its way to our production.


A counterfeit device may possess similar IV characteristics in  certain regions at room temperature as the genuine device. The  difference may eventually show up in different operational


regions (temperature, bias etc) when the counterfeit device  does not match the design intent of the genuine device.


In order to find out if a suspect device is consistent with a  genuine device, one or more comparative techniques from the  following may be used:


1. Exterior examinations (company logo, markings, pin  designation dent etc);


2. Lead frame and bond wire routing from X-ray  examinations (Level 1 packaging);


3. Stack-up from device cross-sectioning;


4. Examinations of marking on die surface in active and  non-active areas;


5. Examinations of Level 0 packaging from die surface  and cross-section of die;


6. Functional tests of the suspect device under devicespecified application conditions;


7. Functional tests of end product with suspect device in  product-specified application ranges.


In addition, the background information on the lot code history  from the device manufacturer is extremely important. It can verify if the suspect lot code has a match in the manufacturer’s  lot history, which may provide valuable clues for the overall  investigation direction of a potential counterfeit device.


For devices without lot code information on the markings, the  component traceability at the OEM is crucial in order to keep  track of the date/lot code information of a suspect component.

DNA to Safeguard Electrical Components


and Protect Against Counterfeiting and Diversion


James A. Hayward, Sc.D, Ph.D. and Janice Meraglia


james.hayward@adnas.com · janice.meraglia@adnas.com


DNA is a form of forensic evidence trusted by law  enforcement and recognized by international courts around the  world. This abstract provides an introduction to the utility of  botanical DNA taggants to provide supply chain security for  electronic components and to protect against counterfeiting  and diversion. A detailed treatment of the science behind


Applied DNA Sciences’ botanical DNA technology, its


applications to semiconductors and microchips and an  overview of DNA analysis by PCR and CE analysis is  provided.


Counterfeit Electronics – A Global Threat


The evolution of product counterfeiting as a trade nearly  parallels the evolution of technology itself. The last two  decades have witnessed explosive growth of technology, and  the condensation of travel, communication and the massive  impact of the internet ensured these new technologies were  laterally propagated instantly across the globe. Now  counterfeits emerge on the market nearly simultaneously with  new product launches, in time for the counterfeits to benefit  from the marketing efforts expended by the original. The  International Chamber of Commerce estimated in 2011 that  the global economic and social impacts of counterfeiting and  piracy will reach $1.7 Trillion by 2015, representing between  5% to 7% of all world trade (Source: International Chamber of  Commerce.) [1]. But this is more than a vexing nuisance for  brand owners. Counterfeits threaten economies, destroy health  and take lives, and destabilize the military.


The Defense Standardization Program Office (DSPO) Journal (Oct/Dec 2009) [2] recognizes the definition of a counterfeit


electronic part as “one whose identity or pedigree has been


deliberately altered, misrepresented or offered as an


authorized product.” Early reports of counterfeit electronics


emerged from industry. In May of 2006, the New York Times  reported a massively coordinated effort of 18 factories in  China to copy the entire product line of NEC. Nearly every  aspect of the company’s brand and product line had been  replicated and sold along parallel paths. NEC even found itself  honoring the warranties for the fakes.


In June of 2007, the US Department of the Navy suspected  that an increasing number of counterfeit electronics was  infiltrating the Department of Defense (DOD) supply chain. In  collaboration with the Department of Commerce (DOC), a


study was initiated to assess the defense industrial supply base  and to determine the statistical frequency of counterfeit  electronics penetrating DOD. The results of this study, finalized in January, 2010 (US DOC “Defense Industrial Base  Assessment: Counterfeit Electronics” [3]) showed:


All elements of the military supply chain have been  directly impacted by counterfeit electronics;


Stricter testing protocols and quality practices are  required; and,


The use of counterfeit detection technologies by parts  manufacturers, distributors and integrators should be  expanded.


It seems that every opportunity to leverage the efforts of  others is taken by counterfeiters. Both obsolete parts and  current production are copied. [4]


Current Anti-Counterfeiting Measures are Inadequate


Efforts to secure the authenticity of electronics are first  encountered at the primary and secondary packaging. Traditional security platforms to prevent counterfeits are now


also part of the counterfeiter’s target and consequently within  the retinue of counterfeiter’s resources. New advances in


holograms, optical strips and RFIDs are often available as  near-perfect copies within days of their initial launch.


Forensic DNA as a High-Content, High-Resolution


Taggant to Track Provenance and Ensure Authenticity


Evolved over eons, DNA (deoxyribonucleic acid) provides the  blueprint for all of biology. The information content is  massive and highly customized by organism. With a capacity  for content that is often compared to computer machine code, DNA is a linear polymer of extraordinary molecular weight  that stores its information as a sequence of infinitely variable


organic bases. However, unlike binary machine code, DNA’s


code is quaternary, storing its content as a linear array of four  organic options for each bit. With the uncanny variability of  DNA (consider all the variable across organisms and the  variation within species), matched by a stringent fidelity, and  detection methods that readily identify single molecules of a  unique sequence (a detectability that will never be matched by  any chemical or physical assay), DNA has become the “Gold  Standard of forensics.” Challenged by courts around the globe  since 1980, there is no better proof of identity, nor is any  better proof of identity likely to evolve.


Used by forensic laboratories all around the world, including  the FBI, DNA authentication is absolute in character. When  used to identify individuals or to establish paternity, the error  frequency for false positives is less than one in a trillion.


SigNature DNA botanical taggants cannot be copied or  reverse engineered and have already been independently  validated through a two-year vetted process conducted by the  Department of Energy (DOE) and the Idaho National  Laboratory. This technology has been selected as the sole  security platform in a program funded by the European  Regional Development Fund (a fund allocated by the  European Union) and Yorkshire Forward. DNA applications  will include the protection against counterfeiting and diversion  of UK manufactured textiles from “fiber to fabric.” Additionally, these taggants have been tremendously  successful with law enforcement agencies across Europe  resulting in criminal convictions and jail time.


In 2010 APDN successfully completed a program to DNA  mark microchips for an agency of the US Government.


An OCM marked 100% of its production for a period of two  months. The microchips themselves were scanned at the OCM  facility, the DNA-marked outer packaging was scanned at a  Distributor.  In a blind sampling, where both marked and  unmarked chips were sampled, forensic analysis confirmed the  authenticity of products DNA-marked as genuine.

Figure 1.  Backside de-processing: (a) schematics of the flipchip device; and (b) a graphic microprocessor unit before and  after silicon removal.


1) Gate-Level Defect Identification Capability


Figure 2 (a) shows an optical image of a 40 nm process GPU (graphics processing unit) from the backside of the die after  silicon has been etched away; and figure (b) shows the  scanning electron microscopic (SEM) image of the gate-level  IC structures in the logic area of the GPU chip. In the absence  of diffusion, two types of transistors can be easily  differentiated. The bare silicide source-drain contacts in  NMOS transistors and the presence of the stressors underneath  the silicide contacts in PMOS transistors are well resolved.


Poly gate between silicide contacts can be identified through  the gate dielectric with spacers on its sides. The trench  isolation oxide surrounding the original transistor active  regions has been preserved after etching; due to the excellent  etch selectivity of the KOH between silicon and oxide.


With IC structures fully exposed at the gate level, unprecedented access is offered for electron microscopic  imaging of IC structural anomalies and defects (such as poly or  silicide stringers, gate dielectric imperfections, or misprocessing from the fabrication manufacturing). If defects are  embedded inside the dielectrics under the surface, backscattered electron (BSE) imaging and secondary electron (SE) imaging can be used complementarily for the analysis.


Due to the superior sensitivity to atomic number (Z) contrast,BSE imaging enables the analysis of embedded defects  composed of high Z materials, such as high k gate materials,


NiSi of the source-drain contacts, W contact plugs, or Cu with  Ta/TaN buried below the surface of the low Z dielectric or  polysilicon layer [10]. Once the defect location is determined,a backside FIB cross section can be readily performed on the  spot without the assistance of CAD navigation.


Figure 2.  (a) Optical microscopic image showing a section of  a GPU chip exposed from the backside of the die after silicon  removal; (b) Close-up SEM image of transistor-level  structures of MOSFETs. Poly gates, silicide source-drain  contacts with W-plugs in nMOSFETs, and Si-Ge stressors in  pMOSFETs are all perfectly distinguishable.


Because all layers of the interconnect metallization are  preserved after KOH etching with only the node contacts  exposed, it is made viable to land nano-probes on any nodes to  perform electron beam assisted defect mapping techniques like  EBIC and EBAC, which are particularly useful for defect  localization on long metal traces [11].


Due to the absence of the silicon after the backside KOH etch,there will be no functional transistors left for characterization.


KOH Etch


Therefore, the de-processing technique cannot be applied to  identifying any crystalline or non-visible defects in silicon.


2) Capacitor Oxide Integrity Evaluation


The oxide integrity of polysilicon capacitors can be evaluated  after the silicon substrate is removed. Figure 3 (a-b) shows  backside optical and SEM microscopic images of polysilicon  capacitor oxide after silicon removal. The etch selectivity of  KOH leaves the remaining capacitor oxide intact over the  entire large capacitor area, as evidenced by the uniform blue  color in the optical image. High resolution SEM imaging  operating at a lower acceleration voltage (<10kV) can be used  to examine the oxide surface for pin holes and other flaws. The  capacitor breakdown site can be easily identified and analyzed.


Figure 3. (a) Optical microscopic image showing a  polysilicon capacitor after silicon is removed by KOH etching. The capacitor oxide, shown as bluish squares, is well  preserved over a large area after etching, as evidenced by the  uniform blue color. (b) A high resolution SEM image taken at  5kV e-beam voltage to highlight the capacitor oxide allowing  for the inspection of the oxide. (c) Increasing beam voltage to  12kV allows imaging of the M1 layer behind the poly/NiSi.


By using increased electron acceleration voltages, the metal  behind the poly capacitor plate can be imaged, making it  possible to identify anomalies located behind the poly  capacitor plate. Figure 3 (c) shows the SEM image of metal 1  located above the poly capacitor when the SEM is operated at  12kV.


3) Physical Evaluation of Electrically  Programmable Fuses (E-fuse)


Backside de-processing provides an excellent opportunity to  examine electrically programmable fuses (e-fuses) for  reliability assessment and fuse failure analysis.


Using traditional front-side hand-polishing methods, it is  difficult to maintain a leveled polishing over a large chip area. As a result, the oxide cover over the e-fuses will vary in  thickness, with the effect that no two fuses will look the same, even under identical SEM imaging conditions. This makes it  difficult to physically compare and evaluate fuses blown under  different conditions. The backside de-processing, on the other  hand, makes it possible for a large number of e-fuses to be  examined under the same sample condition and SEM  operation condition without distortions from artifacts  introduced from the traditional front-side sample preparation.

New method for decapsulation of copper wire devices using LASER and subambient temperature chemical etch.


Matthew J. Lefevre, Frédéric Beauquis, and Michael Obein  Digit Concept, Secqueville en Bessin, France


Pascal Gounet and Sandrine Barberan


ST Ericsson, Grenoble, France


IC packages have been greatly improved over the  past several years. With the adoption of Cu wires  and new green EMC (Epoxy Molding Compound), the suppression of lead, the use of Cu pillars and  the increased number of dies, the verification of the  quality of the assembly and failure analysis  becomes critical. Starting twelve years ago, LASER  ablation was introduced as a means to facilitate the  pre-decapsulation of packages aiming at a  completion by wet chemistry (acids) or dry chemistry (plasma). The decapsulation process with acid at  medium temperature (75°C) does not permit to keep  the Cu wires intact. Our studies and work in the past  several years has consisted in lowering the  temperature of acid use in order to minimize the  effect of acid attack on the Al pads and Cu wires. Currently the thinnest wires used are 0.6 mil in  diameter (approximately 15m). In this article we  will demonstrate that decapsulations at sub-ambient  temperatures are now possible and give expected  results. Moreover, openings at near ambient  temperature reduce the component deformation and  also the deformation of its constituents compared to  decapsulations at medium or high temperature.  Introduction


During the past several years there has been a  gradual but steady shift from Au to Cu for the wires  and bonding materials used in microelectronic  packages.  The advantages of using Cu wires and  interconnects are numerous:  lower cost, higher  strength and better shear resistance for greater  package integrity at smaller sizes, higher thermal  and electrical conductivity for improved heat  dissipation and faster device performance, etc [1].


While these changes in materials and processing  have brought lower cost devices with improved  performance, it has created new challenges for the  failure analysis and reliability testing of these new  products.  Many of the tests the failure analysis or  reliability engineer must perform require  decapsulation of the device in order to access the  wires, bonds, and die within the package, the  majority of which are encapsulations made from  EMCs.  Traditionally these types of packages have  been opened either by chemical means alone (typically nitric acid or a nitric/sulfuric acid mix) or by  first making a pre-cavity by mechanical means or  with a laser, followed by an acid etch to expose the  die.  It is not possible to expose the die itself solely  by mechanical means or by laser without damage, but making a pre-cavity does have the advantage of  reducing the time of acid exposure that the wires


see [2].  This is very important as Cu is attacked (oxidized) by nitric acid.  The degree of oxidation  that will occur on the wires is a function of  temperature, exposure time, and surface state of  the wires (passivation layer such as Pd on wires or  not).  The overexposure of Cu to nitric acid can lead  to wire thinning or pitting, and bond pad corrosion, all of which can impact the values measured for  various mechanical and electrical tests.  A  technique is needed to decapsulate devices with Cu  components without degrading them either  mechanically or electrically.  Much effort has already  gone into developing such techniques, with varying  degrees of success [1, 2, 3, 4].


The objectives of this study were twofold: 1) to show  the impact of etch temperature on the oxidation of  the Cu components, and 2) within the constraints of  the etch temperature used, to optimize the  remaining parameters and procedures of  decapsulation in order to minimize or eliminate  degradation of the Cu wires/bonds.


Temperature studies:


For these studies, openings were made on several  package types; QFP packages with 0.8 mil diameter  Cu wires and Cu lead frames, TBGA packages also  with 0.8 mil diameter Cu wires, and 23mm x 23mm  BGA packages containing 0.6 mil Cu wires with Pd  coating. All decapsulations were made using a  combination of laser ablation (using Sesame 1000  from Digit Concept) and acid etch using an auto  decapper (Sesame 777 from Digit Concept, manufactured by RKD Engineering).  The predecapsulation of each sample type was performed  using the identical program on the Sesame 1000.  The advantage of laser ablation (as compared to  mechanical grinding) is its precision, repeatability, and the ability to make a cavity to the level of the  bonding wires without damaging them.  For the QFP  samples, the laser pre-cavities were made in two  steps, starting with an ablation over the entire  surface area above the die and stitch bonds until the  tops of the wire loops were exposed, followed by  continued ablation uniquely above the stitch bonds (exclusion zone above the die).  This is done with  the goal of exposing the die and stitches at roughly  the same time during the chemical etch step.  It is  also possible to make the laser pre-opening in one  step, expose the die by chemical etch, then replace  the package in the laser to remove any remaining  EMC above the stitch bonds.  This alternative  approach was employed on the TBGA and BGA  samples to avoid nitric acid damage of the PCB  support for these package types.


Next a customized monolithic gasket for the  chemical etch was designed and cut using the  Sesame 1000.  The entire package fits snugly into  the gasket with an opening that precisely defines  the area to be etched. This allows for good accuracy  of the chemical opening without acid leak.  For all  temperature studies, final decapsulation by acid was  made at the following temperatures; 44°C, 27°C, 15°C, and 10°C.


Temperature Study 1, QFP 11mm x 11mm:


For this study a 3:1 Nitric acid to sulfuric acid  mixture was used.


Figure 1a. Optical micrograph of stitch bonds for  opening made at 44°C for 40s.


Figure 1b. SEM micrograph of die bonds and wires  on sample decapsulated at 44°C.


In Figure 1a we can see that the metal coating on  the lead fingers has been mostly removed and that  the Cu wires have been attacked and reduced in  diameter.  The general condition of the wires is  clearer in the SEM micrograph shown in Figure 1b. The state of the wires is very heterogeneous, some  showing high degrees of thinning and pitting due to  acid attack, others in almost perfect state.  However,


at this temperature the vast majority of the wires  show signs of attack and the pads and lead fingers  show signs of corrosion.  The nominal wire  thickness should be 20m (0.8mil), whereas the  wires measured on this sample varied from  approximately 14m to 19m.


Figure 2a. Optical micrograph of 27°C sample  showing state of stitch bonds and lead fingers. An  etch time of 100s was required for this sample.


Figure 2b. SEM photo of die bonds on 27°C  sample.


Already at 27°C there is a vast improvement in the  quality of the wires and bonds after complete  decapsulation.  The state of the wires is much more  homogeneous than at 44°C, with the most part  having a measured diameter between 19m and


19.5m.  Also, it can be noted in Figure 1a that the  metal layer on the lead fingers has remained intact  over the majority of the surface area uncovered, with just small areas on the right side of the image  where it can be seen that this layer has been  removed.


Decreasing the etch temperature further to 15°C we  see that the surface of the lead fingers has  improved again, with the coating nearly intact  homogeneously.  Furthermore, the wires, as shown  in Figure 3b are very uniform, with a measured  diameter of approximately 19.5m.


Figure 3a. Optical micrograph of lead fingers and  stitch bonds for package opened at 15°C for 200s.


Figure 3b. SEM micrograph showing state of die  bonds and wires after decapsulation at 15°C.


The results of the openings performed at 10°C can  be seen in Figures 1a-b.  In this case there is no  observable attack or corrosion on the lead finger  coating or die pads, and the wires are again very  uniform with a measured diameter of roughly


19.5m and no evidence of thinning or pitting.


Figure 4a.  Optical micrograph of lead fingers and  stitch bonds for 10°C, 270s opening.


Figure 4b.  SEM photo of die bonds and wires for  sample decapsulated by acid etch at 10°C.


Temperature Study 2, TBGA 8mm x 8mm:


In this temperature study a slightly more aggressive  5:1 nitric/sulfuric acid mix was used for the final  openings, again at the same four temperatures as in  the previous study.  The wire thickness is the same  here, at 0.8 mil (~20m), and the results obtained  were very similar, though the reduced etch times are  likely attributable to the different acid ratio.


Figure 5a.  SEM micrograph of TBGA sample  opened at 44°C for 20s.


In Figure 5a it can be seen that the state of the  wires is obviously degraded after exposure to the  acid at 44°C, even with a relatively short opening  time of 20s.  There is evidence of pitting on all the  wires, and noticeable thinning on a large  percentage, although all the wires remained intact. Upon reducing the etch temperature to 27°C, the  noticeable wire thinning was eliminated though  there remains a significant amount of pitting visible  on the wire surfaces Figure 5b). In Figure 5b-5d, it  can be seen that one of the wire has been FIB cut to  investigate the thickness of the wires.  Despite the


pitting on the wires opened at 27°C, the diameter  was measured at 19.71m, which is very close to  the expected nominal wire thickness.


Figure 5b.  Wires of TBGA sample opened at 27°C  for 40s.  The wire at top has been cut by FIB for  examination.


Figure 5c. SEM micrograph of 15°C (75s) decapsulation.


There is essentially no evidence of pitting due to  acid attack on any of the wires opened at either  15°C or 10° (figures 5c and 5d).  FIB sections gave  measured wire diameters in the range of 19.71 to


19.92m, suggesting no wire thinning has occurred.


Figure 5d. SEM image showing state of wires for  10°C, 90s opening.


Temperature Study 3, BGA 23mm x 23mm:


The third set of temperature studies were  particularly interesting in that the wires in these  packages were thinner (0.6mil or ~15m) and had a  thin Pd coating on them.  The objective here was to  not only see if the packages could be opened  without attacking the wires, but if the Pd coating  could be conserved as well. As such, a less  aggressive acid mixture of 3:1 HNO3/H2SO4 was  used for these openings.


As in all the other studies, the minimum etch times  were used in order to completely expose the entire  die surface.  At 44°C, an etch time of 80s was  required to fully expose the die of the BGA  packages.  At this temperature, the vast majority of  the wires had been thinned to breaking, as can be  seen in the optical image in Figure 6a.


Figure 6a. Optical micrograph of 44°C opening with  etch time of 80s.


While lowering the etch temperature to 27°C did  improve the situation somewhat, there are still some  wires thinned to the point of breaking, with pitting  and thinning in evidence on almost all wires, as  seen in Figure 6b.


Figure 6b. SEM micrograph of BGA sample  decapsulated at 27°C for 120s.


Acceptable results were finally obtained at an etch  temperature of 15°C, shown below in Figure 6c.  No  evidence of thinning and only minimal pitting is  observed on the wires etched at this temperature.


Figure 6c.  Image of BGA package etched at 15°C  for 160s.  Note the wire cut in the middle has been  sectioned by FIB for analysis.


At 10°C, see Figure 6d, the wires showed no  evidence of thinning or pitting, and the wire  diameters were measured at 15.25m.  Furthermore, EDX analysis was carried out on both  the 15°C and 10°C samples. Peaks were observed  not only for the Cu in the wires, but also for the Pd  coating and for the Al on the die bond pads, evidence that neither of these coating were attacked  during the etch at either 15°C or 10°C.  The  presence of the Pd coating after etch confirms that  the wires have not been thinned by acid attack.


Figure 6d. View of wires and die bonds for sample  opened at 10°C for 190s.


Figure 6e. EDX spectra taken on wires opened at  15°C near die bond.  Peaks show presence of Cu  from wires, the Pd coating, as well as the Al from  the bond pads.

Parameter optimization


It is clear from our accumulated experiences, that  the degree of acid attack (oxidation) on Cu wires  varies with etch temperature and time, with the  temperature having the greater impact of the two, as  demonstrated here in the preceding temperature  studies. Obviously, lowering the etch temperature is  going to increase the time necessary to complete a  decapsulation, but there are many steps that can be  taken to minimize the etch time necessary at any  given temperature.


The first, which was noted previously in the  introduction [2], is to make a pre-opening, or cavity, in the EMC before commencing the chemical etch. This step can easily cut the etch time in half.  In  these studies laser ablation was used, due to its  rapidity, precision, and repeatability. It is also  possible to remove slightly more material with the  laser than by mechanical means as we can remove  the EMC to expose the tops of the wire loops  without damaging the wires themselves if sufficient  precaution is taken.  Also, while the samples in this  study were relatively large at 8mm, 11mm, and  23mm, the laser can easily pre-decapsulate many of  the smaller packages we encounter at 1mm x 1mm, without the sidewalls being breached by subsequent  chemical etch.


A second parameter that can have a great impact  on the etch time necessary to expose the die is the  ratio of HNO3 to H2SO4 in the acid mixture.  A  higher percentage of nitric acid can significantly  reduce the etch times required, but at the increased  risk of acid attack.  For this reason, an additional  study was performed on the TBGA samples to  determine the optimal mixture ratio.


Acid mixture study:


Openings were made on the same TBGA packages  used in the second temperature study, using the  identical laser program for the pre-opening cavities.  Acid ratios of 9:1, 5:1, 3:1, 2:1, and 1:1 were tested, all at an etch temperature of 10°C. The results of  this study are summarized in Table 2.


The best results for this specific package type were  obtained using nitric to sulfuric acid ratios of 5:1, 3:1, and 2:1 (Figures 7b-7d), with the etch time  required for the 5:1 mixture being approximately half  that using a 2:1 mixture. It is clear that using a  higher percentage of HNO3 results in significant  savings in not only time but also the amount of acid  consumed. However, one must be cautious as using  a mixture too strong in nitric acid, even at subambient temperatures, can lead to significant pitting, wire thinning and breakage (see Figure 7a). It is  also interesting to note that decreasing the amount  of HNO3 too low can result in etch times so long  that the wires can be attacked despite the low etch  temperature which has been used to retard the  onset of acid induced damage. This can be seen in


Figure 7e, which shows pitting and some thinning of  the wires for the package decapsulated using a 1:1  acid mixture.

Industry Deployment of DNA Markers


As shown in the flow chart below, the Procurement entity  within the electronics industry typically services a range of  End Users and would engage Applied DNA Sciences in the  DNA marking process. Applied DNA would work with trusted  supply chain participants, including, but not limited to, the  Integrator, Distributor and Original Component Manufacturers (OCMs) and create unique DNA markers to be embedded into  the microchip. Statistical confidence levels are established to  determine authentication parameters. Lab analysis is then  performed, typically in a non-destructive manner, at any point  along the logistics chain. The analysis would absolutely


distinguish between genuine and counterfeit components and  unequivocal Forensic ID would be declared. The result would


be authentic components in the end users’ product with


counterfeit components segregated and supported with  forensic proof should legal action be deemed appropriate.

Without removing the die from the flip chip package, the  silicon substrate of the die is first mechanically ground, either  locally over the area of interest, or globally over the entire die, until the remaining silicon substrate is thin, smooth and  uniform in thickness. KOH wet chemical etching is then  performed to remove the remaining silicon. The process and  the result are illustrated in figure 1. The wet etching typically  takes less than 30min, depending upon the thickness of the  remaining silicon from the mechanical thinning. Since KOH  etches oxide much more slowly than silicon, the etching will  leave the oxide and nitride dielectric materials intact and  yields a dielectric surface free of silicon. The etching process  can be easily monitored with the naked eye without use of an  optical microscope until the end of the process. After the  silicon is completely removed, the remaining die is


Exacerbating the inadequacy of packaging security solutions, most distributors and integrators store microchips and  semiconductors in high-volume bins, capable of matching the


demands on their supply. This “bin approach” excludes the


packaging to save space and time, so security must be  implemented at the product level.


Product inspections offer limited value as a method of  authentication. External visual inspections should not be used  as a standalone authentication. Physicochemical  characterizations are often destructive and rely on a degree of  similarity to a bona fide original and the tolerance of the  measurements.


Taggants can provide a unique code or fingerprint to  authenticate originality. However, as evidentiary tools, the  value of a taggant increases as a function of the density of its  in formation content. Mineral taggants, which simply provide  parameters of chemical identity and concentration, are only


effective as rapid screening tools, often by handheld detectors. Stochastic arrays of fibers or particles are difficult to  incorporate in the media used to fabricate microchips and  semi-conductors. Stochastic arrays of nanoparticulate ferrite


can generate complex “fingerprint” patterns, but care must be


exercised to ensure the magnetic field does not interfere with  semiconductor function.


oxide


Figure 4 (a) shows an optical image of e-fuses from the  backside of the die, taken through the remaining thin layer of  oxide. After backside KOH de-processing, an additional  Buffered Oxide Etch (BOE) step was used to thin the  remaining oxide to the appropriate thickness for SEM imaging. A large number of e-fuses covered underneath an oxide of  uniform thickness can be consistently examined and compared.   The SEM image in Figure 4 (b) reveals many details of the  physical differences in fuses blown under different  programming conditions. The impact of changes caused by  varying the blowing conditions, or the level of electrical and  thermal stress used in a reliability test can be assessed. The  valuable information obtained this way can be used for  determining the optimal fuse programming (blowing) conditions [12].


Figure 4. (a) Optical image showing an array of e-fuses blown  under various conditions, viewed through a thin layer of oxide  from the substrate side of the die, after Si removal. (b) Closeup SEM images showing details of the fuse physical changes  that results. The differences in the fuse microstructural  changes resulted from various programming conditions are


clearly visible. Images such as these are invaluable for the  systematic study of the fuse blowing mechanism.


4) Backside Parallel Lapping


Once the silicon is chemically removed using KOH, exposure  of different levels of the IC structures for visual inspection and  electrical probing becomes much easier. The SEM and optical  images in Figures 5 and 6 show interconnect metal at different  levels viewed from the backside of the chip after a succession  of backside parallel lapping. Since the smaller geometry circuit  elements and structures lie in front of the larger geometry, it is  easier to see several layers of IC structures during optical or  electron microscopic inspections. This can greatly increase the  likelihood of visually locating the defect that is buried within  dielectric materials. When the defect is found, further deprocessing of the die using techniques, such as FIB cross  sectioning can be performed to expose the physical defect for  analysis.


Combining backside silicon removing capability with backside  parallel lapping technique, FA analysts can easily access the  suspected failure site at any specific level and location in an IC  device. Once the suspected defect is near, voltage contrast  analysis, nano-probing and electron beam assisted defect  mapping techniques like EBIC and EBAC can be used to  further narrowing down the defects for the subsequent physical  analysis.


(a) Polysilicon


(b) Metal 1


(c) Metal 2


(d) Metal 5


Figure 5. SEM images showing exposed CMOS IC structures  at different physical levels after a succession of backside  parallel lapping.


Figure 6. Optical image showing metal 5 after removal of the  layers above it. Metal 6 traces under the surface are visible. Backside de-processing makes it possible for optical  inspection to catch embedded defects in the dielectric.


5) Backside FIB Cross Sectioning


If an emission spot identified from a backside PEM image is  determined to come from the failure site, FIB cross section can  be performed readily at the emission spot from the backside of  the chip. Since the IC features visible on the de-processed die  closely resembles those seen in the images provided by the  backside optical fault isolation tool, FIB cross sectioning can  be performed precisely at the failure site without dense and  very regular interconnect metallization features blocking the  view. CAD navigation becomes unnecessary for backside FIB  cross sectioning.


Figure 7 shows a solid emersion lens (SIL) photo emission  microscopic image and a SEM image of the FIB cross section  of a backside de-processed part with a failing capacitor. The  cross section SEM image reveals a mis-alignment between the  diffusion and the polysilicon layers.


Figure 7. (a) Backside SIL emission image showing a “hot  spot” on a defective poly-diffusion capacitor, taken through  the silicon substrate; (b) SEM image of a FIB cross-section  through the emission location revealing poly to diffusion  misalignment.


6) Conclusion


This paper has demonstrated a novel de-processing technique  for flip-chip ICs that provides an effective and reliable method  for the exposure of gate-level IC structures with a complete  preservation of interconnect metallization. As a result, great  opportunities are presented for direct implementation of  microscopic imaging of gate-level IC structures and FIB cross  sectioning of defects responsible for failures. When combined  with backside parallel lapping, the technique makes it feasible  for electrical nano-probing and electron beam assisted fault  isolation techniques to be applied for PFI and PFA of devices  with defective metallization.


The authors wish to thank William Lo and Steven Kasapi for  reviewing the paper and providing technical feedbacks.

2. The differences in many physical features showed that both  lot codes from the 2nd source were not consistent with the  devices directly from Intersil, while the lot codes from 1st source and directly from Intersil were consistent.

Analyses of a Replacement Lot Code


A lot code (P509BXL) from a 3rd source with sufficient supply  was analyzed. Examinations did not identify any appreciable  difference in part markings, bond wire routing, lead frame and  markings on die surfaces of this lot code from the lot code  directly from Intersil (Figure 7).


It was concluded that devices from this lot code were genuine  Intersil device and were suitable for the production of Harman  amplifiers in the subwoofer [5].


Thus, the devices from a replacement source were certified, and suitable for production which had been disrupted by the  suspect lot codes from the 2nd source.

Is an environmentally friendly, “green” technology  derived from botanical DNA,


Is used as a taggant at extremely low concentrations,  Will not alter the quality of any carrier (such as ink, coatings, adhesives, plastics or  commercial products)


Will not require major changes in manufacturing process, Persists, in physicochemical extremes such as harsh  outdoor environs and high temperatures,


Is chemically compatible in a wide range of security inks (overt and covert), varnishes, adhesives and substrates,  Can be layered with other anti-counterfeiting measures, including barcodes, holograms, RFIDs, etc.


Is detectable in the field, and finally,


Can be forensically authenticated when analyzed in the  laboratory.


Key Attributes of SigNature DNA


Applied DNA has proved that its botanical DNA technology  provides the following advantages over existing competitive  security options.


Resistant to reverse engineering or replication. The  botanical SigNature DNA platform is virtually impossible to


digital video discs (DVDs) in China included 12 anticounterfeiting security platforms. Within nine months of the  launch of the DVDs, 11 of those 12 anti-counterfeiting  technologies were replicated in the market place, with  SigNature DNA being the only exception. Moreover, SigNature DNA taggants on those DVDs remain effective to  date, three years after launch.


Secure. Applied DNA maintains its records of DNA  sequences on a secure server. Sequences are encrypted, available to individuals on only a partial basis.


Low Cost and High Accuracy. SigNature DNA taggants are  relatively inexpensive when compared to other anticounterfeiting measures, such as RFIDs, integrated circuit  chips, and holograms. The costs associated with the  production of DNA taggants are not significant since the  amount of DNA required for each taggant is small, and the  cloning of the DNA segments is performed inside  microorganisms such as yeast or bacteria, which are highly  productive and inexpensive to grow. In addition, incorporating SigNature DNA into products does not require  major changes to the manufacturing process or logistic  chain. The relatively low cost of SigNature DNA does not  affect its reliability. The probability of mistakenly  identifying a SigNature DNA taggant is less than one in a  trillion, making it virtually impossible to wrongly identify  something marked with SigNature DNA.


Easily Integrated with Other Anti-Counterfeit  Technologies. SigNature DNA taggants can be embedded  into RFID devices, labels, serial numbers, holograms, and  other marking systems using inks, threads, and other media. The Company believes that combined with other traditional  methods, the SigNature DNA solution provides a significant  deterrent against counterfeiting, product diversion, piracy, fraud, and identity theft.


DNA for Cash-in-Transit Services: the Ultimate Arbiter of  Secure Logistics


Since January 2008, Applied DNA has been working with Loomis UK, a cash-handling company that moves over £150  billion in cash annually. Backside De-processing of Integrated Circuits 40 nm and Below


Yuanjing Li, Steven Scott, and Howard Lee Marks


NVIDIA Corporation, Santa Clara, CA 95050, USA  Email: janli@nvidia.com


Abstract - This paper presents a novel backside de-processing  technique for effective failure analysis of advanced multi-level  interconnect metallization CMOS ICs  with flip chip package.


The advancement of chip-level failure analysis techniques is  driven by device geometry scaling, the increasing number of  interconnect metallization layers, and increasingly dense  bumps in packaging.


For localizing a failure site in an advanced CMOS integrated  circuit (IC), laser-based backside techniques [1-7] involving  analyses through the silicon substrate have been widely  adopted. However, even when successful, these techniques can only isolate the defect to either a logic gate consisting of  several MOS transistors or a metal net between a driver and a  receiver some distance away [8]. In a photo-emission  microscopy image, a “hot spot” does not necessarily reveal the  level of the defect (z location), especially if there are  overlapping circuit elements spread across numerous device  layers. Further physical failure localization (PFI) to a smaller  area of interest during physical failure analysis (PFA) is  usually required.


During PFA, it is a common practice to perform de-processing  using parallel lapping (p-lapping), starting from the top surface  of the die and working, layer by layer, downwards towards the  transistor level. This allows every layer in the IC to be  examined for anomalies. For a leading-edge CMOS IC chip  with multi-level interconnects and flip chip packaging, this  process can be very time-consuming and difficult, especially  when the defect lies at the lower, smaller-geometry metal  layers of the IC. Since backside optical fault isolation analysis  often involves backside thinning of the die to 100 m, or even  less, there is also a significant risk of breaking the device  under test (DUT) during the process of removing the thin die  from the package and remounting it for the lapping operation. Finally, when starting de-processing from the front-side, there  can be a bias towards identifying defects in the upper metal  layers, even if they were not the root cause of the original  failure. This can occur, for example, if p-lapping is stopped at  the first indication of a defect to allow closer analysis of the  defect by focused ion beam (FIB) cross-sectioning. FIB crosssectioning can destroy evidence of the underlying, root-cause  defect. For these reasons, backside de-processing can be


particularly advantageous for failure analysis of flip chip  packaged devices.


Backside


de-processing


technique


using  Tetramethylammonium hydroxide (TMAH) wet chemical  etching for silicon-on-insulator (SOI) technology devices was  previously reported [9]. However, the technique takes  advantage of the natural etch-stop formed by the buried-oxide (BOX) layer in SOI devices and cannot be used on bulk  CMOS technology devices.


This paper presents a novel method of de-processing bulk  CMOS ICs that reliably exposes physical structures of IC  devices at different levels. The technique uses a potassium  hydroxide (KOH) chemical wet etch to etch away all silicon  from the backside of the die. The gate dielectrics, source-drain  silicide contacts, and all layers of interconnect metallization  remain intact after etching. This allows a direct access to  transistor level structures and lower level interconnect for  electron microscopic imaging, FIB cross section, and electron  beam assisted fault isolation techniques. Using this technique,the entire FA process including sample preparation (mechanical thinning and wet chemical etching), optical fault  localization, nano-probing, microscopic imaging, and FIB  cross sectioning can be performed on the backside of the die  without detaching the die from the package substrate. The  technique opens new avenues for failure analysis of advanced  multi-level interconnect metallization IC chips with flip chip  packaging at the 40nm technology node and beyond.


Backside De-processing Procedure


Thus, until now, the front side approach was not used on these  technologies [2]. However, for some kinds of defects, it  becomes mandatory to access the die surface. To be able to  perform failure analyses with the front side approach, new  solutions were evaluated for the package removal. This paper  presents a method able to safely reach the die. A possible  artifact was identified, and the related physical mechanism was  explained. Thus, the method presented below is optimized and  enables a reliable and repeatable front side access to the die  for BCB/Cu technologies in package.


New decapsulation technique description


Dry etching


Because the standard wet chemical etching is not suitable on  this BCB/Cu technology, for safely reaching the die surface, another technique must be used to allow successful  observation of possible defects.


We have evaluated several alternative solutions including  mechanical polishing, laser ablation, plasma and FIB etching. However, used independently, all of them are not appropriate.


- Mechanical polishing cuts the wires preventing from  further advanced electrical tests and it requires a high  experience to obtain a perfect parallel polishing.


- Laser ablation cannot be used down to the die  because it would damage the die by melting the active  layers.


- FIB etching is too local and cannot be used to access  the front side surface of the die.


The solution using dry chemical etching was finally identified  as the most promising. Dry etching presents advantages of wet  chemical etching and grinding. Like wet etching, it works with  selectivity between the different materials, especially between  the wires and the die, keeping the electrical integrity. Like  grinding, it has an efficient control of the process in the  component.


The resin of the package contains several materials, mainly  epoxy and fillers. In order to etch this material a specific dry  etcher is required. The plasma etcher that was used (FA2000P) can deliver CF4 and O2 gases with different mixes. The plasma  can be used for epoxy removal; however, these two gasses are  not able to etch the fillers included in the package resin. As a  result, these fillers would remain on the die surface, and  generate a mask stopping the chemical reaction. Thus, on the  contrary to a standard RIE process, a filler blast system has  been used. It consists in periodically blasting CO2 at high  pressure at the surface of the component. This step enables the  ejection of the mask generated by the fillers allowing the resin  to be properly removed down to the die.


This process could be effective on the decapsulation of  BCB/Cu technology in package but it would be too timeconsuming for most of the packages (with an etch rate of about  100μm/h) and is not compatible with production. This solution  alone cannot be adopted.


Pre-opening of the package


The dry etching is useful for reaching the die but it is not  required at the beginning of the decapsulation. Thus, a preopening step of the package has to be performed with another  technique. To rapidly remove the major part of the package  material, mechanical grinding and laser ablation are usable to  reduce its thickness. Laser ablation was chosen because of four  main advantages over mechanical grinding: it keeps the  integrity of the wires and the rigidity of the package, it is faster  and it is not user dependent. Moreover, the laser enables a


local or global decapsulation thanks to the accurate definition  of the beam scanning.


Laser and plasma combination


The laser used is a pulsed Nd-YAG laser with a wavelength of  1064nm and a continuous power of 10W. The Q-switching  enables high energy pulses that lead to the sublimation of the  resin. This laser ablation is performed down to approximately  100μm from the die to prevent the melting of the active layers


[3]. After this pre-opening step, the package resin is thin  enough for reactive ion etching (RIE) to reach the die in an  acceptable time (a couple of hours). The slow etch rate of the  reaction becomes an advantage for good control of the  process. Pure O2 plasma can be used for epoxy removal but  adding CF4 significantly accelerates the reaction. The fluorine  atoms enhance extraction of the hydrogen atoms of the epoxy  and improve the molecule breakdown of the oxygen gas. It  increases the density of reactive molecules in the chamber  leading to a higher etch rate.


In a standard way, two different processes are sequentially  used to reach the die. The first one is performed with 20% CF4 and 80% O2. The second one, used close to the die, is done  with pure O2. Because CF4 can etch the BCB layer, this second  process seems valuable in order to preserve the BCB layer. The main plasma parameters of the two processes are given in  Table 1.


Table 1: Plasma parameters


This sequential use of laser ablation and plasma opening leads  to an optimized result:


•


The total opening time is short enough as about 3  hours (including some minutes for laser ablation and the main  duration for plasma removing of the resin of about 100μm  thick)


The laser ablation offers a clean pre-opening of the  package.


Due to the local ablation, the rigidity of the package  is preserved and all contacts are usable for further electrical  tests.


The BCB material remains after opening.


However, after plasma etching, cracks in the BCB were  sometimes generated (fig. 3). Plasma parameters were varied  to either reproduce or avoid these cracks; the purpose being to  understand the mechanism of creation of these cracks.


Process 1


Process 2


Gas


20%CF4   80%O2


O2

(bottom electrode)

Filler blast


Figure 3: Crack in BCB after a non optimized RIE  process


Cracks in BCB: causes and solutions


The investigations were split into two studies to understand the  cause of the occurrence of the cracks in the BCB.


Influence of the plasma parameters on crack generation


The first one is the identification of the parameters influencing  the generation of cracks. Three main results were obtained.


The crack formation is linked to the kinetics of the  reaction. Cracks are accentuated when the chemical reaction is  enhanced in the chamber (higher temperature, higher  power…). As a consequence, the outbreaks of cracks could  partly be explained by a physical and chemical phenomenon.  •


The sample holder has a crucial influence. For  processes performed with a holder of low thermal  conductivity, cracks were generated. This leads to consider a  thermal aspect in the mechanism of cracks formation.


Adding CF4 in the plasma prevents crack occurrence, meaning that CF4 is an inhibitor of crack generation.


Physical analysis


The second part of the investigation consisted in performing  physical analysis on three different samples: one reference  sample that has not sustained any plasma treatment, one  sample with crack after plasma etching, one sample without  any crack after plasma etching. The analysis revealed that after  oxygen plasma the samples present an added layer above the  BCB (fig.4).


A.


B.


Figure 4: SEM view of the top of the BCB after FIB crosssection: A. before RIE; B. after RIE with O2


This phenomenon can be explained by the BCB composition  analysis. BCB is a polymer containing C, H, O and Si atoms (fig.5.A). Oxygen plasma etches carbon bonds but not silicon  ones [4]. Thus, in an oxygen plasma, carbon is etched out but  silicon stays on the surface of the die. Silicon combines with  the oxygen of the plasma and generates a modified BCB layer, a kind of silicon oxide layer (fig. 5.B).


Figure 5: A. BCB composition; B. Example of modified BCB  composition


This phenomenon was confirmed by EDX analysis performed  on the cross-section of a sample with cracks (fig.6). The  analysis shows that the added layer is poor in carbon and rich  in silicon and oxygen (spectrum 1) compared to the initial  BCB (spectrum 2).  As the plasma process goes on, this  modified BCB layer grows up to 200nm and then acts as a  mask that stops the etching process.


Figure 6: EDX spectrum in BCB and in modified BCB layers: A. SEM image; B. EDX : spectrum 1: modified BCB with a  low C peak and high Si and O peaks ; Spectrum 2: BCB: with  a high C peak and low O and Si peaks.


Moreover, it was demonstrated that cracks can be generated on  a sample with a modified BCB layer if it is exposed to high  temperatures. A sample exposed to pure oxygen plasma but  without cracks after plasma process was heated outside of the  RIE equipment to 250°C during few minutes. A reference  sample not exposed to the plasma was heated up in the same  way. The heating step does not affect the reference sample  whereas the studied sample becomes cracked.


Because of the new structure of the modified BCB, its CTE  changed. Due to this difference in CTE between the BCB and  modified BCB, it is assumed that the plasma induced thermal  stress generates a mechanical stress between the two layers. This relaxation may then induce cracks.


Synthesis on the crack generation mechanism


The plasma process creates cracks if the BCB is modified by  the plasma etching reaction and if a thermal stress is then  applied to the component. In the plasma chamber, we showed  that a temperature elevation at about 100°C during 1hour (a  third of the total process) is critical to the die and generates  cracks.


In oxygen plasma, both conditions are gathered: the layer of  modified BCB is generated due to O2 plasma, and the  temperature continuously ramps up in the chamber due to the  plasma reaction. If samples do not present cracks after pure  oxygen plasma, it means that the temperature was not enough  to mechanically stress the structure.


Solutions to avoid crack generation


As a consequence, in order to avoid crack generation during  plasma etching, two solutions are available: either the  modified BCB should not be generated and/or the temperature  should be reduced.


It would be preferable to keep the pure oxygen plasma etching  to reach the die because of the high selectivity of the reaction  between epoxy and BCB. However, as shown in figure 6, the  oxygen plasma etching modifies the top surface of the BCB  down to approximately 200nm. It could be a solution to use  this phenomenon for reaching the die without any risk of  etching the whole BCB.


In order to take the advantage of this physical and chemical  phenomenon, a solution would be to reduce the temperature in  the chamber to avoid the BCB/modified BCB inducing crack  in the sample. Trials demonstrated that decreasing the  temperature regulation in the chamber reduce the crack  occurrence but is not sufficient to avoid them. The main factor  would be the nature of the sample holder. To limit BCB  heating and enable the next steps of the analysis, it is necessary  to define a very specific sample holder. It should have an  excellent thermal conductivity, a good electrical conductivity, a resistance to the temperatures involved in the plasma  chamber, and a chemical resistance to the gasses. Moreover, it  should be easily removed after decapsulation without  damaging the BCB for the next step of the analysis. Today, this kind of sample holder has not been found. Therefore, reaching the die with pure O2 is not possible without  generating cracks.


To preserve the BCB, the only solution is to prevent the  formation of the modified BCB layer. In contrast to O2, CF4 is  able to etch C and Si bonds. Thus, with a mix of O2 and CF4, Si atoms do not remain on the die surface and the modified  BCB layer is not generated. Consequently, the high  temperature in the chamber does not create specific stress at  the surface of the die, avoiding the occurrence of cracks. This  is why CF4 acts as an inhibitor in the mechanism of crack  generation.


The trials showed that by adding from 5% to 20% of CF4 in  the oxygen plasma, cracks are not generated.


This mix does not generate cracks, however, it can etch the  whole BCB if the process is not controlled. Thanks to the slow  reaction, it is easily possible to stop the process on time. The  validity and repeatability of this method were shown on  numerous samples (several samples in 4 different design  families).


Modified BCB


BCB


Spectrum 2


Figure 7: SEM view of the die surface after decapsulation  with a mix of CF4 and O2 gases


This new method combining laser and plasma with the specific  RIE recipe was validated on different components and was  applied, in particular, in one failure analysis case published in  reference [5]. The main points of this analysis are presented  here below to illustrate the advantage of the developed  approach.


Failed product description


The failed product concerns a RF component used in the  module transceiver of a mobile phone. It is composed of  several inductors and capacitors and the electrical circuit of the  area of interest is presented in figure 8. Inductors are noted  with a “B” and capacitors with a “C”.


Figure 8: Electrical circuit of the component under test


The corresponding schematic layout of the circuit is presented  in figure 9.


Figure 9: Schematic layout of the circuit


The inductors are made of copper metal layers and they are  isolated by BCB. A schematic cross-section of the die is


shown in figure 10.  The product is encapsulated in a SO20  plastic package.


Figure 10: schematic cross-section of the component


On the failed component, the I-V characteristic between pin 2  and pin 4 is equivalent to a weak resistance of few ohms, whereas these two pins should be isolated. The electrical  analysis indicated that, somewhere, there should be an  undesirable connection between the two isolated regions of the  circuit.


Failure analysis


X-ray and SAM inspections did not reveal any anomaly. In  order to localize the defect, mechanical grinding of the die  backside was performed and followed by Thermal Laser  Stimulation (OBIRCH localization). This technique has  enabled to identify the electrical current path (fig. 11).


Figure 11: OBIRCH localization (backside view) of the  leakage current path between the inductor B5 and the  inductor B2


In order to access to the physical defect and understand the  failure mechanism, a front-side access to the die was required. Due to the BCB/Cu technology of the component, wet  chemical etching could not be used, and the developed  technique combining laser and plasma was performed on this  component (fig.12). The optimized recipe described earlier in  the paper was used.


Figure 12: Optical view of the failed component after resin  removal. The red circle corresponds to the defect area


After this access to the die, and in order to reach the metal  lines and the defect, a slight BCB removal with standard RIE  equipment was then performed. In that aim, a mix of SF6(80%) and O2 (20%) was used. The defect was observed  thanks to a tilted SEM view (fig. 13).


Figure 13: SEM view of the defect


It shows a melting point without foreign particles. The failure  mechanism has been identified as an electrical overstress  which has melted the copper layers and created a current path  between the two inductors.


This failure analysis on a BCB/Cu technology in package  could not have been completely performed without reaching  the front-side of the die. The new decapsulation method  presented in this paper was the key to conclude on the failure  mechanism.


When working with BCB/Cu technologies in plastic package, it can be required to perform a front side approach of the die. The standard wet chemical etching is not appropriate since it  damages the layers stack. A complete solution was developed  and is presented in this paper. The optimized process consists  in performing a pre-opening of the resin package by laser  ablation and using a mix of CF4 and O2 (from 5% to 20% of  CF4) for plasma etching down to the die. This plasma recipe  was defined after a specific study on the impact of the plasma


on BCB. Depending on the plasma etching parameters, cracks  could be generated in the BCB. The described recipe is an  optimized process avoiding this artifact. It should be noted that  a small part of the BCB is etched by CF4 so that the top  surface of the BCB cannot be analyzed. However, this method  makes the analysis of the whole component possible without  damaging the active layers.


In the near future, an alternative to our solution will be  studied: the wet etching of the resin at low temperature (10°C). This new technique is described in reference [6]. This low  temperature significantly reduces the etch rate. It could lead to  a better control of the etching, giving the possibility to stop the  reaction before BCB layers etching. If this technique is  concluant, the advantage over our technique would be to  decrease the global process time of the decapsulation.

A Novel Low Cost Technique to Perform Concurrent Topside and Backside  Analysis of a Bare Die


Raj Kabadi, Win Thandar Swe


ON Semiconductor, Product Analysis Lab, Phoenix, Arizona, USA  Raj.Kabadi@Onsemi.Com


A novel low cost technique to facilitate concurrent topside and  backside imaging of a bare die for optical fault localization  purposes is presented. The technique overcomes the  restrictions posed by unavailability of a suitable package or  limited choices that may be present at a small scale packaging  lab. The difficulties imposed on backside preparation of  commonly used ceramic packages are overcome by providing  an alternative that is relatively less expensive and easier to  implement. This is accomplished by mounting the bare die on  a glass cover slip using a suitable adhesive and wire bonding  the bond pads to a specially designed printed circuit board. This method is being successfully utilized on multiple failure  analysis requests received in our lab.


HNO3:H2SO4 ratio


9:1
