Table 2. Results of acid ratio study showing mixture  and etch times, along with general indication of the  state of the wires.


Figure 7a. Opening using a 9:1 nitric acid to sulfuric  acid mixture.


Figure 7b. SEM micrograph for 5:1 mixture  decapsulation.


Figure 7c. SEM image of wires for 3:1 opening.


Figure 7d. SEM micrograph for 2:1 opening.


Figure 7e. Damage can be seen in SEM image of  opening performed using a 1:1 acid mixture at 10°C. The choice of acids is also important.  All openings  in these studies were performed using HNO3 100% fuming and H2SO4 20% fuming, which have faster  etch rates than lower quality (non-fuming or 90% fuming) acids.  In addition, these acids have been


demonstrated to be less damaging to metals and to  have a wider usable temperature range [5, Sesame  cookbook].


Etch volume is another parameter that plays an  important role in the etch rate.  A higher etch  volume contributes to the etch rate in several ways.  A higher etch volume creates a more turbulent flow  in the cavity which distributes the acid more evenly  and the increased pressure flushes out more quickly  any waste generated.  This insures that there is  always a fresh layer of material for the acid to  attack.  The pump used for these studies has a very  high efficiency pump, which creates very turbulent  flows, with etch volumes up to 8ml/min.


Furthermore, the rinse procedure following  decapsulation is very important in conserving the  state of the Cu wires. The rinse procedure  employed in these studies consisted of spraying  with acetone immediately after decapsulation to  rinse away any remaining waste products, followed  by a bath in hot DI water (60°-90°C) for roughly a  minutes, then a final jet rinse with isopropanol  before drying with nitrogen.  If the DI water bath is  not included, crystals will begin to form on the wires  after several hours.  The nature of these crystals  has been reported here and elsewhere [2] to be  copper sulfide.  It was also reported that a layer of  cupric oxide existed, which passivates the Cu wire  from acid attack along with the CuS.  The DI water  bath dissolves the sulfide and preserves the quality  of the wires.  We have achieved better results with a  DI bath at elevated temperature, rather than just a  simple rinse, which from our experience does not  always inhibit crystal formation.


Figure 8.  SEM photo of Cu wire cross section after  decapsulation by nitric/sulfuric acid mix.  In this case  the hot water rinse was not used and the resulting  crystal growth analyzed by EDX.


There exists an alternative to the initial two step  laser pre-decapsulation/acid etch approach  described here, where the objective was to expose  the die and stitches simultaneously. It is possible to  make a simpler laser pre-cavity above both die and  stitches, then expose just the die with the acid etch, followed by a return to the laser to expose the stitch  bonds. This can often give better results as it can be  difficult to expose the die and stitches  simultaneously, meaning that slightly longer etch  times than the absolute minimum would be


necessary.   It is possible to remove the remaining  EMC above the stitches in a very precise fashion  with the laser without damaging the wires or any  metallization layers that may exist on the lead  fingers.


A technique has been developed to decapsulate  EMC packages containing Cu wires/bonding without  damaging or diminishing the properties of the  device.  This method uses a combination of laser  ablation and low temperature chemical etch to fully  expose the die and stitch bonds without wire  thinning, or pad corrosion.  This approach takes  advantage of the fact that nitric acid attack on Cu is  greatly reduced at near and sub-ambient  temperatures. The goal is then to etch as fast as  possible within the constraint of the low temperature  levels.  The many steps which can be taken to  reduce the etch time include creating a pre-cavity in  the package above the areas of interest, choice of  optimum acid ratio, using high quality acids, and  employing turbulent, high volume acid flow rates.

New Front Side Access Approach for Low-k Dielectric/Cu Technologies in  Plastic Package


Amandine Aubert, Lionel Dantas de Morais, Stéphanie Pétremont


Technology & Failure Analysis Laboratory, STMicroelectronics, Tours, France   amandine.aubert@st.com


Nathalie Labat, Hélène Frémont


IMS laboratory, University Bordeaux 1, Bordeaux, France


This paper presents a new sample preparation process for front  side access for die with organic dielectric layers that are  encapsulated in plastic packages. The limitation of the  standard failure analysis flow is firstly described, showing the  damage caused by wet etching. Then, the decapsulation  method combining laser ablation and plasma etching is  presented. It is completed by the process optimization. The  final process makes it possible to perform failure analysis on  low-k/Cu technologies in plastic package either by the front  side or by the backside of the die.


Low-k/Cu technologies are more and more used in the  semiconductor industry. Their advantages over standard  structures are the reduction in signal delay, the layer  thicknesses and the improvement of thermal dissipation. On  this type of devices encapsulated in a plastic package, a limit  for applying a standard failure analysis flow was revealed. This paper shows a new approach to go beyond this limit.


Technology description


A schematic cross-section of the technology of interest is  presented in fig. 1.


Figure 1: Schematic stack of a BCB/Cu technology in package


The low-k dielectric is Benzocyclobutene (BCB), which is an  organic material. Copper is used as the conductive layer for


interconnections. The die is encapsulated in a plastic package  composed mainly of epoxy and glass fillers.


Standard failure analysis flow impact


In the failure analysis flow of plastic-encapsulated  components, either front side or backside access is needed  depending on the defect location. If front side access is  required, the standard process consists of a wet etching of the  plastic package using a mixture of hot fuming nitric and  sulfuric acids [1]. However, with BCB/Cu technologies, this  method is not usable. When the component is immersed in the  wet solution, the organic layers are also etched because both  materials are sensitive to the mix of nitric and sulfuric acids. The consequence may be catastrophic as presented in fig.2.


Figure 2: BCB/Cu technologies front side view after chemical  etching of the package


With increased complexity and functionality of integrated  circuit (IC) devices, presence of multiple metal layers, bond  over active structures, dummy metal layers and high density  routing signal lines, the need for performing backside imaging  for fault localization using various Scanning Optical  Microscopy (SOM) techniques has become more of a rule  rather than an exception. Many analyses require the dice to be   packaged in failure analysis friendly packages that can be  easily prepared to expose the silicon through the package  backside while still allowing for full functional testing of the  device [1][2][3]. Packaging choices can depend on the size  and capabilities of the resident packaging lab within a  company and might thus offer limited options. In many  instances, readily available open top side braze ceramic  packages are frequently used. These ceramic packages can  limit the analysis to topside only because the hard and brittle  nature of the ceramic presents a significant challenge for  backside preparation. Specialized tools like a precision milling  and polishing system might be needed for successful and  repetitive backside preparation of a die mounted within a  ceramic package.


The sample preparation method for backside and topside  imaging and analysis presented allows for functional testing of  a die while minimizing the dependence on availability of a  specific package type. This approach offers a low cost solution  that enables a failure analyst to perform both backside and  topside analysis anytime during the course of the analysis. Topside electrical probing, scanning electron microscopic (SEM) imaging and focused ion beam (FIB) device  modifications can also be carried out. The approach described  here is accessible to most failure analysis chemical  laboratories. Other than an ultraviolet (UV) source, the tools  needed are common to all IC failure analysis chemical labs.


Additional benefits of this approach include easy cleanup, an  ideal method for wafer yield analysis where multiple dice  from a Pareto of failures need to be analyzed, the possibility of  quick turn at the packaging lab and easy mount and demount  of the die once electrical analysis is completed.


Procedure


Printed Circuit Board


A special printed circuit board daughter card was designed and  fabricated using FR4 high Tg laminate 0.031” in thickness. Forty-four pads were laid out in a circular pattern with a board  cutout in the middle. The size of the board cutout was selected  to be such that the wire loops from the center of the circular  cutout to any of the forty-four pads would not exceed 10mm. This was based on discussions with our packaging lab and their capabilities. The forty-four pads were routed to forty-four  edge fingers. The pads and the edge fingers were electroplated  with soft gold to a thickness of 50 – 60 um and hardness less  than 90 on a Knoop scale.


Figure 1: Generic daughter card with a cutout in the middle. Forty-four pads are arranged around the periphery of the  cutout routed to each edge finger.


In order to hold the daughter card from figure 1 and to be able  to bias each of the forty-four pads, another test card was  designed and fabricated as shown in figure 2 below.


Figure 2: Interface Test card. The Test card has an  appropriate right angle edge connector that can accommodate


the 0.031” thick daughter card.


The test card was equipped with multiple test points and loops  so that external components like resistors, capacitors etc. could be inserted across pins and oscilloscope probes could be connected to any of the forty-four pins as needed. Subsequent  revisions of this test card also have a ribbon cable connector to  allow for the test card to be held inside a dark box during  analysis and bias can be applied at the other end of the ribbon  cable as needed.


Die Mounting Material


Figure 3: Materials needed to mount a bare die on to a glass  cover slip.


Figure 3 shows the materials needed for mounting a bare die  on to a microscope glass cover slip. The glass cover slip  selected was No. 1 (0.15mm thick) and 25mm x 25mm in size. The bare die is glued to a glass cover slip. The common  criteria we needed in the adhesives were as follows:


- Good bonding to glass and silicon surface.


- Low coefficient of thermal expansion.


- Ability to withstand mechanical stress.


- Remain transparent after cure.


Figure 4 shows the three types of adhesives that we used in  our experiments.


a) Allied EpoxyBond 110: A fast curing epoxy adhesive  commonly used to bond glass cover slips to small or  delicate samples. Once cured, it is chemically  resistant to most etchants and will not outgas under  vacuum.


b) Loctite clear Cyanoacrylate (CA) instant adhesive: Commonly available, easy to use, dissolves in  acetone and cures at room temperature within  minutes.


c) Master Bond UV14-3: is a flexible, UV curable  polymer system for bonding, sealing and coating with  the convenient feature of being removable by most  conventional solvents including Acetone. The cured  adhesive can be used over the exceptionally wide  temperature range of -50°C to +120°C. Since this  UV14-3 can be cured at room temperature it is ideal  for analysis of failing die which may recover or  change electrical behavior when subjected to elevated  temperatures.


Figure 4: Various adhesives that have been used to mount  bare die to glass cover slip.


Sample Preparation


As is typically the case with backside imaging, it is important  to backside polish the die to be analyzed. This die could be  one extracted from an existing package or from a sawed wafer  that was background. Follow the procedure below:


Step 1: Take a clean number 1, glass cover slip.


Step 2: Place a drop of either Allied EpoxyBond 110 or  Loctite CA adhesive, or MasterBond UV 14-3 at the center of  the glass cover slip. A drop of this adhesive is also placed at  the four corners of the cover slip, so that the cover slip can be  attached to the underside of the daughter card from figure 1.


Figure 5: A drop of adhesive is placed at the center of a glass  cover slip using a pipette.


Place the die on the drop of adhesive at center of the cover  slip. Allow the die to settle so that no air bubbles are trapped  in between the die and cover slip. The die can also be held  with Teflon tipped tweezers and gently moved laterally to  allow for proper spreading of the adhesive under the back  surface of the die.


Step 3: When using EpoxyBond 110, the daughter card is vacuum baked at 70°C for 30 minutes. Samples using  MasterBond UV14-3 are UV cured for 30 - 45 minutes using a


Logical Devices UV Eraser model QUV – T8. See figure 6  below. Samples using Loctite CA adhesive are cured at room  temperature for five minutes.


Figure 6: UV Eraser used to cure samples mounted using  Master Bond UV14-3. Daughter card is placed upside down to  expose the epoxy to the UV source.


Once sample preparation is completed, these units are  submitted to our packaging lab for wire bonding as shown in  figure 7.


Figure 7: Example showing a sample die that is ready for  backside imaging analysis using a SOM.


Backside Images


Once wire bonded, the samples were imaged backside using a  Hamamatsu PHEMOS-1000 (SOM) equipped with a 1.3um  Laser, a Si CCD and an InGaAs detector. Figure 8 below  shows IR laser scanned images of a single metal layer die  which was prepared the conventional way for backside  imaging by drilling a small cavity in the back of the package  to remove the mold compound and expose the copper flag. The copper flag was then etched away using concentrated  nitric acid. The die attach material was carefully scraped off  using a tooth pick.


Figure 8: Backside laser scanned images of a die still inside a  traditional plastic package, exposed backside for imaging  using conventional backside preparation method.


Figure 9 are images of an identical die removed from its package and mounted on a glass cover slip using EpoxyBond


110.


Figure 9: Backside laser scanned images of a die identical to  that from figure 8, removed from package and mounted on a  glass cover slip using EpoxyBond 110.


As is evident from these two sets of images from figures 8 and  9, the quality of the laser scanned image remains unaffected  when mounted on the glass cover slip. The above experiment


was repeated using Loctite and UV14-3 and the quality of the  images were found to be comparable to those from figure 8.


Next, the die from figures 8 and 9 were subjected to backside  Optical Beam Induced Resistance CHange (OBIRCH) analysis in order to compare the OBIRCH response to the


1.3um laser scan. Identical bias conditions and scan time were  used for both samples. The response to the 1.3um laser scans  are near identical to each other as seen in figures 10 and 11. This exercise was performed and compared on samples that  were mounted using Loctite and UV14-3 and the quality of the  results were found to be comparable to each other.


Figure 10: Backside OBIRCH analysis results for sample from  figure 8.


Figure 10 above is the response to the 1.3um laser under  presence of bias. This die is still mounted inside the plastic  mold compound.


Figure 11: Backside OBIRCH analysis results for sample from  figure 9.


Figure 11 above is the response of the die mounted on a glass  cover slip to the 1.3um laser under presence of identical bias  and same scan time as sample from figure 10. Note the  similarity in OBIRCH response between the two dice.


Further, the quality of the backside halogen illuminated image  for units from figure 8 and 9 were studied using a Si CCD and  an InGaAs detector. Figure 12 represents the halogen  illuminated Si CCD image of die from figure 8. Note that the  die substrate was not thinned.


Figure 12: Backside halogen illuminated Si CCD image of the  conventionally prepared die from figure 8.


Figure 13 is the halogen illuminated Si CCD image of the  glass cover slip die using EpoxyBond 110 from figure 9. Note  that the quality of the images was found to be comparable with  any of the three adhesives. Again, the die substrate in this case  has not been thinned.


Figure 13: Backside halogen illuminated Si CCD image of the  die from figure 9. Die mounted on glass cover slip.


Figure 14: Images captured through backside of die using an  InGaAs detector.


Figure 14, the images in the top row are those of  conventionally prepared sample (figure 8) and images in the  bottom row are those of glass mounted sample (figure 9). Note  these images are nearly identical with no discernible loss in  image quality when mounted on a glass cover slip. In fact  better contrast is observed for die on cover slip.


Backside Photoemission Microscopy (PEM) analysis was  performed to compare conventional approach with glass  mounted die. Identical bias and time were used. Backside  PEM images were obtained using a 50x objective on both the  unit with exposed silicon and the bare die mounted on a glass  cover slip. An equal sized area was defined around the PEM  site in both units and an intensity profile plot is shown next to  the superimposed photoemission image for each unit. As is  seen from the intensity profile images below, pixel count  corresponding to the PEM data is noted to be much higher for  the unit mounted on the glass cover slip under identical time  and bias conditions.


Figure 15: Backside PEM analysis comparing the PEM  response under identical bias and time between a  conventionally prepared unit (bottom row) with exposed  silicon and a bare die mounted on a glass cover slip (top row).


It is believed that the epoxy and glass cover slip combination  reduces the refractive index mismatch between the silicon and  air which reduces the reflection at the silicon interface  allowing more PEM signal to reach the detector. This results  in improved sensitivity and higher contrast images.


Case study one involved a 0.35 micron process wafer which had low yielding devices due to power-up overcurrent failures. Two-point probe testing on the multiple die confirmed


50x objective


Exposed die in mold compound


Bare die on glass cover slip


resistive shorts between power supply and GND. Since the  devices had five metal layers including a top dummy metal  layer covering the whole circuitry of interest, topside fault  isolation analysis was unfeasible and it was decided to prepare  the failing dice for backside analysis using the glass mounting  method. The failing dice were mounted and wire bonded to the  specialized daughter card (from figure 1). Subsequent  backside OBIRCH analysis performed in the failing state  revealed a single point site as shown in figure 16.


Figure 16: Backside OBIRCH analysis site identified with die  biased in the failing mode.


Subsequent destructive physical analysis and optical  inspection revealed an anomalous active area causing an  electrical short between a NWELL and an adjacent PWELL as  shown in figure 17.


Figure 17: Optical image of the large active area defect  observed at the OBIRCH site within the failing unit (left).


Case study two involved a 0.25 micron process wafer which  had numerous N-channel transmission gate structures failing  for high IDSS leakage. Six probes were needed to electrically  bias the individual devices. Furthermore, the transmission gate  devices were covered by top metal requiring backside


technique for fault isolation. The failing dice were mounted  and wire bonded to the specialized daughter card (from figure  1). Backside photoemission analysis performed in the failing  IDSS mode revealed a single point site within the transmission  gate structure as shown in figure 18.


Figure 18: Backside Photoemission Microscopy (PEM) analysis site identified with die biased in IDSS failing mode.


Focused ion beam (FIB) cross section analysis subsequently  confirmed a breach in silicide layer aligning with the PEM site  as shown in figure 19.


Figure 19: FIB cross section image of a submicron sized  breach at the edge of the contact silicide layer.


A simple low cost technique to perform concurrent backside  and topside analysis on a bare die has been described. The  technique aids in overcoming the limitations imposed by  unavailability of suitable failure analysis friendly packages  and sockets.

[1] Daniel L.Barton, Edward I. Cole and Karoline BernhardHofer, “Flip-Chip and Backside Sample Preparation  Techniques.” Microelectronics Failure Analysis: Desk  Reference, Fifth Edition, p 42-48.


[2] Micheal A. Gonzales, Joanna Kiljan, “A Novel Wet Etch  In-Situ decapsulation of Devices on Boards,” 36th ISTFA  Proceedings 2010, p 122-126.


[3] Dat Nguyen, Cuong Phan, Jeff Conner, Martin Smith, John Drummond, “A Novel Approach to Front-side  Deprocessing for Thinned Die after Backside Failure  Isolation,” 29th ISTFA Proceedings 2003, p 105-109,


Wei-Chih Wang, San-Lin Liew, Hua-Sheng Chen, Kuang-Liang Chen, Jian-Shing Luo. Physical & Electrical Failure Analysis Department

As the complexity of integrated circuits (IC) in dynamic  random access memory (DRAM) devices evolves to smaller  feature sizes and higher densities, the task of performing  successful failure analysis (FA) is getting increasingly difficult   and challenging. Traditional physical FA techniques involving  microscopic imaging of plan view or cross sectional structures  using scanning electron microscopy (SEM) and/or  transmission electron microscopy (TEM) are often not capable  of identifying  non-visible defects (NVD) such as junction  leakage, crystal lattice defects and gate oxide leakage which  have gradually become dominant root causes for IC failure.


To complicate matters, conventional fault localization  techniques, e.g.,  Emission Microscopy (EMMI) and  Light  Induced Voltage Alteration (LIVA)  are not adequate enough  to localize defects due to the resolution limits of the  techniques. On the other hand, while passive voltage contrast (PVC) can provide contact level inspection, this technique has  a  sensitivity limitation especially when dealing with marginal  fails and NVDs. Owing to the above mentioned limitations, the nano-probing tool which has both the device  characterization ability as well as the necessary sensitivity to  characterize the NVDs and marginal fails has emerged to be a  powerful tool in the FA community [1-5].


In this paper, we present a nano-probing technique on two  yield impact cases in DRAM technology. With the simulation  results and wafer acceptance test (WAT) data, the root causes  of the failure are identified. The first case is related to a die  that exhibited a high pin current issue during the parametric  test sequence at the early stage of our probe test. Based on the  transistor electrical characterization, simulation results, and  WAT data, insufficient Source/Drain (S/D) contact dopant  which makes the p-MOS transistor slower can be concluded.


The second case is related to a column fail expanding across a  shared sense amplifier (SA) circuit. Prior electrical failure  analysis (EFA) revealed that the SA transistor suffered poor  drive strength (IdSat). By using the nano-probing technique, the specific p-MOS transistor with a resistive contact issue


was successfully identified. This finding was in agreement  with the TEM cross-section (x-section) observation whereby  an additional oxide interlayer was observed at the resistive  contact. This case example shows that by probing and  studying the electrical behavior of the failing transistor, the  SA circuit’s low driving ability can be explained.


In our work, atomic force probe nano-probing system  equipped with an Agilent 4156C parametric analyzer  measurement system was used.


A. Case I: High pin current issue under parametric test


Parametric probe test revealed that the dice from the first  wafer in a wafer lot (up to 25 wafers) suffered high pin current  issue. By referring to WAT data, it was found that all  abnormal parametric results were p-MOS related, especially  the higher p-doped contact string resistance at the fail region.  To find the root cause, we de-processed the chip to device  level for electrical characterization of select p-MOS transistors  which have only one side with a LDD implant as shown in  Figure 1. In addition, the second wafer from the same lot was  used as the reference sample for the following electrical  comparison.


Figure 1: A schematic diagram showing the p-MOS implant  profile. Note that the LDD implant was only  on the drain side.


Figure 2 shows the fail and reference p-MOS Id-Vd curves.   The failing p-MOS had approximately 35.6% decreased drain  current in both forward sweep (FS) and reverse sweep (RS) test conditions as compared to the reference.


The Id-Vg curve in figure 3 shows the failing p-MOS to have  75mV more linear threshold voltage compared to the reference  p-MOS; This indicates that the failing p-MOS has slower  transistor turn-on ability. In order to identify the cause, the  off-state drain current analysis was performed. We applied  zero volts at the gate, well, and source; then swept the drain  voltage from 0V to -1.5V.


Figure 2: The fail and reference p-MOS Id-Vd curve under  Forward Sweep (FS) and Reverse Sweep (RS) conditions, at  Vg=1.2V, in linear scale.


Figure 3: The fail and reference p-MOS Id-Vd curve at Vd =-50mV, in log scale.


From the Idoff curve shown in Figure 4, an approximated  20pA lower off-state drain current was observed at the failing  p-MOS compared to the reference p-MOS, indicating a higher  channel resistance for the failing p-MOS. In order to clarify if  Gate Induced Drain Leakage (GIDL) or junction leakage  attributed to this higher resistance phenomenon,  the Idoff  current flow analysis was performed and the result is shown in  Figure 5. The result shows that the fail and reference Id and Is  profiles are  symmetric with each other, therefore it is purely


drain to source current flow. In other words, there is no sign of  GIDL or junction leakage contribution. These observations are  in agreement with TEM x-section analysis whereby no  obvious dimensional (such as thinner gate oxide thickness,larger gate bottom width) or physical defect (such as crack, crystal dislocation, or additional interlayer) was found. These  findings have led us to doubt if an implant anomaly is the  reason for causing the high resistance issue. In order to clarify, Id-Vg characteristics of 5 implant conditions (including the  POR condition) were simulated and compared with the failing  p-MOS Id-Vg curve. According to the simulation results  shown in Figure 5, if the Well (condition 2E) or the Halo (condition 4E) implant does not exist in the specific transistor, an increase of drain current compared to the POR (condition  1C) would be expected. On the other hand, if the LDD implant  does not exist (condition 3E) or the S/D implant (condition  5E) is insufficient, a decreased drain current characteristic that  is similar to the failing p-MOS Id-Vd characteristic shown in  Figure 3 will be exhibited. Note that for the LDD implant (condition 3E) simulation curve, we have assumed that the  LDD implant is completely missing. Indeed, in the case of the  LDD implant being insufficient, the drain current shift will be  comparable to the insufficient S/D implant condition (condition 5E). Nevertheless, up to this point, we are able to  comment that the failing p-MOS has no Well and Halo  implant issue. In addition, the remaining possible fail causes  for the high resistance p-MOS can be listed as followed:


(1) Missing LDD implant


(2) Insufficient LDD implant


(3) Missing S/D implant


(4) Insufficient S/D implant


(5) Combination of insufficient S/D and insufficient LDD  implants.


Figure 4: The fail and reference p-MOS off-state drain current  curve, at Vg=Vs=Vwell=0V. The drain voltage was swept  from 0 to -1.5 voltages.


Figure 5: The fail and reference p-MOS current flow situation  under the Idoff curve measurement.


Figure 6: p-MOS simulation Id-Vg curve of different implant  conditions.


Figure 7: The fail and reference p-MOS contact diode curve. The fail p-MOS contact shows higher resistance.


To investigate whether the LDD implant anomaly induced the  high resistance issue, the fail wafer and good wafer WAT data  was analyzed. It was found that the absence of LDD implant


would give the same ~35% decreased IdSat current and higher  Vt shift as those p-MOS with the LDD implant. This means


that the LDD implant is not the dominate cause of this device  anomaly; thereby we excluded the (1), (2) and (5) possibilities. Subsequently, we performed the p-MOS contact diode curve  analysis to clarify (3) and (4), i.e. insufficient or missing S/D  implant possibilities. In Figure 7, the failing p-MOS S/D  contacts show a higher but not serious resistive issue at the  forward bias region; therefore, this result excludes the (3) S/D  implant missing possibility.


Finally, insufficient S/D implant root cause is concluded. With  this anomaly, it is understood that the p-MOS device has  slower on-off ability and has a lower driving current. When  this occurs at circuit level, it will cause an operation error, and  then generates abnormal high current. This explains why the  die would fail during the parametric test.


B. Case II:  Low driving ability transistor in sense  amplifier circuit


In this case, the fail signature is a column fail expanding  across both sides of a sense amplifier circuit. Figure 8 is  showing a prior electrical failure analysis (EFA) result with  write timing tightening condition; the fail bits start from two  sides of the array edge as the write timing is tightened. Compared to the room temperature test condition, the higher  temperature test condition gives less fail counts. In reference   to WAT data; there is no digit line resistive issue, so the  possibility of a resistive column fail can be excluded. In  addition, the low driving ability caused by a problematic SA  transistor was identified. As a result, subsequent device  characterization was focused on transistors in the SA circuit.


Figure 8: Switching from loose to tight test conditions, the fail  bits situated farthest away from the SA failed first, indicating  that the SA suffered a low driving ability issue.


In brief, a SA circuit consists of two p-MOS and two n-MOS  as shown in Figure 9(a). The layout of n and p-MOS and the  source side x-section construction are shown in Figure 9(b) and(c). At first, we performed via level measurement at both n  and p-MOS region for diagnosing the resistive issue beneath  via level. This measured circuitry can be illustrated by the  schematic diagram in figure 9(d). The results in Figure 10 and  11 show that only the p-MOS via has resistive issue.


Figure 9(a) an electrical schematic of Sense Amplifier circuit.


(b) a layout of p and n-MOS in SA circuit. (c) a x-section  construction of n and p-MOS source contact of Sense  Amplifier circuit and (d) the electrical schematic of via level   measurement.


Figure 10: p-MOS via level I-V curve. Resistive issue was  observed at this via level I-V curve result.


Figure 11: n-MOS via level I-V curve. No resistance  difference between the fail and its neighboring reference nMOS.


In reference to the above-mentioned results; we have localized  the failure site to p-MOS region. Therefore, the subsequent  transistor characterization and electrical analysis was focused  on the p-MOS devices.


In Figure 12 and 13, there was no apparent Vt shift, but  around 65% decreased drain current is observed at the failing  p-MOS in the saturation region. This decreased saturation  current explains why the fail bits start from two sides of the  array edge  as the write timing was tightened.


Figure 12: The fail and neighbor reference p-MOS Id-Vg  curve analysis in linear scale.


Figure 13: The fail and neighbor reference p-MOS Id-Vg  curve analysis in log scale.


Next, we performed the contact diode analysis at both source  and drain sides of the failing p-MOS. In Figure 14, by fail and  neighbor reference I-V curve comparison, it was found that  the failing source contact was more resistive at the forward  bias region. This abnormality was supported by TEM bright  field x-section analysis whereby an additional 1.6nm oxide  interlayer was observed at the resistive source contact bottom.


Figure 14: The fail and neighbor reference p-MOS contact  diode curve in linear scale.


Figure 15: TEM bright field images of the fail source contact


(a) and the reference contact (b).


Note that a ~ 1.6nm abnormal interlayer which consists of  oxygen element was observed at the fail p-MOS source contact.

The use of a multifunctional current amplifier provides many  additional benefits to the EBAC analysis. It improves the  EBAC image signal-to-noise ratio (SNR). It also improves the  bandwidth response, as well as providing for additional  functionality such as offset current compensation and ability  to apply a voltage bias on the analyzed node.


Previously we reported on EBAC results obtained using the  differential voltage amplifier on SOI flip chip samples. This  paper will report on results obtained by using the new current  amplifier setup on additional sample types. A final section is  included to show stage current imaging analysis methods on  selected samples.  This paper will describe enhanced EBAC  analysis in further detail. The following topics will be  covered:


While it may seem obvious that increased bandwidth of the  EBAC amplifier would be beneficial to produce images faster, there is also a benefit for analyzing long traces with a single  nanoprobe. Several amplifiers available on the market have  bandwidths that come close to that of the SEM detector itself. This bandwidth can be used to perform EBAC at faster SEM  scan rates and enables an AC approach to finding opens on  long metal lines while only using one nanoprobe as an  alternative to the two nanoprobe approach described in [6]. Figure 6 shows the rise time versus gain specs of three current  amplifiers. Current amplifier “A” was used in our work. Also  added to this figure are the observed points where sharp  EBAC images were obtained with respect to scan rate (in  seconds/pixel) for current amplifier “A”. The “Sharp EBAC  Image” curve shows that the amplifier comes reasonably close  to keeping up with the max scan rate of the SEM. The gain  typically used in EBAC analysis is approximately 1e9 so that  further improvements may be obtained by using the faster  current amplifier “B”.


Figure 6.  Plot showing rise time of amplifiers versus gain  setting. The max speed of the SEM scan rate is shown at a  level of 0.1μs/pixel. Current amplifier “A” was used in this  work and a significant bandwidth improvement may be  possible by using current amplifier “B”. Current amplifier “C” is faster but has a maximum gain of only 1E8 V/A.


By using increased bandwidth, EBAC analysis can be  performed on nodes with significant amounts of capacitance. The resistance of the “open” along with the capacitance of the  un-probed side of the routing forms an RC time constant. If  the amplifier has a higher bandwidth than the RC of the  resistive node then a resistive open can be detected. In order to  show this we used the polysilicon fuse array again.


For a single nanoprobe EBAC image of a fuse with a low  resistance, the entire fuse array appears connected since all  fuses share the common node. The capacitance of the common  node is significant, but since resistance is low the resulting RC time constant is significantly faster than the current amplifier. Figure 7a shows a slow scan of the array when a low  resistance fuse is analyzed. Figure 7b shows the fast scan of  the same setup and all fuses again look the same. (Note that  the EBAC image of Fig. 7b is beginning to smear due to the  bandwidth limitations of the current amplifier.)


Figure 7: Reference EBAC images with low resistance  connection to fuse array. (a) Scan rate of about 10μs/pixel. (b) Scan rate of 1μs/pixel. There is no identified “open” in the  EBAC image when analyzing a low resistance fuse.


Figure 8: EBAC images of a high resistance connection (~60MΩ) to the fuse array. (a) Scan rate of about 100μs/pixel.


(b) Scan rate of 10μs/pixel. (c) Scan rate of 1μs/pixel. (d) A  simplified circuit schematic. In this case since the RC time  constant of the nanoprobed node routing is fast and the RC of  the common node is slower than the amplifier response time,the resistive element (fuse) can be detected while using a single nanoprobe.


A high resistance fuse connection was analyzed next and the  results are shown in Fig. 8. In this case the common node and  adjacent polysilicon nodes have the same capacitance as the  case shown in Fig. 7, but in this case there is also a significant


resistance (60MΩ) between the capacitance and the EBAC


nanoprobe. The resulting RC time constant for the routing  beyond the high resistance fuse is slower than the bandwidth  of current amplifier “A” and the resistive element is detected  as shown in Figs. 8b and 8c. At faster scans the routing  between the EBAC nanoprobe and the open is fast and is  highlighted by the AC EBAC scan.  A similar result was  obtained for a 20MΩ fuse.  Figure 8d shows a simplified  schematic representation for this particular sample. These  results are a proof of concept of the AC scanning technique for  identifying resistive connections.


Detection Limits


By using the results of this section the detection limits of  resistances can be summarized. There are different resistance  detection limits for different configurations. Keep in mind for  long nodes it is desirable to use only one nanoprobe since the  second nanoprobe may need to be landed hundreds of microns  away. The trace resistance detection limits for various setups  including single nanoprobe, two nanoprobe, DC (slow) and  AC (fast) configurations are shown in Fig. 9. In order to  quantify the AC detection limits the results are given in terms  of trace length.  An average value of capacitance per trace  length for all lower metal layers was used to determine the RC  time constants. Note that the noise inherent to our system  limits the two nanoprobe configuration to about 7.5kΩ, but the  amplifier itself is capable of an order of magnitude better  detection limit.


Figure 9: This image shows detection limits for several  different configurations.


Bulk Technology Sample Prep for EBAC


A deprocessing recipe similar to that originally developed for  SOI technology [6] was modified and used to successfully  prepare bulk technology samples for back side EBAC  analysis. The resultant deprocessed surface has more  topography than the equivalent SOI surface as shown in Fig. 10, but the 45 degree approach angle of the nanoprobes is  adequate for landing on the exposed silicide interconnects. The modified recipe is shown in Table 1 and a successfully  prepared 40nm bulk sample is shown in Figs. 11 and 12.


Figure 10: The upper image shows the topography of an SOI  sample that has been prepared for backside EBAC. The lower  image shows the equivalent bulk sample.


Back-Side Sample Preparation


Planar backside deprocessing that leaves silicided  source/drain/gate regions exposed and electrically isolated is  fundamental to backside EBAC. The use of wet chemical  etchants with good selectivity makes this a straightforward  and repeatable process. The main difference between the wet  etch preparation of an SOI device compared to a bulk sample  is that the polysilicon is removed on the bulk sample by the  TMAH. This is because there is no SOI buried oxide layer to  act as an etch stop during the silicon substrate deprocessing  step. On bulk devices only the gate oxide acts as a TMAH  barrier and it is removed faster than the total etch time  required to clear the die of all active silicon. However, EBAC  can still be performed successfully. This is accomplished by  landing nanoprobes on the remaining silicided region of the


gate as compared to landing on the actual gate polysilicon  material as described for SOI devices in [6]. Furthermore, the  nitride spacer left behind acts as a guide when landing the  nanoprobe in the channel where poly once resided.


Table 1: Deprocessing sequence to prepare bulk-technology  device for backside EBAC .


Step


Action


Comment


Physical Removal of  Silicon substrate via  mechanical polishing


The substrate is thinned  to within 25um of active  silicon


Tetramethylammonium  hydroxide (TMAH) etch


Removes remaining  substrate silicon, stops  on trench oxide and  silicided regions of all  source/drain/gate areas(all poly is etched)


10:1 BOE etch (optional)


Can be used to thin  remaining trench oxide  for better nanoprobe access to ultra small W  devices


Figure 11: A back-side SEM image of a bulk technology  device after de-processing using the procedure of Table 1. Back side de-processing allows for nanoprobes to be landed  on any node and is non invasive to the back end of line  metallization.


In this work, we perform AFP nano-probing technique on two  yield impact cases in our DRAM technology. By comparing  the nano-probing electrical results with simulation data and  WAT data, (1) insufficient (S/D) contact implant which causes  slower p-MOS turn-on and (2) resistive source contact that  causes lower driving ability in a SA transistor issue are  concluded.


By coupling WAT data, simulation IV curves and nanoprobing electrical results, the analysis of marginal defects and  NVD is more conclusive and successful.


This paper describes the use of Electron Beam Absorbed  Current (EBAC) mapping performed from the backside of the  device as a means of locating metallization defects on state of  the art bulk silicon and SOI based microprocessor  technologies. It builds on previous work which focused   only on flip-chip SOI samples. This paper will demonstrate  additional EBAC techniques and the ability to analyze devices  processed in bulk silicon technology.  Also included are the  results obtained from an SOI device mounted in a non flipchip package type. Additional details related to sample  preparation, equipment used, and improved practices are  described.


In order to keep pace with the increasing number of transistors  within the modern microprocessor, it has become necessary to  use ten or more levels of back end metallization as  interconnect. During the failure analysis of such products it is  common to encounter instances of defectivity in the  metallization. Typical defect mechanisms can be caused by  issues during fabrication such as contamination, metal  patterning defects, resistive interconnects or other longer term  problems such as electro-migration.


Locating defects of these types can be challenging and  problematic on modern devices. Laser assisted device  alteration (LADA) in combination with time resolved light  emission (TRLEM) probe techniques are very effective at  locating failing nets [1-3]. However, the specific location of  any physical defect in most cases is still only known to be  somewhere between the transistor driving the net of interest  and the gate at the other end some distance away. Unfortunately, it is common for signal nodes to traverse eight  or more metal layers and route hundreds of microns.


Nanoprobing can effectively provide electrical I/V  characterization of any suspect routing. Conventional wisdom  dictates that nanoprobing and EBAC analysis are done from  the top side of the processor in a binary search cut and  measure type methodology. Due to the complexity of the  modern microprocessor this approach is rarely feasible. On  state of the art technologies using bulk or SOI engineering, it  is possible to perform the necessary nanoprobe work from the  back side after chemical deprocessing and removal of various  layers of materials to expose silicide landing pads required for  electrical contact.


Once nanoprobes are landed from the back, Electron Beam  Absorbed Current mapping is a technique that allows for  mapping the current flow in a net in its entirety. This has  advantages over using topside EBAC approaches [4-5].  To  minimize higher kV exposure to the sample initial EBAC  analysis is performed at low acceleration voltages and once a  faulty node is identified then the acceleration voltage is chosen  based upon the penetration depth required for a given metal  layer [6-7]. Defect locations can be rapidly pinpointed for  physical analysis. Nodes that are shorted together are quickly  identified, as well as electrical opens/resistive defects if they  exist anywhere along that net.  Figure 1 shows a typical EBAC  setup as previously reported [6].


Figure 1: Schematic of current sensing circuit based upon a  differential voltage amplifier that was used in previous work.


The results detailed in this paper were obtained using an  improved EBAC detection circuit consisting of a dedicated  current amplifier instead of the previous differential voltage  amplifier setup of Fig. 1. Figure 2 shows the new current  amplifier configuration.  Improved amplification and detection  capabilities with the new current amplifier setup allow for


defect localization of resistive type issues down to the kΩ


regime.


1. Amplifier Functionality and Advanced Uses


2. Bulk Sample Preparation and EBAC Results


3. Non-Flipchip Package Type Case Study


4. Stage Current Imaging


Amplifier Functions and Features


Previously EBAC results in [6] were obtained using a series  sampling resistor and a rack based voltage differential  amplifier. Current amplifier products are commercially  available that offer improved performance and additional  features for EBAC analysis. One such current amplifier has  been used to achieve significant improvements. The following  improvements and features will be discussed in this section:


1. Improved SNR


2. Biased Nanoprobe EBAC Capabilities


3. Offset Current Capabilities


4. Improved Bandwidth


5. Improved Detection Limits


Improved Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR)


The SNR improved significantly with use of the dedicated  current amplifier and has been quantified. Figure 3 shows  typical EBAC images obtained using the differential voltage  amplifier and the current amplifier. Analysis of the image data  reveals a SNR improvement of approximately 2.5x. More  importantly, using the increased SNR amplifier allows the  analyst to produce a reasonable secondary electron image  using the nanoprobe as the detector as can be observed in the  surrounding area of the EBAC image of Fig. 3b. Often times  the nanoprobe detected secondary electron image is sufficient  so that a separate SEM image is not required for overlay  purposes.


Figure 3: (a) Typical image obtained using a differential  voltage amplifier like that of Fig. 1 to detect EBAC. (b)Typical image using the current amplifier. The SNR of (a) is  approximately 4.6 while that of the current amplifier image in


(b) is 11.9.


Note surface secondary  electron image is strongest near detection nanpoprobe


Detection  nanoprobe


EBAC Signal


Adjacent nanoprobe is not


landed but is detected by  secondary electrons.


(A) Differential Voltage Amplifier   Based Image


(B) Current Amplifier


Based Image


Biasied Nanoprobe EBAC Capabilities


Biasing the nanoprobe with respect to the grounded sample  allows the analyst to explore and create the most definitive  image for identifying the signal routing layers as well as  potential defects. The negative bias voltage (-5V) applied to  the nanoprobe in Fig. 4 increases the secondary electron yield  and causes metal layers closer to the surface to have a higher  secondary electron emission rate (and appear darker in the  image). At deeper metal layers there is no significant emission  so that the absorbed electrons are dominant and result in a  bright response. When a positive voltage bias is applied to the  nanoprobe, the entire EBAC trace of the routing will appear  bright.


Figure 4: Example of EBAC image using a -5V nanoprobe  bias. The polarity of the connections used for this image cause  dark responses where electrons are emitted and bright  responses where electrons are absorbed. Note that since all  silicon is removed, the use of a -5V bias is safe to the sample.


Offset Current Capabilities


Attempting to measure a low resistance open (< 1MΩ) using a  two-nanoprobe setup has a noise issue to consider. Small  voltage differences between the nanoprobes are typical due to  differences in ground shielding (~mV noise levels are typical). A 1mV noise source through a 1MΩ resistance creates a 1nA  noise source. This amount of current is approximately 10x that  of the EBAC signal itself and if large enough can saturate the  current amplifier. Minimizing ground differences is important,but the configuration and integration of the equipment itself  often limits what one can do in this regard. By using offset  current, the effects of the unwanted ground noise can be  reduced. One important note is that the offset current can only  be used to reduce the average DC noise. Any AC noise  component will still be present, but if the AC component is  not too large, its effect can be reduced by image averaging.


In order to investigate the effectiveness of the offset current a  polysilicon fuse array was analyzed. The array of polysilicon fuses are shown in Fig. 5a. Some of these fuses have been  programmed while some have not resulting in a range of


resistances with which to experiment. All fuses share a  common node that gets routed to the active region connection  at the right nanoprobe location in Fig. 5. Figures 5b-d show  fuses with successively lower resistances that are being  analyzed.  It was empirically determined that a voltage offset  of about 15-20mV existed between the nanoprobes.  As the  fuse resistance decreases, the increasing noise current  saturates the current amplifier, making the EBAC analysis  impossible.  To compensate, successively larger offset  currents were set using the “auto-compensate” function of the  amplifier to enable detection of the EBAC current.  The  resulting EBAC images for lower resistances have lower SNR,but the location of the resistive element can be detected.


Figure 5: Two-probe EBAC imaging of a polysilicon fuse  array is shown in this figure. (a) SEM image showing fuse  bank and the two nanoprobes. (b) A 50MΩ polysilicon fuse. The strong EBAC signal shows where the high resistance fuse  has been opened. For this level of resistance, offset current is  not required, but if used was found to be about 400pA. (c) A  160kΩ fuse. In order to image this fuse the offset current was  set to 161nA. (d) At a polysilicon fuse resistance of 7.5kΩ the  offset current was set to 70uA and a barely useable image was  produced.  Noise in these images increases as lower  resistance fuses are nanoprobed.
